Preface

Primary agriculture in the developed economies is increasingly subject to environmental,
human, plant and animal health and animal welfare regulations. Research in this volume was
motivated by the use of cross compliance as a means of harmonizing enforcement of these
types of regulations in the EU. Cross compliance makes the receipt of farm support
payments contingent on meeting regulatory standards. In general, compliance with
regulation gives rise to increased costs at the farm level. A commonly expressed concern, by
EU farmers but also by farmers in other jurisdictions, is that compliance with regulations
has adverse effects on competitiveness and ultimately on farm business viability. As
agriculture is increasingly integrated into global trade liberalization, differentials in national
regulatory compliance costs, potentially, can have significant effects on trade flows.
Comparisons of regulatory compliance costs, even comparison of regulatory standards,
across jurisdictions, is complex. Different polities operating with differing constitutional
allocations of authorities, can have different agencies or even levels of government being
responsible for different categories of regulations and standards. It is not uncommon for the
farming industry in any particular jurisdiction, in the face of these challenges, to perceive
the standards that it is required to meet as higher than those which its competitors must
achieve. This volume investigates the cost implications and competitiveness effects
resulting from regulations and standards in the fields of environment, health and animal
welfare and production-linked rules of „good agricultural conditions‟ for a range of
agricultural products.
     The book is the product of an international collaboration among researchers from the
EU, North America and New Zealand. The work presented in this volume has been
(co-)funded by the project „Facilitating the CAP reform: Compliance and competitiveness
of European agriculture‟ (EU 6th Framework Programme for Research, Technological
Development and Demonstration, Priority 8.1, contract no. SSPE-CT-2005-006489). In
addition to chapters based on research conducted under this project, we have solicited
additional chapters to enrich the range of perspectives in the volume. The content of this
book does not represent the official position of the European Commission and is entirely
under the responsibility of the authors.
     Collaboration on this project has increased our understanding as researchers of the
range of modalities and forms of environmental regulation that are being applied to
agriculture in the developed countries. We also came to appreciate the importance of
differences in production systems and in environmental, social and economic pressures
impinging on agriculture in the countries considered in the study. We are grateful to the
European Commission for their financial support for this research.

Floor Brouwer, Glenn Fox and Roel Jongeneel
July 2012

This page intentionally left blank
Chapter 1

Introduction

Floor Brouwer, Glenn Fox and Roel Jongeneel



Standards, compliance costs and competitiveness1

Farmers in developed countries are increasingly subject to regulations and standards for
environmental protection (e.g. protection of groundwater, quality of surface water, air and
soil; conservation of habitats and species), food safety (e.g. hygiene rules, requirements on
animal feed) and animal welfare (e.g. housing standards). These regulations and standards
can influence production costs, but the extent of this influence can vary across agricultural
sectors, geographical areas and farming systems. Brouwer and Ervin (2002) identified and
compared policy approaches that have been used to internalise external costs related to the
environment, food safety and animal welfare across developed country agricultures. On-
farm constraints were compared, taking into account differences in policy models (form of
standards and legislation affecting agriculture, and a mix of national, federal, regional and
local measures), farming practices (e.g. prevailing production and farm management
systems, technologies available to the farmer, and the investment cycle on the farm) and
biophysical conditions of the farm and its surrounding area.
     Much has changed in the decade since Brouwer and Ervin‟s (2002) research was
published. The scope of standards and regulations has broadened, the performance
standards are generally higher and it is becoming more and more important for farmers to
consider the full range of options at their disposal to meet these standards and regulations.
The range of measures taken by farmers depends on the nature of the constraints and the
management and technology options available to address those constraints. Measures can
include the construction of new housing systems to comply with animal welfare rules,
buying new fertilization systems to comply with nutrient control measures and introducing
milking cooling systems to comply with food safety rules. Examples of the costs of
compliance in the field of environment are increased labour requirements, particularly for
record keeping, increased use of land (through leasing or buying land to satisfy standards
for manure application), higher capital investments (e.g. housing systems to control

1
  Competitiveness is rooted in the concepts of price, quality and market share and broader concepts
like innovative capacity of firms in the agrifood chain. From a producers‟ perspective,
competitiveness can be described as „the ability to supply goods and services in the location, form
and place sought by buyers, at prices that are as good or better than those of other potential suppliers,
while earning at least the opportunity cost of the return on resources employed‟ (Porter, 1990).

©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                                    1
2     Introduction

emissions of ammonia) and increase in variable costs (e.g. for the storage, transport and
spreading of livestock manure, feeding of animals, permit costs, training and record
keeping). The main cost components involved in meeting higher food safety standards
include increased labour (e.g. for registration of animals, administration, audits), capital
investments (e.g. computers, cleaning equipment) and higher variable costs (e.g. introduce
ear tags, record keeping). With regards to animal welfare, the most important categories of
costs are housing and management costs and labour and operational costs.

Modelling framework

In addition to our review and comparison of policies and standards across the jurisdictions
represented in this study, we also wanted to investigate the magnitude of competitiveness
and trade effects quantitatively. Given the range of commodities and countries included in
this study, it was important to have a consistent modelling approach to facilitate
comparisons across countries and products as well as with other research. We elected to use
the GTAP (Global Trade Analysis Project) model. The standard GTAP model is a multi-
region, multi-sector, computable general equilibrium (CGE) model, which assumes perfect
competition and constant returns to scale. An important property of the model is its ability
to cope with bilateral trade and to account for potential heterogeneity between goods from
different origins. GTAP is coordinated by the Center for Global Trade Analysis, which is
housed in the Department of Agricultural Economics at Purdue University. GTAP‟s goal is
to improve the quality of quantitative analysis of global economic issues within an
economy-wide framework. Since its inception in 1993, the GTAP model has become a
common „language‟ for conducting global economic analysis. The modelling system offers
a variety of products, including: data, models, and utilities for multi-region, applied general
equilibrium analysis of global economic issues. We use the GTAP model database
calibrated on 2001 data. The sectoral or commodity coverage for our modelling includes
dairy, beef, cereals and pork. Limitations on data and modelling capacity precluded using
the GTAP model for fruits and vegetables. Our general strategy was to use the 2001
calibration to solve the model without the additional costs of meeting regulatory standards,
then to recalibrate the relevant portions of the model to reflect unilateral regulation in EU
countries, then to recalibrate the model once again to reflect multilateral regulation in the
developed countries. The specific regulatory costs reflected our comparison and review of
policies for each specific commodity or industry. Adjustments in the calibration of the
GTAP model were based on farm level estimates of compliance costs or estimates obtained
from secondary sources.

Sectoral aggregation

Aggregation is the second step in developing the relevant modelling framework to assess
the impact of standards on the external competitiveness of European agriculture. The lowest
aggregation of the GTAP 6 Data Package is 87 regions, 57 sectors and 5 production factors.
The sectors are aggregated to three main sectors, i.e. food, manufacturing and services. In
our modelling framework, we will always consider four sectors, i.e. (i) the sector under
research (beef, pigmeat, dairy or cereals), (ii) the rest of the food sector, (iii) manufacturing
and (iv) services.
                                                                                     Introduction        3

     It is important to note that the beef sector is not disaggregated in GTAP. In Table 1.1
we compare GTAP sectors (GSC) and standard central product classification (CPC) 2.
GTAP‟s „cmt‟ variable, for instance, includes eight subsectors. A splitcom utility enables
splitting existing GTAP sectors for all regions of interest. If the appropriate data are
lacking, reasonable assumptions can be made. The program will make the necessary splits
based on any input provided.

Factoral aggregation

We used the full disaggregation3 of five production factors available in GTAP to model
production factors, i.e. (i) land, (ii) unskilled labour, (iii) skilled labour, (iv) capital and (v)
natural resources.

Regional aggregation

Defining representative regional aggregation models for each sector is important in GTAP
modelling. The regional aggregation of the model should represent the major forces at stake
in global trade. In order to identify the major trade partners, we followed the following
procedure:




2
  The central product classification (CPC) is a classification based on the physical characteristics of
goods or on the nature of the services rendered. Each type of good or service distinguished in the
CPC is defined in such a way that it is normally produced by only one activity as defined in
International Standard Industrial Classification (ISIC). The CPC covers products that are an output of
economic activities, including transportable goods, non-transportable goods and services.
3
  Some authors have modelled the effects of standards by shocking two production factors in GTAP
factors, i.e. (i) unskilled labour and (ii) capital (Cassells and Meister, 2001).
4    Introduction

   Generate the full regional disaggregated trade matrix of 87 regions using GTAP.
   Create a EU-15 aggregate. We used the EU-15 as the EU aggregate because the GTAP
    database is constructed on 2001 data, i.e. before accession of the 10 New Member
    States.
   Select the trade partners that represent at least 5% of global exports or imports of the
    commodity or sector being studied. This step ensures that the major exporters and
    importers of the global sector are included.
   Aggregate the remaining countries in two regions: (i) the rest of OECD (ROECD)
    countries and (ii) the rest of the world (ROW).
   Correct for intra-trade. In GTAP, aggregating does not eliminate intra-trade. Therefore,
    the trade matrix has to be corrected for this by setting the intra-trade of the EU-15, the
    ROECD and the ROW to zero and recalculating the totals of the matrix.

The organization of the book

The book focuses on the key environmental, human health and animal welfare issues
affecting agriculture in the EU, US, Canada and New Zealand. The competitiveness and
trade implications of domestic policies and regulations are assessed for a range of
agricultural commodities, differentiating between farm, sector and global levels of
competitiveness. The first part of the book highlights changes taking place in developed
country agricultures.
     Chapter 2 documents the EU experience with cross compliance as a means to improve
the linkage between the receipt of CAP payments and a minimum level of environmental
protection. Kaley Hart and her co-authors review the rationale and objectives of cross
compliance within the CAP. This form of cross compliance introduced is likely to increase
rates of compliance with existing legislation. Its extension beyond the environmental
domain to animal diseases and food safety, as well as farm animal health and welfare
legislation means that it provides incentives to increase compliance with a range of EU
legislation that is relevant to farming.
     Chapter 3 provides the reader with an understanding of US agri-environmental policy
including a review of the approaches that have been used and a description of the overall
regulatory context. Jonathan Winsten and his co-author present case studies that
characterize compliance costs of key regulations and standards affecting the dairy and swine
sectors in selected states. Comparisons between US and EU agri-environmental policy
strategies are presented.
     Chapter 4 examines agricultural environmental policy in Canada. Canadian agriculture,
relative to most of the developed world, is a low-input intensity production system. Predrag
Rajsic and his co-authors highlight the implications of this for the demand for
environmental regulation in Canada compared to the EU. The nature of agricultural
production in Canada may explain the voluntary nature of many important aspects of
agricultural environmental policy in Canada. Canadian voluntary programmes, such as the
Environmental Farm Planning Initiative, have been successful in involving farmers in
actively solving environmental problems on their farms. Nevertheless, Canada does have a
comprehensive system of federal, provincial and municipal environmental laws functionally
equivalent to the EU Directives. Costs of environmental compliance are lower than in most
of the EU but should be expected to increase in the future, as the environmental pressure
from agriculture and urban-rural conflict increases.
                                                                            Introduction    5

     Agricultural environmental issues in New Zealand are discussed in Chapter 5. Anton
Meister and Nicole Beechey clarify the decentralized and outcome oriented approach that
has been adopted by a country that is proud of its clean and green image. However, more
intensive pastoral land use practices continue to put pressures on soil and water quality, and
biodiversity. Guidelines for sustainable land management are implemented and sustainable
practices need to be adopted by land managers to control effluent discharge. Following a
time period of voluntary action and non-regulatory approaches, regulation has gained
importance for the management of water quality, soil erosion, the preservation of
biodiversity and greenhouse gas emissions.
     Farmers are increasingly involved in supply-chain driven certification schemes,
targeted towards the quality and safety of products, as well as the production methods
adopted regarding environment and animal welfare. In Chapter 6, Roel Jongeneel and
Thomas Herzfeld compare voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU.
There are similarities between cross compliance and certification schemes, but the benefits
of one approach compared to the other depend on the ability to set appropriate standards.
Also, membership of a certification scheme may enhance the likelihood of the farmers
complying with environmental standards.
     Part II of the volume offers a commodity approach linking public concerns with trade
and competitiveness.
     In Chapter 7, Irina Bezlepkina and her co-authors analyse the impacts of the Nitrates
Directive and the Identification & Registration Directive on the EU dairy sector (Germany,
France, Italy, the Netherlands and the UK) and its key competitors (the US and New
Zealand). The negative impact of the analysed measures on EU imports and exports is less
than 1%. When the standards for nitrate pollution control taken by the US and New Zealand
are taken into account along with a full compliance assumption in all countries, this would
only marginally improve EU exports. The trade impacts obtained under the assumption of
no changes in key competitor countries can be interpreted as upper bounds of the likely
trade impacts.
     Chapter 8 examines two categories of regulatory compliance costs for the beef industry
in the EU and the US. Kees de Roest and his co-authors argue that environmental
regulations related to protecting water quality can increase farm costs in the beef sector.
Among the very different beef production systems existing in the EU and in the US,
intensive large scale finishing farms are most affected by this policy. Food safety policies,
including regulations on the use of growth hormones, have effects on all types of beef
farms. Compliance costs associated with these policies have a larger effect on farm costs
than environmental legislation. Both policies have a significant impact on the international
competitiveness of beef on the world market. In particular, a ban on the use of growth
hormones in the US would reduce US world beef exports by 36%.
     Chapter 9 assesses the impact of compliance costs for farms producing cereals. Roel
Jongeneel and his co-authors provide insight into the cereal market in the EU and main
production regions around the world. The requirements related to Good Agricultural and
Environmental Conditions (GAECs) in the EU have a bigger impact on cereal production
than do compliance with other environmental standards. The authors conclude that
production costs of cereals increase by some 1% as a result of compliance with regulations,
resulting in changes in international trade of cereals of less than 2%.
     Chapter 10 explores the trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in
the hog sectors of the EU, US and Canada. Kees de Roest and his co-authors assess the
6    Introduction

impacts of improved compliance with the Nitrates Directive by hog producers in the EU.
Total compliance costs could increase production costs by up to 5%. Given current
compliance rates with this legislation, however, the additional costs of full compliance
across the EU might be less than 1% of total production costs. Implementation of the Clean
Water Act (CWA) in the US could increase production costs by around 1%, and
implementation of nutrient management regulations in Canada could increase production
costs in the range between 2 and 4%. Such cost increases can have considerable
implications for international trade. Cost increases of animal welfare rules in the hog sector
of the EU have limited implications for international trade.
     Compliance with environmental standards and the adoption of GAECs are important in
the fruit and vegetables sector. Consuelo Varela-Ortega and Paloma Esteve compare costs
of compliance with the Nitrates Directive and pesticide regulations across farms in parts of
Spain. The authors argue that compliance with standards might even reduce costs, mainly in
highly productive regions where changes in management practices (e.g. introduction of pest
management practices) improve efficiency. The authors conclude that environmental
standards present important challenges to the fruit and vegetables sector in Europe.
However, they claim that certification schemes could potentially strengthen the competitive
position of this sector, largely through better market access, premium prices and possibly
cost savings.
     Emerging policy perspectives are identified in Part III of the volume. Chapter 12
provides a perspective of developing countries on the role of the WTO in solving SPS
agreement and TBT agreement trade conflicts caused by government regulations. The role
of private standards impacting trade patterns is also highlighted by Sílvia Helena Galvão de
Miranda. Four Brazilian international disputes related to sanitary and technical issues
illustrate the complexity of challenges: the risk analysis requirements to import wheat from
the US; the Japanese phytosanitary requirements to control fruit-fly in imported mangoes;
the European aflatoxins regulation on Brazil nuts; and the regionalization principle in FMD.
The ability of developing countries to comply with technical requirements is particularly
influenced by investments on technological infrastructure and human capital.
     The changing policy context of meeting standards in agriculture is explored by Vicki
Swales in Chapter 13. She explores the role of, and need for, policy interventions to
promote standards in relation to agriculture and environmental protection in the EU. She
concludes that while there are broad similarities in policy approaches in the EU, US,
Canada and New Zealand, there are however important differences, possibly due to the
complex interactions between agriculture and the environment.
      A long-standing concern in environmental policy making is that setting high standards
for environmental protection in one jurisdiction might prompt emission intensive industries
to relocate to less demanding regulatory environments. This is sometimes referred to as the
pollution haven hypothesis. In Chapter 14, Alfons Weersink reviews the results of two
studies of this hypothesis for the livestock sectors in North America. The analysis for the
US livestock sector suggests that regional production shares for hogs, and to a lesser extent
dairy, have increased in regions in relatively more lenient regulatory regimes.
     Regions within Canada, the EU, and the US differ in the way they attempt to control
pollutants stemming from potato production. Chapter 15 applies the actual agro-
environmental policy approaches from each region to the same watershed to simulate
differences in profits, costs, and sediment abatement levels. John Jatoe and his co-authors
compare the cost-effectiveness of three different policies. Their general finding is that a
                                                                              Introduction    7

uniform approach – e.g. requiring the cover crops for all farms – is more cost-effective than
approaches targeting additional conservation practices in more erosive regions within the
watershed.
     Chapter 16 reflects on the environmental compliance costs in developed country
agricultures, and its implications for trade and competitiveness. Glenn Fox and Floor
Brouwer highlight the importance of enforcement mechanisms and implementation, subjects
which often have been undervalued in research on environmental policies. The authors
highlight some of the achievements from this volume:

1.   Clarification of the relevant reference standards for environmental performance in
     agriculture;
2.   Documentation of the increasing importance of voluntary private standards;
3.   Explanation of the importance of functional equivalence in the comparative analysis of
     environmental regulation; and
4.   Examination of the relationship between historical trends in input intensity and
     demands for environmental regulatory response.

This concluding chapter also compares market and non-market failures as contributing
causes of environmental degradation. The authors argue that important legal and
institutional dimensions often are overlooked, when examining externalities. They draw
attention to Coase‟s concept of legalized nuisance and its connection to „Right to farm‟
policies.

References
Brouwer, F. and D. Ervin (eds) (2002), Public Concerns, Environmental Standards and Agricultural
     Trade, CAB International, Wallingford, UK.
Cassells, S.M. and A.D. Meister (2001), Cost and trade impacts of environmental regulations:
     effluent control and the New Zealand dairy sector. Australian Journal of Agricultural and
     Resource Economics, 45:257-74.
Porter, M.E. (1990), The Competitive Advantage of Nations. Free Press, New York.
This page intentionally left blank
Chapter 2

The role of cross compliance in greening EU
agricultural policy

Kaley Hart, Martin Farmer and David Baldock



Introduction

Over the past thirty years farming in Europe has become subject to an increasing body of
EU legislation, addressing agricultural practices and setting new standards in relation to the
environment, food safety, and farm animal health and welfare. This process continues, but
has given rise to growing concerns about unsatisfactory levels of compliance, and raises
questions about the extent to which these standards are delivering, particularly for the
environment. Against this background a debate began in the 1990s about cross compliance
as a policy tool for improving conformity with standards at farm level. After an initial
period in which Member States could adopt certain forms of cross compliance if they
wished, a stronger formulation of the policy was introduced as part of the far reaching 2003
Mid Term Review of the Common Agricultural Policy (CAP). In doing so EU Agricultural
Ministers sought not only to improve compliance with basic standards enshrined in EU
legislation, but also to avoid land abandonment and to ensure that agricultural land was
maintained in good agricultural and environmental condition. Member States were provided
with the authority to reduce or withhold direct payments made to farmers under the CAP if
the farmer failed to comply with any of these requirements. The same principle was to apply
to a further set of standards outside the sphere of EU legislation, designed to conserve soils,
habitats and landscape features on agricultural land on which the direct payment, now a
central feature of the CAP, was claimed.
     This chapter documents the EU experience with cross compliance as a means of
addressing environmental issues associated with agriculture. It reviews the rationale and
objectives of cross compliance within the CAP, tracing its development from earlier forms
of environmental conditionality in the 1990s. To set it in context, the role of this relatively
innovative policy tool within the CAP is considered in relation to that of other forms of
intervention, notably binding legislation and voluntary measures. The latter part of the
chapter provides an assessment of the initial performance of cross compliance during the
period in which it has become operational, considering in turn some of the key issues that
have been raised in relation to its potential as a tool for integrating environmental concerns
within agricultural policy. These include the variable application of cross compliance in the
EU-27, the level of costs imposed on farmers, issues relating to compliance and
enforcement as well as monitoring and evaluation.
©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                           9
10     The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy

Ways of regulating farming in Europe

Cross compliance is commonly conceptualized as occupying a policy space separate to that
of regulation and the sphere of voluntary incentive measures. This section explores the
different roles of regulation and incentives in seeking the achievement of environmental and
related outcomes in order to situate cross compliance within the architecture of European
agricultural policy.

The theoretical basis

In this discussion, the „reference level‟ comes into play. It is a concept that was developed
by the OECD in the 1990s to define the dividing line between the level of environmental
provision that farmers are expected to deliver at their own expense, and an enhanced level
of environmental quality, the delivery of which farmers may expect to be paid, for example
through agri-environment schemes (OECD, 1998; Scheele, 1999). It builds upon the
Polluter Pays Principle (OECD, 1974), and effectively articulates an individual‟s property
rights, stipulating the extent of obligations that apply with respect to the management of the
land.
     In classical economic theory there is no prima facie reason for granting or denying
property rights to private farmers as opposed to society as a whole. The question of where
to set the reference level is largely a question of political and legal choices and
considerations of equity and fairness in different social and historical settings, rather than
determined by the desired environmental outcome, scientific evidence or the biophysical
characteristics of land. This explains why reference levels often are set at different levels in
different countries (Cooper et al., 2009).
     In relation to agriculture, the reference level is either enshrined in binding legislation or
expressed in standards of good agricultural practice. Cross compliance, therefore, is an
interesting case. In so far as it is based on existing legal standards, it forms part of the
reference level, alongside any other EU, national or regional legislation that is relevant to
farmers but not included within the cross compliance framework. If, however, it introduces
standards that apply only to farmers claiming direct payments then it is a form of
conditionality on those payments and not embedded in the underlying reference level.

In practice

The majority of standards which apply to farming practices are set out in binding
legislation, established by the institutions of the EU and which has legal force in each of the
EU‟s 27 Member States. Regulations are directly applicable in Member State law, while
Directives, a common form of EU legislation, need to be implemented at the Member State
level within a defined time period. This legislation concerns the environment, public and
animal health and animal welfare, and has been gradually introduced since 1979, the point
at which the EU‟s first nature protection requirements came into force. Much of this
legislation is not focused exclusively on agricultural activity and farmland. For example, the
need to secure the appropriate management of areas protected under the 1979 Birds
Directive and the 1992 Habitats Directive extends to a wide range of terrestrial and marine
habitats, including many without any agricultural management.
                           The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy   11

     This body of EU legislation is complemented by standards set through legislation at the
national level, introduced by the Member States to address particular issues, such as to limit
water abstraction or to provide for the protection of landscape features.
     Agricultural policy, mainly embodied in the CAP, operated within an (almost) entirely
separate policy sphere to this body of legislation until 2005, when mandatory cross
compliance was first introduced. Earlier efforts in the 1990s to apply a level of
environmental conditionality to the receipt of CAP support payments had resulted in limited
impact. In accordance with Council Regulation 1782/2003, cross compliance allows for
most of the main types of payments received by farmers under the CAP to be reduced or
withdrawn if the recipient is found not to be compliant with specified legislation affecting
farming activity. The relevant obligations in this legislation are rearticulated as „Statutory
Management Requirements‟ (SMRs) to which cross compliance applies. The principle of
reducing or withdrawing payments also applies if recipients of direct payments fail to
maintain their agricultural land in „good agricultural and environmental condition‟ (GAEC).
While EU and national legislative requirements apply to all land and the actors responsible
for its management, irrespective of whether it is farmed or in receipt of public money, the
GAEC standards, in contrast, only apply to agricultural land in receipt of the direct
payment, and thus are more limited in their coverage.
     The CAP also includes a system of incentive payments for farmers undertaking
environmental management activity, introduced progressively since 1985. Farmers may be
compensated for the income foregone and costs incurred in undertaking commitments that
are more demanding than those which form part of the reference level, under the agri-
environment provisions of the Rural Development Regulation (Council Regulation
1698/2005). For example, as the owner of a plot of land that provides a habitat for a rare
species, but it is not protected by legislation or standards of good practice, the farmer has
the right to plough it for conversion to a more profitable land use, and therefore to destroy
the habitat. However, payments can be offered, under the agri-environment measure, to
compensate for the income forgone and additional costs incurred, to persuade farmers to
undertake the management required to maintain or restore the habitat in order to meet
targets for biodiversity conservation.
     Outside the sphere of public policy, privately administered quality assurance schemes
for food and other products also set a range of standards that farmers must comply with if
they wish to sell into certain markets – such as some large supermarket chains. These
schemes tend to require conformity with legal standards, and the more demanding schemes
will require compliance with more ambitious standards, typically for the environment or
farm animal welfare. There is, therefore, some overlap with cross compliance standards.
Although participation in such schemes is voluntary, meeting these standards may allow the
producer to access desirable or virtually essential markets, take advantage of any associated
product branding, and can help to generate a premium price for the product concerned.
Quality assurance schemes are explored further in Chapter 6.

The development of cross compliance in the EU
The introduction of cross compliance as part of the mid-term review CAP reform followed
from earlier experiences, in the 1990s, of making the receipt of certain CAP payments
conditional on meeting specified environmental standards. This development mirrored the
growing linkage between the agricultural and environmental policy spheres which had
12     The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy

stemmed from the increasing environmental critique of the impact of farming activities on
water, soil and biodiversity. In parallel, the EU had also started to articulate its priorities for
the environment more clearly.

The development of environmental conditionality within the CAP

The term „cross compliance‟ has its origins in the USA, where the notion of cross
compliance was first considered as an agricultural policy option in the early 1980s to
address the problems of soil erosion and water conservation (Baldock and Mitchell, 1995)
and to attempt to integrate the environmental and agricultural policy domains through a
focus on compliance with mandatory standards.
      The 1985 Food Security Act linked the eligibility for certain agricultural support
payments to compliance with environmental standards. Farmers claiming support under a
commodity support programme had to meet the rules of that programme and certain
obligations embodied in other federal government programs. This link across programs
gave rise to the term „cross compliance‟ (Benbrook, 1994). Some key lessons emerged from
the early experience in the US. These included difficulties in monitoring compliance, the
fact that the scale of coverage was limited to those in receipt of support payments, and that
the motivation to comply was linked to the degree to which farmers depended on
government support (Baldock and Mitchell, 1995).
      In the EU, the importance of protecting the environment became an increasingly
significant policy issue in the 1990s. More environmental legislation was adopted, much of
it, such as the 1991 Nitrates Directive, related to water. There was an increased emphasis
placed on articulating the EU‟s priorities for the environment, alongside the development of
an increasing number of targets. The 1992 Maastricht Treaty underpinned an EC
commitment to integrate environmental considerations into other areas of EU policy
including agriculture. The 1999 Amsterdam Treaty made sustainable development an
explicit objective of the EU and the subsequent 2001 EU Sustainable Development Strategy
stressed the need for all European policies to have sustainable development as their core
concern. These changes occurred amid a growing critique of the environmental impact of
agriculture (Baldock, 1989; Taylor and Dixon, 1990; RSPB, 1996). At the same time, it was
recognised that agricultural policy, at this time largely targeted at supporting production,
could be reformed so as to help secure the appropriate management of the rural environment
(Lowe and Baldock, 2000).
      The use of environmental conditionality within the CAP was a rather modest element in
the 1992 reform but this sowed the seeds for the transition to contemporary cross
compliance by the time of the mid-term review of the CAP, as summarized in Table 2.1.
      The MacSharry CAP reform package, agreed in May 1992, was partly motivated by the
need to address surplus production of some commodities. The guaranteed output prices of
the CAP regime at the time had created an incentive for farmers to maintain a high level of
production, and as a result of the reform, these were gradually lowered. Farmers were
compensated by the introduction of direct payments, made in the form of area based
payments for arable producers and increases in headage payment rates for the livestock
sector. Set-aside was also introduced as a supply control measure, requiring most cereal
farmers to withdraw a portion of their land from production.
      Over time, the CAP and the nature of its payments had contributed to an increase in
intensive farming practices in parts of Europe which had led to significant levels of
                             The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy         13

environmental degradation (Taylor and Dixon, 1990). For example, headage payments to
the livestock sector encouraged overgrazing in Ireland and the UK for example, which in
turn harmed soil and water quality, and reduced the value of grasslands for biodiversity. In
the arable sector, CAP support payments had led farmers to seek to increase yields,

14     The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy

involving the use of high levels of pesticides and fertilizers, negatively impacting on soil
and water quality. In order to increase the productive area, landscape features such as
hedgerows, ponds and in-field trees of value for biodiversity, as well as the visual landscape
were removed. The response to the growing critique of the environmental impact of
agriculture at the time of the MacSharry reform was to introduce environmental
conditionality on livestock headage payments in an attempt to limit stocking densities, as
well as set-aside to reduce over-production in the arable sector. However, these measures
were only partially successful.
     First, Member States were permitted to withhold headage payments for beef cattle and
sheep if the recipient failed to comply with „appropriate environmental measures‟ (Council
Regulation 125/93). These measures were to be defined by the Member State. Second, as
part of an effort to encourage more extensive beef and veal production, recipients of certain
beef headage payments were required to comply with a maximum stocking rate. This was
set at 3.5 livestock units per hectare (LU/ha) in 1993, to decline to 2 LU/ha by 1996 for the
Suckler Cow Premium and Special Beef Premium as set out by Council Regulation
2066/92. A maximum stocking density of 2 LU/ha was set for the basic beef premia, and
those farms that met an average density of 1.4 LU/ha or less could qualify for an additional
extensification premium (Lowe and Baldock, 2000). Third, with respect to land entered into
the set-aside scheme, Member States were required to apply „appropriate measures … to
ensure the protection of the environment‟ (Commission Regulation 2293/92) and to reduce
or withdraw benefits received through the scheme for those found not to be compliant.
     The measures were limited to the beef cattle, sheep and arable sectors, and not to other
sectors, such as milk and sugar that also received substantial support under the CAP
commodity regimes, although in a different form. In the targeted sectors these measures
were largely ineffective. Many Member States were reluctant to introduce additional
requirements, possibly because these would result in further costs for individual farmers.
Some Member States failed to establish standards for the appropriate care of set-aside
although progress was made in others (Lowe and Baldock, 2000). Only the UK and Ireland
took the opportunity to introduce „appropriate environmental measures‟ (Dwyer et al.,
2000) in relation to the receipt of beef cattle and sheep headage payments (in both cases, to
address problems related to overgrazing). The stocking density limits were criticized for,
amongst other reasons, being set too high for environmentally beneficial management, and
for only applying to the number of animals for which premia were claimed rather than the
total number present on the holding which in some cases was considerably greater (Lowe
and Baldock, 2000). The maximum stocking rates for the Suckler Cow and Special Beef
Premium were also above the average stocking rates on most farms at the time and hence
had limited impact in reducing average stocking densities (Alliance Environnement, 2007b).
Due to the patchy uptake in the Member States, and the over-elastic standards in the beef
sector, these three forms of conditionality had a partial and limited impact in addressing
some of the prevailing environmental problems associated with farming practices.
     The Agenda 2000 reform of the CAP, agreed in 1999, altered the approach to
environmental conditionality under the CAP in two respects: first, with the introduction of
„environmental protection requirements‟, and second, with the decision that farmers
benefiting from payments under the LFA and Agri-Environment measures should meet a set
of standards called „usual good farming practice‟.
     At the same time, requirements were introduced for all Member States to apply
„appropriate‟ environmental measures to the receipt of all direct payments under the
                            The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy   15

European Agricultural Guidance and Guarantee Fund (in accordance with Regulation
1259/99) in order to ensure a certain degree of environmental protection. Known as
„environmental protection requirements‟, they extended the scope of conditionality beyond
the arable cropping and beef and sheep sectors for the first time. Member States were
provided with some discretion in determining what sort of requirements to introduce and
were permitted to „take the environmental measures they consider to be appropriate in view
of the situation of the agricultural land used or the production concerned and which reflect
the potential environmental effects‟ (Article 3 of Council Regulation 1259/99). These
measures could take the form of „support in return for agri-environment commitments‟,
„general mandatory environmental requirements‟, or „specific environmental requirements
constituting a condition for direct payments‟. Member States were obliged to determine
penalties „that are appropriate and proportionate to the seriousness of the ecological
consequences of not observing‟ the environmental measures, and could include a reduction
or withdrawal of direct payments.
     Many Member States believed that existing environmental legislation and voluntary
agri-environmental programs were sufficient, and hence did not see the necessity to develop
environmental protection requirements and an associated sanctioning system. Denmark,
France, the Netherlands and the UK were, however, among a small number of Member
States that chose to introduce requirements (Dwyer et al., 2000; Guyomard and Le Bris,
2003).
     „Usual Good Farming Practice‟ was defined as „the standard of farming which a
reasonable farmer would follow in the region concerned‟ (Article 29 of Commission
Regulation 445/2002) and the standards were intended to „safeguard the environment and
maintain the countryside, in particular by sustainable farming‟ (Article 14 of Council
Regulation 1257/99), by ensuring compliance with a set of verifiable standards, which as a
minimum had to include „general mandatory environmental requirements‟. As conditions for
receipt of payments, they were, however, limited to those in receipt of LFA or agri-
environment payments. A more thorough inspection and sanctioning regime was introduced,
with Member States required to monitor the compliance of five per cent of farmers
receiving either payment each year. Failure to comply could lead to a reduction or
withdrawal of the payment. There was no systematic European framework for the definition
of what became known as good farming practice (GFP) standards. As a result, Member
States chose a diverse range of standards, variously covering nutrient management,
pesticide use, irrigation, soil management, animal husbandry and biodiversity and landscape
(IEEP, 2006a). Most of these standards were selected from items of national or EU
legislation with which farmers already needed to comply (IEEP, 2006a). Despite the
diversity of standards at the European level, and the limited number of farmers to whom
they applied, GFP increased the pressure on farmers to comply with baseline environmental
standards and raised their awareness of environmental legislation.
     The incomplete application of environmental protection requirements across Member
States and the inconsistency of the implementation of GFP standards raised concerns that
farmers were being treated differently in different Member States. This had implications
both for the degree of environmental protection afforded in different Member States as well
as for the costs faced by farmers in different countries and thus the prospect of distortions in
competition. These concerns were to be addressed by the mid-term review with the
introduction of cross compliance as a more standardised approach to environmental
conditionality, mandatory for all Member States.
16     The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy

Cross compliance as introduced in 2005

Two successive CAP reforms in the 1990s had demonstrated a political commitment to
improve the linkage between the receipt of CAP payments and a minimum level of
environmental protection. However, on both occasions the majority of Member States were
reluctant to introduce environmental conditions on direct payments. There was wider uptake
of GFP standards, but little published analysis of their effectiveness exists. During this
period, concerns about the environmental performance of farming continued to be
highlighted alongside questions about the rationale for direct payments. If the EU were to
succeed in marrying respect for baseline standards with receipt of public money under the
CAP, it was increasingly apparent that a more structured approach to environmental
conditionality would be needed. At the same time, the EU, along with other countries that
supported their agricultural sectors with a high level of public expenditure, were coming
under increasing pressure from trading partners to liberalize their agricultural markets as
part of the World Trade Organization (WTO) Doha Development Agenda.
     These various concerns were recognized by the Agriculture Commissioner Franz
Fischler, who identified the need for swift ameliorative action and instigated an urgent
review of the CAP in order to deal with these and other issues. A proposal for reform was
developed that envisaged a more central role for cross compliance as an integral part of a
shift to a decoupled system of direct payments. In order to increase the market orientation of
farmers, the new system of direct payments largely broke the link between the payment
received by a farmer and the level of production of any commodity.
     The resulting reforms, implemented in 2005, led to the term „cross compliance‟ being
used for the first time in a piece of European agricultural legislation (Chapter 1 of Council
Regulation 1782/2003). It became compulsory for all Member States for the first time and
applied to all recipients of the requisite direct payments. It was no longer confined to the
environment and included several standards in the areas of animal welfare as well as public,
animal and plant health. In contrast to the preceding environmental protection requirements
and the GFP standards, cross compliance was to apply to the receipt of all Pillar 1 direct
payments and to eight relevant Pillar 2 measures. As a result, cross compliance superseded
GFP in becoming the set of standards on which receipt of payments under many rural
development measures was based. These measures included:

    natural handicap payments (as the LFA measure was renamed in Council Regulation
     1698/2005);
    Natura 2000 payments and payments linked to the Water Framework Directive
     (Council Directive 2000/60/EC);
    agri-environment payments;
    animal welfare payments;
    first afforestation of agricultural land;
    Natura 2000 payments for forests; and
    forest environment payments.

The rationale for the introduction of cross compliance remains somewhat ambiguous, with
more than one objective in play. In the initial proposals, the emphasis was on „supporting
the implementation of environmental ... legislation‟ and creating an obligation to „maintain
land in good agricultural condition‟ (CEC, 2002). It was extended to „good agricultural and
                           The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy   17

environmental condition‟ following pressure from environmental interests who stressed the
opportunities that cross compliance provided to more fundamentally integrate sustainable
land management obligations with the receipt of direct payments. As the negotiations
proceeded, it became clear that cross compliance was intended to serve two main purposes.
The first was to promote more sustainable agriculture, a goal that can be traced back to the
policy heritage established by the 1992 „environmental measures‟ and the 1999
„environmental protection requirements‟. Its second purpose was to act as a flanking
measure to address undesirable side-effects of the introduction of decoupled direct
payments, most notably the cessation of the active management of farmland and the risk of
land abandonment. The decoupling of support payments to farmers from production led to
fears that, without the link between the direct payment and the production of agricultural
commodities, some farmers would find it more profitable to disengage from any form of
agricultural management, yet would still be able to receive payments (IEEP, 2006b). Cross
compliance served to thwart this threat by ensuring that those in receipt of the direct
payment would need to continue to undertake a minimum level of agricultural management.
It was seen as unacceptable for an agricultural policy to support the transition to a land use
that would not be suitable for agricultural production, and hence it became an objective of
cross compliance „to avoid the abandonment of agricultural land and ensure that it is
maintained in good agricultural and environmental condition‟.
     Together with the new system of largely decoupled direct payments, known as the
Single Payment Scheme (SPS), cross compliance was „to promote more market oriented and
sustainable agriculture‟ (all quotes in this paragraph are from the preamble to Council
Regulation 1782/2003). To work towards this Member States were obliged „to incorporate
in the common market organizations basic standards for the environment, food safety,
animal health and welfare and good agricultural and environmental condition‟. These „basic
standards‟ were defined according to a list of Statutory Management Requirements (SMRs)
and were composed of selected Articles from European legislation in the areas of the
environment, food safety, animal health and animal welfare, and set out in Annex III of the
Regulation and shown in Table 2.2. Although the rationale for including existing legislation
in cross compliance is not particularly well articulated in the regulatory texts, it is founded
on the principle that those who breach their legal obligations should not be entitled to
receive the amount of public money for which they would otherwise be eligible.
     The legislative framework allowed for the SMRs to be introduced at national level over
a number of years, with the pace of implementation slower for those Member States that
acceded to the EU in 2004 and 2007. The 17 Member States that operate the SPS (i.e. the
EU-15, Malta and Slovenia) were required to introduce a first swathe of SMRs concerned
with the environment and the identification and registration of animals in 2005, followed by
further SMRs regulating public, animal and plant health and the notification of livestock
diseases in 2006. The final SMRs cover animal welfare and were introduced in these
Member States in 2007. The remaining eight Member States which joined the EU in 2004
and that operate the Single Area Payment Scheme (SAPS) began introducing the SMRs
over a three year period from 2009. Bulgaria and Romania will begin to implement the
SMRs from 2012. The SMRs are entirely based on specific articles from pre-existing
European legislation as transposed by the Member State. The SMRs are not new standards
and are best characterized as providing an additional incentive for complying with current
legal requirements. Despite this, there were some initial, largely unrealized fears, that
farmers would forgo the direct payment in order to avoid cross compliance inspections.

The SMRs are complemented by a framework of standards for GAEC, which are specified
in a different way in the Regulation, with considerable scope for national or even regional
standards within the EU framework. Annex IV of Council Regulation 1782/2003 provides
this framework and lists a total of eleven very broadly phrased standards. Six of these relate
to the management of soils, and specifically address soil erosion, soil organic matter and
soil structure. Five other standards are set out with the aim of securing a basic level of
maintenance of agricultural land (see Table 2.3). Article 5 of Council Regulation 1782/2003
allows Member States to define their own GAEC standards in line with this framework. The
standards should be adapted to national circumstances and „take into account the specific
characteristics of the areas concerned, including soil and climatic conditions, existing
farming systems, land use, crop rotation, farming practices and farm structures‟. The
standards should also take into account the management options for which farmers are able
to obtain payment under voluntary incentives under agri-environment schemes. Those
measures attracting payment should be more demanding than the „reference level of good
agricultural practice‟. The introduction of GAEC put the onus on Member States to consider
issues of particular relevance to them and whether to initiate new measures to meet
Community standards. In the absence of any relevant pre-existing national legislation,
Member States would in principle need to introduce new standards in order to meet the
requirements of the GAEC framework or address any other environmental problems that
were not already satisfactorily dealt with by European or national legislation.
     In contrast to the earlier forms of environmental conditionality, which allowed the
Member States to decide on appropriate penalties themselves, Council Regulation
1782/2003 introduced a consistent European approach to the inspection, control and
application of penalties for breaches of the cross compliance SMRs and GAEC standards.
Article 44 of Commission Regulation 796/2004 states that a minimum of one per cent of all
farmers receiving direct payments are to be inspected each year, with Article 45 stating that
20% to 25% of farmers should be selected randomly and the remainder according a series of
risk based criteria. The penalty applied for non-compliance would be graded according to
20     The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy

four categories: severity (in relation to the impacts of failing to comply, expressed in
relation to the objectives of the standards); extent (whether the impact of the breach is
limited to the farm or has a wider impact); permanence (the period of time that the impact
arising from the non-compliance will last, and whether this may be rectified); and repetition
(cases where the same breach on a particular farm is identified more than once in a three
year period). Articles 66 and 67 of Commission Regulation 796/2004 set out the penalties
for non-compliance. Where the breach has occurred as a result of the negligence of the
farmer, a penalty of a 1% to 5% reduction to the direct payment may be made, rising to a
15% reduction in the event of repeated breaches. If the breach is judged to have occurred
due to intentional actions by the farmer, the penalty may range from a 15% to a 100%
reduction. From 2008, Member States have had the option not to apply reductions to
farmers if these amount to €100 or less. These penalties are additional to any that may arise
as a result of non-compliance with the underlying national legislation.

The system of cross compliance implemented in 2005 was both more comprehensive in the
scope of issues it sought to address and more rigorous than previous policies in this domain
in requiring action from all Member States. This common approach helped to reduce earlier
concerns that farmers across the Union would be treated differently in terms of the
standards they would need to meet and with respect to the penalty they would face if
identified as non-compliant. This helped to temper arguments put forward by some Member
States that their competitive position in the EU would be undermined as a result of the
introduction of cross compliance, although considerable differences in GAEC standards
arose relatively rapidly.
                           The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy   21

Cross compliance in practice

The application of cross compliance since 2005 has undoubtedly expanded the area of land
subject to basic environmental management requirements. However, it has been subject to
criticism from both sides. On the one hand farming organizations have expressed
dissatisfaction with the burdens that they feel that it has introduced (Alliance
Environnement, 2007a). On the other hand environmental organizations have argued that
the potential of the cross compliance mechanism to deliver environmental benefits has not
been maximized, with a number of reports setting out its shortcomings in this respect (ECA,
2008; BirdLife International, 2009). In particular, issues have been raised in relation to the
variation in GAEC standards implemented in different Member States, levels of compliance
and enforcement and the lack of monitoring and evaluation. These issues and the questions
of cost burdens for farmers are explored in turn.

The variation in GAEC standards between Member States

As permitted by the legislation, there are considerable variations between Member States
both in terms of the total number of GAEC standards being applied and in the level of
ambition in using these standards to address environmental problems (Farmer et al., 2007).
For example, most GAEC standards are based on the requirements of pre-existing national
legislation or have otherwise been adapted from previous GFP standards (Alliance
Environnement, 2007a). The soil erosion GAEC standards introduced by the Netherlands
and the Slovak Republic are, for example, based on pre-existing national legislation, as are
many of the minimum maintenance requirements in Belgium (Wallonie), Finland, Malta and
the UK.
     Responsibility for determining which GAEC standards should be translated into
obligations on farmers rests with the Member State. According to evidence collected by
Alliance Environnement (2007a), only twelve Member States, including Austria and the
UK, implemented a comprehensive set of GAEC standards, introducing relevant standards
for each of the four GAEC „issues‟ of soil erosion, soil organic matter, soil structure and
minimum level of maintenance. Of the remaining countries, ten Member States introduced
GAEC standards for three of the issues, whilst three Member States introduced GAEC
standards for two of the issues. The GAEC standards vary from those that are relatively
simple, in that they approximate to normal practice on the majority of farms, to those that
are more demanding and require the introduction of some management changes. It is
difficult to determine the equivalency in the number and scope of standards between
Member States, and thus to determine whether the burden of standards is greater in one
country than another but it is fairly clear that there are significant variations.
     This is not surprising given the freedom provided to the Member States to implement
the GAEC framework, the lack of any requirement for Commission approval of the
standards introduced, differing institutional capacities to define and enforce compliance
with an array of standards across various policy domains (soil, water, biodiversity, animal
registration and animal welfare), and varying levels of enthusiasm for cross compliance
amongst the Member States, in part explained by differing political priorities and the
severity of particular environmental problems.
     Variation is not a concern per se, but the issue is whether Member States have too
much latitude to adopt a minimalist approach, with adverse consequences both for the
22    The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy

environment and for the level playing field. The division of GAEC standards into those that
are compulsory for Member States to implement and those which are optional, as part of the
CAP Health Check agreement in December 2008, should help address this to some extent.
A greater degree of consistency in terms of the type of standards that are implemented in the
EU-27 can be expected although the detailed requirements of these standards, and hence the
degree to which they are designed to address the specific environmental needs of an area, is
still in the hands of the Member States.

Costs to farmers

It is rarely possible to introduce a new policy intervention of this kind without incurring
some form of cost. An element of this will be administrative cost but there are also costs to
farmers of complying with new conditions. At the time cross compliance was introduced
there were concerns among a number of farm lobby groups that cross compliance would
result in additional costs over and above the cost implications of existing legislation.
     However, at the European level, very few additional costs that arise at the farm level
can be attributed to cross compliance. This is because the universe of standards that forms
the reference level for agricultural management in the EU did not substantially change after
the introduction of cross compliance. Almost all the standards packaged together under the
banner of cross compliance were already a feature of the existing legislative environment.
The SMRs were entirely based on selected articles of pre-existing pieces of EU legislation
and, in many cases, Member States chose to base the GAEC standards on pre-existing items
of national legislation. Because of this continuity in standards, cross compliance, for the
most part, did not result in any significant additional costs to farmers that they did not
already need to meet. The exception was the cost related to complying with these new
GAEC standards that did not have a legislative precedent.
     For SMRs, the costs of meeting the requisite standards may be significant on some
farms, for example as a result of a need to make management changes or investments to
achieve compliance. However, these costs arise from the obligations of extant EU
legislation, which are applicable to all farms, irrespective of whether they receive direct
payments or not. The only costs attributable to cross compliance per se arise from any
specific administrative or new inspection burdens introduced. Where cross compliance
increased farmers‟ motivation to comply with such legislation set out in SMRs, this may
have given rise to increased costs, but in so doing, they have also avoided penalties under
national law.
     New GAEC standards may require farmers to adjust their management practices, and
thus to incur additional costs, especially in those less common cases where they are
relatively ambitious. Austria, Finland, France and England, for example, all introduced a
standard that, whilst formulated differently in each case, required the introduction of a
narrow buffer strip along certain field margins in order to protect a watercourse or boundary
feature from agricultural pollution (Farmer et al., 2008). The compliance costs to farmers of
meeting such standards are difficult to estimate, not least because the extent to which
farmers need to adjust their management practices in order to comply is difficult to appraise
and yields along field margins tend to be below average. Box 2.1 provides examples of how
the costs for two GAEC standards, implemented for the first time in England through cross
compliance, were assessed.
                                 The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy               23

Box 2.1     Attributing costs to GAEC standards - two examples from England

Costs attributable to the soil protection review (SPR) GAEC standard
    Each farmer in receipt of the Single Payment is obliged to complete a review of the soil problems on their
     farm, in accordance with a common template, and subsequently implement measures in order to address the
     problems identified.
    Costs vary depending on the seriousness of the soil problems on the farm. The highest costs are likely to
     occur on arable, general cropping, outdoor pig farms, dairy farms (with maize) and intensively managed
     stock farms where these activities coincide with vulnerable soils, slopes and watercourses.
    In many cases compliance costs may be low and are likely to comprise good husbandry which might be
     undertaken irrespective of cross compliance.
    The Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra) estimated that cost of developing a SPR
     for a medium-sized farm of 130ha would equate to £2/ha (€2.80/ha) with a 10% variation for smaller and
     larger farms (reflecting the higher unit costs of overhead management on smaller farms and lower costs on
     larger farms).

Costs attributable to 2m margins for the protection of hedgerows and watercourses GAEC
standard
    To comply with the standard, a strip of land must be left uncultivated adjacent to hedgerows and
     watercourses. The strip should measure 2m from the centre of a hedge and a minimum of one metre from the
     top of the ditch bank. Fertilizers, herbicides or pesticides cannot be applied to it. The standard does not
     apply to fields that are smaller than 2 ha.
    There could be a financial impact on farm businesses from the loss of a crop on the 2m uncultivated strips,
     although most field margins tend to be less productive than the field as a whole.
    In some areas the total hedge width may be equal to 4m or more and therefore, there would be no need to
     have an additional uncultivated strip. As a result, areas where hedgerows are traditionally quite narrow with
     minimal shading will experience the larger yield reductions.
    The impact will also fall disproportionately on those with relatively small fields and/or those farms with a
     high density of watercourses and hedgerows.
    Based on income foregone, Defra estimated the cost of this measure at £7 (€10) per 100m of 2m margin on
     arable and dairy farms. This equates to around 0.5% of total farm costs or 2-3% of the Single Payment. For
     example, a 150 ha cereal farm with 50% of its margins against hedgerows or watercourses could have a cost
     for the loss of a full 2m of production of around £500 (€700) per annum (Defra, 2004).
Source: Farmer et al. (2006)



Compliance and enforcement

During 2005, the first year of cross compliance controls, 71% of all instances of non-
compliance in the EU were for breaches of those SMRs concerned with the identification
and registration of cattle, and usually arose due to the absence of eartags. A further 10% of
all breaches – the second greatest number of breaches – arose in relation to the SMRs for
the Nitrates Directive (European Commission, 2007). Just over two-thirds of all penalty
reductions (68%) were made at the 1% level, with only 6% made above the 5% level,
indicating that the majority of these breaches are likely to have been relatively minor. From
this starting point, it might be anticipated that the number of SMR breaches will decrease
over time, as cross compliance raises awareness amongst farmers of the most relevant
aspects of EU legislation affecting agricultural management, and as farmers take note that
penalties are being applied for identified compliance failures. Alternatively, it may be that
the penalty system is not acting as an adequate deterrent. For example, in many cases, the
cost of complying with the standards is higher than the potential reduction in payment for
non-compliance, thereby reducing the incentive to comply (ECA, 2008). In addition, the
European Court of Auditors concluded that „Member States had not implemented effective
24    The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy

control and sanction systems and that as a consequence the control system provides
insufficient assurance on farmer compliance‟ (ECA, 2008). They also demonstrated that the
environmental SMRs and GAEC standards are less well checked during inspections than
other standards, often due to them being less straightforward to inspect, and therefore the
data on breaches may under-represent the real situation. Despite this, a large proportion of
Member States have backed calls for changes to cross compliance to introduce more
flexible rules in relation to enforcement in line with the simplification agenda (Agra Facts,
2009).
     There have also been criticisms about the nature of the sanctions applied, particularly
the fact that these are related to the size of direct payments received by the farmer rather
than proportional to the severity of the infringement (ECA, 2008; BirdLife, 2009).

Monitoring and evaluation

It is more challenging to assess the performance and impact of cross compliance than many
other policies within the CAP. At the political level it has led to a greater focus on
implementing EU legislation at farm level by both governments and farmers but it is
difficult to appraise the impact of this. Many farmers have become more aware of their
environmental obligations than previously and are concerned to avoid penalties which are
subject to discussion in the press, for example (Alliance Environnement, 2007a).
      The lack of formal assessment, reporting or monitoring requirements in relation to
cross compliance is a significant weakness of the system. While GAEC standards can be
tailored to local circumstances, priorities and needs, the Commission has provided only
limited guidance for Member States on the SMR element of cross compliance. No guidance
documents have been provided on the development or implementation of GAEC standards.
The lack of guidance, the absence of any formal requirement for such standards to be based
on an assessment of needs, together with the lack of checks by the Commission on the
quality or appropriateness of the standards introduced by Member States, has led to a mixed
pattern of implementation with some Member States taking limited measures.
      Although Member States are required to report on the number of cross compliance
checks undertaken and the results of these checks, they are not obliged to report on which
standards they have introduced, and their success in addressing either the objectives set out
in Council Regulation 1782/2003 or more local environmental needs and priorities, where
these have been identified. The lack of performance indicators, for example, to measure the
extent to which land abandonment has been prevented or land has been maintained in good
agricultural and environmental condition, is particularly notable, an issue that has been
picked up by the European Court of Auditors (ECA, 2008). Without such information,
evaluations of how cross compliance is performing to achieve its objectives is problematic,
which raises questions about its effectiveness, particularly in relation to the environment.

Conclusions

The form of cross compliance introduced into the EU in 2005 demonstrates a form of
environmental conditionality that is likely to be more successful than its predecessors in
improving the management of agricultural land from an environmental perspective (albeit
with some variation between Member States and sectors). This will be achieved partly as a
result of increasing rates of compliance with existing legislation. Its extension beyond the
                            The role of cross compliance in greening EU agricultural policy   25

environmental domain to animal diseases and food safety, as well as farm animal health and
welfare legislation means that it incentivizes compliance with a breadth of EU legislation
that is relevant to farming. As a sanctioning mechanism, it operates without resulting in
many additional costs to farmers that they were not already obliged to meet. It provides an
incentive for both governments and farmers to pay greater attention to the implementation
of standards, some of which have not been widely adhered to.
     However, the significant benefits for the environment that the introduction of GAEC
standards has the potential to deliver are yet to be realised in most Member States. Many
have demonstrated a very low level of ambition in designing GAEC standards, choosing to
reframe national legislation or introduce standards that simply approximate normal farming
practice on the majority of farms. This, combined with the fact that the SMRs consist of
legislation that farmers already were obliged to comply with, would suggest that criticisms
of cross compliance as imposing significant additional costs on farm businesses are not
borne out in practice. The lack of formal assessments of the quality and appropriateness of
the standards that are put in place and the absence of indicators to aid monitoring of how
cross compliance is delivering against its objectives, mean that it is difficult to assess how
well cross compliance is performing in practice although there are clearly substantial
variations between Member States. Significant efforts continue to be needed to improve the
design and delivery of cross compliance if its potential for integrating environmental
concerns within agricultural policy is to be maximized.
