This page intentionally left blank
Chapter 3

State and federal responses to increasing input
intensity in the US

Jonathan Winsten and Lynn G. Knight



Introduction

The fundamental economic tenet of comparative advantage, as first described by Ricardo
(1891) states that countries should specialize in the production of goods or services for
which they are relatively more efficient. The theory follows that production and trade,
according to comparative advantage, will increase total world production and wealth. As
sound as this theory is, the subsequent issues surrounding international trade require further
attention and analysis, as evidenced by the importance of international conventions, such as
the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade (GATT) and the World Trade Organization
(WTO).
     The heterogeneity across nations in per capita wealth, agricultural productivity, natural
resources, and attitudes toward government intervention combine, with numerous other
factors, to determine the standards and regulations, or lack thereof, that each nation
institutes as the policy context in which its agriculture and food systems operate. The areas
of greatest concern within this agricultural policy context tend to include environmental
quality, protection of natural resources, human health, and animal welfare. However, the
absolute and relative weights and priorities associated with each concern will also vary
among nations and, even, regions, states, and provinces.
     Agricultural production is inextricably linked to environmental quality, natural resource
management, human health, and animal welfare (in the case of livestock agriculture).
However, the issues of environmental quality and human health seem to dominate the
debate regarding trade in agricultural and food products. Trade in these products includes,
as externalities to farm production decisions, risks to a nation‟s environmental quality and
human health. These risks represent disservices (i.e. economic bads) that, while implicit in
the trade of goods, are nonetheless transferred across national boundaries (Runge, 1990;
Runge and Nolan, 1990).
     How nations, states, or other jurisdictions decide to address these risks (or use the
threat of such risks to justify protectionist trade policies), is likely to have nontrivial impact
on trade patterns, the competitiveness of their agricultural products on the world market,
and resulting producer and consumer surpluses, as well as overall welfare. Enforced
regulations and standards related to a nation‟s food production system, whether to protect
environmental quality, human health, animal welfare, or other concerns, are likely to
©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         29
30    State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

increase average production costs and potentially reduce competitiveness. This assumption,
however, has been refuted by Porter (1990) and others who believe that stricter regulations
will increase innovation and reduce costs of production in the longer-term.
     In the US, agriculture, specifically farming and ranching, has been largely exempt from
regulations and standards. As such, there has not been significant costs imposed on farmers
and ranchers for compliance and the resulting impact on competitiveness of US food
products has generally been small (Carpentier and Ervin, 2002). There are indications that
this relatively lax regulatory approach towards agriculture is changing. Litigation over the
past decade has resulted in more comprehensive enforcement of the Clean Water Act and
Clean Air Act. As the consequences of this federal litigation trickle down, individual states
are starting to impose more rigid compliance requirements on farms and ranches, as is
discussed in more detail below.
     The intent of this chapter is to explain the approach and main policy tools that are used
to address the impacts of agricultural production on society, with a primary focus on
environmental impacts, and to quantify the impact of the current regulatory burden on
representative dairy and swine operations. The chapter provides an overview of US
agricultural production, as well as details on the primary policies and programmes that
comprise the policy context in which US farmers operate.

US agriculture: a primer

The intensity of agricultural production in the US has increased greatly since 1950. Some
indications of this trend include a many-fold increase in chemical fertilizer and pesticide
applications, as can be seen in Figures 3.1 and 3.2. Increases in stocking rates (animal units
per area of land) for livestock production have also been dramatic for certain locations and
sectors. The darker colours shown in Figure 3.3 indicate the areas where the greatest
increase in the number of larger livestock operations occurred from 1982 through 1997. The
greatest increases are shown to be in North Carolina (swine), Minnesota (swine), Wyoming
(beef), the Western states (dairy and beef), and various other areas scattered throughout the
country (Kellogg et al., 2000). Farms with greater than 1,000 animal units that feed animals
primarily in confinement are designated by Clean Water Act rules as Concentrated Animal
Feeding Operations (CAFOs) and are the focus of water quality regulations at the federal
and state levels.
     Although the US has federal regulations that apply in all states, each of the 50 US states
can and will have its own interpretation and enforcement of these regulations. Additionally,
many states have their own regulations that are not part of federal mandates. Because the
implementation, enforcement, and farm-level impact of regulations pertaining to agriculture
vary from state to state, a description of the leading states for several major commodities is
presented below. The regulatory context is described in the following section.
     Corn production is dominated by the North-central states, known as the Cornbelt. Table
3.1 shows that over 63% of US corn production in 2007 originated from just 5 states, led by
Iowa with over 18%. A very similar production pattern exists for soybeans. With regard to
wheat, North Dakota, Kansas, Montana, South Dakota, and Texas led the US in production
in 2007, accounting for more than 50% of the total production.

Dairy production is more dispersed across the US than is production of many of the major
crops. In recent years, western states such as California, Idaho, and New Mexico have
greatly increased their milk production. California has overtaken Wisconsin, the historical
hub of US dairying, to be the largest producer (Table 3.1). Average herd sizes in the
western states are vastly greater than those in the traditional Dairybelt (the northern tier,
from Maine to Minnesota). The larger herd sizes are the object of recently enforced Clean
                          State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US   33

Water Act and Clean Air Act regulations, making some western states, particularly
California a logical choice for a case study analysis of regulatory costs in the dairy sector.
     As can be seen in Table 3.1, poultry production is dominated by the south-eastern and
Mississippi Delta states, led by Georgia, Arkansas, and Alabama. Important structural
changes over the past 15 years include widespread vertical integration in the poultry
industry. The majority of large producers are contract growers for poultry processing and
marketing firms. Most often, contract growers do not own the birds; they are supplied with
chicks, feed, and management oversight. This arrangement has been the focus of much
debate regarding the appropriate level of liability that the integrator (i.e. processor) should
assume for environmental degradation resulting from poultry production.
     In recent years, North Carolina has overtaken Iowa as the leading swine producing
state. Vertical integration is also common in the US swine industry, which, along with
poultry, has had the fastest rate of growth of CAFOs. Air quality and odour control have
become important issues facing large-scale hog production in many regions of the country.
Together, North Carolina and Iowa produced 34% of the US swine production in 2007.
     The top steer producing states are Texas, Nebraska, and Kansas. The beef cattle
industry in the US has two distinct components. Cow-calf operations produce young stock
primarily on pasture and rangeland. This occurs in the western and inter-mountain states, as
well as in the southern states. Feedlot operations take yearlings and steers and fatten them
for slaughter. It is feedlot operations that are facing increasing regulation as CAFOs. The
western plains states, such as Nebraska, Colorado, Wyoming, Oklahoma, and the Texas
Panhandle are where the majority of beef CAFOs are located.

US agricultural exports and the world market

Because an objective of this book is to quantify the impact of regulations on the relative
competitiveness of trading partners in the world market, data have been assembled on total
world trade in the major US agricultural commodities, as well as the percentage of the
world market that is supplied by EU and US farmers. Table 3.2 provides an indication of
the relative importance of US and EU exports into the world market as of 2004. This
analysis aided in the identification of the most relevant commodities for the case study
analyses presented in this chapter.
     The numbers provided in Table 3.2 show that the US dominates world trade in maize
and soybeans. Because maize and soybeans are both very minor exports from the EU, the
impact on relative competitiveness between the US and EU is not of paramount importance
for these commodities. Of the major crops, Table 3.2 shows that the EU and US each supply
about one-quarter of the world market for wheat. As important rivals in this market, wheat
could be an appropriate crop for analysing the impact of regulations on competitiveness.
However, as discussed below, the majority of the regulatory burden on the US agriculture
falls upon the livestock sector.
     Of the major livestock products from the EU (dairy, pork, poultry, and beef), dairy
comprises the greatest value of world trade by far, with over US$6 billion in 2004. Beef and
pork production from the EU each represented slightly more than US$3 billion of world
trade in 2004, and poultry represented slightly more than US$1 billion. The US was a much
smaller supplier of dairy and beef, representing only 2.5% and 0.61% of the world market,
respectively, in 2004. US beef exports were down dramatically in 2004 due to fears over
mad cow disease; prior to 2004, the US represented 12-14% of the world market trade in
34    State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

beef. The reduced US exports in 2004 also have the effect of inflating the importance of EU
beef exports in Table 3.2. The case study analyses presented in this chapter will focus on
the dairy and swine industries.

The regulatory context surrounding US agriculture

A handful of federal agencies are in charge of the implementation, oversight, and
enforcement of regulations affecting agricultural production in the US. These include the
EPA, the USDA, the FDA, and the Department of Interior. However, because many federal
regulations are administered at the state level, the aforementioned federal agencies often
delegate authority to respective state agencies for implementation and enforcement. The
states then have some leeway in how they administer the regulations.
     In general, agriculture in the US seems to have fewer mandatory standards imposed
upon producers through regulations as compared with the directives that are relevant to
agriculture in the EU (Carpentier and Ervin, 2002). It has been stated that US federal
policies (mostly environmental) affecting agriculture have generally been created to prevent
or alleviate specific problems; they have not been designed to produce positive amenities,
such as environmental improvement, maintaining an aesthetic working landscape, or
bolstering rural communities (Bernstein et al., 2004). The creation of such positive
amenities has been the goal of several policies within the EU and many other nations.
                         State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US   35

Environmental legislation and regulations

There are several important pieces of federal legislation that require mandatory compliance
from certain US farmers. These include the CWA, the CAA, the CERCLA, the FIFRA, the
FQPA, and the ESA. It is important to note that the applicability and level of enforcement
of these regulations varies widely. Of the aforementioned regulations, the CWA has the
largest potential impact on US farmers, but until recent years CWA regulations were largely
unenforced for the agricultural sector. The CAA has a large impact, but only on farmers
located within certain areas that are designated to have existing air quality problems.
     These laws notwithstanding, the primary approach for addressing environmental issues
from agriculture in the US has been to seek voluntary participation and compliance that is
supported with funding to offset the direct, indirect, and opportunity costs of participation.
Such programmes generally fall into two major categories; land retirement and working
land conservation programmes. The largest of the land retirement programmes, by far, is the
CRP. The Wetlands Reserve Program (WRP) and Grassland Reserve Program (GRP) are
smaller, but similar programmes that also pay farmers for voluntarily restricting the use of
specific parcels of land. These programmes are described below.
     Some regulations have been the subject of recent litigation based on claims that they
have not been enforced strictly enough. Legislation regarding the CWA has resulted in the
CAFO rule and has impacted the way that USDA delivers some of its voluntary
programmes (e.g. Environmental Quality Incentives Program [EQIP]) to help ensure that
farmers required to comply with the CWA are doing so.

Federal Water Pollution Control Act Amendments of 1972 (Clean Water Act)

Background and general description

The CWA is the primary law protecting surface water quality in the US. It employs a variety
of regulatory and non-regulatory tools to reduce pollutant discharges into the nation‟s
waterways. Initially, the primary focus was on point sources of pollution from industry and
wastewater treatment plants (WWTPs), with little attention paid to nonpoint sources. The
focus in recent years has been expanded to include nonpoint source pollution (NSP).
Agriculture has been identified as the primary source of NSP affecting US surface waters
(US Environmental Protection Agency, 2002).

Impact on agriculture

The CWA includes regulation of farming activities. However, it was not until 15 December
2002 that the federal government issued specific rules governing CAFOs. CAFOs are
typically defined as animal feeding operations with greater than 1,000 animal units (700
dairy cows, 1,000 beef cows, 2,500 hogs or 100,000 chickens). Smaller operations can also
be designated as CAFOs, however, if specific environmental conditions apply. Medium
CAFOs are those operations with between 300 and 1,000 animal units (AUs) with a stream
running through the confinement area, or a man-made conveyance to surface water. Small
CAFOs are those operations with less than 300 animal units (AUs) that, through on-site
inspection, have been determined to be a significant contributor of pollutants to surface
water (US Environmental Protection Agency, 2003).
36    State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

     Under the recent rule, all farms designated as CAFOs are required to obtain a permit
under the NPDES. The NPDES permits have been used to control point sources of pollution
since the Clean Water Act was established. The CAFO rule is the first application of the
NPDES permit to agricultural non-point sources of pollution. The rationale for this rule is
that large farms (CAFOs) are potential point sources of pollution because of their large
manure storage requirements.
     Obtaining an NPDES permit for a CAFO requires the design and implementation of a
comprehensive nutrient management plan (CNMP) for the operation. The CNMP must be
specific for the operation and detail the proper management of all animal manure produced.
It must address the assimilative capacity of the farm‟s land for the manure and other
nutrients applied to the land. The CNMP must be focused on the control of nitrogen (N) or
phosphorus (P), based on state or local priorities. The implementation of CNMPs often
results in operations seeking additional land on which to spread manure and/or the use of
alternative nutrient control strategies for manure.
     It is important to note that the CAFO regulations currently affect a very small
percentage of livestock operations. The USDA estimated that CAFOs accounted for 2% of
all confined livestock operations in 1997 (Gollehon et al., 2001). Although an updated
estimate is not available, the current percentage is likely to be higher, but probably less than
5%. The percentage of CAFOs varies across livestock types, with the greatest percentage of
CAFOs in the swine and poultry sectors (Kellogg et al., 2000; Speir et al., 2002). Due to
their larger size, the percentage of hectares affected by the CAFO regulations is much
greater than the percentage of operations, but is still estimated to be a fairly small
percentage.
     The CWA also provides regulations concerning the application of sewage sludge to
agricultural land. During the 1980s and 1990s, numerous studies and reviews were
conducted in the US to ascertain the risks of using sewage sludge as an amendment on land
used for food crops. Both the EPA and USDA have been proponents of using bio-solids on
agricultural land. The regulations that govern the use of this practice set concentration limits
for arsenic, cadmium, copper, lead, mercury, molybdenum, nickel, selenium and zinc.
Additionally, proper practices for field storage are provided through this rule.

Implementation and approach

The implementation and enforcement of the CWA is delegated to the states. Each state has
an authorized NPDES authority, usually the state agency responsible for natural resources
and/or environmental quality. Each state‟s NPDES authority is responsible for issuing the
permits and enforcing the related regulations and standards. This includes implementation
of the CAFO rule. In most states, the agency of agriculture is also involved in this process,
as it affects some portion of livestock operations. The most relevant issues in the CAFO
process are (i) the threshold size of an operation for designation as a CAFO and (ii) the
stringency of CNMP standards and enforcement.

Interaction between EPA’s CAFO rule and state and local regulations

Most states already have some water quality regulations that affect farms. Some states have
more stringent criteria, while in other states EPA‟s regulations will prove to be more
stringent than their existing regulations. The USDA Natural Resources Conservation
                          State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US   37

Service (NRCS) practice standards are designed to meet federal, state and/or local
requirements, whichever are most stringent. State and local NRCS offices must be aware of
all applicable regulations and adapt their planning activities as required.
     State and local regulations can vary greatly, depending upon how the states chose to
define CAFOs and related criteria. These definitions are not consistent among states or with
the previous EPA definition. For example, the level of acceptable discharge may be
different between existing state and local regulations and the current CAFO rule. Finally,
prior to the current CAFO rule, CAFOs that already have implemented practices meeting
the 24-hour, 25-year storm event criteria were exempt from the NPDES permit requirement.
These farms must now acquire a permit, even though they are most likely meeting the
permit requirements.
     In the states that have less restrictive local and state regulations, relative to the CAFO
regulations, NRCS and its conservation partners in these states can expect to see an increase
in technical service assistance requests from farm operators that now must apply for, and
comply with, CAFO NPDES permits. To more thoroughly assess the types of assistance that
will be required, each state and local office will need to determine and understand the
relationship between EPA‟s CAFO regulations and their state and local laws. If the CAFO
rule is more restrictive than current state and local regulations, NRCS will need to assure
that their Field Office Technical Guide (FOTG) Quality Criteria and conservation practice
standards reflect the CAFO requirements.
     Many states are anticipating future changes to the CAFO rule and creating regulations
for operations that are smaller than those currently defined as CAFOs in the rule. Maryland,
central in the Chesapeake Bay watershed, is one of the few states to require CNMPs for all
livestock operations, regardless of herd size. Unfortunately, the issue of stocking rate is not
currently used as a criterion for designating farms as CAFOs, even though it may be a more
relevant guideline than herd size alone.

Farm-level costs of adhering to regulation

The costs of implementing a CNMP will vary widely across farms and regions of the US.
As an example, it was calculated that the average hog CAFO in the Mid-Atlantic region
would need to increase the amount of available land for manure spreading by almost 500%
in order to comply with a nitrogen-based CNMP. Obviously, the amount of additional land
required, and the associated costs, vary widely from farm to farm and region to region.
However, the overall effect will most likely be an increase in land prices and rents.
     Additional costs for compliance with CWA regulations may include investments in
manure storage, treatment, and handling, disposal of milkhouse wastewater and minimizing
run-off from the production area. Also, the costs of third party technical service providers to
assist farming operations with planning and compliance issues are incurred by many farms.
However, to varying degrees across states and sectors, the majority of these costs are often
borne by federal and state government programmes.
     The USDA has estimated the costs for developing and implementing a successful
CNMP. Documentation of these costs can be found in USDA's „Costs Associated with
Development and Implementation of Comprehensive Nutrient Management Plans. Part 1 -
Nutrient Management, Land Treatment, Manure and Wastewater Handling and Storage and
Recordkeeping‟. Table 3.3 provides the typical costs of the various components required in
a CNMP by animal sector and farm size in the US. These costs are the basis for estimating
38    State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

the CNMP costs of representative dairy and hog described later in this chapter. Obviously,
the actual costs are going to vary greatly from farm to farm, based on each farm‟s current
infrastructure and, hence, the required improvements necessary to comply with the
standards in each state.

The Clean Air Act

Background and general description

The CAA was created as Federal law in 1970 in order to regulate air emissions. The law
authorizes the EPA to establish National Ambient Air Quality Standards (NAAQS) to
protect public health and the environment. The CAA directs the states to develop state
implementation plans (SIPs) applicable to appropriate industrial sources in the state.
Originally, the goal was to attain the NAAQS in each state by 1975. However, progress was
very slow. The law was amended in 1977 primarily to set new goals (dates) for achieving
attainment of NAAQS since many areas of the country had failed to meet the deadlines. The
CAA was amended again in 1990 to address problems not previously focused on, such as
acid rain, ground-level ozone, stratospheric ozone depletion and air toxics.
     The CAA requires the EPA to set NAAQS (see Title 40 CFR part 50) for pollutants
considered harmful to public health and the environment. The CAA established two types of
national air quality standards. „Primary standards‟ set limits to protect public health,
including the health of „sensitive‟ populations such as asthmatics, children and the elderly.
„Secondary standards‟ set limits to protect public welfare, including protection against
decreased visibility, damage to animals, crops, vegetation and buildings.
     The EPA Office of Air Quality Planning and Standards (OAQPS) has set NAAQS for
six principal pollutants, which are called „criteria‟ pollutants. They are Carbon Monoxide,
Lead, Nitrogen Dioxide, Ozone, Sulphur Dioxide, Particulate Matter PM10 (which has been
revoked), and PM2.5. PM10 pollution includes „inhalable coarse particles‟, with diameters
larger than 2.5 micrometers and smaller than 10 micrometers and PM 2.5 includes „fine
particles‟, with diameters that are 2.5 micrometers and smaller.
      EPA uses these six „criteria pollutants‟ as indicators of air quality and has established
for each of them a maximum concentration above which adverse effects on human health
may occur. Areas of the country where air pollution levels persistently exceed the national
ambient air quality standards may be designated non-attainment areas. Once these non-
attainment areas are determined, states must develop Implementation Plans which outline
what actions will be undertaken to address the pollutants and the timeframe needed.

Impact on agriculture

Agricultural production can emit a wide variety of material into the air. These include dust,
a variety of nitrogen gases, pesticides, particulate matter from diesel engines, and controlled

                                                                                                                                                           39
40    State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

burning of fields. Potential pollutants associated with agriculture include internal
combustion engines, dust from dirt access roads, hydrogen sulphide, ammonia, odours,
other volatile organic compounds from animal manure, methane from dairy cows and cattle
and nitrogen oxides from fertilized fields. Such pollutants can affect human health, reduce
visibility, contribute to global warming and/or be a nuisance to neighbours. When the air
quality standard for any of six air pollutants is exceeded, states must inform the US EPA on
how they plan to respond. Any farm found to be a major source of regulated emissions in a
region where air quality standards are exceeded could be required to apply for and comply
with an operating permit.
     Like other nonpoint source pollution issues, air pollution emissions from agriculture
tend to be generated diffusely over a broad land area, rather than from a single pipe or
smokestack. As such, it has not been cost-effective to accurately monitor emissions from
individual agricultural sources using current technology. The potential cost of monitoring
agricultural air pollution is one reason that agriculture has been lagging behind in
compliance with environmental regulations (Ribaudo et al., 1999).

Approach and implementation

Similarly to the CWA and other federal legislation, each state is responsible for
implementing the CAA. It makes sense for states to take the lead in carrying out the CAA,
because pollution control problems often require special understanding of local industries,
geography, housing patterns, etc. Each state has a SIP that explains how the NAAQS will be
attained. The EPA must approve each SIP; EPA can take over enforcing the CAA in that
state if a SIP is unacceptable.
     New regulations in many states are seeking to reduce air emissions from agriculture,
particularly from animal feeding operations (AFOs). Air quality regulations seem to be most
significantly affecting agriculture in California. Ozone and particulate levels in California‟s
San Joaquin Valley have led to new requirements for agricultural producers, as this region
has some of the most polluted air in the country. Farmers in this area must develop
management plans showing how they will reduce dust, the burning of crop residue (e.g., rice
straw, orchard trimmings) is restricted, and large dairies must manage their manure to
reduce ammonia emissions (Ribaudo et al., 1999). Two specific air-quality regulations
impacting agriculture in the San Joaquin Valley include rules 4550 (for Particulate Matter
10), and 4570 (for Volatile Organic Compounds, including ammonia emissions). Operations
meeting the defined criteria must obtain air quality permits and agree to implement
conservation management practices which document reductions in air emissions.

Methyl bromide phase-out

Methyl bromide has been used in the US for decades to control insects, nematodes,
pathogens, and weeds. It is used for soil fumigation before planting many fruits, vegetables,
ornamentals and agricultural nurseries; for post-harvest fumigation of commodities in
storage and prior to shipment; and for government-required quarantine treatment to prevent
the spread of regulated exotic pests.
     The Montreal Protocol is a treaty designed to protect the stratospheric ozone layer,
which protects the earth from harmful solar radiation. The Protocol controls global
production and trade of ozone depleting substances. The Parties to the Protocol classified
                         State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US   41

methyl bromide as an ozone-depleting substance and agreed to phase out the use of methyl
bromide by 2015, except for critical uses.
     The net annual loss to consumers and producers of not having methyl bromide for
selected pre-plant uses is estimated to be in the range of $400-450 million (Carpenter et al.,
2000). This includes $150-200 million for annuals (strawberries, tomatoes and other
vegetables), $140 million for perennial crops, and $100 million for ornamentals and
nurseries (excluding forest nurseries). These losses represent 8-10% of revenues for the
annual crops (15-20% for strawberries, 4% for tomatoes), 3% for the perennial crops and
15% for the ornamental and nursery crops.
     Florida fresh tomato producers use significant amounts of methyl bromide due to the
more humid and higher rainfall climatic conditions there. The combination of methyl
bromide and chloropicrin has been used extensively on Florida tomatoes to suppress crop
pests such as nematodes, diseases/fungi and weeds. In 1992 and 1993, approximately 3,500
to 4,000 metric tons of methyl bromide, approximately 14% of the US total annual methyl
bromide consumption was used on the Florida tomato crop (US Environmental Protection
Agency). It is estimated that in order to comply with the methyl bromide ban by switching
to what EPA considers an equally effective alternative treatment, would cost Florida tomato
producers an additional $56 per acre. Additionally, telone, a substitute for methyl bromide,
is also a highly regulated chemical that has its own restrictions and considerations to
contend with, and several counties in Florida have restrictions on its use.
     In California, some methyl bromide is used in the southern coast region (where there is
higher rainfall and humidity), but it is not regularly used in the Central and San Joaquin
Valleys, where a Mediterranean-type climate limits fungal and bacterial disease problems in
tomatoes (Rivara, 2007). However, California strawberry producers have historically been a
significant user of methyl bromide in their production practices.

Quasi-voluntary programmes: conservation compliance programmes

Typically known as Sodbuster and Swampbuster, USDA compliance provisions require
agricultural producers to implement soil conservation systems on highly erodible cropland
(HEL), land converted from permanent sod, and refrain from draining wetlands in order to
remain eligible for benefits from selected Federal agricultural programmes, including price
support loans, income support payments and USDA conservation programme payments and
technical assistance. These programmes are considered to be quasi-voluntary because the
potential cost of foregoing income from federal programmes, by violating one or more of
these provisions, may represent financial risks beyond what the farm can bear. As such,
these programmes may be the most rigorous environmental requirements that US farmers
face (Bernstein et al., 2004). The programmes are provisions of the 1985 Farm Act (as
amended in 1990, 1996, 2002 and 2008).
     On HEL that has been previously cropped, a conservation system must be implemented
that provides for a substantial reduction in soil erosion from the level existing before the
conservation measures were applied. The conservation system must include treating sheet
and rill, wind, ephemeral and classic gully erosion. Erosion on HEL cannot exceed twice the
soil loss tolerance level (referred to as T) for the predominant highly erodible soil type in
the highly erodible field.
     On HEL cropland that is broken out of native vegetation (Sodbuster), a conservation
system must be implemented that provides for no increase in soil erosion from the level
42    State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

existing before the land use change occurred. The conservation system must include all
treatments and measures needed to meet the HEL requirements.
     The swampbuster provision is violated if a farmer converts a wetland to cropland.
Violations of either sod- or swampbuster are supposed to result in the loss of all USDA
programme payments for that year and for the years that follow. In the case of swampbuster,
the penalty can apply until the wetland‟s functions and values are restored. There are
allowances for producers to convert wetlands for production of an agricultural commodity if
they compensate (through mitigation) for the wetland functions that are lost.

Animal welfare and food safety regulations

The Animal Welfare Act (AWA) was signed into law in 1966. The original intent of the Act
was to regulate the care and use of animals in the laboratory. However, it has since become
the accepted minimum standard for animal care – the AWA is currently the only Federal
law in the US to regulate the treatment of animals in research, exhibition, transport and by
dealers (USDA Animal Welfare Information Center, 2000). It is important to note that the
AWA regulates the care and treatment of all warm-blooded animals, except those, such as
farm animals, used for food, fibre or other agricultural purposes (USDA Animal Welfare
Information Center, 2002). Because the AWA specifically does not apply to farm animals,
the Act has a minimal impact on agriculture. However, there are state and local efforts to
regulate some farm practices.

National Animal Identification System (NAIS)

The National Animal Identification System (NAIS) is intended to identify animals and track
them as they come into contact with animals from other premises of origin. The principal
aim of the NAIS is to enable animal health officials to identify locations where infected or
exposed animals have been and to track animal movement from those locations. In the event
of an animal disease outbreak, it is understood that the NAIS will allow officials to locate
exposed animals within 48 hours. The plan for implementing the NAIS was already well
underway when a case of bovine spongiform encephalopathy (BSE) was detected in the US
in December 2003. As a result, USDA quickly announced that it would expedite the
implementation of a verifiable national animal identification system (USDA Animal and
Plant Health Inspection Service, 2005).
    Currently, there is no mandatory requirement for agricultural producers to participate in
NAIS; it is completely voluntary. However, debate over mandatory participation in NAIS
by all premises that contain livestock is still underway in the US At this point, it is difficult
to ascertain the exact impact of a proposed nationwide, USDA-mandated animal
identification system.

The impact of regulations on farm profits: the cases of dairy and swine

This section contains empirical examples of the farm-level costs of compliance with the
types of regulations discussed above. The analysis of compliance costs for dairy farming
looks at two distinct dairy production systems: smaller, traditional dairy farms that are
typical in Wisconsin and throughout the Dairybelt states and large, modern confinement
dairy farms that are typical in California and the western states. The analysis of compliance
                          State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US   43

costs for swine production focus on Iowa, which is one of the leading swine producing
states in the US.

Dairy

Dairy production represents the second largest agricultural sector in the US in terms of cash
receipts at the farm level (Miller and Blayney, 2006). Beef cattle is the only larger sector as
of 2006. The structure of the US dairy industry has changed markedly since 1980. The most
notable changes have affected farm size and number, as well as the regional distribution of
milk production across the country. Since 1980, the number of dairy farms in operation in
the US has fallen by 75%. However, the number of dairy cows on farms has only fallen by
16.5% (Miller and Blayney, 2006). The logical outcome of these trends is that the average
herd size on dairy farms has increased from 32 to 111 cows.
     The decrease in total dairy cows on farms (16.5%) has been more than offset by an
increase in average annual per cow milk production of almost 60%. This number reached
18,967 lbs per cow for 2004 (Miller and Blayney, 2006). This rapid increase in productivity
is generally attributed to improvements both in technology and management.
     Significant changes have also occurred in the regional distribution of milk production
in the US. The traditional dairybelt states, from Maine westward to Minnesota, have seen
decreasing total milk production since 1990. These declines have been more than offset, and
in part caused by, rapidly increasing production in the western and mountain states,
particularly California, Idaho and New Mexico. In these states, the representative dairy farm
has several hundred to several thousand cows kept in modern confinement facilities. During
the 1990s, California eclipsed Wisconsin as the leading dairy state in the US and in 2007
produced almost twice as much milk as Wisconsin (see Table 3.1).
     This analysis has estimated the costs of compliance with relevant environmental
regulations impacting dairy farms typical of Wisconsin and California. These two states
were chosen not only because they are the top two dairy states, but also because they
represent two distinct milk production systems. Wisconsin dairy farming is typical of the
traditional, smaller dairy farm, with an average herd size of 92 cows per farm in 2006.
California dairy farming is typical of the modern, expanded confinement feeding systems
found in increasing numbers in various locations throughout the western states, with an
average herd size of 980 cows per farm in California. Obviously, the relative burden of
complying with environmental regulations is expected to be markedly different between
these two types of farms.

Compliance costs associated with the Clean Water Act and the Clean Air Act

As described above, the primary manifestation of environmental regulations for livestock
agriculture in the US is the requirement to develop and follow a CNMP in order to comply
with the EPA‟s CWA regulatory requirements. These mandatory regulations apply in
Wisconsin and California. With regard to the CAA regulations, Wisconsin does not have
any non-attainment areas for PM10 or PM2.5, hence is not required to develop an EPA
mitigation plan. Therefore farmers in this area do not have to apply additional measures to
comply with the CAA. As described below, however, California does have non-attainment
areas in dairy producing regions, so the regulations do apply.
44    State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

     The dairy costs presented in Table 3.3 represent estimates of the average cost of all
dairy farms in the US and are presented on a per 1,000 lbs of body weight (AU) basis.
Because many of the costs associated with compliance require capital investments in farm
infrastructure (e.g. manure storage facilities), herd size and additional factors will impact
individual farm costs. In order to account for these variations, more specific actual cost
information was obtained for the Wisconsin example. Unfortunately, similar state-level cost
information, based on actual data, was not available for California. Therefore, the USDA
national compliance costs were modified to reflect the much larger dairy farms found in that
state. The costs per AU for larger farms were used to construct farm-level costs for the
average California herd size of 980 cows. These numbers were adjusted from a per-animal
unit basis to a per-cow basis (using 1.4 animal units per dairy cow), to be consistent with the
Economic Research Service (ERS) Agricultural Research Management Survey (ARMS)
financial data. Then, a CNMP cost per hundred weight of milk was estimated from the
CNMP cost per cow estimate. The procedure used to estimate typical California dairy
CNMP costs are discussed in more detail below.

Wisconsin case study

The Wisconsin office of the USDA provided data for three years (2003, 2005 and 2006) of
EQIP applications for Wisconsin dairies needing to upgrade their facilities in order to
comply with CNMP regulations (Table 3.4). These data include the type of practices
needed, total cost to implement those practices, and the number of animal units to be
treated, as well as other variables. On average, the farms applying for EQIP funds in
Wisconsin were larger than the average Wisconsin dairy farm. Because larger farms have
the ability to spread investment costs over a larger number of AUs, the average costs of
compliance per AU from this sample of dairies would most likely underestimate the true
cost of an average dairy in Wisconsin. As described below, costs of compliance for the
average Wisconsin dairy farm were calculated from these data by using a subset of the
EQIP dairy farm data. This subset was used to create estimates of compliance costs in order
to determine the costs of a farm that is more representative of a typical size dairy farm in
Wisconsin.
     A total of 262 dairy farms applied for EQIP assistance in the three years of data
obtained. The distribution of herd sizes across these farms was heavily skewed toward
larger herd sizes. This is demonstrated by the fact that the average (mean) number of animal
units for the 262 Wisconsin farms applying for EQIP assistance was 402, while the median
number was 246. The average Wisconsin dairy farm has 92 dairy cows or 129 animal units.
Therefore, to develop a CNMP cost estimate that is representative of Wisconsin dairy
farms, a subset of the data from the 262 farms that applied for EQIP funding to comply with
the Clean Water Act regulations was used. Of the 262 farms, 111 had 200 or fewer animal
units. Of these 111 farms, the average (mean) number of animal units was 122, which is
close to the Wisconsin average of 129.
     The total costs for CNMP implementation for the smaller farms over the 3 year period
is US$10,685,406, which would provide compliance for a total of 13,702 animal units.
Therefore, the average total cost can be calculated to be $780 per animal unit or $1,092 per
dairy cow (1 dairy cow = 1.4 animal units).

The USDA CNMP analysis assumes that a typical structural life of a full CNMP is 10 years
and an 8% interest rate was used in the annual cost calculations. Therefore, to remain
consistent, the same assumptions were used in this analysis. Table 3.3 shows the average
annual cost estimate per animal unit (10 year, 8% interest) for all US dairy farms is $50 (or
$70 per dairy cow). The three years of EQIP data shows that average CNMP cost for all
Wisconsin dairies that applied for EQIP assistance was $48 per animal unit. However, the
impact of compliance costs on smaller farms is illustrated by comparing the average costs of
the 111 smaller farms (200 or fewer AUs) with the average costs of the larger (>200 AUs)
dairy farms requesting EQIP funding in Wisconsin. The average annual cost (10 year, 8%
interest) for smaller farms is $163 per animal unit, while the cost for larger farms is
significantly less at $43 per animal unit. The difference between these two estimates
(smaller EQIP farm applications vs. larger EQIP farm applications) highlights the impact
that farm size can have on CNMP costs per AU and per unit of output.
     These data lead to the conclusion that compliance with CNMP requirements costs the
typical Wisconsin dairy farm $0.83 per cwt (equivalent to 45.4 kg) of milk produced. As
shown in Table 3.5, this translates into an increase of 4.19% in total production costs and
7.46% of direct costs.

California case study

Since no actual California-specific CNMP cost data are available for this analysis, the
national USDA estimates were used. As illustrated in the Wisconsin EQIP data, the CNMP-
related costs per animal unit significantly decrease as farm size increases. Table 3.3 does
provide average costs per animal unit for large, medium and small farms for all livestock
types, as well as average per animal unit costs for all dairies. Data from USDA indicate that
the average US dairy herd is 155 cows. Therefore, a typical California dairy with 980 cows
would be considered a large dairy on a national scale.
     Table 3.3 illustrates that dairies have the greatest per animal unit CNMP
implementation costs of all livestock farms. Additionally, Table 3.3 illustrates that for all
US livestock farms, the average costs per animal unit decrease as farm size increases. The
estimated CNMP implementation cost for all US livestock farms therefore underestimates
the costs for dairy farms. To account for the higher CNMP costs associated with dairies, an
adjustment factor was calculated. To estimate the decreasing costs on large dairy farms the
46     State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

adjustment factor was obtained by dividing the $21/AU average annual costs for all US
large livestock farms by the $39 average annual costs for all US medium livestock farms in
Table 3.3. The resulting adjustment factor of 0.538 was then applied to the $50/AU cost for
a typical US dairy to estimate the per animal unit costs of $26.90/AU (or $37.69 /dairy cow)
for a typical California dairy to comply with CNMP regulations.

requirements is less than 25% of that of the average Wisconsin farm.
                          State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US   47

     California dairies must also comply with various air quality regulations, which are
determined by Air Quality Districts. California‟s San Joaquin Valley is a high dairy
production region. This area is a designated air quality non-attainment region for PM 2.5,10
and ozone. Therefore, all farms within these areas must submit plans to their local Air
Quality District. Currently, plans must be developed for PM10, documenting dust control
practices, and Ozone plans documenting a 20% reduction in nitrogen oxide and volatile
organic compounds. By June 2008, these farms will also be required to have completed a
PM2.5 plan.
     Agriculture within the San Joaquin Valley has recently been determined to be
compliant with EPA‟s PM10 standards. To be compliant with these standards every farm
must have developed and met a dust reduction (conservation management) plan, which
addresses all on-field activities (pre and post harvest) and other activities which include dust
control on roads, wind erosion control, equipment yards and staging areas and diesel engine
replacement or conversion. The local Air Districts do random spot-checks to assure that
every farm is in compliance with its dust reduction plan.
     EPA mandates that there must be separate plans for each principal pollutant not
meeting standards within an area. That includes ozone in the San Joaquin Valley. Therefore,
farms within this area must also complete an 8-hour Ozone plan by June of 2007 and are
required to comply with Tier 3 EPA/ARB off-road engine standards by the year 2015. All
farms with calculated NOx and VOC emissions from the agricultural operation that is equal
to or greater than 12.5 tons/year (about 350 or more contiguous acres or more than 1,900
milk cows) are required to have an air permit (San Joaquin Valley Air Pollution Control
District). The San Joaquin Valley Air Pollution Control District provides a calculator on its
website for each producer to self-determine whether they would need to comply with these
regulations. Also known as Rule 4550, it requires owners/operators of dairy and feedlot
facilities with population equal to or more than 500 mature dairy cows (whether milked or
dry) to provide a Conservation Management Practices (CMP) Plan to control dust.
Additionally, operators who also grow field crops on land greater than 100 acres of
contiguous or adjacent farmland are required to submit CMP applications for their field
crops.
     The CMP plan is designed to document reductions in PM10 emissions. In order to
comply, operators must:

   select one CMP for implementation from each of the specified categories;
   implement the selected CMPs starting July 1, 2004 and keep a record of the
    implementation of CMPs to be available upon request by the District representative;
   complete the applications and submit them to the District by December 31, 2004 for
    approval. The submitted applications constitute a CMP Plan once approved by the
    District; and
   keep a copy of each submitted application and each CMP Plan to be available upon
    request by the District representative.

Dust control, especially on farm and field roads, can be a large expense item for a typical
San Joaquin Valley dairy producer to comply with EPA‟s Clean Air Act PM10 regulations.
Treatment measures range from daily spraying with water, to spraying a sealant every other
year. Both treatments can be expensive, running about $2/foot, or $10,000 per treated mile
of road (Beyer, 2007).
48    State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

     Draft Rule 4570 would provide CAFO with the flexibility to comply with the Volatile
Organic Compound control requirements by choosing any mitigation measures from a list of
multiple measures listed by the air district or by implementing district-approved alternative
mitigation measures that would achieve equivalent emissions reductions as those listed in
the rule. The rule impacts dairies within the San Joaquin Valley with 1,000 or more milk
cows (not including dry cows). This rule is believed to only impact in a limited number of
dairies (233 dairies out of a total of 1,500 in the region), requiring them to implement
additional mitigation measures not already used on the facility.
     For those dairies that are required to comply with rule 4570, the costs of compliance
are estimated to be $18 per cow. Since our example dairy falls below the 1,000-cow
threshold, no additional compliance costs are assigned for compliance with this rule.

Swine

The structure of the US pork industry has changed markedly in the last several years.
Although inventory dropped in the late 1990s, it has risen steadily since then to almost 63
million head in 2007 (Figure 3.4). What is most remarkable is the steady decrease in the
number of operations, which has decreased to roughly one tenth the number of operations
25 years ago (Figure 3.5). The recent trend toward increasingly large hog operations has
resulted in just 2.5% of the operations (those with greater than 5,000 hogs) controlling 54%
of the hog inventory in the US (Figure 3.6). Although almost 40% of US hog operations
have fewer than 99 hogs, these operations only account for 1% of the hog inventory.
     Vertical integration is common in the US swine industry, which has experienced the
fastest rate of growth of CAFOs, along with the poultry sector. The greatest concentrations
of swine exist in the North-central states, led by Iowa and Minnesota, as well as in North
Carolina, where recent breaches of large manure lagoons have caused serious environmental
concerns. Air quality and odour control have become important issues facing large-scale
hog production in many regions of the country. As of 2007, the top five pork producing
states produced 57% of the nation‟s pork supply. Iowa produced 16% and has recently been
eclipsed by North Carolina as the number one pork producing state with a total of 17.8
million hogs and pigs. Iowa‟s hog production generally represents over $4 billion in cash
receipts (USDA National Agricultural Statistics Service, 2003).

The Clean Water Act - Regulations in the Iowa swine industry

This analysis has estimated the costs of compliance with regulations concerning water
quality for hog farms typical of Iowa. This state was chosen because at the time of the
analysis it was the leading hog producing state in the US Iowa‟s hog farms represent a
more modern, confined feeding operation found in increasing numbers in the top hog
producing states. The average hog operation size in this region of the country is
approximately 4,300 head. This is well above the threshold size to be considered a CAFO.
    The primary manifestation of environmental regulations for livestock agriculture in the
US is the requirement to develop and follow a CNMP in order to comply with the EPA‟s
CWA regulatory requirements. The USDA estimated national average costs for developing
and implementing a CNMP for a swine operation were used for this case study (Table 3.3).
Because many of the costs associated with CNMP compliance require capital investments in
farm infrastructure (e.g. manure storage facilities), the number of AUs over which such
                           State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US   49

investment costs can be spread will have an important impact on the compliance costs
incurred by individual farms.


Figure 3.4 US quarterly hogs and pigs inventory




Figure 3.5 Number of hog operations




     This analysis attempted to secure actual CNMP cost data specific to Iowa, but this was
not available. Therefore, the national estimates for the CNMP cost for swine operations
(Table 3.3) were adjusted from a per AU basis (US$44) to a per 45.4 kg of gain basis
(US$1.57) to be consistent with the available financial data for swine operations. The
ARMS data from USDA-ERS was used to provide production costs for a swine operation
that is typical of the Heartland region (Table 3.6). The direct and total costs per 45.4 kg of
gain are estimated to be $39.03 and $46.67, respectively.
50    State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

Figure 3.6 US hog operations, number and per cent of inventory, 2006




A typical Iowa swine operation produces two cycles of market hogs, or „turns‟, per year.
This implies that, on average, it takes six months to raise a typical market hog from feeder
pig to market. Therefore, at any one time there is only half the number of a farm‟s total
marketed hog capacity on the farm. Feeder pigs generally enter the operation at 50 pounds
in weight and leave the operation at 250 pounds. Therefore, over the life of the pig the
average weight is 150 pounds, which implies that 1 AU equals 6.67 pigs.
     Using the national average cost per AU for CNMP implementation for swine operations
of US$44, the average cost per 45.4 kg of gain is estimated to be US$1.65. This is
calculated by multiplying the average weight gain per pig of 90.9 kg by 6.67 pigs by 2 turns
per year, which results in a weight gain of 1,210 kg (or 2,666 lbs) of gain per AU. This cost
of compliance translates into a 3.54% increase in total production costs or a 4.23% increase
in direct costs.

Comparing US and EU agri-environmental policy

Differences in US and EU agri-environmental policy have been described in the literature as
having a focus on controlling extensification versus intensification, reducing negative
consequences versus promoting positive ones and focusing on outcomes versus practices,
respectively (Baylis et al., 2005). Although there are valid arguments supporting these
assertions, in some instances the assertions can also be shown to be false.
     The claim that the focus of US agri-environmental policy is focused on controlling
extensification of agriculture is supported by some prominent examples. First, the US
Sodbuster policy, created in the 1985 Farm Bill, attempts to control the amount of soil lost
from highly erodible lands (HEL) by requiring the use of approved conservation systems.
Similarly, the Conservation Reserve Program (CRP) seeks producers to sign voluntary
contracts to take HEL out of production. Second, Sodbuster‟s sibling, Swampbuster,
attempts to minimize the loss of wetlands to agricultural production. In both cases, the
policy effect is to minimize the intrusion of agricultural production on marginal lands,
                             State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US     51

hence, combating extensification. It is important to realize, however, that Sodbuster and
Swampbuster represent only a small portion of US agri-environmental policy and that CRP
is a completely voluntary programme that compensates producers for land taken out of
production.

                                                                                             3
equipment, and housing, manure handling, and feed storage structures, and breeding herd; Annual
costs based upon farm capacity = 2 'turns'/year (0.5 number of annual market hogs), $44/animal
unit/year, 6.667 feeder pigs (average weight 150 lbs)/AU. These costs are already incorporated into

Source: USDA Economic Research Service, 2006



     The assertion that US policy is focused on reducing negative consequences from
agriculture, as opposed to EU policy that is focused on promoting positive consequences
seems to have been, until recently, completely valid. Promoting positive consequences from
agricultural production is the core meaning of the term multifunctional agriculture. This
term has been an important part of the lexicon of EU agri-environmental policy since the
end of the twentieth century, but neither this term nor the concept has ever taken residence
in the US capitol. The Conservation Stewardship Program (started as the Conservation
Security Program in the 2002 Farm Bill) of the 2008 Farm Bill, however, does partially
52    State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US

embody the concept of promoting positive outcomes from agriculture. As this programme is
implemented nationwide, starting in 2009, US policy starts its migration toward rewarding
positive outcomes from agricultural production.
     Although the agri-environmental programs being implemented in the US have an
ultimate focus on environmental outcomes and make use of tools such as the Environmental
Benefits Index (EBI) to prioritize programme spending, the programmes remain almost
completely prescriptive in nature. This means that as the programmes are implemented the
focus is on getting a specific practice or set of practices implemented on the ground. There
is an obvious reason to use a practice-based approach when dealing with nonpoint source
pollution from agriculture; it is extremely hard to measure or monitor emissions. However,
there are two important drawbacks to this approach. First, there is very little quantification
of the actual environmental impact of the policies and programmes being implemented.
Second, the set of practices (BMPs) that are approved for cost-sharing represent only a
fraction of the possible actions that a producer could take on their farm and fields. This
greatly reduces the incentive for farmers to seek out or implement other more appropriate or
cost-effective actions for their farms. If US (or EU) agri-environmental policy were to be
more focused on outcomes or performance, then appropriately designed incentives could
encourage producers to incorporate environmental management into their farm business
decision-making (Winsten, 2009).
     Lastly, the EU is often viewed as having a more precautionary approach to
environmental issues. In many instances this may be true (prominent agricultural examples
include hormones used in livestock production and the use of genetically-modified
organisms), but not in all instances (Wiener and Rogers, 2002). The US policy response to
the threat of mad cow disease provides an example of the precautionary principle in use.
     As of 2009, the concept and use of market-based approaches to addressing agri-
environmental issues in the US is small, but increasing. The concepts of ecosystem services
and environmental credit trading are, although still on the fringes of the policy discussions,
gaining ground. By 2009, the USDA has created the Office of Ecosystem Services and
Markets. Although still in its infancy in the US this and other related efforts will help to
move policies and programs in this direction.

Summary and conclusions

The size and diversity of the US agricultural sector renders a comprehensive and concise
description of agri-environmental policy extremely difficult. An analysis of US agri-
environmental programmes points to the continued use of voluntary adoption of BMPs and
idling selected tracts of land, coupled with adequate cost-sharing as the primary approaches
for addressing agri-environmental issues across the US In many areas environmental quality
problems persist despite over US$2 billion in spending per year on these issues. The
increasing frequency of litigation over agri-environmental problems has led to greater
enforcement of existing CAFO regulations under the federal Clean Water Act. Although
CAFO regulations only apply to a very small percentage of US farming operations,
increased enforcement will result in increasing compliance costs.
     For most US farming operations there are likely to be some level of costs associated
with compliance to environmental regulations. The case studies presented in this chapter
indicate that the cost of regulatory compliance in the dairy industry is estimated to be 4.2%
of total farm operating costs for traditional dairy farming typical of Wisconsin and 1.3% for
                            State and federal responses to increasing input intensity in the US    53

large confinement operations typical of California. For the swine industry, a case study
presented for the state of Iowa estimates that the cost of regulatory compliance is 3.5% of
total farm operating costs.
     In the context of this book there are two important questions related to the costs of
regulatory compliance. First, what is the impact of regulatory compliance costs on the
competitiveness in the world market for US agricultural products? The answer to this
question obviously depends on the relative cost burden of regulatory compliance placed on
farms in the nations that represent the US‟s main trading partners. Other chapters in this
book provide estimates of compliance costs from many of these countries. Second, what
value to society is being gained from the current suite of regulations being imposed on US
farms and are the regulatory compliance costs justified by these benefits? This question
represents an important area for further research.

Chapter 4

Canadian agricultural environmental policy:
from the right to farm to farming right

Predrag Rajsic, Elizabeth Ramlal and Glenn Fox



Introduction

This chapter describes key features of Canadian agricultural environmental policy, assesses
the effectiveness of the Canadian approach and compares this approach to agricultural
environmental policies in the EU and the US. The chapter provides an overview of key
environmental issues involving primary agriculture in Canada and also describes the policy
instruments that have been applied in response to those issues. Two main themes are
developed. First, Canadian agriculture, relative to most of the developed world, is a low-
input intensity production system. This has important implications with respect to the
demand for environmental policy targeted at the sector, especially in comparison with some
high input intensity European countries. Secondly, as a federal state, environmental policy
that is applied to agriculture in Canada is exercised by the federal government, the
provincial governments, municipal and regional governments. In addition, agricultural
industry associations and potential common law or civil liability can and have had a
significant effect on the environmental performance of Canadian agriculture.

Connections to and comparisons with relevant literature

The environmental performance of Canadian agriculture is coming under increasing
scrutiny. Brubaker (2007) and Boyd (2003) have linked the adverse environmental effects
of Canadian agriculture, especially intensive livestock agriculture, to the adoption of „right
to farm‟ legislation at the provincial level. They argue that right to farm legislation has
absolved farmers from liability for what would otherwise be nuisances, in the form of
contamination of water or air, against their neighbours. This situation is an example of what
Coase (1960) called legalized nuisance. In the absence of legislative interference, a property
owner against whom a trespass or nuisance had been committed, would be able to seek an
injunction or damages. Right to farm legislation forestalls that option. As long as a farmer
has acted in a manner consistent with current industry standards, no liability attaches. Both
Brubaker and Boyd attribute at least part of the expansion of intensive large scale livestock
production facilities in Canada to right to provincial right to farm statutes.
     Montpetit (2002), on the other hand, maintains that what he sees as the inferior
performance of Canadian agricultural environmental policy, relative to its counterpart in the
©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         55
56    Canadian agricultural environmental policy

US, can be attributed to the contrasting structures of federalism and to differences in the
structure of constitutional law between the two countries. Montpetit describes the situation
in Canada, which he observes is similar to many OECD countries, as „agricultural
exceptionalism‟, indicating that agriculture in Canada has been exempted from general
environmental policies until relatively recently. Although he predicts that agricultural
exceptionalism is coming to an end, he expects that this process will be politically
contentious and ultimately less successful in Canada, as a consequence of the less federally
centralized structure of agricultural policy relative to the US. Also, the unique treatment of
agriculture during the sustained period of multilateral trade liberalization following World
War II, which amounted to granting an exemption to agriculture, left developed countries to
pursue divergent agricultural policies unconstrained by international trade rules. As a result,
the particular structure of agricultural policies in the US differs in important ways from
Canadian agricultural policy.
     The US experience, shaped significantly by federal farm legislative processes
beginning with the Agricultural Adjustment Act of 1933, and reformulated frequently in
subsequent farm bills, established the US Federal government as a leading institution in the
development of agricultural policy. Establishing a strong federal presence in agricultural
policy in the US created both a large federal bureaucracy dedicated to the sector and also
created an incentive for national farm organizations to develop and to establish themselves
as participants in policy development in Washington. One of the artefacts of the
Agricultural Adjustment Act arose because of the differing market shares of the US and
Canada for many agricultural commodities. Montpetit does not acknowledge this factor, but
it is critical to the emergence of agricultural environmental policy in the US. For many
agricultural commodities, the US has what economists call a large country effect. Variations
in US demand or supply often are large enough to have an impact on international
commodity prices. In many instances, because of the smaller size of its agricultural
economy relative to global commodity markets, variations in Canadian demand or supply
are not large enough to have a significant impact on international prices. This means that
curtailing commodity production in the US can be used to support farm prices and incomes.
This is not generally the case for Canadian farm production. Consequently, US farm policy
has often relied on land set asides, which took agricultural land out of production in an
attempt to reduce supply and increase prices. Land set asides have not been used in
Canadian agricultural policy, given the relatively smaller market share of Canada in
international agricultural commodity markets.
     The use of land set asides created an opportunity to transform agricultural policy into
conservation and ultimately environmental policy. Since farmers were receiving payments
from Washington to keep agricultural land out of production, it was a short step to make
these payments conditional on the use of conservation and environmental protection
measures, a process that culminated in the conservation reserve policy of the Reagan
administration. The absence of a farm payment and land set aside system in Canada meant
that the federal government had less leverage and that there was less incentive for interest
groups to organize efforts to control the levers of federal policy, either in the cause of
supporting farm incomes or in the cause of environmental policy.
     Montpetit concludes that the Canadian federal system, influenced by constitutional
allocation of authority for natural resources to the provinces, has produced a lower level of
environmental protection when it comes to agriculturally based pollution sources when
compared to the US experience. Part of the basis for this conclusion is the widespread
                                             Canadian agricultural environmental policy    57

reliance on provincial codes of practice and a range of voluntary programmes and
initiatives. We will discuss the role of codes of practice later in this chapter. But Fox and
Kidon (2002) have previously discussed the role of such codes in Canadian environmental
policy. While codes of practice often appear to be weak voluntary guidelines, in practice
they often form the basis for local building approval processes and they serve as the
industry standard in the determination of negligence in liability issues. As a consequence,
the impression of limited enforcement of these codes is more apparent than real. In any
case, most provinces in Canada have developed nutrient management policies and
regulations that do apply to especially larger livestock operations.

General features of Canadian agriculture

There were 67.5 million hectares (mha) of farmland in Canada in 2001. The Prairie
Provinces, consisting of Manitoba, Saskatchewan and Alberta accounted for about 81.5% of
that total. The Western Provinces (British Columbia and the Prairies) accounted for about
85.3% of total farmland. Central Canada (Ontario and Quebec) accounted for about 13.2%
and the Atlantic Provinces accounted for the remaining 1.5% of total farmland. Cultivated
land and pasture land represent about 61% and 30% of the total farm area in Canada
respectively. Average farm area (size) varies substantially across Canada. The average farm
area in Ontario is 92 hectares (ha) while the average farm size in Saskatchewan is 519 ha.
There is a general trend towards increasing scale of operation and consolidation in
agricultural production reflecting the large productivity gains that have occurred in
agricultural labour and capital inputs. Over the last 60 years, farm size has been steadily
increasing in terms of land area and the average farm size in Canada is currently about 273
ha. Farm size is also increasing in terms of herd size. For example, the average number of
dairy cows per farm has more than tripled over the last 30 years while the average number
of pigs per farm has increased by more than tenfold. This expansion in farm size, however,
has not been obtained at the expense of the family farm. It is still the case that 98% of all
farms are family owned and operated in Canada.
     Canadian agriculture is diverse. As can be seen from Table 4.1, red meats, grains and
oilseeds and dairy are the most important commodities. They contribute about 80% of the
$36.5 CDN billion (€24.3 billion) in market receipts for the major agricultural commodities.
However, the importance of the different commodity groups varies by region. In British
Columbia, market receipts are balanced across a range of commodities, but the most
important are fruit and vegetables, and floriculture and nursery. In the Prairies, red meats
and grains and oilseeds account for over 80% of market receipts. In Ontario and Quebec,
red meats and dairy dominate market receipts. In Atlantic Canada, potatoes, eggs, apples
and dairy are the most important commodities. Table 4.1 also indicates that grain and red
meat are the major Canadian export commodities. In 2008, more than a third of the
domestic production of beef, about 60% of pork production, about three quarters of wheat
production and virtually all of the flax seed, dry beans and lentils were exported. The export
dependency of red meat producers almost doubled between 1991 and 2001 from 30% to
55%. Dairy and poultry producers are largely dependent on the domestic market for their
income. Dairy and poultry are managed under a policy of supply management, with farm
level production quotas, formula prices and import controls.

Environmental issues in Canadian agriculture

The quality of Canadian soil and air has generally improved in the last two decades
(McKitrick, 2005; Lefebvre et al., 2005). As shown in Table 4.2, of the four agricultural
environmental quality indicators examined by Lefebvre et al. (2005), soil quality and
greenhouse gas emissions are indicating improvement, while nature conservation and
biodiversity and risk of water contamination with nitrogen indicators are showing
unfavourable trends. Table 4.2 summarizes major contributors to the changes in each of the
four indicators. Nature conservation and biodiversity are deteriorating due to increases in
the percentage of farmland being used for cropping. However, there have been some
offsetting effects from reduced summer fallow area, increased area under woodlots and
wetlands, and conversion of marginal croplands in the Prairies into tame pastures. Increases
in fertilizer use, in area under legume crops and in livestock numbers has led to a higher risk
of water contamination by nitrogen. On the other hand, reduction in summer fallow,
increases in the share of cropland under reduced tillage, no-till and forages contributed to
better soil quality and lower greenhouse gas emissions.

Even though the risk of water contamination by nitrates from agriculture is increasing,
Canada is still at relatively low levels of nitrogen application intensity, compared to other
OECD agricultures. Table 4.3 summarizes authors‟ estimates of nitrogen additions from
commercial fertilizer and manure and removal by crops in Canada by province. On average,
71 kilograms of nitrogen from manure and commercial fertilizer were applied per hectare in

the estimated figures are likely higher than the true values because nitrogen use data include
non-agricultural uses for lawns and greenhouses. It should, however, be noted that there are
differences not only across provinces, but also within provinces. Agriculture and Agri-Food
Canada (AAFC) (2007) identifies areas with high levels of residual nitrogen (above 40
kg/ha) in all provinces, with largest areas being in southern Manitoba, south-western
Ontario and parts of Quebec.

International comparison of nutrient input intensity of Canadian agriculture

Figue 4.1 compares the nutrient intensity of Canadian agriculture to an international sample
of developed and developing country agricultures. The figure shows total fertilizer use per
hectare of agricultural land per year on the horizontal axis and livestock units per square
kilometre of agricultural land on the vertical axis. The total nutrient intensity, as a national
average, would be represented by the area of the rectangle bounded by the origin and the
point in the interior of the graph identified for each country. The larger the area of this
rectangle, the higher the intensity of nutrient application, in the form of livestock manure
and commercial fertilizer application, for a given country. By this measure, for the countries
included in this comparison, only Australia, Argentina and Latvia have less nutrient
intensive agricultures than Canada. Several of the countries whose agricultures are
discussed elsewhere in this book, including the US, New Zealand and several EU Member
States (e.g. Spain, Poland and Italy) are more nutrient intensive than Canadian agriculture,
but still fall below 100 kg/ha/yr of average fertilizer application and 100 livestock units per
square kilometre. But, the axes need to be extended to 500 units in each dimension to plot
the highly nutrient intensive agricultures of Belgium and Luxemburg, the Netherlands and
even the United Kingdom, Germany, Denmark and France. Admittedly, this two
dimensional comparison abstracts from differences in yields, which would result in different
crop removal rates for nutrients, as well as a number of geophysical factors such as growing
season length and average precipitation, both of which influence input intensity of
agriculture and also condition the environmental risks of excessive nutrient use, and there
                                                                                                                                     Canadian agricultural environmental policy                                       61

are important regional variations in nutrient intensities within countries, but the broad
comparisons in this figure do suggest that the demand for agricultural environmental
regulation in countries like the Netherlands and Belgium might be much more intense than
would be the case in relatively low nutrient intensity agricultures like Australia, Argentina
and Canada. These differences in intensity should be taken into account in international
comparisons of regulatory systems and also in comparisons of environmental compliance or
abatement costs.
