Comparison of indices of environmental pressure across Canada

Large differences in environmental pressure, indicated by human and livestock population
density, can be observed within Canada as well. Figure 4.2 compares livestock and human
population densities (per km2 of agricultural land) between 1981 and 2006 across Canadian
provinces. Animal population density represents an approximate measure of nutrient
emissions, odour, and other adverse effects of livestock farming while human population
represents both negative environmental effects of human activity and a measure of conflict
between agriculture and non-agricultural population. Quebec and Ontario are the most
densely populated provinces, both in terms of human and animal populations. The highest
human population density in Quebec was at above 200 persons per square kilometre of
agricultural land, while the livestock population was about 160 animal units. For Ontario,
human population density was slightly higher while for livestock it was lower. In
comparison the remaining three major agricultural provinces – Manitoba, Alberta and
Saskatchewan – are far below these numbers. In Alberta and Manitoba, there are about 10
persons and between 20 and 60 livestock units per square kilometre of agricultural land.
Saskatchewan is at the bottom of the list, with human and livestock population densities of
about 5 and 15 per square kilometre respectively, making it a low land intensity economy by
any standards. These differences in intensity, both internationally and within Canada, should
be taken into account in international comparisons of regulatory systems and also in
comparisons of environmental compliance or abatement costs.

agricultural land (over 1,000) resulting from a small agricultural area in this province.
                                                           Canadian agricultural environmental policy   63

Regulations and standards

Canadian farmers are subject to a growing number of federal and provincial environmental
regulations. Canadian farmers also participate in voluntary industry or government-led
standards that aim to mitigate the negative impacts of agricultural production practices on
the environment. These voluntary standards are embodied in voluntary programmes, often
in partnership with governments, which may be certified by a government agency or
otherwise. These standards often are expressed as industry codes of practice. While these
codes are not generally backed directly with legislative enforcement, they often have
considerable influence on farm practices because they have become the standards used to
review applications at the municipal level to modify or expand ongoing operations,
especially livestock operations, or to construct new facilities. Industry codes of practice are
also widely used to determine standards of negligence in the determination of civil or
regulatory liability under general common law actions or under general environmental
protection legislation. This raises an important analytical point that needs to be emphasized
in international comparisons of agricultural environmental compliance costs of the type
undertaken in this volume. Environmental regulation and agricultural policy are undertaken
by different jurisdictions in different countries. Canada is a federal state, consisting of a
national government and also provincial or territorial governments. Under Canadian
constitutional law, agriculture is a shared jurisdiction between the federal government and
the provincial governments. Non-marine natural resources, on the other hand, are more
generally areas of provincial jurisdiction. And, especially in recent years, municipal
governments have been playing a more active role in local permitting, land use policy and
enforcement issues related to environmental protection. Of course, as a mostly1 anglo-
american common law country, civil liability for trespass and nuisance can also trigger
environmental sanctions. One of the implications of the complex division of jurisdiction for
environmental protection in federal nation states is that international comparisons of
regulations need to examine actions and policies of multiple levels of government and also
the effectiveness of civil litigation as a means of protecting environmental values.

Federal legislation

Regulations related to water quality

There is no national counterpart of the EU Nitrates Directive in Canada. However, both
federal and provincial regulations and guidelines are aimed at protecting the environment
from agricultural pollutants such as nitrates (i.e. Canada Water Act, 2009 and provincial
nutrient management regulations). The water quality guideline for nitrate-nitrogen
concentration is 10 milligrams per litre (Fairchild et al., 2000). This is equivalent to 50
milligrams of nitrates (NO3-N) per litre.

Regulations related to nature conservation and biodiversity

Several pieces of federal legislation are aimed at biodiversity conservation. The Migratory
Birds Convention Act (2009), first passed in 1916, and the Migratory Bird Sanctuaries

1
    Quebec is a civil code jurisdiction within the Canadian federation.
64    Canadian agricultural environmental policy

(MBSs) established under that Act are aimed at protecting migratory birds and their nests
against physical disturbance and hunting but they did not protect habitat or species of
wildlife other than migratory birds. The Canada Wildlife Act (2009) was passed to authorize
the establishment of sites known as National Wildlife Areas (NWAs) in which migratory
birds and other wildlife, particularly species at risk and more importantly, their habitat,
could receive protection.
     The Migratory Birds Convention Act (2009) establishes a regime for the protection of
migratory birds, whose habitat occurs on agricultural lands. The Migratory Birds
Regulations stipulate the conditions for the conservation and protection of migratory birds.
These regulations control, for example, hunting and possession of migratory game birds;
sale, purchase, or shipment of migratory birds, their nests or eggs; scientific collection,
aviculture, and taxidermy; activities designed to reduce the damage migratory birds cause to
crops or other property and the danger they pose to aircraft; and requirements for hunters to
use non-toxic shot for most migratory game bird species.
     The Migratory Birds Sanctuary Regulations prohibit disturbance of migratory birds,
their eggs, and their nests within an MBS (Migratory Bird Sanctuary). There are 92
Migratory Bird Sanctuaries in Canada. The regulations also prohibit disturbance of
migratory birds‟ habitat when MBSs are established on federal Crown land.
     The Species at Risk Act (SARA) (2009) protects wildlife found on federal lands as well
as their critical habitat. The accord for the Protection of Species at Risk, which allows the
Government of Canada to work collaboratively with the provinces and territories in the
development of a joint species at risk protection approach in Canada, including laws and
complementary programmes for the protection of habitats and the recovery of species and
the Habitat Stewardship Program, which encourages Canadians to join their efforts under a
landscape level approach in order to protect habitats, inter alia.
     A number of other laws and agreements are also in force to help in the effort to protect
and recover species at risk. These include the Canada Fisheries Act (2009), the Canada
National Parks Act (2009), the Wild Animal and Plant Protection and Regulation of
International and Interprovincial Trade Act (2003) and the Convention on International
Trade in Endangered Species (1979). The SARA also complements the work done by
provincial and territorial governments under the Accord for the Protection of Species at
Risk. This recognizes that species protection is a shared responsibility.

Public, animal and plant health

There are several pieces of federal legislation concerning plant and public health that apply
to agriculture. The purpose of the Plant Protection Act (2005) is to protect plant life and the
agricultural and forestry sectors of the Canadian economy by preventing the importation,
exportation and spread of pests and by controlling or eradicating pests in Canada. The Act
includes provisions for compensation for lost plants, inspection and treatment costs, given
that the provisions of the Act have not been breached by the farmer.
     The primary objective of the Pest Control Products Act (2006) is to prevent
unacceptable risks to people and the environment from the use of pest control products by
defining and setting standards for registration of pest control products. Unless an
authorization to use an unregistered product has been made by the Minister, using an
unregistered product represents a punishable offence. The Act also sets export controls on
pest control products.
                                                   Canadian agricultural environmental policy            65

    The Food and Drugs Act (2008) sets standards aimed at preventing sale of food that
has in or on it any poisonous or harmful substances, is unfit for human consumption,
consists in whole or in part of any filthy, putrid, disgusting, rotten, decomposed or diseased
animal or vegetable substance, is adulterated, or was manufactured, prepared, preserved,
packaged or stored under unsanitary conditions.

Provincial legislation

As we explained earlier, under Canadian constitutional law, most environmental issues
related to agriculture are managed mainly on the provincial and municipal levels. The extent
to which agricultural production is regulated at the provincial level is illustrated in Table
4.4. This Table illustrates the number of provincial legal acts related to environmental
protection under 9 thematic areas, specifically general environmental legislation, water use
and conservation policy, wildlife habitat protection, land use planning and animal health
protection in Saskatchewan, Alberta, Manitoba, Ontario and Quebec. Each asterisk
indicates a provincial statute related to the policy issue in question. A detailed examination
of each of the pieces of legislation and its associated regulations is beyond the scope of this
chapter, but the table does show an important pattern. In each of these thematic areas, the
primary agricultural provinces in Canada have at least one and often multiple provincial
legislative measures to address different aspects of agricultural environmental policy issues
within its jurisdiction.

Another inference that can be made from the inventories of legislation in Table 4.4 is that
the combination of federal and provincial legislation in Canada does, taken together, span
66    Canadian agricultural environmental policy

similar types of issues as the EU Directives. It could be said that there is a sort of functional
equivalence between Canadian agricultural environmental policy and EU policy. Table 4.5
aligns Canadian federal and Ontario provincial legislation with the EU Directives on the
basis of functional equivalence. For example, the Canada Water Act (2009) generally
applies to issues covered by the Protection of Groundwater Directive, the Sewage Sludge
Directive and the Nitrates from Agriculture Directive. Additionally, in Ontario, the Ontario
Nutrient Management Act (2009), the Drainage Act (2007), the Safe Drinking Water Act
(2002), Guidelines for the Utilization of Biosolids and Other Wastes on Agricultural Land
(1996), the Environmental Protection Act (2009), and the Lakes and Rivers Improvement
Act (2006) cover more specific issues related to the same areas of concern – water quality,
land application of agricultural and other waste materials.
     The idea of functional equivalence generally applies across provinces as well. In most
provinces, regulations regarding the design of livestock operations and storage and
application of agricultural source materials have been emerging in recent years. Intensive
livestock operations in Saskatchewan are regulated under the Agricultural Operations Act
(1995) and the Environmental Management and Protection Act (2007) and Regulations (i.e
Environmental Spill Control Regulations concerning agricultural liquid). In Alberta, the
Environmental Protection and Enhancement Act (2008) (i.e. under Waste Control
Regulation: Agricultural Waste), Standards and Administration Regulation (i.e. under
Manure Storage and Nutrient Management and Minimum Distance Separation Standards)
and the Agricultural Operation Practices Act (2007) and Regulations (i.e. under Livestock
Manure) address the issues of design of livestock operations and storage and application of
agricultural source materials. Similar acts and regulations can also be found in other
provinces with significant field crop and livestock production – Manitoba, Ontario and
Quebec. The Ontario Nutrient Management Act (2009) and new nutrient regulations in
Manitoba will be examined in more detail in the compliance cost section.

A case of nutrient management regulations in Ontario

The Ontario Nutrient Management Act (2009) sets out standards for land application of
agricultural source materials. Existing, new and expanding operations with more than 300
livestock units (≥ 300 LU) or located within 100 metres of a municipal well must obtain a
certified Nutrient Management Plan and a certified Nutrient Management Strategy if they
apply nutrients on their farm unit. All generators of prescribed materials must complete
certified and approved Nutrient Management Strategy. In addition, all farms are subject to
construction and siting standards and setbacks from wells and surface water. Brethour et al.
(2006) identified the following standards applicable to all farms set by the Ontario Nutrient
Management Regulations:

    Liquid manure application within 15 metres of a bank of a river if the maximum
     sustained slope of the soil is 25% and more is prohibited.
    Application of agricultural source materials within 15 metres of a drilled well (with
     depth of at least 15 metres and watertight casing) or within 30 metres of any other well
     or within 100 metres of a municipal well is prohibited.
    Application of nutrients to a field adjacent to surface water unless there is a vegetated
     buffer zone in the field that lies between the surface water and where the nutrients are
     applied is prohibited.
                                                        Canadian agricultural environmental policy                67

    Using a high trajectory irrigation gun capable of spraying liquid more than 10 metres to
     apply manure or non-agricultural source materials to land except if the material being
     applied is an aqueous solution or suspension containing more than 99% water by
     weight is prohibited.
    Having a manure storage within 15 metres of a drilled well (with depth of at least 15
     metres and watertight casing), or within 30 metres of any other well or within 100
     metres of a municipal well is prohibited.
    Manure storage facilities must be sufficient to contain 240 days of manure.
    On land which is subject to flooding or land where water collects during a rainstorm or
     thaw and flows directly into surface water, applying manure during the period
     beginning on December 1 of one year and ending on March 31 of the following year or
     at any other time when the soil is snow-covered or frozen is prohibited2.

Ontario Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Affairs (OMAFRA, 2009a), along with other
provincial governments‟ ministries of agriculture, provides formulae for calculating
Minimum Distance Separation (MDS) between new or expanding livestock facilities and
other land uses. The formulae are incorporated into provincial legal acts in some provinces
(i.e. Alberta, Quebec, Manitoba and New Brunswick) or provincial codes of practice and
municipal bylaws (i.e. Ontario, Saskatchewan, Prince Edward Island). The MDS is a
function of the size of expansion, livestock type, manure storage method and nature of the
neighbourhood. The purpose of MDS requirements is to minimize land use conflicts and
nuisance complaints related to odour but not related to dust and noise. Table 4.6 compares
the authors‟ calculated values of MDS for a mid-sized livestock facility near a rural
residential area for beef, dairy, hog and laying hen farms in selected provinces. It is evident
that there is quite a bit of variation across provinces as well as within some provinces (i.e.
Saskatchewan). The details of MDS in Ontario have been covered at some length by Fox
and Kidon (2002).

Municipal regulations

Municipal bylaws affect farming operations mainly through zoning and manure
management standards. The degree to which nutrient management and siting of livestock
operations is divided between the provincial and municipal jurisdiction varies across
provinces. Brethour et al. (2006) summarize bylaws for selected municipalities with high
livestock populations in each province related to siting and construction of livestock
facilities and manure management. They note that in the Ontario county of Huron, which is
composed of nine municipalities, farmers have to obtain building permits from the
municipal authorities for any new facilities. However, the Ontario Nutrient Management
Act (2009) for new operations greater than 5 livestock units supersedes local bylaws on
nutrient management. On the other hand, in the Manitoban Rural Municipality of Hanover ,
any new livestock operation with more than 600 livestock units has to be approved by the
Livestock Technical Review Committee. In addition, land parcels for livestock production
have to be at least 40 acres (16.2 hectares) in size. Operations with more than 250 livestock
units have to acquire at least 160 acres (64.6 hectares) of land. The barn on these operations
must not be less than 400 meters and the earthen manure storage must not be less than 500
                                                    Canadian agricultural environmental policy           69

meters away from nearby dwellings. Manure cannot be stored within 100 metres of any
body of water.

The compliance mechanism

The Ontario Ministry of Environment is responsible for compliance under the
Environmental Protection Act (2009), the Ontario Water Resources Act (2009), the
Pesticides Act (2009) and the Nutrient Management Act (2002). The compliance
mechanism is based on working together with farmers to develop appropriate farm
management strategies. Ontario Ministry of Agriculture, Food and Rural Affairs (2009b)
provides an overview of this process. Farm operations could roughly be divided into three
groups: (1) operations that are already in compliance with the law, (2) operations with
minor violations, and (3) operations with major, repeated violations. Operations that are in
compliance with the law may still benefit from additional Best Management Practices. The
compliance programme is designed to move the operations with minor violations into
compliance. The process is generally initiated through voluntary self-reporting or a
neighbour complaint. An Agricultural Environmental Officer (a provincial officer with
specialized agricultural training) would work with farmers to achieve compliance with the
legislation. For operations with major repeated violations, enforcement action is an option
but the aim of the compliance programme is still to provide help through problem solving to
move these operations into compliance.
70    Canadian agricultural environmental policy

Promoting compliance: Environmental farm plans

In Canada, management practices similar to the Good Agricultural and Environmental
Condition practices in the EU are implemented through various cost share programmes.
These agricultural practices are known as Beneficial (or sometimes Best) Management
Practices (BMPs). Agriculture and AgriFood Canada (AAFC, 2006) defines BMPs as „farm
management practices that: minimize and mitigate impacts and risks to the environment, by
maintaining or improving the quality of soil, water, air and biodiversity; ensure the long
term health and sustainability of natural resources used for agricultural production; and,
support the long-term economic and environmental viability of the agriculture industry‟. In
addition to management practices not legally required, BMPs also include Nutrient
Management Plans, which are required under most provincial nutrient management
regulations.
     In order to help agricultural producers develop and implement BMPs, the Government
of Canada initiated Canada‟s National Environmental Farm Planning Initiative under the
2003 five-year Agricultural Policy Framework (APF) through provincially delivered
Environmental Farm Plan (EFP) programmes. The programme was first introduced in
Ontario and soon after in Atlantic Provinces as a pilot project in 1993. The new Growing
Forward federal policy framework that took effect March 31, 2009 retained the EFP as its
integral part in Ontario, while in other provinces different elements of the previous EFP are
offered as separate programs (AAFC, 2010).
     EFP is a voluntary programme consisting of several stages. Participating farmers may
opt out of the programme at any stage. Farmers first attend an Environmental Farm Plan
workshop and complete a workbook designed to assess the current state of the farm and
identify areas of concern. Confidentiality of identified environmental on-farm issues is
guaranteed. The second workshop is designed for identifying possible actions and learning
how to develop a realistic plan. The next stage is developing an action plan for addressing
the areas of concern. The action plan is confidentially reviewed by a group of locally
appointed farmers (i.e. Peer Review Committee). Once the Peer Review Committee
approves the Action Plan, the EFP is completed and a farmer can participate in a Cost-Share
Program that helps cover a portion of the costs of implementing eligible projects from the
Action Plan (OMAFRA, 2009b; Robinson, 2006).
     Under the previous 2003 AFP framework, producers were eligible to apply for cost-
share incentives through the Canada Farm Stewardship Program, Greencover Canada, and
the Canada Water Supply Expansion Program. These incentives generally apply to initial
capital investments necessary for implementing BMPs. There were 36 BMP categories,
each containing several practices eligible for funding. The federal government covered up
to 60% of the cost of implementing eligible practices. Many practices covered through
federal cost share programmes were also eligible for funding under different provincial cost
share programmes. As a result up to 90% of the total project cost could be covered by
combining federal and provincial funds. The coverage varied depending on farmer
eligibility, province and type of Best Management Practices. In Ontario, funding was
available through the Nutrient Management Financial Assistance Program, Wetland Farm
Stewardship Incentive Program, Oak Ridges Moraine Environmental Enhancement Program
and Greenbelt Farm Stewardship Program.
     The new Growing Forward framework includes 28 eligible BMP categories. The
majority of the practices from the preceding version of EFP was retained. While some
                                                Canadian agricultural environmental policy        71

practices were completely excluded from the new plan, some were redistributed among the
remaining 28 categories. Some new BMP categories, such as 3 categories related to energy
conservation and production of renewable energy, were added to the list of eligible
practices. All practices are covered under the Canada Farm Stewardship Program and
various provincial programmes up to 100% of the total eligible cost. Table 4.7 outlines
examples of Best Management Practices that are eligible for cost-sharing under federal and
provincial programmes. Provincial Ministries provide a further detailed description of
eligible farmer activities, expenses and investments for each of the listed practices.

Recently, there have been initiatives for EFP framework to include provision of
environmental goods and services in addition to legally required level of environmental
stewardship. Kristen Thompson, the coordinator for the Alternative Land Use (ALUS)
72    Canadian agricultural environmental policy

project, reports (personal communication, August 2009) that the ALUS pilot project,
launched in Ontario‟s Norfolk county in 2003, is aimed at compensating farmers for
providing services such as creation and expansion of wetland habitat; riparian buffer
establishment; creation of tall grass prairie and oak savannah; reforestation using native
Carolinian species; pollinator hedgerows; and grassed windbreaks. A similar project was
launched in Manitoba in 2005 and is currently at the evaluation stage. The process of
establishing pilot projects is currently underway in Saskatchewan and Alberta as well. In
Prince Edward Island (PEI), ALUS has been part of the provincial policy since 2008. The
programme is administered through the PEI Department of Environment, Energy and
Forestry (Hager, 2009). As a general statement of future ambitions, the ALUS organization
aims at full integration of the alternative land use approach into agricultural and
environmental programmes that are currently in place on a provincial and national level.
According to Bailey (2008), ALUS can „build on and incorporate existing environmental
farm programs such as the EFP and allow farmers to do more for the environment on their
land.‟

BMP adoption levels

Levels of adoption of environmentally beneficial management practices across Canada vary.
A recent survey shows that the level of adoption for most of the practices that were
examined is between 40 and 50% (Brethour et al., 2007). For some practices, such as
variable rate fertilization, adoption is fairly low, at only about 10%. For others, it is quite
high. Conservation tillage and soil testing are examples of practices that were being used by
close to 80% of surveyed farmers. Next were nutrient application timing and using the
services of a certified crop advisor, at about 65% and somewhat less than 60% respectively.
Adoption rates for buffer strips, no-till, cover crops, and Nutrient Management Plan ranged
between 40% and 50% (Brethour et al., 2007).
     Even though the implementation of Beneficial Management Practices is partly
subsidized by the federal and provincial governments, it is not costless to the farmers. Based
on data for farmer cost shares under different programmes and the provincial expenditures
on the implementation of the 36 BMPs available from the Ontario Soil and Crop
Improvement Association (2008) and Conservation Ontario (2006), we estimated the
farmers bore about a third (about $44 million) of the cost of implementation of the
management practices eligible for funding between 2005 and 2007. More than 11,000 of
57,211 Ontario farmers implemented or were in the process of implementation of BMPs in
this period. Together with more than 14,000 farmers that implemented an EFP prior to 2002
(Robinson, 2006), this amounts to almost half of all Ontario farmers.

Lessons learned from the EFP experience

The voluntary adoption of sometimes costly BMPs indicate that farmers see value in such a
choice. One explanation is that farmers value environmental stewardship. Smithers and
Furman (2003) offer some support for this claim. In a survey of 123 farmers in four
counties, they found that farmers‟ „intrinsic concerns‟, such as attitudes toward soil and
water quality, human health and desire for more knowledge about the farm environment,
were deemed more important by farmers than community image, fear of penalty and
financial reward. This indicates that there is a demand for environmental management
                                              Canadian agricultural environmental policy     73

education within the farming community. The initial stages of EFP program are designed as
an educational programme whose main purpose is to help farmers learn how to manage their
farm in a more environmentally responsible manner. Thus, EFP could be seen as a response
to an existing demand for such a programme. But, in this case, the demand was not coming
only from the general public but from the farming community as well.
     For those farmers who do not see as much value in environmental stewardship, a
possibility of statutory liability if found in non-compliance with the existing regulations
through third party complaint (i.e. fees or imprisonment) often makes voluntary
identification of a problem the least-cost option for farmers. While completing an EFP does
not absolve farmers of legal liability in cases of violations of federal, provincial or
municipal environmental regulations it is generally considered an indication of due
diligence. On the other hand, farm assessment results under the EFP are confidential, and it
cannot be a source for a legal suit. Due to the confidentiality and the due diligence attributes
of the EFP, participation in the programme often reduces the overall risk of legal liability
for environmental violations.
     Another feature of the EFP identified by farmers to be an important contributor to
farmer confidence is that the programme is designed „by farmers for farmers‟. EFP was
largely developed and facilitated by farmer organizations. Robinson (2006) identifies this as
the key distinguishing feature between the Canada EFP and environmental policies in the
EU, which were characterized by a top-down design. Farmer surveys and direct interviews
indicated that farmers viewed positively farmer involvement in the identification of on-farm
environmental issues.
     In summary, there are several key features of the EFP that play a role in shaping farmer
incentives. First, the programme is responsive to farmers‟ desire for learning. Second, it
ensures that farmers are not punished for admitting they have an environmental problem. In
addition, there is a reward in the form of the due diligence clause in case of legal liability.
Finally, involvement of the farming community in programme design and administration
raises the level of farmer trust in the programme.

Available estimates of environmental compliance costs in Canada

An earlier survey of agricultural environmental compliance costs was compiled by Fox and
Kidon (2002). Their assessment was that then existing modelling and estimation work
indicated that compliance costs for environmental performance in Canadian agriculture
were generally in the range of 2% to 4% of overall costs. More recent work has confirmed
this earlier finding. Lintner and Weersink (1999) applied a multi-contaminant pollutant
control model with endogenous transport coefficients to an Ontario agricultural watershed
to determine the effectiveness of policy instruments for achieving multiple environmental
objectives (i.e. nitrate and phosphate emission reductions). The 280-hectare study watershed
was chosen for the Pilot Watershed Study by Agriculture Canada to examine the effects of
alternative farming practices on soil erosion and phosphorus contamination (Agriculture
Canada, 1990 as cited by Lintner and Weersink, 1999). While nitrogen water concentrations
did not exceed the legal limit, phosphorus concentrations did and the authors estimated that
a 20% reduction in phosphate concentrations would be sufficient to meet the phosphorus
standards. This could be achieved at a cost of about $11/ha, which is equivalent to 1.5% of
total costs.
74     Canadian agricultural environmental policy

     De Laporte (2007) used a combination of GIS and economic modelling to estimate the
opportunity cost of wildlife preservation and erosion reduction in south-western Ontario.
The economic model was based on actual costs of wetland drainage, representative crop
mix for the observed watershed, yields, prices and costs of production for south-western
Ontario. De Laporte‟s estimates varied depending on the achieved reduction in erosion and
wildlife habitat preservation. The maximum estimated reduction in sediment accumulation
(67%) and preserving of about 83% of the watershed wetland area from draining would
involve foregoing a net farm income equivalent to about 3.7% of total farm cost 3, given that
conservation tillage is used. Introducing conservation tillage as an option in addition to
wetland preservation can lower the cost of sediment abatement in areas where conservation
tillage is a low cost erosion control option compared to wetland preservation. This is
consistent with the finding of Yang et al. (2005), who estimated the effect of conservation
tillage on erosion and greenhouse gas emissions for the Fairchild Creek Watershed and
found that, in order to achieve a relatively low reduction (20% to 35%) in sediment loading
compared to De Laporte‟s estimate by using only conservation tillage, similar costs at the
watershed level need to be incurred (3.6 to 4% of total cost).
     Oginskyy (2005) estimated the cost of compliance with The Ontario Nutrient
Management Act (2002) for a typical Ontario swine farrow-to-finish farm by using a linear
programming technique. The model allows changes in the land base, herd size and
composition of ration as means of achieving compliance with phosphorus and nitrogen
emission standards. If a farm must comply only with a nitrogen standard, the opportunity
cost of compliance ranges from 0 to 6.1% of total farm cost, depending on the selected level
of nutrient standard, pig diet (involving the use of lysine and phytase) and farm size. As
expected, lower nutrient level standards involve lower compliance costs. Similarly, adding
lysine and phytase to the pig diet reduces compliance costs. Cost increases with farm size.
While tightening the nitrogen to the current 224 kg/ha involves no cost for smaller farms
(400 sows), it does create opportunity costs equivalent to a 3.8% increase in cost of
production for larger farms (600 sows). However, adding phytase reduces this cost to about
1.8%. In order to satisfy the phosphorus standard, a maximum opportunity cost of about
8.1% would be incurred and this is higher than the cost for either N or K standard. This
indicates that phosphorus is a limiting nutrient (i.e. satisfying the P-standard automatically
satisfies the N and K standards but not vice versa). Consequently, running the model after
imposing a combined N-P-K restriction produces results similar to the phosphorus
restriction. However, adding phytase reduces the P content of manure so that in the case of a
combined N-P standard, nitrogen becomes the limiting nutrient, and in the case of a
combined N-P-K, potassium is limiting.
     Salvano et al. (2006) estimated the effect of the proposed phosphorus-based regulations
on farrow-to-5 kg and on finishing swine operations in two most swine-intensive townships
in Manitoba - La Broquerie and Hanover. When sufficient land was available for manure
application on the farm, compliance costs ranged from 0.2% to 0.35%. Compliance costs
increased when sufficient land base was not available and manure either had to be
transported or treated. The increase in costs in this situation varied depending on the
method of transportation and distance as well as the type of operation and the presence of
phytase in the ration. The increase in cost ranged from 0.31% to 5.75% for transport with

3
 The percentage of total cost was calculated by dividing the monetary cost values estimated by De Laporte by the
average total cost for a corn-wheat-soybean rotation in Ontario in 2007.
                                               Canadian agricultural environmental policy     75

tractor-drawn tanker over a 20-km distance and from 0.25% to 4.66% for transport with a
truck over a 40-km distance and from 0.15% to 2.72% for a 20-km distance. Aerobic
manure treatment when not enough land is available seems to be the most expensive
compliance strategy, with increases in cost ranging from 3.48% to 8.42% depending on the
size and type of operation. Liquid separation is about 3 times cheaper than aerobic
treatment while construction of an additional storage facility due to no winter application
involves even lower increases in costs of 0.41% to 1.87%.

Concluding remarks

Constitutionally, Canada is a federal state in which a range of agricultural and
environmental issues are areas of shared jurisdiction between the federal and provincial
governments. It is important to remember this constitutional arrangement when conducting
international comparisons of agricultural environmental policy processes, institutions and
standards in Canada with those that have been developed elsewhere. This chapter has
argued that a functional equivalency approach is needed in constructing international
comparisons of agricultural environmental policies. It is the sum of actions by all levels of
government, in concert with standards established in civil litigation and with what is
sometimes called industry self regulation that results in environmental outcomes.
     Canadian agriculture is a relatively extensive agriculture, especially compared to the
input-intensive agricultures that exist in many EU countries. This variation in input
intensities has important implications for the environmental effects generated by agricultural
production and also for the demand for environmental policies.
     Available estimates of environmental compliance costs for Canadian agriculture
indicate that compliance costs are relatively low, especially when producers have flexibility
in the means that they can use to achieve compliance. Low compliance costs, coupled with
relatively low input intensities, may account for the effectiveness of voluntary
environmental measures that are used widely in Canada. In addition, the provincial and
federal governments have offered cost-sharing arrangements of various types to assist
producers in meeting environmental policy targets.

Chapter 5

Decentralized, outcome oriented management
of agricultural environmental issues in New
Zealand

Anton Meister and Nicole Beechey



Introduction

New Zealand lies in the southwest Pacific Ocean approximately 1600 km south east of
Australia and consists of two main islands, the North and South Islands, plus several smaller
islands. The combined size of the two main islands is 26.8 million hectares, which is similar
in size to Japan or Britain but with a population of only 4.0 million (Ministry of Agriculture
and Forestry, 2003). The geography is predominantly hilly and mountainous, with 50% of
the land classifiable as steep, 20% as moderately hilly, 30% as rolling or flat, and only 25%
below 200 metres above sea level (Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Trade, 2002).
Approximately 30% of the land is protected in a network of national parks and reserves, few
of which are found on the lowlands. Only 3% of land area is used for urban areas and roads,
leaving the majority of the remaining land for agriculture and forestry (Ministry of Foreign
Affairs and Trade, 2002).

New Zealand’s agriculture

New Zealand‟s temperate climate and fertile soils support almost every kind of farming
which dominates New Zealand‟s landscape (Parliamentary Commissioner for the
Environment, 2004). There are about 70,000 farms in the country and in 2007, 14.7 million
hectares were used for agriculture which represents almost half of New Zealand‟s total land
mass (Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, 2007b; Parliamentary Commissioner for the
Environment, 2004). Livestock farming accounts for 83.5% of agricultural land usage while
forestry represents a further 12.5% of land usage leaving approximately 4% of land for the
production of horticultural and grain crops (Statistics New Zealand, 2007). Agricultural
land is predominantly privately owned, and generally farmed by the landowner as a
commercial operation.
    Farming is a significant part of contemporary New Zealand society. In the international
community, New Zealand‟s image is that of „a land where there are more sheep than



©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         79
80 Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues

humans‟ and the „Clean and Green‟ image1 (Meister and Shakur, 2003). Although over 85%
of the population now lives in urban areas, the agriculture and forestry sectors continue to
play a vital role in New Zealand‟s economy (Meister, 2002; Parliamentary Commissioner
for the Environment, 2004). The New Zealand economy is based on its specialized natural
advantage: pastoral farming, horticulture, forestry, seafood, tourism, and niche
manufacturing (Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, 2005).
     Sheep and beef farming extend over approximately 10 million hectares and dairy
farming another 2 million hectares (Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, 2007c). Over the
20 year period from 1987 to 2007, the sheep population has declined from 64 million to
approximately 38 million. The beef cattle population has seen a smaller decline from 4.8
million in 1987 to 4.3 million in 2007 while the total dairy cattle number has increased over
the same period from 3.2 million to its current level of 5.2 million (Ministry of Agriculture
and Forestry, 2007c). Total hog, goat and horse populations have declined over the same 20
year period while poultry numbers have remained relatively steady and the deer population
has grown (Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, 2007c).
     Horticultural exports in New Zealand represent 15% of New Zealand‟s food and fibre
exports, totalling over $2.6 billion in 2007 (Aitkin et al., 2007). In 2009, New Zealand‟s
horticultural exports grew in value to over $3.4 billion. New Zealand‟s major fruit crops
include kiwifruit, wine grapes2 and apples which represent 29%, 23% and 13% of
horticultural exports respectively (Aitkin et al., 2007). The area planted in arable crops has
been declining and the area planted in outdoor vegetable has remained fairly steady while
there have been noticeable gains in the production of indoor vegetables (Ministry of
Agriculture and Forestry, 2007a).
     Agriculture remains New Zealand‟s dominant export sector. As the trend towards more
intensive farming systems in New Zealand continues the impacts on „natural capital‟ must
be observed and mitigated as necessary (Parliamentary Commissioner for the Environment,
2004). Since agricultural products represent a large percentage of exports, natural resources
must be managed carefully to ensure sustainable economic growth for the benefit of present
and future generations. The positive image New Zealand has created with regards to
environmental issues and green space attracts foreign tourists and provides a differentiated
product for the export of meat, wool, timber and fish. To protect this image and the
environment, New Zealand must take strong measures to curb pollution, manage the
landscape and protect its indigenous flora and fauna which will directly affect the
agricultural sector (Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development, 1996).

Agriculture and environmental issues
A number of environmental issues have been raised over the past century by the
development of New Zealand‟s agriculture. While farming in New Zealand is generally less
intensive than in other countries, significant soil erosion problems have resulted from
removal of the natural forest cover for pastoral farming in some areas of New Zealand. The
removal of indigenous vegetation raises the issue of protecting biodiversity. The resulting
sediment along with nutrient run-off and discharge of agricultural wastes has contributed to
1
  „Clean and Green‟ is an image New Zealand projects of the environment in which agriculture is conducted
which is characterized by abundant supplies of clean water and air, soil protected by low use of fertilizers and
pesticides, reliance on all year outside grass feeding and low human population density.
2
  Exported as wine.
            Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues   81

water quality concerns in some areas. The conversion of land to horticultural and dairy
production has made the issues of water allocation, water quality and minimum stream flow
important to New Zealand‟s environment.
     Production rates from pastoral farming have increased in the last decade, mainly due to
more intensive farm operations and more productivity per animal (Organisation for
Economic Co-operation and Development, 2007). In the dairy sector, farmers responding to
economic signals have intensified their operations by converting suitable dry-stock pasture
and exotic forestry into dairy farms while existing dairy farms have become more intensive.
Examples of increased productivity per animal include higher lambing percentages and
greater milk production per cow. The results of more intensive farming in some regions
include changes in soil health, reduction of freshwater quality and increased greenhouse gas
emissions (Ministry for the Environment, 2007a; Parliamentary Commissioner for the
Environment, 2004).
     These environmental issues have been recognised and environmental management of
land, water, air and biodiversity resources is occurring under the Resource Management Act
1991. To retain the clean and green image New Zealand is proud of, the country must take a
serious approach to handling environmental problems like surface and groundwater quality
which continue to deteriorate (Ministry for the Environment, 2007a; Parliamentary
Commissioner for the Environment, 2004). Although scientists have pointed to the
possibilities of nutrients causing eutrophication, reduced clearness of lakes and other nitrate
problems, New Zealand has been slow to enact policies to deal with the issue.

Environmental management in New Zealand

Current policy directions reflect the increasing concern about the effects of farming systems
on the environment. Directing policy development to deal with environmental concerns
provides opportunities for New Zealand to position its products as coming from a high
quality, sustainable farming environment. New or existing policies acknowledge the need to
protect the land resource which includes biodiversity and landscape preservation with some
of the physical threats being soil erosion, weeds, pests, and water pollution. Animal health
and food safety associated with current agricultural practices and systems are being dealt
with under appropriate legislation. The protection of the consumer and the environment is
also reflected in the current moratorium on genetically modified organisms and the
establishment of an Environmental Risk Management Authority (ERMA). The ERMA will
deal with current and future proposals for the growing or introduction of genetically
modified crops or organisms.
     Management of New Zealand‟s natural resources is governed by a number of statutes
including the Resource Management Act (1991), the Local Government Act (2002), the
Hazardous Substances and New Organisms Act (1996), the Conservation Act (1987) and
the Fisheries Act (1996). The Resource Management Act 1991 (RMA) is the overriding
statute and has a single purpose: to promote sustainable management of natural and physical
resources (New Zealand Government, 2005).

The RMA‟s central functions are to:

   manage the environmental effects of activities on air, water, and land;
82 Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues

   manage the use of publicly owned or managed natural resources (water, geothermal
    resources, the surface of lakes and rivers, river beds, and the foreshore and seabed);
   control the discharge of contaminants to land, air, and water (Ministry for the
    Environment, 2007a, p. 47).

The RMA involves several key concepts (Organisation for Economic Co-operation and
Development, 1996, p. 32):

   the development of comprehensive effects-based legislation for environmental
    management, requiring examination and consistent treatment of all factors in any issue
    (removing the need for sector-specific responses);
   the desirability of intervening only where required and clearly justified;
   the requirement of clearly focused outcomes (targets) where intervention is justified;
    and
   the need to use appropriate policy instruments to achieve cost-effective solutions.

The RMA entails a shift away from mandating technologies or discharge standards to
protect the environment towards a focus on ambient environmental quality, with flexibility
as to how it is to be achieved (Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development,
1996). The reform moved legislation from a prescriptive method to an enabling mode. As
Le Heron and Pawson (1996) state, the planning regime established by the RMA regulates
the environmental effects of resource development, rather than directly controlling land
usage.
     Communities most closely affected by the resource usage are responsible for the
implementation of the RMA. Thus, local government is a critical part of environmental
management in New Zealand, much more so than in other OECD countries (Ministry for the
Environment, 2007a, p. 47). As a result, the majority of resource management programs
relevant to agriculture are now carried out at the regional level. These include soil
conservation activities, water quality monitoring and control, pest management, etc.
Councils develop policies in consultation with their communities to address the issues under
the RMA. Under the RMA, central government has the authority to develop policy
statements on matters of national significance, national environmental standards, and water
conservation orders. However, no set of overriding rules or measures exists as each regional
authority is responsible for identifying environmental risks in their respective areas and
developing regulations to address these threats.
     The Ministry for the Environment (MFE) coordinates the development of
environmental standards and guidelines to help local authorities and resource users
implement their responsibilities under the RMA. Standards and guidelines help define the
„environmental bottom line‟ of sustainable management. Standards and guidelines prescribe
the minimum amount of regulation to best achieve the desired environmental outcome.
Furthermore, national standards should only be developed where the advantages of
protecting national values or providing national consistency outweigh the advantages of
regional resource management.
     In 2003, the Ministry for the Environment and the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry
jointly launched the Sustainable Water Programme of Action (SWPoA) to identify priorities
for government action to improve freshwater management in New Zealand. The SWPoA
has a particular focus on addressing the pressures on water bodies from land-use change and
            Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues   83

intensification. The SWPoA is now leading the development of national and regional
policies to deal with freshwater demand and water quality management (Ministry for the
Environment, 2007a, p. 278).
     In 2004, New Zealand‟s central government introduced 14 national environmental
standards for air quality. National policy statements on water management, flood
management, electricity transmission and telecommunication infrastructures are also being
developed. Regional councils manage weeds and pests under the Biosecurity Act of 1993.
The regional councils must identify weed and animal pests, develop a pest management
strategy, and then choose the most effective method for pest control.

Implementation of the RMA

Regional councils develop regional plans and policy statements, set environmental
standards, and implement the plans by granting permits for resource use (Ministry of
Agriculture and Forestry, 2005). The regional councils are responsible for resource use
consents which permit the following:

   take, use, dam or divert water. All abstraction of water from any natural source requires
    permission from a Regional Council;
   discharge contaminants to land, air or water (including marine waters). Point source
    discharge requires permission from the relevant Regional Council to enable the council
    to protect native flora and fauna, drinking water supplies, commercial needs as well as
    cultural and recreational values for clean water;
   occupy the coastal marine area;
   place structures or carry out other activities in river and lake beds;
   control activities on land for the purpose of soil conservation, the maintenance and
    enhancement of the quality or quantity of water in a water body, the avoidance or
    mitigation of natural hazards and the prevention or mitigation of any adverse effects
    from storage, use, disposal or transportation of hazardous substances.

Policies and rules within Regional and District Plans manage the effects of land use. Each
rule in Table 5.1 outlines the requirements, if any, applying to a land use activity falling
within that rule category (Horizons Regional Council, 2003; Morris and Workman, 1998).
     Activities which affect air quality, water quality and quantity, lake and river beds, and
the coastal marine area are restricted under the RMA unless allowed by a rule or resource
consent. By contrast, land use activities are allowed under the RMA unless restricted by a
rule. When permitted, the activity is allowed given adherence to standards and conditions
set. When not permitted, a consent must be obtained which in turn has a set of conditions
associated with it. Table 5.2 below illustrates the implications of the Resource Management
Plan for the Manawatu-Wanganui Region (Horizons Regional Council, 2006).

Water quality management and impacts on farming

The decline in water quality due to nutrient loading has not been effectively reduced
through effluent controls because of the increasing intensity of farm operations
(Parliamentary Commissioner for the Environment, 2004). Effluent controls ignore the
impact of diffuse factors such as run-off from pastoral land, cattle in streams and rivers,
84 Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues

over fertilization, stocking density and nutrient leaching. According to the Environmental
Performance Review conducted by the OECD for New Zealand, the largest sources of
nitrogen leaching in New Zealand are livestock urine and manure (Organisation for
Economic Co-operation and Development, 2007).
