Fonterra, which is a large dairy cooperative, is leading an effort to control the damaging
effects of nutrient loading. The majority of dairy farmers in New Zealand belong to
Fonterra. The „Dairying and Clean Streams Accord‟ was signed by Fonterra in 2003.
Farmers are provided with information on the importance of compliance with the Accord
through field days and discussion groups (Ministry for the Environment, 2008). The Accord
outlines five targets to reduce effluent pollution as determined by regional councils, the
Ministry for the Environment, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry in conjunction with
Fonterra to be achieved by 2012. The targets are:

    Exclude dairy cattle from 50% of streams, rivers and lakes by 2007, 90% by 2012.
    By 2007, 50% of regular crossing points have bridges or culverts, 90% by 2012.
    Immediate, 100% compliance with resource consents and regional plans.
    A nutrient input and output management system in place by 2007 on 100% of dairy
     farms.
    By 2005, 50% of regionally significant wetlands are fenced, 90% by 2007.
86 Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues

The Dairying and Clean Streams Accord Snapshot of Progress 2008/2009 outlines the
current progress in reaching the above targets (Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, 2010).
The progress report shows that with respect to target one, 80% of dairy cattle are now
excluded from streams, rivers and lakes, with two years remaining to meet the 90% target
for 2012. Target number two has been achieved and number four is at 99% of dairy farms.
Producers are struggling to meet targets three and five. In the case of target three, only 60%
of producers are compliant. For target five, only six of 13 regional councils have defined
and identified regionally significant wetlands. Only three of these six regions have met the
2007 target.
     In some areas of New Zealand water quality has deteriorated to the point where it may
be unsafe for livestock to drink or, in the worst cases, lakes have become eutrophied
(Parliamentary Commissioner for the Environment, 2004). Regional councils have begun
taking further actions to address the water quality issues by enacting policies to limit
nitrogen leaching into waterways and groundwater. This move is also supported by the New
Zealand government‟s SWPoA 2003 which is designed to identify priorities for government
action to improve freshwater management in New Zealand. In 2007, the government
approved the development of a national policy statement on freshwater to safeguard water
quality and aquatic ecosystems (Ministry for the Environment, 2007a).
     In the Waikato region, one of New Zealand‟s most well-known lakes, Lake Taupo, is
threatened by nutrients. The Waikato Regional Council has decided that the flow of
nutrients into the lake must be capped and nitrogen runoff into the lake must be reduced by
20% (Environment Waikato Regional Council, 2010).

Case Study: Nitrogen Management in the Lake Taupo Catchment
The problem

Scientific evidence over the past 30 years shows that the water quality of Lake Taupo has
been declining. Historically, the lake had extremely low levels of nitrogen and other
nutrients, which limited the growth of nuisance plants in its waters (Yerex, 2009). However,
major land developments in the catchment since the 1930s and changes to more intensive
land uses around the lake have increased the amount of nutrient and sediment entering the
lake (Yerex, 2009). Landowners have worked to protect the lake from sediment, through
extensive fencing, tree planting and land retirement under the Taupo Catchment Control
Scheme. These actions successfully controlled sediment and phosphorus entering the lake,
but have not prevented increasing nitrogen levels. The increase in nitrogen levels creates
greater numbers of tiny free-floating algae, and nutrient dependent weeds, slimes and
potentially toxic algae near lakeshore settlements (Environment Waikato Regional Council,
2004). Thus, the clarity and quality of Lake Taupo has been significantly reduced as a result
of the increased nutrients in the lake. Since pastoral uses of the land contribute almost 93%
of the manageable nitrogen entering the lake, nitrogen management focuses on agricultural
uses as illustrated through the method Environment Waikato has established for protecting
Lake Taupo as outlined below.
            Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues   87


Nitrogen management

Applying less than 75 kg of nitrogen fertilizer per hectare for growing crops will be
permitted under the nitrogen management rules for Lake Taupo (Environment Waikato
Regional Council, 2010). For producers that graze animals, the stocking rates outlined in
Table 5.3 shows the maximum number of animals per hectare that is allowable under the
new nitrogen management rules so long as nitrogen fertilizer is not used.
     If nitrogen fertilizer in excess of 75 kg is applied or the stocking levels exceed their
respective maximums consents are required. This involves approximately five steps which
are outlined below (Environment Waikato Regional Council, 2010).

   Meet with Environment Waikato to identify and provide farm data for benchmarking.
   Environment Waikato checks information and sends to AgResearch to prepare draft
    report.
   Review of draft report to check for accuracy.
   Final report prepared, sent with a letter confirming Nitrogen Discharge Allowance
    (NDA).
   Prepare a Nitrogen Management Plan and apply for consent.


Table 5.3 Maximum stocking levels for grazing animals in Lake Taupo Region

                                   Animal                Animal/ha
                                   Dairy cow             0.55
                                   Beef cattle           0.8
                                   Calves                3.3
                                   Horses                0.8
                                   Sheep                 7.7
                                   Deer                  3.3
                                   Alpaca/Llama          3
                                   Goats                 10
                                   Free range pig        2.5

Source: Environment Waikato Regional Council (2010)



Benchmarking

Essentially the benchmarking process represents a grandfathering scheme, assigning a
nitrogen discharge allowance (NDA) based on historical output. To assign a NDA there is a
substantial amount of information required by Environment Waikato. The most obvious
items revolve around the application of fertilizer including the method of application, total
amount of fertilizer utilized as well as the quantities of nitrogen, phosphorus, potassium and
sulphur applied including when and where it was applied. Additional information is
required regarding the stock grazing the land including detailed records, percentage of male
stock, supplements fed to the stock and the number of stock purchased, sold, born and those
that died (Environment Waikato Regional Council, 2010).
88 Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues

Obtaining consents

Four major options exist for what to do after the benchmark has been established. In each
option a producer must submit Forms A and B as well as a $500 deposit (Environment
Waikato Regional Council, 2010). These three submissions are all that is required to
maintain the same fertilizer application and stocking rates. In order to alter production
practices and retain all of the nitrogen, a Nitrogen Management Plan (NMP) must also be
prepared which meets the NDA established through benchmarking (Environment Waikato
Regional Council, 2010). If a producer wishes to sell nitrogen to permanently remove it
from the Lake Taupo catchment, a NMP is again required and producers are encouraged to
communicate with the Lake Taupo Protection Trust. To purchase or sell nitrogen, thus
altering nitrogen levels, a producer must ensure the party that is being traded with also
applies for consent and prepares a NMP to meet the proposed NDAs (Environment Waikato
Regional Council, 2010).

Biodiversity

New Zealand‟s international identity is a result of the unique indigenous biodiversity and
distinctive landscapes. Many indigenous species are found nowhere else on earth as a result
of the country‟s isolated evolution and the diversity of land and seascapes (Ministry of
Foreign Affairs and Trade, 2002; Taranaki Regional Council, 2004). New Zealand is home
to many extraordinary species, including the world‟s only flightless parrot, the kakapo, the
kiwi, a bird with nostrils at the end of its beak, a primitive frog that lays eggs that hatch into
adult frogs Leiopelma species and the giant weta, a large insect which fills a role that small
rodents play in other countries. Both species of New Zealand bat are endemic, as are all
four frog species, more than 90% of insects and marine molluscs, 80% of vascular plant
species3 and 25% of all bird species (Taranaki Regional Council, 2004).
     New Zealand was one of the last places on earth to be settled by humans. Since that
time, indigenous biodiversity has been in a state of decline. In the last 700-800 years,
humans and their accompanying pests have caused the extinction of:

    32% of native land and freshwater birds;
    18% of sea birds;
    three of seven frog species;
    at least 12 invertebrate species (snails and insects);
    one fish; one bat and up to three reptile species;
    11 plant species (Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Trade, 2002; Taranaki Regional
     Council, 2004).

The 2007 Environment of New Zealand review states „In 2007, New Zealand‟s biodiversity
faces the same pressures as 10 years ago. Introduced animal and plant pest species remain a
serious threat to New Zealand‟s biodiversity. Native plants and animals that survived the
initial habitat modification caused by human settlement continue to be threatened by
introduced predators and competitors.‟ (Ministry for the Environment, 2007a, p. 401).

3
 Vascular plants include ferns, flowering plants and trees, but do not include mosses and liverworts (of which
New Zealand has over 1,000 species).
            Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues   89

     New Zealand‟s biodiversity is important for scientific discovery as well as cultural and
economic purposes as these ecosystems perform many important services. The ecosystem
provides clean air and water, decomposes wastes and recycles nutrients, maintains healthy
soils, aids pollination, regulates local climate, and reduces flooding. The services provided
by the diverse New Zealand ecosystem help sustain the country‟s agricultural and forestry
production (Ministry for the Environment, 2007a, p. 351).
     In response to New Zealand‟s decline in indigenous biodiversity, in the 1980s and
1990s the Government placed emphasis on reforming the environmental protection and
management legislation as well as administrative structures. Of particular importance were
the establishment of the Department of Conservation in 1987 and the enactment of the RMA
1991, which includes the duty of those administering the RMA to protect areas of
significant indigenous vegetation and significant habitats of indigenous fauna.
     Much of the effort to protect New Zealand‟s native vegetation occurs on approximately
8.4 million hectares of legally protected land (Ministry for the Environment, 2010). Within
the OECD, New Zealand has the greatest amount of land protected for conservation
purposes at 33.4% with an average 15.5% of land protected across all OECD countries. The
majority of the upland and mountainous land is protected whereas the coastal areas,
lowlands, wetlands and dunelands have little protection (Ministry for the Environment,
2010).
     New Zealand‟s biodiversity is also managed through regional and territorial authorities.
Indigenous biodiversity is managed through the RMA under section 5(2)(b) which describes
„safeguarding the life-supporting capacity of air, water, soil and ecosystems‟ (Ministry for
the Environment, 2007b). Section 6(c) of the RMA describes the „protection of areas of
significant indigenous vegetation and significant habitats of indigenous fauna as a matter of
national importance‟ (Ministry for the Environment, 2007b). Lastly, section 7(d) and
section 2(1) of the RMA state that it has „regard to the intrinsic value of ecosystems‟
wherein „intrinsic values include genetic and biological diversity‟ (Ministry for the
Environment, 2007b).
     The Biosecurity Strategy 2003 has three major goals to protect New Zealand‟s
biodiversity and indigenous life forms. They are as follows:

   Prevention and exclusion - preventing the entry and establishment of pests and
    unwanted organisms capable of causing unacceptable harm to the economy,
    environment and people‟s health.
   Surveillance and response - early detection, identification and assessment of pests and
    unwanted organisms capable of causing unacceptable harm and, where appropriate,
    deployment of a rapid and effective incursion response that maximizes the likelihood of
    eradication.
   Pest management: effective management (including eradication, containment and
    control) of established pests and unwanted organisms capable of causing harm to the
    economy, environment and people‟s health (Biosecurity Council, 2003).

Land conservation and management
Conservation of private land mostly occurs through voluntary efforts. The New Zealand
Landcare Trust, Queen Elizabeth II National Trust (QEII), Nga Whenua Rahui (NWR) and
90 Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues

Nature Heritage Fund (NHF) are all examples of programmes that individuals can
voluntarily participate in to help preserve New Zealand‟s natural landscape.
     The New Zealand Landcare Trust was established in 1996 to promote sustainable land
management, including the conservation of biodiversity (New Zealand Landcare Trust,
2010). The Landcare Trust currently funds projects that will improve and maintain
indigenous life forms in New Zealand by restoring waterways, inventorying biodiversity and
enhancing protection for biodiversity (New Zealand Landcare Trust, 2010). Projects funded
through the Landcare Trust include the Waikawa River Restoration Project, Biodiversity
Southland Partnership, Biodiversity Restoration Northland and Biodiversity Restoration
Southland (New Zealand Landcare Trust, 2010).
     The QEII was established in 1977 to protect, preserve and enhance open space in New
Zealand (Queen Elizabeth II National Trust, 2010). Through the QEII landowners sign
covenants which are legally binding protection agreements registered on the land title. Once
this occurs the land is protected for both the current and any future owners. Each QEII
covenant is visited every other year to monitor the site (Queen Elizabeth II National Trust,
2010). Additionally, landowners may receive funding to fence the land and for surveying
and registering the land. As of June 2009, 3,189 covenants have been registered with an
additional 524 approved and waiting to be registered. Over 109,000 hectares have been
protected through this programme (Queen Elizabeth II National Trust, 2010).
     NWR is a contestable fund that was established in 1990 to assist in the voluntary
protection of native forests on Maori-owned land (Department of Conservation, 2010).
Since then it has been expanded to include other indigenous ecosystems. It is administered
by committee but serviced by the Department of Conservation (DOC) and receives an
annual allocation from the government (Department of Conservation, 2010). There are three
methods of protecting land under the NWR, covenanting, Maori reservations and physical
protection. Covenants under NWR are similar to those under QEII whereas the reservations
are a more stringent protection of the land. The fund will also provide funding to help
establish fences to protect indigenous land from farmlands (Department of Conservation,
2010).
     The NHF is a contestable fund like the NWR but is open to all private landowners not
just Maori and was initiated in 1990. The NHF also began specifically to assist in the
protection of forests until 1998 when it expanded to non-forest ecosystems (Nature Heritage
Fund, 2010). There are two options under the NHF, direct purchase and covenanting.
Similarly to QEII, the NHF will provide some funds for fencing, survey and legal costs of
covenanting. The criteria to receive funding through the NHF include that it must be
representative of the natural ecosystem, sustainable, contribute to the natural integrity of the
landscape as well as the physical and spiritual well-being of the people (Nature Heritage
Fund, 2010).

Land management and soil erosion control
Many areas in New Zealand are prone to soil erosion due to natural ecological and
geological conditions. Soil erosion is a serious environmental issue in New Zealand and can
occur in many forms including fluvial, stream bank, surface and mass movement erosion
(Ministry of the Environment, 2007c). Fluvial erosion occurs when flowing water cuts
channels or gullies into the soil and is common in the Marlborough and Manawatu regions.
Stream bank erosion is a particular version of fluvial erosion wherein the banks of
            Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues   91

waterways have lost their tree cover and are easily worn away. Surface erosion most
commonly occurs outside the hill country in New Zealand and is caused by wind and rain
washing soil particles off the surface of the land (Ministry for the Environment, 2007c).
Mass movement erosion is commonly caused by storms in hill country and can severely
damage fences, drains, roadways and buildings. This type of erosion can cause a whole
slope to slide, with hills steeper than 28 degrees having the worst possibility of erosion
(Ministry for the Environment, 2007c). Regions with land that is considered hill country
have over 7 million acres at risk of erosion of which 48% is being farmed, thus increasing
the risk of erosion (Ministry for the Environment, 2007c).
     Under the RMA, regional and district councils are to ensure that natural resources,
including ecosystems and biodiversity, are managed sustainably. While the central
government‟s financial support for farmers has largely ended, landowners have increasingly
taken responsibility for soil conservation. Many regional councils operate extension
advisory services on resource management issues and some provide financial assistance for
farm and riparian erosion control schemes. Voluntary and government-subsidized soil
conservation programs, including the East Coast Forestry Project, have achieved localized
reduction in soil erosion on farmland in hill country (Ministry for the Environment, 2007a).
     Regional and district councils have developed policies and rules for sustainable land
use. Some regional councils have approached farmers individually for partnerships and to
develop an advisory role in the preparation of farm plans. Some councils subsidize the
development of the plan, while others make subsidized tree stock available to farmers.
Several councils operate ratepayer funded programs to assist farmers with soil conservation
and sustainable land management activities.
     The approaches to land disturbance include both regulatory and non-regulatory
approaches. Most rules and regulations apply when changes are being made in terms of
afforestation, tracking, vegetation removal through burning and uprooting. With grazing
being an existing use, farmers are not affected except in areas that have been declared
sensitive.
     Increasingly, farmers are taking collective action to achieve sustainable land use. Over
250 land-care or community based groups have been formed throughout the country to
address local land-management issues, which include soil conservation. Many of these
projects work in partnership with local authorities and research agencies. Federated
Farmers, farm discussion groups, and producer boards are also working on a variety of
issues related to sustainable land care.
     In the East Coast region the soil erosion problem is far greater than elsewhere in New
Zealand. Because of the magnitude of the problem voluntary approaches are no longer
acceptable and a strict regulatory scheme is being put into place (See East-Coast Case
below).

Case study: The East-Coast region

The Gisborne region comprises 7.8% of the North Island and 26% of the country‟s severely
erodible land. The impacts of erosion (flooding and sedimentation) in the Gisborne region
are greater than other areas of New Zealand, particularly for the southern part of the region,
the Waipoa catchment. After a major disaster in 1988 the Government established the East
Coast Conservation Forestry Scheme aimed at planting the most severely eroding land in
the headwaters of the catchments. The scheme was replaced in 1992 by the East Coast
92 Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues

Forestry Project (ECFP) which aimed to plant 200,000 hectares over 28 years at a rate of
7,000 hectares per year. The project provides a grant for private growers for planting non-
forested areas and tending sites (Bayfield and Meister, 2005).
     When forestry returns declined, this scheme lost its effectiveness and the erosion
problems remained. The Gisborne District Council has implemented regulations on the
lands with the worst erosion which fall into Land Overlay 3 A (LO3A). Within the LO3A
regulations, activities are permitted provided that effective tree coverage on all treatable
land has been established or maintained or a Sustainable Hill Country Project Works Plan is
developed and implemented for the treatable land (Gisborne District Council, 2009). Under
either method of protecting the land from erosion a District Conservator of the Gisborne
District Council must certify the plans and an annual report on the nature, extent, progress
and results of the plans must be provided to the District Conservator (Gisborne District
Council, 2009). Both methods are contingent upon the ECFP continuing to exist in order to
provide some financial compensation to farmers.

Climate change
The government as a signatory to the Kyoto Protocol is committed to returning New
Zealand‟s net emissions to 1990 levels by 2012. Agricultural greenhouse gas emissions in
New Zealand account for 48% of total emissions (Climate Change Information - New
Zealand, 2010b). The Emissions Trading Scheme (ETS) was created to address the
commitments made by New Zealand in the Kyoto Protocol. Under the ETS, New Zealand
Units (NZUs) are traded to aid in the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions (Climate
Change Information - New Zealand, 2010d). One NZU represents the right to emit one
tonne of carbon dioxide or the equivalent of one of the following greenhouse gases covered
under the Kyoto Protocol: methane, nitrous oxide, hydrofluorocarbons, perfluorocarbons
and sulphur hexafluoride (Climate Change Information - New Zealand, 2010d). A NZU is
submitted for each tonne of emissions produced by a firm or individual required to
participate in the ETS. Each sector of New Zealand‟s economy which produces greenhouse
gases will be obligated to participate in the ETS, however, different timelines apply as
outlined in Table 5.4 below.


Table 5.4 Entry in the ETS of different sectors in New Zealand

 Sector                  Emission reporting        Surrender NZU       Allocations of NZU
 Agriculture             January 2012              January 2015        Output related
 Energy                  July 2010                 July 2010           None
 Forestry                January 2008              January 2011        Per hectare
 Industry                January 2010              July 2010           Some activities
 Liquid fossil fuels     January 2010              July 2010           None
 Waste                   January 2012              January 2013        None



The period from July 1, 2010 to December 31, 2012 is a transition period for all sectors that
are required to surrender NZUs. During this period, participants will only be required to
submit one NZU per two tonnes of emissions. Participants will also be able to buy NZUs
from the government at a fixed price of $25 per NZU (Climate Change Information - New
            Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues   93

Zealand, 2010c). Sectors including energy, liquid fossil fuel and waste sectors will not
receive allocations of NZUs as they are able to pass the costs of implementing the ETS on
to their customers. In the case of agriculture, the allocation of NZUs will be 90% of the
baseline that has yet to be established. The baseline will be the industry average emissions
per unit of output and will be phased out by 1.3% per year from 2016 (Climate Change
Information - New Zealand, 2010a).
     Agricultural processors must participate in the ETS in order to minimize administrative
and compliance costs. In future, the participation may be extended to farmers as issues
surrounding compliance, verification and administration of a farmer based participation are
solved. In the short term, opportunities for emissions abatement in the agricultural sector are
limited making strong emissions reductions unlikely. Methane presents a particular
challenge and accounts for two-thirds of the emissions produced by agricultural activities.
Some opportunities exist for nitrification inhibitors.
     In a recent study by the New Zealand Institute of Economic Research (2008) a model
was developed to measure the impacts of the proposed ETS. The results of the model show
that costs would notably increase in the agricultural sector. In 2025, dairy farming would
decline by 12.9%, and sheep and beef farming by 6.6%. The price of land for dairy farming
would fall by 40.6% and 23.4% for sheep and beef farming. The study also mentions that
the current design of the ETS does not account for domestic and export market exposure to
competition from countries that do not regulate their emissions. Additionally, much of the
emissions abatement in New Zealand under the ETS would be replaced by other countries
increasing production and approximately 25% of the emissions reduction would be nullified
by increased production in external markets (New Zealand Institute of Economic Research,
2008).

Conclusion

While in many ways agriculture is less intensive than in other countries, agriculture does
impact the environment in New Zealand. Over the last decade, especially, the shift to more
intensive pastoral land use has placed greater pressures on the health of soils, water quality
and biodiversity.
      Under the New Zealand government‟s guidelines for sustainable land management and
the RMA pressure has increased on land-users to use sustainable practices. These pressures
have imposed costs on farmers as they operate under effluent discharge control regulations
and costs will likely increase under nitrogen leaching regulation.
      Voluntary actions and non-regulatory methods are achieving positive results with
regards to soil erosion. Cost sharing is used in some regions to encourage farmers to change
their land use strategies, however in most cases the costs associated with soil erosion control
fall to the farmer.
      District plans can limit private property rights in the interest of protecting indigenous
biodiversity, especially in wetland areas. In order to protect and enhance waterways,
wetlands dunes and native vegetation, some regional councils provide incentives to farmers
for biodiversity management. Biodiversity protection on private land is predominantly
achieved through voluntary action and covenants. Some of the transaction costs of entering
a covenant are covered through funds set up to aid in this process; however, the majority of
the cost of not using the land lies with the land owners.
94 Decentralized, outcome oriented management of agricultural environmental issues

     New Zealand is using many of the tools and approaches commonly used overseas to
tackle the issue of agricultural sustainability. The growing intensification of agriculture in
New Zealand has caused concerns about the current approach to environmental
management. Perhaps the non-regulatory emphasis on environmental management is not
sufficient to achieve sustainable agriculture. While voluntary action and non-regulatory
approaches have made significant strides towards a more sustainable agricultural industry, it
is now clear that regulations will form a key part of effective environmental and natural
resource management. Throughout the chapter, illustrations have been provided of the
growing regulations for the management of water quality, soil erosion, the preservation of
biodiversity, and greenhouse gases. While this imposes constraints and costs on agricultural
producers, it also offers opportunities for partnerships with government and other
organizations. Through these partnerships, more sustainable farming can be achieved
through adaptation and cooperation.

References
Aitkin, A. G., Hewett, E., and Wallace, B. (2007). Fresh Facts: New Zealand Horticulture 2007.
     Auckland: HortResearch.

Chapter 6

Voluntary certification schemes and legal
standards in the EU

Roel Jongeneel and Thomas Herzfeld



Introduction

Farmers in the EU face increasing interest on production processes from two sides. Whereas
farmers are increasingly involved in supply-chain initiated certification schemes in order to
provide assurance about the quality and the safety of their products and the environmental
sustainability of their production techniques, agricultural policy requests the compliance
with certain legally enforced minimum standards. Private certification schemes and cross
compliance requirements target often related fields, which raises the concern to what extend
they could interact.
     The development of certification schemes is fuelled by developments in consumer
markets and in the food chain. In the modern economy only few market transactions take
place on the basis of product appearance and price alone (Keyzer, 2002)1. The food chain is
recognized more as key to fulfilling consumer expectations for improved quality,
provenance with regard to products as well as desired guarantees about the nature friendly
and animal welfare securing way of production. Food chains play important communication
roles, embodying among others sustainability and food related discourses.
     As a consequence downstream industries have a clear interest in schemes enhancing
and guaranteeing quality and safety of agricultural products. Several food crises occurring
in Europe only increased the urgency and consumer concern. The various schemes existing
nowadays are often overlapping and not always consistent with each other. The initiative to
certify products and production techniques may come from producers‟ organizations, from
the processing industry, from multiple retailers or from regional public authorities.
     The EU‟s obligatory cross compliance rules are aimed to enforce minimum standards,
and (voluntary) certification schemes represent two approaches to validate whether certain
minimum standards are met by farmers. On the one hand, there has been growing interest in
developing closer links between the two approaches, as demonstrated by the European
Commission‟s March 2007 paper on cross compliance (COM (2007) 147). On the other

1
   From a welfare theoretical perspective certification and legal standards might appear to be peculiar
interventions, since they impose restrictions on market exchanges with the purpose of improving social welfare.
However, it can be shown quite generally that in the absence of certification and standards the variability and
uncertainty with respect to product composition and characteristics may cause market failure, even under perfect
competition (see also Keyzer (2002) for some proofs).
©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         97
98      Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU

hand, blurring the distinction between public and private sector responsibilities has been
heavily criticized in the literature (e.g. Busch, 2009). Therefore, this chapter explores the
extent to which there are similarities and differences between certification schemes and the
EU‟s (obligatory) cross compliance. The analysis shows that there are potential synergies
between the two approaches, but also suggests that there are clear limits to the extent to
which these synergies can be developed, if this was to be considered desirable by the actors
involved.
     This chapter focuses on private certification schemes, and those standards that apply at
farm level, although EU or member state driven schemes are acknowledged where this is
relevant to the analysis. In the following, private certification schemes and cross compliance
criteria will be briefly described. Subsequently, a comparison of the two approaches to
guide production processes and product quality follows. Beside a comparison of attributes,
the potential synergies between cross compliance and private standards will be discussed
along three levels of aggregation: the farmer, the supply chain and the regulator/ legislator.
Finally, we will reflect on the interaction between stricter cross compliance and the
willingness to adopt private standards as a strategy of statutory over-compliance - the
farmer‟s strategy to keep regulated outcomes far below or above the statutory defined limits.

Basic characteristics of certification schemes and cross compliance
Characteristics of certification schemes

Following Meuwissen et al. (2003; p. 172) certification is defined as the (voluntary)
assessment and approval by a (accredited) party on a (accredited) standard. Key elements
according to this definition are „assessment‟ and „approval of some standard‟. The goals/
motivations of the innovator of a specific standard cover a wide range. They can be used to
increase transparency and to reach defined performance and practice standards in the supply
chain and by means of this improve consumer trust and competitive advantage (i.e. generate
a price premium), to reduce risk liability claims, to enhance the control of livestock
epidemics, to improve recall efficiency, etc2. Certification schemes can be ordered
according to the criteria they impose. A brief examination of certification schemes applied
in EU‟s agricultural sector shows that they comprise several criteria, among which:

    Special quality and food safety standards
    Cooperation in the supply chain
    Environmental and nature friendly character of product
    Social and ethical aspects of the production process
    Organic production method
    Small-scale production
    Touristic values
    Products coming from certain origin or region.

Combinations of the different criteria could also be possible. In addition, the requirements
of schemes vary. Some schemes involve a farm audit, whilst others may require record
2
  For a theoretical study of costs and benefits see Leland (1979) and for more applied studies discussing (some)
costs and benefits see Meuwissen et al. (2003), Caswell et al. (1998), Unnevehr and Jensen (1999), and Roberts
et al. (1996).
                             Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU   99

keeping or the production of farm management plans. Furthermore, attaining certification
often allows the producer to take advantage of any associated branding, such as the use of a
logo, to help communicate the quality of the product to stakeholders such as processors,
retailers and consumers. Some schemes are not visible to consumers because they have a
business to business focus.
     Alternatively, certification schemes could be classified according to their (potential)
geographical spread:

   local: e.g. Protected Designation of Origin (PDO), Protected Geographical Indication
    (PGI);
   national: e.g. Red tractor label in the United Kingdom (Assured Food Standards, AFS),
    the Integrated Chain Governance (IKB) in the Netherlands; and
   international: e.g. organic agriculture, GlobalGAP.

The certifiable parties are potentially all parties in the supply chain. Certification schemes
can focus on specific groups (e.g. organic farmers, compound feed industry) within the
supply chain, as well as encompass several groups at the same time (e.g. traceability
systems and meta management schemes). As such the certification schemes can be ranked
according to their scope or degree of encompassing supply chain activities. Certification
schemes relevant when considering primary agriculture can be focusing on:

   Feed companies (suppliers)
   Farms (primary production)
   Processing companies (downstream industry)
   Chain (any chain participant)

Certification schemes in general differ from the activities by national surveillance and
control systems. The main activity of the latter is to check and evaluate whether the systems
implemented at the farm (and company) level fulfil the regulatory requirements. The main
motivation of public regulation is the creation of a common playground for agricultural
producers in the EU with respect to environmental and food safety aspects. The „approval of
good practice‟ by the certification schemes is likely to go beyond the requirements imposed
by the regulatory system, assigning public regulation a role of securing a minimum food
safety and product quality level.
     Figure 6.1 provides a general overview of the private (or public-private) certification
schemes (see left part), the certifiable parties in the supply chain (see middle) and the
regulatory standards (see right part). The regulatory standards taken into account are the
SMRs and GAECs requirements as they are part of the EU‟s cross compliance package.
     Certification schemes may also be classified according to the certifying parties (see left
side of Figure 6.1). In principle schemes may arise due to the initiative of any individual or
group of stakeholders. Such an individual or group is the certifying party which ultimately
formulates the standards used for certification. The standard‟s development might be done
in more or less close cooperation with the supply chain partners. If an accredited standard is
used, the certification procedure needs to be carried out by an accredited party. All other
types of (non-accredited) certification can be certified by either accredited parties or other
secondary (e.g. interest groups, clients) or third party organizations (product boards, branch
organizations, semi-governmental organizations). Secondary and third parties may thus
100         Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU

choose for schemes to be inspected and monitored by themselves or own surveillance
agencies, or for management of the scheme by an accredited party. The choice for an
accredited certification might improve the credibility of the scheme to outsiders.
Accordingly, certification schemes can be ordered based on the accreditation procedure.
The following three-way classification can be made:

       first degree schemes: accreditation is granted by independent certification agencies, that
        are acknowledged by a national governmental body or Board of Accreditation (in line
        with the European Standard for Product Certification EN45011);
       second degree schemes: accreditation is granted by a branch organization;
       third degree schemes: certification and quality schemes of individual producers, who
        link their label to own products.

In the case of accredited standards, chain certification implies that each participant acquires
the certification scheme under consideration. For non-accredited standards chain
certification generally implies that specific requirements are specified for subsequent stages
in the chain.
     Finally, the certification schemes can be distinguished with respect to the kind of
inspection. As for the accredited standards, schemes linked to ISO39/EN45004, are
inspection schemes, based on a checklist, and in principle only valid on the day of
inspection. Other schemes, based on ISO65/EN45011 norms, are product certification
schemes in which not only processes, but also products are tested on specified standards.
They enable visible claims on the end products (e.g. organic product or eco-labels). Lastly,
                            Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU   101

there are certification schemes aimed at evaluating complete management systems, the so-
called system certification schemes. The latter ones are based on ISO62/EN45012
(ISO9001:2000).
     The certification bodies in charge of controlling compliance with the certification
schemes are primarily private. The work of private control bodies is usually surveyed by the
organization responsible for the certification scheme, i.e. either by government or by private
institutions. In particular since product and system certification schemes do not use
straightforward checklists, individual auditors‟ interpretations of the standards are of utmost
importance. For example, an auditor has to judge whether a farmer fulfils the specified
requirements. In order to prevent large interpretation differences between auditors,
accreditation of the inspection agencies has to be carefully crafted. The national body or
Board of Accreditation takes care of this, and certifies the inspection and certification
institutes.
     The basis for collaboration of farmers with the certification scheme is always voluntary
and is considered to be a way to facilitate and improve the marketing of agricultural
products. As such, participation in certification schemes not only involves costs but might
also generate benefits. Sometimes they might be called quasi-voluntary as the downstream
industries make compliance with quality schemes obligatory and don‟t accept delivery
without compliance (Bredahl et al., 2001; Caswell et al., 1998, p. 555).

Characteristics of EU’s cross compliance

Cross compliance was introduced as part of the CAP Reform of 2003 with Regulation
1782/2003. Cross compliance involves Member States making receipt of the direct payment
aid, called the Single Farm Payment (SFP), conditional on farmers meeting two sets of
standards. The first, called Statutory Management Requirements (SMRs), relate to 19 pieces
of EU environmental, public, animal and plant health and animal welfare legislation. These
are listed in Annex III of the Regulation. The second set, referred to as standards of Good
Agricultural and Environmental Condition (GAEC), relate to the appropriate management
of soils and the minimum maintenance of agricultural land and features found on that land.
The framework for the GAECs (including about 10 separate standards) is outlined by Annex
IV of the Regulation (see Table 6.1). These SMRs and GAEC standards apply to all farmers
who claim the SFP, and apply to the entire farm holding. Member State authorities must
undertake inspections on at least one per cent of farms claiming the SFP to ensure that the
standards are being met and those found not to meet the required standards may face
reductions or total withdrawal of the SFP. The level of this deduction depends on the
severity of the identified infringement.
     The GAEC framework and the obligation to preserve the national ratio of permanent
pasture at the 2003 level were, at least in principle, new requirements. With the Health
Check, the original (obligatory) standards were subdivided into those which are compulsory
and those which are optional, though Member States that had already implemented these
before 2008 should keep them (see EU Regulation 73/2009 for further details and Table
6.1). Following the Health Check, Member States must introduce a new standard for buffer
strips next to watercourses, and another new requirement is compliance with water use
authorization standards. Moreover, the retention of landscape elements was revised to refer
more specifically to features to be maintained.

Results from a comparative analysis
It is evident that there are a number of differences and similarities between cross
compliance and certification schemes. The underlying objectives of certification schemes
and cross compliance differ. Whilst the aim of cross compliance is to promote a more
sustainable agriculture, enforce compliance with existing legislation, avoid land
abandonment and maintain the area of permanent pasture, the aim of certification schemes
is typically to set standards that respond to market requirements. As such these standards
may or may not correspond to those set by cross compliance. This means that there are
limits to any comparison that can be made. The key similarities are that both approaches
establish minimum standards, operate an inspection protocol to check compliance with
these standards and enforce sanctions if these standards are not met.
     An additional qualification before engaging in a comparative analysis relates to the
implicit assumptions of the researcher. Economic theories of regulation can be organized
                            Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU   103

into two groups: the so-called public interest and the private interest theories (den Hertog,
2010). Whereas the first assumption departs from a benevolent regulator which aims to
pursue the public interest, regulators are assumed to optimize their own individual objective
functions within the second theoretical framework. By definition, for all certification
schemes the existence of private aims should be assumed. A second difference between the
two theoretical categories relates to the amount of information available to the regulator.
Public interest theories assign the regulator sufficient information to effectively promote the
public interest. Conversely, private interest theories deny the public regulator sufficient
information with respect to firm behaviour. In the following, we rely on the body of private
interest theories as a foundation of the comparative analysis.
     In the remainder of this section, first, the (more theoretical) comparative analysis of
cross compliance and certification schemes will be structured along an increasing degree of
aggregation from the micro-level (the farm) to the macro-level (the society). Second, an
empirical assessment is made in which the overlap between cross compliance and voluntary
certification schemes in the Netherlands, the United Kingdom (UK), Germany and Spain
are examined.

Micro level

Whereas third-party certification requires a voluntary action of the respective farmer, cross
compliance is compulsory for any farmer who claims the SFP. In this respect, each farmer
has the freedom to adopt the system which serves best his or her goals. However, obtaining
a certification might act as a de facto market entry requirement, at least for the
commercially dominating supply chains. Such a characteristic stands in contrast to cross
compliance which should secure an equal market access given the compliance with legal
(minimum) food safety requirements. At the same time, the farmer is free to dis-adopt the
certification scheme. That is, not to renew the certificate after its term expires.
     Furthermore, cross compliance criteria cover the whole farm business. In this respect
they are similar to those third-party certification schemes which follow a systemic approach
like organic agriculture. Alternative, non-systemic, standards provide repetitive control
points for different modules for specific products or groups of products and, therefore,
disregard other agricultural activities. A mixed dairy farmer with grain production might
choose to comply with just one of the GlobalGAP modules  crop or beef  or all of them.
     Another difference relates to the inspection rate and definition of inspection sample.
The minimum inspection rate for cross compliance, as specified in Regulation 1782/2003 is
one per cent of farmers claiming the Single Farm Payment, meaning that, in theory, farmers
may only be inspected once every one hundred years. However, agencies apply a risk based
sampling which results in significantly higher probabilities of inspection for farms situated
in environmentally sensitive areas or those suspected not to comply with a higher number of
SMRs. Additionally, the risk based sampling relies on issues like detected non-compliant
behaviour in earlier inspections. For many certification schemes all members are inspected
much more frequently, often on an annual basis. Whereas public sanctions following a
detection of non-compliance display a high degree of differentiability ranging from different
fines through prison sentences to closure of business, certification schemes stick mainly to
warnings or the withdrawal of the certificate. Indirectly, farmers could face the exclusion
from marketing channels depending on supply chain characteristics.
104         Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU


Meso level

From the perspective of the supply chain the compliance with public rules guarantees a
common ground. Chain partners complement these rules with tailor made own standards
and agreements given. As mentioned above supply chain characteristics might yield the
voluntary certification schemes quasi-mandatory. Increasing pressure to adopt schemes is
expected with a diminishing number of marketing channels or the existence of bottlenecks
in supply chains. High adoption rates in pig, cattle and dairy farming serve as an illustration.
For example, 90% of the pig meat traded in the Netherlands satisfies the IKB-Pig standard.
Meat not having this standard will face obstacles hindering smooth trade. At the same time,
lack of alternatives might create lock-in situations where farmers stick with specific
marketing channels and processors/ demanders of their products. The anti-trust agency
might have an interest in following the increasing role and pseudo-obligatory character of
certification schemes because nation- or EU-wide quality assurance schemes can violate
competitiveness laws.

Macro level

As regards cross compliance, standards are part of national legislation ratifying EU
regulation. With respect to these obligatory standards implicit in the EU‟s cross compliance
package, it turned out that not all Member States were at macro level compliant. This is
reflected in several infringement cases on the lack of properly implementing the underlying
EU cross compliance regulations. Whereas proper implementation is one thing, adequate
monitoring, inspection and sanctioning is another thing, which is left with the authority of
Member States. A non-negligible side effect of cross compliance is that the European
Commission contributed to a more homogeneous approach to the sanctioning of farmers
when being non-compliant, irrespective in which member state they live (Jongeneel and
Brouwer, 2010, 194).
     As compared to the cross compliance standards, voluntary certification schemes are
relatively independent (e.g. standard choice, conditions and target levels). It remains safe to
assume a more coherent implementation of certification schemes and their monitoring,
inspection and sanctioning across nation states compared to cross compliance. However,
these schemes can be stated still to be subject to national legislation. For example, certifying
bodies and auditors need to be accredited. Moreover, when these schemes get a pseudo
obligatory character they might conflict with EU and/or national competition law. Since a
number of these certification schemes are not or no longer tied to national domains the need
for EU-wide harmonization needs attention. Whereas national and European Parliament
elections secure checks and balances of EU regulation, society‟s influence on private
certification schemes is very unclear.

Empirical assessment of overlap between certification schemes and cross compliance3

Below we give more details of the standards set by selected certification schemes as they
relate to the requirements of cross compliance. These standards are discussed in relation to
the four headings under which the Statutory Management Requirements (SMRs) in Annex
3
    This part relies on Farmer et al. (2007).
                            Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU   105

III of Regulation 1782/2003 are listed: environmental; identification and registration of
animals; public, animal and plant health; and animal welfare. The linkages between
certification schemes and standards set to achieve GAEC are also discussed. A comparison,
albeit based on a small number of schemes, allows an evaluation to be made as to whether
certification schemes include standards that farmers are legally obliged to meet for cross
compliance. If common ground is identified, potential synergies between the two
approaches could be explored further, if this is considered desirable to the stakeholders that
might be involved. An alternative and equally acceptable scenario is that the two
approaches are mutually exclusive and deal with separate standards in order to meet
differing objectives. Certification schemes may include standards relevant to cross
compliance as well as other standards not covered by the framework of cross compliance.
These other standards are beyond the scope of this comparison.
     The overview provided attempts a first qualitative assessment of the current level of
synergy between SMRs and GAEC standards and the standards set by certification schemes.
This analysis concentrates on seventeen schemes in the Netherlands, ten schemes in the
United Kingdom (England) and four schemes in Spain. An indication of the degree of
overlap is also provided for Germany. This analysis shows where certification schemes
operate below the baseline standards set by cross compliance and where certification
schemes operate above cross compliance standards. Part of the schemes that were
considered, appeared to be not applicable to any of the cross compliance requirements and
are omitted from the tables provided below. It should also be noted that the SMRs and
GAEC standards in the three countries examined in this section are substantially different,
and therefore the comparison is made in terms of national implementation of the SMRs and
GAEC standards. This analysis relies on expert judgement because in many cases the
standards set for certification schemes do not explicitly refer to cross compliance standards,
or in the case of SMRs, the legislation on which the SMRs are based. Identifying the
equivalence of private and public standards is not a straightforward task given the use of
different wording.
     The overlaps between certification schemes and the environmental SMRs in England,
the Netherlands and Spain are summarized in Table 6.2. The environmental SMRs are for
the Birds Directive, the Habitats Directive, the Nitrates Directive, the Sewage Sludge
Directive and the Groundwater Directive. Some schemes, such as Assured British Meat and
Assured Chicken Production in England, include a general statement that members must
comply with all current legislation. Assured Chicken Production goes further, as do some
other schemes such as Assured Combinable Crop Schemes, by stating that producers should
notify their certification body of any prosecutions brought or likely to be brought against
them with respect to animal welfare, animal movements, food safety or environmental
legislation (including cross compliance requirements). In a similar vein, the Soil
Association standards state that members‟ agricultural activities should comply with all
relevant cross compliance requirements.
     The review summarized in Table 6.2 shows that many schemes set standards that fulfil
the requirements of the SMRs relating to the Sewage Sludge Directive, the Nitrates
Directive, and to a smaller extent, the Groundwater Directive. Most of the schemes
reviewed are much weaker in setting standards that meet the requirements of the SMRs for
the Birds and Habitats Directives. None of the Spanish schemes reviewed for this exercise
include equivalent standards. The main exceptions are what might be referred to as the
higher level environmental schemes, which are EKO, Demeter, LEAF and Soil Association

organic and apply also to other Member States. Some of the other schemes reviewed loosely
address these SMRs through a requirement to introduce an environmental plan. For
example, Assured Chicken Production requires free range poultry producers to setup either
an environmental plan which covers the conservation of wildlife or a Farm Biodiversity
Action Plan. With GlobalGAP, for example, there are recommendations with respect to
environmental issues meaning that the farmer does not need to meet them in order to be
awarded certification. The LEAF Marque standards make no specific reference to the five
environmental Directives included in the cross compliance SMRs. However, the LEAF
Marque requires farmers to maintain a nutrient management plan, a manure management
plan and a whole farm conservation plan.
     In Germany, not included in the table, the only scheme that might deal with the
requirements of the Birds and Habitats Directives is the EU eco-management and audit
scheme (EMAS). This is because although EMAS has no defined set of standards, it has the
scope to include relevant requirements. EMAS may therefore also include requirements for
the Groundwater, Sewage Sludge and Nitrates Directive SMRs. GlobalGAP and some of
the Qualität und Sicherheit schemes also include standards which match the environmental
SMRs implemented in Germany.
     The analysis of Dutch, English and Spanish certification schemes shows that where the
identification and registration of animals SMRs are applicable, standards equivalent to the
SMRs have either not been implemented by the certification scheme, or at the other
extreme, have been fully implemented (see Table 6.3). The GlobalGAP dairy scheme, the
Soil Association scheme and the Assured British Meat scheme all fully cover the
requirements of the animal identification and registration SMRs. However, in England only
the Assured British Pigs scheme lists the specific legislation relating to the relevant SMRs.
Assured British Meat has specific standards relating to the identification and traceability of
cattle and sheep and refers to the need for correct tagging and movement records as
required by the legislation. The EKO label in the Netherlands only addresses the
requirements in vague terms. Three of the four Spanish schemes reviewed have good
coverage of the animal identification and registration SMRs. The PROduCERT and
Freedom Food schemes do not include identification and registration standards. In the case
of Freedom Food, this may be because the standards are largely focused around animal
welfare issues. In Germany, the Qualität und Sicherheit schemes for cattle and pigs, and the
Qualitätsmanagement Milch scheme fully match the national SMRs for animal identification
and registration.
     Table 6.4 indicates the degree of overlap between a number of Dutch and English
certification schemes, and the nationally derived SMRs for EU plant, animal and public
health legislation. There appears to be a strong overlap between the certification schemes
and the SMRs for food safety given that a key objective of many certification schemes is to
ensure food safety by undertaking checks along the food chain. In the Netherlands
GlobalGAP dairy and the KKM milk scheme are considered to exceed the SMRs. In
comparison the PROduCERT schemes do not cover the food safety SMRs.
     The arable and higher level environmental schemes tend to cover the statutory
requirements for plant protection products. With GlobalGAP, the SMRs are only met if the
producer obtains both the arable sector base certificate and the combinable crop certificate.
With the Assured Combinable Crops Scheme there is a direct reference to the need to
comply with the national legislation for plant protection products and codes of practice.

EKO, Demeter and Soil Association organic have a strong focus on pesticide and herbicide
application, and thus their requirements match or exceed the national plant protection
SMRs. In Germany, the Qualität und Sicherheit schemes for fruit and vegetables and
potatoes as well as GlobalGAP also match the SMRs for the use of plant protection
products. In comparison with the apparently wide coverage of the food safety SMRs in
England and the Netherlands, only the Qualität und Sicherheit scheme satisfies the German
requirements, although GlobalGAP and Qualitätsmanagement Milch partly satisfy the
requirements.
     A number of the meat schemes do not include standards which cover the animal disease
SMRs, including Assured British Meat and Assured British Pigs in England. Similarly in
the Netherlands a direct reference to the need to make immediate notification of the
occurrence of contagious diseases is not mentioned in the literature for the relevant
schemes.
     As Table 6.5 shows there is a substantial overlap between the requirements of
certification schemes and the likely animal welfare rules that will be implemented in the
Netherlands and England. All the schemes examined have full or substantial coverage of EU
requirements, and in some cases exceed them. With respect to the standards for the housing
of calves, the organic labels EKO and Demeter (both for the Netherlands) include
requirements which exceed statutory requirements. All the schemes reviewed in England
appear to comply with the national requirements. The certification scheme standards for the
housing of pigs also exceed the legislative requirements in the Netherlands. In England, the
Freedom Food and Soil Association Organic standards go beyond legislative requirements,
whilst in Germany, the Qualität und Sicherheit scheme meets the national statutory
requirements for the housing of calves.

Discussion of results
The synergy between cross compliance and certification scheme raises some interesting
questions about the benefits of one approach when compared to the other.
                              Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU      111

     The benefits at least partly depend on the ability to set appropriate standards given the
objectives set, enforce those standards and ensure appropriate sanctions are applied. This
implies that compliance with standards must be able to be checked during an inspection
visit, and that these inspection visits must occur on a frequent basis in order to verify
continued compliance.
     It may also be noted that if a certification scheme has been in existence for longer than
cross compliance, membership of that particular certification scheme may enhance the
likelihood of the farmer meeting cross compliance standards if at least some of the scheme‟s
standards match those required by cross compliance.

Cross compliance and certification schemes are similar to the extent that they are both
systems that seek to ensure compliance with a set of standards. Their approach to ensuring
compliance is also similar in that they both establish inspection protocols and enforce
sanctions.
     There are also some clear differences between private certification schemes and cross
compliance:

   One difference is in terms of the issues covered by the schemes. Private certification
    schemes do not tend to include standards which farmers are legally obliged to meet.
112      Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU

     There appears to be an assumption that the legal baseline is already met. Among the
      countries examined, there appear to be very few, if any, schemes that incorporate all of
      the cross compliance standards that apply in the Member State concerned.
     Certification schemes rather focus on a specific commodity sector, a farming system, or
      the quality of an end product. Very few schemes take a whole farm approach by
      seeking compliance with a range of standards that apply to all land on the holding, as is
      the case with cross compliance.
     In a few cases, certification scheme standards exceed national cross compliance
      standards. This occurs for those standards which the scheme specializes in, particularly
      animal welfare requirements for schemes with an animal welfare focus and
      environmental requirements for higher level environmental schemes.
     Cross compliance applies to the majority of EU farm holdings, whilst sector specific
      private certification schemes often apply to only a minority of holdings, although some
      schemes do have wide coverage. Thus certification schemes, at least among those in the
      Member States reviewed, do not set a horizontal, uniform baseline of minimum
      standards in the way cross compliance does.
     All producers who seek certification tend to be inspected every year, whilst just one per
      cent of farmers claiming the Single Payment are inspected for cross compliance in any
      year. The higher frequency of certification scheme inspections underlines their
      credibility, so long as standards are meaningful and well defined, compliance is
      verifiable during an inspection and appropriate sanctions are rigorously enforced. The
      difference in inspection rates needs to be contextualized by the respective scale of
      public and private approaches, with cross compliance applying to a much larger
      number of farmers than private certification schemes do.
     The difference in inspection rates is somewhat influenced by the accompanying
      sanctions, with the financial sanctions applied through cross compliance potentially
      more severe than those applied by most certification schemes, where farmers are
      generally provided with time to rectify a breach before certification is withdrawn. In the
      event a farmer‟s membership of a certification scheme is cancelled, the farmer may lose
      access to the market and encounter financial consequences as a result.
     Also, despite the fact that many schemes do not comprehensively cover all the legal
      requirements designated by cross compliance, the branded logos provide evidence that
      certain standards have been met and these logos are generally visible and meaningful to
      the consumer.

There is also an argument that membership of a certification scheme may ease compliance
with cross compliance standards. However, as the evidence in this analysis shows,
compliance with certification scheme standards cannot act as a proxy for compliance with
EU legal standards.

Potential benefits and limitations to developing synergies between certification
schemes and cross compliance

To explore the apparent similarities and differences between certification schemes and cross
compliance a subset of certification schemes needs to be excluded. First of all, certification
schemes must be accessible for all farmers within the EU. The development of these
synergies could have some advantages, as well as disadvantages. The arguments presented
                            Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU   113

here very much depend on the intentions of public and private bodies and whether there is a
common interest, or not, in facilitating a collaborative approach to either setting standards
or enforcing those standards.
     Firstly, the identification of synergies could lead to the harmonization of standards
between certification schemes and those found in EU legislation, thereby reducing potential
consumer confusion in differentiating product labels and determining the sustainability of
farm produce. Harmonization would mean that the standards in private certification schemes
include within them the standards for cross compliance, where these are relevant. Secondly,
harmonization could be of benefit to the cross compliance inspection regime and result in
increased administrative efficiencies. For example, it may be the case that farmers who are
certified for meeting specific standards are less likely not to meet cross compliance
standards. Membership of certain certification schemes could be a factor in the risk sample
that Member States use to target farms for cross compliance inspections. Such an approach
would require some confidence that certification schemes rigorously enforce standards that
closely match those set for cross compliance. Harmonization may therefore be to the
advantage of certification schemes if such schemes can credibly demonstrate to potential
members that the standards set by the scheme meet the requirements of cross compliance.
This could be an enabling factor in helping farmers to meet the EU legal standards. It may
also be a selling point of a scheme if scheme membership is taken into account in the risk
sample for cross compliance inspections, meaning that members will be less likely to
receive a cross compliance inspection.
     The case against harmonization can also be argued on the grounds of duplication of
standards. This may not be desirable if a certification scheme sets a standard that the farmer
is legally obliged to meet through cross compliance. It may also be inefficient if the farmer
is inspected for the same standard during a cross compliance inspection and an inspection
required for certification scheme membership.
     Harmonization could, of course, raise fundamental questions about the mutual role of
government and private bodies in ensuring legal standards are met and in encouraging
farmers to meet standards that exceed the legal minima. It may be argued that it is the role
of government to ensure that farmers meet minimum regulatory standards and that
certification schemes have a complementary role to play in ensuring additional standards
not provided for by existing legislation are met by farmers in order to satisfy the demands of
the market.
     It may be inappropriate and not in the public interest for certification schemes to
inspect mandatory standards such as those for animal disease control. National
administrations may rightly be reluctant to entrust the private sector with the control of
mandatory standards, even if the certification bodies are accredited by state-run
accreditation services. Member State administrations would also need to have the ability to
sanction private bodies if they fail to adequately inspect and control legal standards. It may
also be argued that certification schemes may not want to set overly ambitious standards, as
this could dissuade farmers from applying for membership. Certification schemes may have
an inherent conflict of interest in this respect.

Voluntary overcompliance – upgrading

Taking into account a further development of cross compliance it might be beneficial for
farmers to comply with more demanding standards now. That is, farmers‟ uncertainty about
114      Voluntary certification schemes and legal standards in the EU

future minimum requirements will be a determinant of adoption of certification schemes
which are closest to cross compliance but go beyond cross compliance requirements. From
a purely economic point of view such a behaviour seems to be irrational. However, there are
reasons for farmers to do more than required. Given the size of most European farms we
rule out the motive to use overcompliance as a marketing tool, e.g. by developing an own
standard, and focus on the two aspects risk aversion and expectation of future regulatory
changes.4
     Risk aversion: Product quality and agricultural process characteristics are mostly
mediated through natural processes. By definition, farmers lack means of overly precise
management of clearly defined outcomes. However, in many cases standards and cross
compliance focus on precise levels neglecting variation induced by nature. Risk averse
producers act rationally in using less of the regulated input like fertilizers or pesticides.
Similarly, the use of more labour input than required adds to this behaviour.
     Expectation of future regulatory changes: Any investment within European agriculture
is highly influenced by local, national or EU regulations. Forming expectations on future
regulatory changes and taking them into account in planning investments might deliver
comparative advantages in the future. The longer the investment horizon the more beneficial
overcompliance can be. However, uncertainty with respect to future statutory changes
increases too.

Conclusion

The evidence presented in this chapter shows that there are some fundamental similarities in
the objectives of certification schemes and cross compliance, as well as the way they
establish an operational framework. However, there are also a number of clear differences
in terms of the scope of the schemes and the way in which they function which may impose
limits to any collaborative approach. Examples are differences in coverage, domain,
monitoring and inspection, punishment, communication to consumers, etc. Overall, it seems
that the extent to which cross compliance and private certification schemes are mutually
compatible approaches to meet standards deserves further consideration. Our analysis
demonstrates that there is an opportunity for the mutual exchange of best practice between
certification schemes and cross compliance. Although limitations exist, there is scope to
further explore the synergy between public and private approaches.
     The European Commission and members states‟ governments explore the framework of
the future agricultural policy. Proposals include a further greening of the CAP by increasing
conditionality on direct payments eligible to farmers. In case more targeted payments will
be introduced, the synergies between public and private requirements might get even more
attention. Furthermore, a consolidation of certification schemes within the private sector
could not be excluded. In the case that smaller regional or national certification schemes
disappear or merge into larger international ones, synergies might become more obvious.
