Chapter 7

Environmental and health standards in the
dairy sectors of the EU, the US and New
Zealand

Irina Bezlepkina, Roel Jongeneel, Floor Brouwer, Koen Dillen,
Anton Meister, Kees de Roest and Matty Demont



Introduction

With the 2003 Mid-Term Review (MTR) of the EU Common Agricultural Policy (CAP) a
number of adjustments to agricultural support were introduced. One of the most substantive
changes was the introduction of a system of decoupled payments per farm (known as the
Single Farm Payment). These decoupled payments were made conditional on recipients
meeting environmental, food safety, animal and plant health, and animal welfare
requirements as well as standards of good agricultural and environmental conditions
(obligatory cross compliance). Also outside the EU measures are taken to address
environmental harm, with the US Concentrated Animal Feeding Operations (CAFOs)
legislation as a notable example. Compliance with standards potentially might affect
production costs and thus competitiveness.
     This chapter focuses on environmental compliance costs issues in the dairy sector of
the EU mainly and the implications of those issues for farm level and external
competitiveness between the EU and two other main players: the US and New Zealand. The
EU‟s presence on the world market is strong for all the major dairy products. In 2007 the
EU-27 shares of world trade are 24% for butter, 18% for skimmed milk powder, 20% for
whole milk powder and 37% for cheese (DairyCo, 2010). New Zealand exports about 33%
of global dairy products (Blayney et al., 2006). With the enlargement of the EU in 2007, the
dairy industry in the EU-27 became very large with 24.2 million dairy cows in comparison
with 9.2 and 4.2 million dairy cows in the US and New Zealand respectively (USDA, 2010).
Milk production in the EU-27 in 2007, where dairy represents the largest agricultural sector
(European Commission, 2006), is 1.6 times larger than that of the US, where dairy is the
second largest sector in terms of cash receipts at the farm level (Miller and Blayney, 2006)
and about nine times larger than in New Zealand (USDA, 2010).
     Would the competitiveness of the EU dairy sector that deals with perishable products
and has to comply with environmental standards be influenced? Not much evidence is
available to assess the costs of compliance with regulations. An EU study assessing the
administrative burden on farms arising from the CAP identifies that cross compliance
controls represent 1% of the total administrative costs (DG-AGRI, 2007). However, costs
could rise due to changes in production strategies in complying with regulations. Kuik
©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         117
118      Environmental and health standards in the dairy sectors

(2006) reviewed studies that estimated farm level costs of measures to reduce nitrogen
pollution in agriculture across Europe. He found that the differences in costs, ranging from
€6 to €236 per hectare, can be explained by the structure of the sector (dairy, beef, pigs and
poultry, crops, mixed), livestock intensity, historical rates of fertilizer application and the
vulnerability of soils to nitrate leaching. In terms of the trade implications of environmental
regulations, research has mainly investigated the effects within the manufacturing sector
(see e.g. Xu, 2000; Le Roux et al., 2008 for an overview of studies). For the agricultural
sector, the work of Cassells and Meister (2001), who simulate the effects of compliance to
water quality standards in the New Zealand dairy sector, is most closely related to the
objective of this chapter. The contribution of their paper is that it analyses the extent to
which the imposition of farming standards gives rise to on-farm costs. The cost increase due
to compliance with regulations at the farm level needs to be further scaled up to the sector
level in order to be integrated into the analysis of trade thereby assessing external
competitiveness.
     As presented in Chapter 2 for Europe and in Chapters 3 and 5 for the US and New
Zealand, various agricultural environmental policy initiatives have been considered before a
selection of comparable standards influential for the competitiveness of the dairy sector was
made. The next section describes the data employed in the analytical approach. Thereafter
the impacts of the Nitrates Directive and the Identification & Registration Directive at the
sector level in the EU (Germany, France, Italy, the Netherlands and the UK) and its key
competitors (the US and New Zealand) are presented. Representative farm studies were
used as a basis for the cost calculations. Best-estimates of compliance are borrowed from
the literature and expert opinions. A computable general equilibrium model which is
presented in Chapter 1 is used to assess the impact of compliance costs on competitiveness
of the three trading nations in global trade. The negative impact of the analysed measures on
EU imports and exports is less than 1%. If a smaller increase in compliance takes place,
trade impacts will be even smaller. When the standards for nitrate pollution taken by the US
and New Zealand are taken into account along with a full compliance assumption in all
countries analysed, this would only marginally improve EU exports. The trade impacts
obtained under the assumption of no changes in key competitor countries can, hence, be
interpreted as upper bounds of the likely trade impacts.

Analytical approach and data

The concept of competitiveness has no unique definition and is used in a broad set of
contexts and levels of aggregation (firm, product, country, industry). As presented in
Chapter 1, the analytical approach and design of this study is based on qualitative as well as
quantitative modelling assessments, differentiating between farm, sector and global levels of
competitiveness.
     Firm level competitiveness addresses the comparison of firms in a market. A firm is
more competitive compared to the other if it can supply a product at lower costs, without
affecting its overall profitability in the long term. In the case of farms, the profitability can
therefore be approached by the farm‟s gross income (revenues and farm support payments)
minus the costs of variable and (quasi-)fixed factors.
     If compliance with standards affects production costs at the farm level, aggregate
agricultural production is affected resulting in a shift of the supply curve, i.e. a supply
response. Other well-known mechanisms shifting the supply curve are the introduction of
                                  Environmental and health standards in the dairy sectors   119

milk quotas (introduced in 1984), reductions in support prices for butter and skimmed milk
powder (effectuated with the Luxembourg Reform in 2003), introduction of milk premiums
as part of the decoupled payments (Bouamra-Mechemache et al., 2009). The supply
response, in turn, displaces the equilibrium between supply, demand and international trade.
Differing standards and degrees of compliance with standards, and heterogeneity of farming
conditions between trading partners within the sector can affect the market share of those
trading in this sector. Market share between sectors is also affected, due to substitution and
complementarity and spill-over effects between produced commodities in the economy.
     In this chapter the assessment of potential cost implications due to the compliance with
standards is made for selected Member States to demonstrate the effects of considerably
different variation in the implementation of standards. At the global level, the analysis for
the dairy sector is carried on with the GTAP model ensuring for comparability of
competitiveness between EU and non-EU countries by including in the analysis comparable
standards and potential cost implications for New Zealand and the US.
     The standard specification of GTAP model as presented in Chapter 1 has some
properties that do not align with the situation in the 2005 European dairy market. First of
all, an alternative closure is specified making the output level exogenous and thus fixed.
This ensures for implementing the bounding quota constraint in the European milk
production. Secondly, to account for the 2003 Mid-Term Reform in the dairy sector as
specified above, the base year of the model which refers to 2001 is adjusted. While
decoupled payments are assumed not to affect production decisions, price reductions are
implemented in the model uniformly by a change in taxes. In this way a new base scenario
for the competitiveness analysis in the dairy sector is created. Finally, the improvement in
compliance is estimated as the change in compliance as compared to 2005, the year for
which best estimates of compliance could be obtained. This is done by shocking the model
by the percentage cost increase (evaluated at sector level) relative to the base year due to the
simulated increase in compliance. The results of the GTAP model are then interpreted as
estimates of improvement in compliance since 2005, i.e. they are evaluated as if they were
happening in 2001 (the GTAP base year data). For this reason, percentage changes as
compared to the baseline scenario are analysed and not absolute numbers. For the EU-15,
we impose compliance only on Member States which represent a significant share (5%) of
EU exports. We assume that other countries are too small to significantly affect European
competitiveness on the world market. The remaining countries are aggregated into: (i) the
rest of OECD (ROECD) countries and (ii) the rest of the world (ROW). The analysis is
performed for selected EU-countries, the EU-15 as a whole and two selected non-EU
countries namely the US and New Zealand.
     As elaborated in Chapter 1, the additional costs associated with compliance to
environmental regulations had to be assessed at the farm level and consequently scaled up
to national level. The necessary data assessments are the initial and target level of
compliance with the standard, percentage of affected farms, per unit cost estimates arising
due to compliance with the standard. In the case of the EU dairy these costs are derived for
representative specialized dairy farms as recorded in the Farm Accountancy Data Network
(FADN) in 2003. For non-EU countries, the season 2005/2006 farm as well as regional data
are used from the US Department of Agriculture (USDA), the Ministry of Agriculture for
New Zealand, and regional authorities. The representative farm approach is also used for
the US and New Zealand dairy where farms from different regions are distinguished for
illustrative purposes.
120     Environmental and health standards in the dairy sectors

    Since the observed costs include the already implicit costs due to current level of
compliance, for calculating the percentage of additional costs, the total costs are thereby
corrected for the level of compliance. At the sector level both affected and non-affected
farms count through the share of specialised farms in total milk production. The assessment
of costs at the sector level in the US and New Zealand is also done using representative
farm approach, distinguishing farms in different regions for illustrative purposes.
    The analysis of competitiveness at the farm level by means of profitability indicator is
done using the 2006 data from the International Farm Comparison Network (IFCN, see
Hemme, 2007). The advantage of these data is that they have been harmonized across EU as
well as non-EU countries and distinguish family labour costs (see Table 7.2), thereby
ensuring comparability and allowing for focusing on the net results (gross margin and
profitability).

Standards, farm and sector cost implications

For our detailed cost analysis, we selected the expected three most influential standards
relevant for the EU: Nitrates Directive, Identification and Registration of farm animals and
Food safety and its competitors. The US and New Zealand generally face less regulation.
Although the policy-approaches in these countries currently rely more on voluntary actions,
more stringent forms of regulation are being implemented or might be introduced in the
future. Therefore scenarios simulating the effect of the Nitrates Directive will consider
changes in regulations for key competitor countries as well. For the US case, we analyse the
requirement to develop and follow a Comprehensive Nutrient Management Plan (CNMP) in
order to comply with the Environmental Protection Agency‟s Clean Water Act (CWA)
regulatory requirements. For the case of New Zealand, the impact of the water quality
regulations is assessed.

Nitrates Directive

The Nitrates Directive (91/676/EEC) is perceived by EU dairy farmers as one of the most
restrictive standards (Jongeneel and Brand, 2010, p. 202). The Directive was adopted in
1991 aiming at the protection of waters against pollution by nitrate from agricultural
sources. If land is located within a Nitrate Vulnerable Zone (NVZ), the farmer must comply
with NVZ Action Programme Measures which limit the organic manure loading per hectare
at 170 kg of nitrate per ha (with exception for grassland in Germany and The Netherlands
allowing for 250 kg/ha). Such limits on the disposal of manure by animals while grazing
require sufficient slurry storage facilities (or alternative arrangements) to cater for the
closed period.
     In the US, the approach toward managing the environmental impact from farming has
been largely voluntary or with compliance being a condition for cost-sharing assistance with
best management practices. The CWA, which was originally focused on point sources of
pollution, has been expanded to non-point pollution, with agriculture identified as one of the
key sectors. The farms that feed animals primarily in confinement are designated by Clean
Water Act rules as Concentrated Animal Feeding Operations (CAFOs) and are the focus of
water quality regulations at the federal and state levels. However, it was not until 2002 that
the federal government issued specific rules governing CAFOs, requiring the design and
implementation of a CNMP. CAFOs are treated as a regulation „similar‟ to the EU Nitrates
                                  Environmental and health standards in the dairy sectors    121

Directive. Since only 2% of all confined livestock operations are CAFOs, this policy
currently only affects a small proportion (0.04%) of US dairy farms.
     In New Zealand, effluent from the farm is to be disposed on the land (after ponding)
and fertilizer applications should follow a Code of Good Practice. The latter states manure
discharge to be a controlled activity, where the rate of application may not exceed 150 kg N
per ha annually (and no more than 50 kg per ha within a period of 24 hours), buffer zones
should be respected and runoff and ponding of effluent should be avoided. Because the
issue of nutrients derived from intensive farming has become a big concern, a private-public
partnership tries to come up with self-regulation (the partnership includes the nationwide
dairy cooperative Fonterra as well as regional councils).
     Table 7.1 reflects the calculation of costs at the farm and sector level for selected
countries for the situation when all affected farms improve their degree of compliance in
such a way that they all achieve full compliance. Dairy farming in the US does not rely on
pasture system; therefore the acreage per farm is not mentioned (row c in Table 7.1). A
slightly different procedure was followed for the US and New Zealand cases which are
presented below therefore leaving some places in Table 1 non-filled for these countries.
     For selected EU Member States in Table 7.1, the number of affected and non-affected
farms is assessed through farming intensity and location of farms in NVZs of Europe. Farms
with lower densities than 2 LU/ha („extensive farms‟) are assumed to face no significant
cost increases. Having studied the available literature (Andrews et al., 2000; Kuik, 2006),
we rely on per hectare cost estimates from Kuik (2006), adjusted for inflation (row d). Total
costs (FADN, 2003) per an average size dairy farm are corrected for record keeping costs of
manure applications (in row e) since Kuik‟s estimates did not include these costs
(approximated at €150 per dairy farm in a NVZ-area in all selected countries). For
calculating the percentage of cost increase at the farm level (row j), the total costs (in row g)
are corrected for the initial level of compliance (row a). At the sector level both affected
and non-affected farms count through the share of specialized farms in total milk production
(row i).
     For the full-compliance situation, the percentage increase in total sector costs varies
between 0.10% and 4.07% (row k). The absolute cost increases for this group ranges from
€400 to €8,800 per farm (row e minus row f). In reality this effect might be somewhat
lower, since (temporary) derogation provisions are not taken into account. Moreover, it
should be noted that many of the less intensive specialized dairy farms, as well as the non-
specialized dairy farms are assumed to have zero compliance costs. For Europe, the highest
percentage cost increase was estimated for Italy based on a case study carried out for dairy
farms of different size and structure exercising various manure management systems as
presented in De Roest et al. (2007).
     Formulae mentioned in Table 7.1 are also not applicable to the last two columns. For
these non-EU countries separate calculations of percentage cost increase were carried out.
Further details on cost assessment are summarized in Jongeneel et al. (2008). For New
Zealand, the farm monitoring data on 21 dairy regions (MAF, 2005) are used to assess the
costs associated with similar standards in this country. Compliance costs relating to water
quality regulations are calculated at the farm level for the land-based disposal of dairy shed
effluent. The percentage of farms that still had to comply for the period 2005/2006 is
assessed to be 15%. Regional differences in average herd sizes and in consent and
monitoring costs are incorporated into the analysis. Compliance costs at the farm level
based on the data from 2003 are approximated at 10,447 NZD (or €5,886). Farm costs are
122       Environmental and health standards in the dairy sectors

then aggregated to obtain a total cost for the New Zealand dairy industry to comply with
water quality regulations. From this total cost the compliance costs per kilogram of Milk
Solids produced are estimated. The national dairy farm budget is used to calculate the
percentage cost increase at the farm level which amounts to 3.2% (Table 7.1, row j). For the
industry as a whole, the percentage cost increase is 0.48%, given that 85% of farms are
already compliant.

Two typical dairy farms from Wisconsin and California were chosen for the cost analysis in
the US not only because they are the top two dairy states, but also because they represent
two distinct milk production systems (see also Chapter 3). Wisconsin dairy farming is
represented by a typical traditional smaller dairy farm with 92 cows, whereas Californian
dairy farming is represented by a modern farm with 980 cows (based on the agricultural
season 2006). To calculate costs per farm, costs of nutrient management, keeping costs, off-
farm transportation costs, land treatment and manure costs are included, amounting to an
                                 Environmental and health standards in the dairy sectors   123

average of €7,308 per farm or €37 per animal on annual basis. The cost of compliance with
the CNMP requirements on the typical Wisconsin dairy farm amounts to €1.37 per 100 kg
of milk produced (€0.31 per 100 kg in California). For an average US dairy farm this
translates into an increase of 2.0% (Table 7.1, row j). Given the low percentage of farms
which are subject to CAFOs and the fact that about 44% of farms are already compliant, the
percentage cost increase at the national level averages to 0.02%.
     The above example for two US farms with different size, cost level and structure
provides a range of farm level cost increases from 1.3% to 4.2% and clearly illustrates that
compliance can have different effects on individual farm performance and, hence, internal
competitiveness. Table 7.2 illustrates competitiveness analysis with respect to the Nitrates
Directive for the EU and comparable standards in non-EU selected farm types. The
percentage rates of cost increase as presented in Table 7.1 are applied to the data from
Hemme et al. (2007) on typical dairy farms in selected countries. The cost data from these
farms have been harmonized and recalculate per energy corrected milk (ECM) which is
adjusted to 4% fat, and 3.3% protein. These typical farms are of quite different size but with
rather high yields (6,500- 9,000 kg per cow per year) with one exception. Grazing dairy
systems in New Zealand produced 4,361 kg milk per cow per year since they are mainly
producing from grass, grass silage or hay with no concentrates or only a small amount.
     Among all 38 countries, reported in Hemme et al. (2007), all studied EU countries
group under the same high costs category per 100 kg of ECM (> €30). Costs are
significantly higher due to decoupling of direct payments which has altered the non-milk
returns of the dairy enterprise. Variable costs include costs for purchased feed, fertilizer,
seeds, fuel, maintenance, land rents, interest on liabilities, wages paid, veterinary services
and medicine, water, insurance, etc. Variable costs of milk production are quite comparable
over countries with the exception of New Zealand. Here the costs are quite low, which
compensates for rather unfavourably low milk price in this country. The difference in total
costs per 100 kg ECM between countries is mainly due to fixed costs, which include
depreciation on purchase prices for buildings and machinery and costs for using own
production factors (land owned, family labour input, and equity including quota). Labour
costs per 100 kg ECM are rather different reflecting differences in scale of farming and is
also an important factor determining long run competitiveness.
     In Table 7.2, farm financial results are presented for the situation when full compliance
with the Nitrates Directive (or similar regulations outside the EU) is assumed. As Table 7.2
shows, relatively small (0.2-4.8%) changes in costs can significantly reduce gross margin
(0.4-8.4%) and even more so profitability (4.1-91.7%).

Identification and registration of farm animals

The EU Directive on Identification and Registration (I&R) of animals (92/102/EEC, and
Regulations 911/2004, 1760/2000, and 21/2004) is one of the most important burdens
according to the farmers (DG-AGRI, 2007). Most time consuming is the check of animal
identification especially in extensive farms or in cattle breeding, as animals are often
outside and in different fields and sometimes difficult to approach, compared to dairy cows
kept indoors. I&R of farm animals is characterized by a significant degree of non-
compliance, with 30% non-compliance being no exception. Besides, the inclusion of animal
I&R results is very demanding for controlling agencies (about 36 hours per farm in England
or 40 hours in the Netherlands).

The results of the cost estimates at farm level for five selected Member States are presented
in Table 7.3. It is assumed that about 0.8 cow units of other animals (heifers/bulls) per dairy
cow is present at the farm and that 95% of the dairy cows give birth to a calf, which has to
be registered (row c). In addition it is assumed that there is an eartag loss rate of 15%,
which requires a proper and timely eartag replacement (row e). The costs due to
requirements of the Identification and Registration standards consist of the eartag costs and
the labour (per animal) required for registration (row h).
                                       Environmental and health standards in the dairy sectors         125

    The procedure of approximating the additional costs is similar to that for the Nitrates
Directive. The percentage cost increase at the farm and sector level is the same since all
farms in the sector are affected. In selected Member States the estimated additional costs are
below 0.14%, which is relatively marginal.

Food safety (hormone use)

Consumer concerns as regards food safety have led to hormone use prohibition in the EU.
In the US, recombinant bovine somatotropin (rBST), a growth hormone that stimulates milk
production, has been approved for use in dairy cows since 1994. Alongside the US, BST (or
rBST) is used by at least 16 other dairy producing countries (Jarvis, 2002). The EU, Canada
and Japan rejected legal BST use, and also within the US the technique was (and still is)
controversial, at least within certain groups and regions. See Jarvis (2002, p. 103) for
further details.
     Monsanto – the monopolistic supplier of the product (brand name: POSILAC) – reports
that in 1999 about 13,000 US dairy farms were using BST, applying it to 9 million dairy
cows (approximately 30% of the cows in supplemented herds). The use of this hormone
126       Environmental and health standards in the dairy sectors

leads to production increases. Tauer (2002) shows that application of rBST in the first year
may increase an average herd‟s milk yield by 419-575 kg, whereas late adopters are able to
directly realize an annual milk yield increase of about 480 kg per animal. Jarvis (2002), who
analyzed the potential effects of BST on world dairy markets, estimates that the total US
milk supply increased by 3% due to the (pure) BST application1.
     Whereas BST will increase yields and thus revenues, it simultaneously increases input
use (feed) and thus costs (including the technology fee farmers have to pay for BST use). In
an ex ante analysis, Perrin (1991) estimated per unit cost savings varying from 0.5% to
4.4%, although milk yields were increasing by approximately 15% (note the simultaneous
cost increase effect). Jarvis (2002, p. 109), using a slightly different approach, provides a
maximum per unit cost decline of 5% (per unit average). When simulating the trade impact
of hormone use ban in the US (see next section), we use the latter estimate and interprete
the cost advantage as foregone which translates into a 5% cost increase for US farmers.

Standards and external competitiveness

External competitiveness is analysed along six scenarios. The first three scenarios in Table
7.4 simulate compliance to the Nitrates Directive in EU Member States at various levels of
compliance. Additionally to the full-compliance calculations presented in Table 7.1, the
cost increases were assessed for scenarios 2 and 3 as follows: „compliance improved by by
20%‟ when all affected farms improve their degree of compliance by 20% (to a maximum
of 100%) as compared to the base year level and „minimum of 75% compliance‟ when all
affected farms improve their degree of compliance by 10% to a minimum of 75% as
compared to the base year level. In all three scenarios it is assumed the level of compliance
in non-EU trading partners remained at the initial level. Scenario 4 simulates full
compliance to the Nitrates Directive within the EU and similar regulations in the US and
New Zealand.
     For the case of full compliance to Nitrates Directive in EU countries (scenario 1), the
European dairy sector loses 0.71% of its export mainly to the rest of OECD and the US.
Moreover, it increases its imports by 0.80%. As was already noted in the introduction, EU
imports are playing a very limited role, and the main impact will thus be on exports. Since
the GTAP model does not distinguish disaggregated dairy product markets, it is impossible
to indicate how various product markets (e.g. butter, skimmed milk powder, whole milk
powder, hard and soft cheeses, casein, etc.) will be affected. For example, the EU is known
to export various speciality cheeses to the US market. The exports of such high value-added
products are likely to be less affected than the „average‟ dairy export product simulated
within GTAP. The predicted export reduction of 0.70% of the EU to the US is therefore
likely to be an upper bound. The extra imports may be an overestimation due to the way
tariff rate quotas (TRQ) are modelled in GTAP. All TRQs in the dairy sector are modelled
as bilateral TRQs and an arrhythmic mean tariff is calculated from the in- and out-quota
tariff. However, for the dairy sector this representation is acceptable because of the fixed
nature of the global TRQ in the sector (based on historical trade volumes). The other
countries will increase their export in order to fill the gap the EU leaves. However, the total
traded volume decreases by 0.05%, which is quite small.


1
 For comparative reasons note that the autonomous (genetic) milk yield increase is about 2% per annum.
Whereas bST application creates a one-shot increase, genetic progress creates a continuous milk yield increase.

Source: GTAP calculations



Because of the lower costs for European farmers in Scenarios 2 and 3 compared to the first
scenario (see also Table 7.1) there is a smaller effect on the competitiveness of the
European dairy sector. The decrease in export volume is 0.42% and 0.33% respectively.
Because of the smaller price effect on the world market the total traded volume only reduces
by 0.02%. The rest of the OECD and the rest of the world fill most of the reduced European
export. In scenario 4, when next to the EU countries also their competitors fully comply
with nitrate measures, the results do not change substantially when compared to scenario 1;
total trade declines at a higher rate (1.07%) whereas the total export of the EU still
decreases (by 0.41%). This is not surprising given quite low percentage cost increase at
sector level in the non-EU countries.
     Scenarios 5 and 6 report the simulation results of compliance of EU countries to the
Identification & Registration standards. There are no costs for non-EU countries and
slightly increased costs for EU countries resulting in the smallest trade decline (0.01%)
among all scenarios. A combined effect of full compliance of EU countries to the Nitrates
Directive and I&R leads to an EU export loss of 0.87%. Scenario 7 finally simulates the
impact of the hormone ban in the US thereby affecting mainly US trade and benefiting the
EU dairy sector with an export volume increase of 2.44%.

Conclusions

In this chapter the impact of compliance to standards on production costs in the dairy sector
is estimated using a farm level analysis and taking into account actual farm accountancy
data. Representative farm studies have been conducted and used as a basis for the cost
calculations. Best-estimates of compliance are used, but these still contain a certain degree
of uncertainty. In a number of cases alternative approaches and alternative sources were
used to cross-check both production costs and degree of compliance in order to test for the
robustness of our results. As regards the impact of the Nitrates Directive and Identification
and Registration standards on production, clearly the nitrates standard causes the most
128      Environmental and health standards in the dairy sectors

significant impact. At farm level, the percentage cost increase varies from 0.19% to 6.8%
within the selected Member States, from 2% to 4.2% for US farms and is about 2% in New
Zealand. Profitability and gross-margin of farms with a lower degree of compliance (France,
for example) are substantially affected. At sector level, full compliance to the nitrates
standard for all affected farmers would involve an increase in production costs between
0.1% and 0.6%, with rates varying between Member States. The estimated percentage cost
increase associated with full compliance with the Identification and Registration standard by
all affected farmers would be less than 0.15% and, hence, rather marginal. As shown in
Table 7.4 the negative impact of these measures on EU imports and exports are less than
1%. If a smaller increase in compliance takes place, these trade impacts will be even
smaller. When the standards for nitrate pollution taken by the US and New Zealand are
taken into account along with a full compliance assumption in all countries analysed, this
would impact the trade balance by 1.07% (the highest rate of all the scenarios) and will
result in a lower decline of EU exports (about 0.41%). This suggests that although
approaches and measures are formally different there seems to be a non-neglectible degree
of functional equivalence between the measures addressing a similar class of problems in
different jurisdictions. The EU‟s international competitiveness does not increase
substantially in this situation and therefore improvements in internal farm competitiveness
(higher gross margin and profitability) should remain a high priority in farm management.
The trade impacts obtained when no changes are assumed to happen in key competitor
countries can thus be argued as providing the upper bound of the likely trade impacts.
Moreover, as became clear from analysing the dairy farm costs and revenue structure over
countries, cost differences induced by environmental and animal registration regulations are
only one factor explaining the dairy farm sector‟s competitiveness. Differences in labour
costs (fixed costs) per 100 kg of milk between the considered countries are generally
speaking much more important. These latter differences reflect differences in labour
productivity, which seem to be related to the farm size structure (economies of scale).

Chapter 8

An EU/US perspective on nutrient management
policies and growth hormone bans in the beef
sector

Kees de Roest, Jonathan Winsten and Koen Dillen



Introduction

The beef sector in the EU and in the US is subject to several environmental and food safety
regulations, which may generate extra costs for beef farmers. In other terms, the negative
externalities related to water pollution and to human health uncertainty become internalized
into the beef farm cost structure. The enforcement of environmental and food safety policies
may have implications for the competitiveness of the EU and US beef sectors in the world
market, in particular when decisions are taken unilaterally, but also when significant policy
differences exist between the EU and the US.
     This chapter presents an analysis of the compliance costs for beef farmers in the EU
and the US for specific environmental and food safety regulations and the impact this cost
increase may have on the competitiveness of beef production on the world market. The
compliance costs will be assessed using typical beef farms in the EU and in the US, whereas
the impact on competitiveness will be calculated using the GTAP model as has been
described in Chapter 1. The simulation will be presented first under the hypothesis that the
EU is implementing unilaterally its own legislation followed by an assessment of the EU-US
competitiveness in beef when both jurisdictions enforce their own environmental and food
safety regulations. Other commercial blocs such as South America and Australia enter into
the GTAP simulations but no calculations have been carried out about their eventual
compliance costs with local environmental legislation, as this was beyond the scope of this
research.
     A myriad of environmental regulations have been issued in the EU and the US. It is
beyond the scope of this chapter to undertake a comprehensive and exhaustive examination
of the full set of such regulations. The analysis will focus on what are arguably the most
topical examples of environmental and food safety legislation: the Nitrates Directive and the
Identification and Registration of bovines in the EU and the Clean Water Act in the US. A
comparison will be made between the regulatory tools of the Clean Water Act of the US,
which aims to improve and protect water quality, and the mandatory standards established
by the Nitrates Directive in the EU. An effort will be made to compare the impact on
competitiveness of the EU and US beef sectors in case of full compliance with the
respective environmental regulations.


©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         131
132     An EU/US perspective on nutrient management policies

     Additionally attention is paid to the possible cost and trade consequences of a
prohibition on the use of growth hormones in the US. As far as this last issue is concerned,
we will assess the cost advantage of US beef producers with the use of growth hormones. Of
particular interest will be the examination of the effect that a prohibition on the use of
growth hormones in the US might have on the international trade of beef.
     An important consideration is the fact that the EU has started to become a net importer
of beef in 2003, after several decades of having been a significant beef exporter due to the
market regime for beef which allocated abundant export refunds. The gradual break down
of this policy has opened the EU market to imports, particularly from South America. The
EU beef market is still protected by import tariffs. But it is possible to foresee a further
deterioration of the competitive position of the EU beef industry in the future, as low cost
producers of South America (Brazil, Argentina and Uruguay) may gain a larger access to
the EU market.

Cost implications of the Nitrates Directive

The main requirement under the Nitrates Directive is the maximum limit of 170 kg N/ha
manure (or 250 kg N/ha of manure on grassland in Germany and the Netherlands)
application rate, which may generate extra costs for a manure disposal. In Nitrate
Vulnerable Zones (NVZ), farms exceeding the maximum limit have to find extra land to
spread excess manure, either by buying or renting land or paying a licence to spread manure
on land of nearby farmers. Moreover, they are obliged to invest in manure storage facilities
due to the prohibition on spreading manure in winter time.

Affected beef production

Out of the 491,000 farms engaged in beef production in the EU, about half of them are cow-
calf farms, over one quarter (27%) are specialized beef finishing farms and 20% have a
coupled production of dairy and beef (Sarzeaud et al., 2007).
     Striking to note is that 44% of beef production originates on farms where dairy is the
main livestock activity, another 14% is produced on fattening farms and 35% on cow-calf
farms. Extensive production systems tend to predominate in the EU as on 47% of the cattle
farms the stocking rate does not exceed 1.4 livestock units (LU) per ha. Typically, low
stocking rates characterize cow-calf farms, whereas the pure fattening farms often exceed a
stocking rate of 1.8 LU per ha.
     From Table 8.1, it is clear that many types of beef production systems will not be
affected by the Nitrates Directive, since they have a stocking rate of less than 1.8 LU per ha.
In terms of farm numbers this amounts to 72% of the beef farmers in the EU. For the
remaining 28%, some of the beef finishing farms and a small minority of cow-calf farms
will eventually face costs of compliance.
     In order to assess the share of farms affected, the limit of 1.8 LU per ha has been
compared to the maximum stocking rate allowed within the Nitrate Vulnerable Zones
(NVZs). For beef finishing farms, the 1.8 LU per ha would constrain the stocking rate in
NVZ by 13% and in cow-calf farms by 38% (Table 8.2).

However this number of farms likely overestimates the number of affected farms, because
we have assumed that the whole territory of the EU is declared as a Nitrate Vulnerable
Zone, and the application rate of 170 kg N/ha for disposal of excess manure would therefore
be applicable throughout the EU. In Table 8.3 we estimate the percentage of suckler cows
and of male fattening calves in the NVZs of the EU. The estimate has been obtained by
using the REGIO databank of Eurostat by overlaying the map of Nitrate Vulnerable Zones
on the NUTS III regions of the EU.
     Table 8.4 translates these figures into number of farms. The outcome of this analysis
reveals that 24,842 beef farms (6.7% of the total number of cattle farms) in the EU-15 will
face a cost increase due to the application of the Nitrates Directive.

Impact of improved compliance with Nitrates Directive on beef farm costs

The calculation of the cost of compliance with the Nitrates Directive was carried out
considering a typical beef finishing farm producing young bulls in the North East of Italy
(De Roest et al., 2007). A significant share of the beef cattle fattening farms with high
stocking rates is located in this part of the EU and can be considered typical for this type of
activity. Similar fattening units exist in the North West of Italy and in the North of Spain.
Weaner calves are primarily imported from France.

The related technical and economic data have been drawn from a sample of beef farms
maintained by CRPA (Centro Ricerche Produzioni Animali) for annual monitoring of beef
farm production costs in Italy (ISMEA/CRPA, 2006) (Tables 8.5 and 8.6).
     The yearly slurry production is equal to 15,756 m3 corresponding to a nitrogen content
of 50,904 kg. Currently the average farm consists of 151 ha of agricultural land available
for cattle slurry spreading.
     If this farm is located in an ordinary zone, the only cost deriving from the current
situation is given by storage and slurry distribution on owned farmland. If the area where
the farm is located is declared a NVZ, then it would be necessary to add farmland (75 ha) in
order to respect the maximum allowed limit of 170 kg N manure per ha. This entails an
extra cost for acquiring the right of spreading on lands owned by other farmers and to
transport the excess manure to these farmlands that are supposed to be situated 15
136      An EU/US perspective on nutrient management policies

kilometres from the beef farm. Furthermore, the size of basins for storing slurry must be
increased up to the minimum capacity required, corresponding to 180 storage days.

The option to spread manure on neighbouring land may not be feasible when in the
surrounding area the land supply is scarce and livestock farming density is high. In this
case, manure treatment systems would be needed. The treatment equipment is designed for
reducing nitrogen content in the animal slurry (centrifuge for the solid fraction separation
and aerator for the liquid fraction), reducing the need for land outside the farm from 75 to
21 ha. In addition, the sale of compost is an extra revenue source that partially offsets the
operating costs of the treatment process.
     This latter option does not involve significant differences with respect to the first
(acquire spreading rights from other farmers). The average costs entailed by the two
solutions can be compared to the total production costs as calculated by CRPA
(ISMEA/CRPA, 2006) based on a sample of typical Italian beef farms. Considering that the
total cost in 2006 was equal to 2.57 €/kg live weight (including the purchasing cost of
weaners), the percentage cost increase entailed by both options can be estimated at 5.8%
(Table 8.8).

Impact of improved compliance at sector level

According to the analysis of the structure and the regional distribution of cow-calf and beef
fattening farms, 3.0% of beef produced in cow-calf farms and 4.2% of beef cattle in the
finishing farms would be affected by Nitrates Directive and subjected to an increase of
production costs. If we assume that 50% of the beef farms are already complying with the
Nitrates Directive then 1.5% of beef produced in cow-calf farms and 2.1% of beef produced
in beef fattening farms will face increased costs in order to attain a 100% compliance at EU
level.
     The cost increase for affected fattening farms is 0.155€ per kg beef. In order to estimate
the impact on EU beef sector, the production cost analysis of the Agribenchmark,
coordinated by the Federal Institute for Agriculture (FAL), has been used. This is a data
base representing 10 EU countries and 90% of EU beef production. A weighted average of
Agribenchmark beef farms generates an average production cost of beef of €2.67/kg in the
EU. The increase for those farms located in NVZ which exceed the limit of 170 kg N per ha
138        An EU/US perspective on nutrient management policies

would then be equal to 5.8%. In the case of 25% compliance 0.83 % of beef production
would be affected and the sector cost increase would be 0.048%. If we assume 50%
compliance the sector cost increase equals 0.095% and in the case of 75% compliance the
cost increase will be 0.143%. This limited cost increase related to compliance with the
Nitrates Directive can be primarily attributed to extensive nature of the majority of beef
production in the EU (Table 8.9).

Impact of water pollution measures in the US

In the US the Clean Water Act (CWA) is the primary federal law for the protection of the
surface water quality in the US. Among the other mandatory standards imposed upon US
farmers, the regulatory tools employed by the CWA can be compared to the requirements
established by Nitrates Directive in the EU.
     Initially the primary focus of the CWA was on point sources of pollution from industry
and wastewater plants, but in recent years it has been expanded to include farming activities.
Until December 15, 2002, the Federal Government issued specific rules governing
Concentrated Animal Feeding Operations (CAFOs) defined as animal feeding operations
with more than 1,000 animal units (700 dairy cows, 1,000 beef cows, 2,500 pig or 100,000
broilers)1. Under the current rule, all farms designated as CAFOs are required to obtain a
permit under the National Pollution Discharge Elimination System (NPDES) that entails the
implementation of a comprehensive nutrient management plan (CNMP). The CNMP must
be specific for the operation and detail the proper management of all manure produced. It
must address the assimilative capacity of the farmland for the manure and other nutrients
applied to the land. The implementation of CNMPs often result in operations seeking


1
 Now the EPA has abandoned the Animal Units to define CAFOs and is expressing it in number of heads per
species.
                                   An EU/US perspective on nutrient management policies     139

additional land on which to spread manure and/or the use of alternative nutrient control
strategies for manure.
     Feedlot beef farming has been considered in order to estimate the costs of compliance
with US regulations concerning water quality (Winsten, 2007). Cost of compliance is
related to the implementation of the CNMP that is required for all livestock farms
recognized as CAFO. In the case of beef farms, CAFOs are feeding operations with more
than 1,000 beef cows.
     The US Department of Agriculture (USDA) has estimated the average costs for
developing and implementing a successful CNMP in specialized feedlots farms, as $14 per
head, for an average size of 1,300 head per operation. These figures are the basis for
calculating the average costs of CNMP implementation in a typical cattle feedlot in Texas,
which is the leading beef producing state in the US accounting for 27% of total beef
production (see Table 8.10).


Table 8.10 Estimated expenses and CWA compliance costs for Texas feedlot, 2007

Expenses                                                       $ per head             %
750 lb. Feeder steer                                             814.73             72.0
Total feed, handling, and management charge                      257.52             22.8
Interest on feeder and 1/2 feed                                   44.63              3.9
Death loss (1% of purchase)                                        8.15              0.7
Total expenses                                                 1,125.03             99.5
CAFO CNMP costs                                                    6.21              0.5
Total costs                                                    1,131.24            100.0

Source: USDA (2003)



For this analysis, the CNMP compliance cost per head was constructed for the average size
of Texas feedlots with more than 1,000 head, equal to 22,462 head per farm. Because the
average CNMP costs decrease as farm size increases, the USDA estimated costs per head
(14$) for all US feedlots would overestimate the total costs for the larger feedlots. To
account for the lower CNMP costs associated with larger feedlots, an adjustment factor was
calculated. When applied to the $14 per head cost for a typical US fattened cattle operation,
the resulting CNMP compliance cost is estimated to be $6.21 per head. Based on the
average feeder cattle production estimates on Texas feedlots (50,000 head, assumed 450
pound increase in finishing size), the $6.21 CNMP compliance costs per head result in an
estimated 0.55% increase in total feedlot production costs, as compared to a 5.8% cost
increase in a large feedlot in Italy which has to comply with the Nitrates Directive. It has not
been possible to estimate a sector cost increase, because it has been extremely difficult to
estimate in a reliable way the number of feedlots and related beef cattle which will be
affected by the Clean Water Act. At the same time we presume the sector cost increase will
be very small as already the compliance costs at farm level are negligible.
140     An EU/US perspective on nutrient management policies

Growth promoters use prohibition: evaluated standards and cost
implications

Council Directive 96/22/EC of 1996 concerns the prohibition on the use in livestock
farming of substances having a hormonal or thyrostatic action and of beta-agonists. This
section of the chapter investigates the extent of the cost advantage that the US beef industry
enjoys using these substances, relative to the EU.
     The use of hormones in beef fattening cattle increases average daily gain and improves
feed efficiency. There is also evidence of drawbacks which hormones may cause to the
carcass and eating quality, but there are growth implant strategies available which can
considerably alleviate these concerns (Montgomery et al., 2001). Hence, the cost advantage
of the use of growth hormones more than offsets any negative effect on carcass quality.

Impact of the prohibition on the use of hormones

In a meta-analysis combining information from more than 170 research trials carried out in
the last forty years, the economic impacts of pharmaceutical technologies in cow-calf and
beef fattening farms have been assessed (Lawrence and Ibarburu, 2006). The average
effects on critical production parameters obtained from this meta-analysis are reported in
Table 8.11.


Table 8.11 Potential effects of a ban on growth promotants on the technical efficiency in US beef
           sector (%)

                           Beef cow herds                                            Feedlot
Wean rate (%)                   -2.54            Average daily gain (%)               -14.13
                                                 Feed to gain (lbs feed/
Wean weight (%)                 -3.07                                                 -8.79
                                                 lbs weight gain) (%)

Source: Lawrence and Ibarburu (2006)



Taking into account the adoption rate of growth promotants, which in the US is high, an
overall impact on production costs of a ban on growth promotants has been estimated as
7.14%. In other words, a ban on the use of growth promotants would increase the
production costs of beef in the US by this percentage, as the wean rate would decrease by
2.54%, the average daily gain would deteriorate by 14.13% and the feed conversion rate by
8.79% (Lawrence and Ibarburu, 2006).
    An increase in production costs of this magnitude would have significant consequences
for the international competitiveness of US beef production. The GTAP simulations
concerning this issue are presented at the end of this chapter.
                                      An EU/US perspective on nutrient management policies             141


Identification and registration of animals: evaluated standards and cost
implications

Important food safety considerations lay behind the regulations related to the identification
of animals. In order to comply with Regulation 1760/2000, beef farms will have to update
registers and eartags continuously. The costs generated by this regulation are related to the
time necessary to update the registers and to the purchase of eartags for newborn calves, for
imported calves and to replace eartags lost during the lifetime.

Impact of compliance with identification and registration of animal requirements

The costs per head, including costs for the eartags accidentally lost, range from a minimum
of €1.80 per head in France up to €5.00 per head in the Netherlands (see also Chapter 7).

     In order to estimate the impact on the EU beef sector the production cost analysis of the
Agribenchmark coordinated by the Federal Institute for Agriculture (FAL) has been used
(Agribenchmark, 2007). Considering the total production costs per farm it is possible to
calculate the percentage cost increase per kg beef to be attributed to the identification and
registration of beef cattle. The seven countries listed in Table 8.12 represent almost 90% of
beef production of the EU-15.
     Assuming 100% compliance we obtain an average weighted cost increase of 0.455%
for the EU-15. The cost impact of identification and registration varies with the cost per
head of registration and with herd size. In countries with a large herd size some economies
of scale can be exploited, whereas small herds, like for example in Ireland, are facing more
significant effects.

Standards and external competitiveness

Expected impact of Nitrates Directive and identification and registration of animals
on beef trade flows

The impact of full compliance with Nitrates Directive was simulated with the GTAP model
in order to assess the effects of this policy on the competitiveness of EU beef production in
the world market. The previous analysis has found a limited impact on EU beef farms (an
increase of costs 0.095% at a 50% compliance rate), primarily due to the low share of farms
that would be affected. The effects on EU beef exports could be a 0.68% decrease in
quantity, while imports would rise by 0.51%. The main advantage would be taken by Brazil
with an increase of 0.28%. For Australia, the US and Canada no significant changes would
occur.

According to the previous calculations, the EU-15 is facing a cost increase of 0.455% per
kg beef for maintaining a system of identification and registration of beef cattle. Between
EU Member States it varies from 0.16% in France up to 0.89% in Ireland.
    Table 8.14 shows that cost increases due to I&R regulations would increase the EU
beef imports by 2.2% and decrease exports by a similar amount. The EU share of the world
                                       An EU/US perspective on nutrient management policies               143

export market would decline by 2.23%, while a 2.09% increase of the share on global
import is foreseen.
     Negative impact on EU beef competitiveness entails positive changes in the market
share by some of the main beef exporting competitors. In particular Brazil would expand its
exports by 1.1%. The performances of the other competitors on the world market would be
affected to a smaller extent. The share gains of Canada, the US and Australia are expected
to be negligible. The increase of beef traded by these countries should be limited to less
than 0.2%, due to the fact they compete on different markets than those where EU is
engaged. Benefits would be higher for the export from the rest of the world, that on the
whole is projected to rise by 1.21%.
     Combining the effects of both standards (Nitrates Directive and I&R), an overall
decrease of 2.73% would occur to EU exports. The reduction in exports to Japan would
equal 2.67% while exports to other OECD would decrease by 2.54%. On the whole, Brazil
would gain 1.37% of global market share, while changes in the global market shares of
Australia, Canada and the US would be negligible.

144      An EU/US perspective on nutrient management policies

Expected impact of a hormone ban in the US on beef trade flows

Earlier, we presented evidence that a US ban on the use of growth hormones would increase
production costs by 7.14%. According to the simulations carried out with the GTAP model,
a ban on growth promotants would cause an increase in US beef imports of 32%, whereas
exports would decline by 36% and beef production in the US would decline by 5%. In
particular, the gap left by the US in the Japanese import market will be primarily filled by
Australia.
     The decline of American beef production will stimulate a growth of the imports from
Australia and Canada. Also the rest of the OECD countries will import more as the US will
lose about 41% of its exports on these markets.
     In this new situation the EU-15 will remain a net importer of beef but will slightly
improve its competitive position. EU exports are expected to rise by nearly 6%.

Conclusion
Within the EU, beef is produced in a wide range of farming systems, ranging from the
extensive cow-calf farms in Ireland, the UK and the centre of France down to the very
intensive beef fattening systems located in Italy and Spain. The Nitrates Directive affects
4.2% of beef cattle raised in intensive finishing farms and 3.0% of beef produced on cow-
calf farms. This low percentage of farms affected by the Nitrates Directive explains the
limited sector cost increase, which has been estimated at 0.095%. This relatively low cost
impact does not have significant consequences for the competitive position of the EU beef
production on the world market. The EU trade deficit in beef would increase, as exports
would fall by 0.68% and imports would rise by 0.51%. The country which would benefit
most from this situation is Brazil increasing its global exports by 0.28% and in particular to
the EU (+0.52%).
     More important for the beef farms are the regulations concerning the identification and
registration of beef cattle. Implemented as a reaction to the BSE crisis, regulations require
beef farmers to register all cattle movements and make sure that all animals are correctly
identified from birth up to the slaughterhouse. According to our estimates, these measures
generate a 0.454% average cost increase for the beef farms in the EU. This cost effect has a
                                   An EU/US perspective on nutrient management policies      145

significant effect on EU beef trade. Beef imports are higher by 2.2% and exports are lower,
by the same percentage under these regulations. Again Brazil‟s exports to the EU are 2.18%
higher and its global exports are 1.1% higher as a result of these EU regulations. The other
competitors on the world market are affected to a lesser extent.
     Finally, a ban on the use of growth hormones in the US would have a strong effect on
beef production costs, with an average cost increase of 7.14%. Such a production cost
increase would have a significant effect on the competitiveness of US beef production in the
world market. US exports would decline by 36% to those countries which already now
import American beef.

Chapter 9

Good agricultural and environmental
conditions in the EU and their implications for
international trade in cereals

Roel Jongeneel, Xavier Poux and Glenn Fox



Introduction

In the EU, cereals are the most widely produced arable crop. European statistics on cereals
comprise wheat, barley, maize, triticale, rye, oats, mixed grain, rice, sorghum, buckwheat,
millet and canary seed. Cereals account for a third of the EU‟s utilized agricultural area
(UAA). Wheat is the crop that forms the highest proportion in European agriculture
accounting for 46% of the total area used for the production of cereals. Wheat is used in
animal feed, human diet and also for starch and bioethanol. In 2009, a total area of 25.6
million hectares was cultivated to produce 138.7 million tonnes of wheat. Grain maize
constituted 19% of EU 2009 cereal production, the production being 57.7 million tonnes.
     When considering the relative share of production traded on the world market, the
cereals sector is one of the agricultural sectors that is most opened up, with around 11-15%
of the total production being traded each year, and trade depending on weather conditions
and stocks. Wheat and maize dominate world cereals trade, (representing 45% and 35% of
total EU cereals trade respectively), while rice and barley jointly represent 18% of EU
cereals trade (leaving 2% for other cereals such as sorghum, millets and oats) (FAO, 2007-
2008 data). Relative to cereals, other agricultural commodities such as meat or dairy
products are less exported/imported. As such the competitiveness issue is particularly
crucial in the cereals sector. Moreover, in a sense for several countries the price for cereals
can be considered of utter importance for the whole economy, as shown in the recent years
with „hunger fights‟ in 2007/2008 for instance (Dufumier, 2009). As shown in the next
section, the number of countries exporting cereals is quite limited, with a huge domination
of the USA alone.
     Cereals are produced in a great variety of farming systems across the world, mainly in
temperate areas. They can be cultivated in small mixed low input farms as well as in large
specialized and high input farms, concentrated in the most productive land forming various
„grain belts‟. These differences in farming systems entail a great range of environmental
impacts of cereal systems. In some cases, extensive cereal systems can be beneficial for
biodiversity, while in other cases the use of nutrients, pesticides, inappropriate irrigation


©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         147
148    Good agricultural and environmental conditions in the EU

practices and heavy tillage will lead to severe negative impacts on natural resources (Bignal
and McCracken, 1996; IEEP, 2000).
     This chapter addresses the impact of compliance and costs of compliance with
environmental standards on the competitiveness of farms producing cereals. In a context
where the EU is developing environmental conditions on its agriculture, the overarching
question for policy makers is to find a balance between a better integration of environmental
concerns in cereal production - in order to meet a strong need at EU level - while not
imposing constraints which would threaten the competitiveness of EU cereal producers. The
thesis of the chapter is that the system of Good Agricultural and Environmental Conditions
(GAECs), which was adopted by the EU as part of cross compliance, prevents a significant
unilateral loss of competitiveness. The measurable impact on production costs due to the
measures taken is thus found to be limited.
     The next section discusses some sustainability issues with respect to cereals production.
The following section provides an overview of cereal production and trade aimed at getting
further insight into the particular structure of cereal markets and trade. It also serves to
motivate the choice of the countries that will be further analysed in the subsequent part of
the chapter. The following section focuses on the structure and composition of costs of
cereals production in the EU and in main regions of the world. This provides further insight
into the relative share of different cost items that can be attributed to some environmental
constraints. We will then address the environmental standards and their estimated impact on
production costs in cereals. Some comparisons are made with cereals production in Canada.
The potential impact of compliance with environmental standards on world trade are
assessed. This is achieved by scaling up cost impact at farm level to sector level.
Subsequently the change in costs at sector level are imported into the GTAP model, which
includes world trade in cereals. Finally, the conclusion puts the findings in the broader
perspective of a better integration of environmental concerns, and of other factors
influencing the competitiveness of cereal production.

Cereals and sustainability

To start with, we will briefly elaborate on some key environmental issues associated with
cereal production. Cereal yields in the EU have increased since the mid-1970s at an average
rate of 2.2% per annum. The overall use of pesticides has decreased since the early 1990s
(Eurostat, 1997). Arable farming in Europe has impacts on wildlife and on the sustainability
of arable systems. Some of the other environmental impacts associated with cereal
production include removal of soil, pollution of water, deterioration in ecosystems reflected
in aesthetic quality of arable landscape.
     Arable soils are vulnerable to erosion by wind and water, soil compaction through the
use of heavy machinery, and organic matter reduction through frequent crop removal.
Organic matter may erode to water bodies reducing its availability on arable soils. Legume
based leys and integrating livestock into arable farming has been found to improve organic
matter level in comparison to conventional systems (e.g. Drinkwater et al., 1998). As Evans
(1996) shows factors such as the increased area of autumn cultivation, increases in field size
and associated loss of hedges, and permanent arable cropping increase levels of erosion.
Erosion is clearly slope-related. Skinner et al. (1997), for example, reported that 60% of
erosion occurs on slopes of less than 7°. Moreover, they argued that hedge removal
accelerates erosion, therewith pointing to a linkage between sustainability and landscape
                                 Good agricultural and environmental conditions in the EU   149

preservation. Moreover on flat land and light soils, wind erosion is of importance. The use
of heavy machinery and frequent passing with farm equipment contributes to soil
compaction, which accelerates runoff. This might also lead to poor soil drainage, cause
waterlogging and make crops susceptible to drought. Erosion can also cause loss of
nutrients. The risk of soil erosion by wheat cultivation has been found to be low, but is
generally higher with maize than with other cereal crops. Available studies in Europe
suggest that lack of crop cover increases erosion rates on arable fields (Boatman et al.,
1999). Brouwer and Ervin (2002) have found that late harvested spring sown maize
increases the exposure to erosion. Erosion has agronomic as well as economic
consequences. García-Torres et al. (2003), for example, has shown annual economic losses
of erosion to be around €53 per ha and €32 per ha for off-site effects.
     A relatively recent phenomenon is the production of biomass for bioenergy. This needs
to comply with the same environmental standards as food production. As a result of the
Biofuels Directive of 2003, both the use and the production of bioethanol have been rising
steadily in the EU. In 2004, approximately 528 thousand m³ of bioethanol was produced in
Europe (involving 0.7 million tons of cereals). By 2009, the production of bioethanol for
fuel applications had already reached 3.6 million m³. With the further implementation of the
Renewable Energies Directive, which provides for a mandatory biofuel quota of 10% for
the year 2020, the demand for bioethanol is expected to rise strongly in the EU. It is
estimated that the biofuel requirement will imply a demand for ethanol of about 20 million
m³ in the year 2020 (equivalent to about 50 million tonnes of cereals or about 36% of the
EU‟s wheat production in 2009). Grain used for bioenergy production currently constitutes
about 1% of total EU cereal production (2009/10). The use of crop residues, e.g. straw for
bioenergy might become more important in the future as so-called second generation
technologies become available. Collecting this material from the field, however, creates the
risk of depleting soil organic matter content because farmers currently (partly) use it as
mulch or soil cover. This will consequently then reduce water retention and increase the risk
of soil erosion. In good quality soils, taking straw from fields is allowed in North Western
Europe. However there is resistance by many farmers (European Commission, 2006).
Bioenergy production from cereal straw can help to mitigate climate change because of
reduced emissions, but this is likely to depend on the conditions under which production
will take place. The production of bioenergy from cereal straw could also lead to the
competition for agricultural raw materials with food production. Cereal straw as raw
material for biofuel also serves as raw material for the production of pulp and paper, animal
bedding and feed. The use of straw as bedding and feed for animals may contribute to the
reduced amount of straw for bioenergy.

Production, trade and consumption of cereals

Three countries (China, US and India) dominate world cereals production and trade (Table
9.1). France and Germany are key producers. The US is the world‟s dominant net exporter
of cereals, followed at a distance by countries like France, Canada, Argentina, and the
Russian Federation. As regards the EU, France is the most important net exporter of cereals.

As regards trade, the US dominates the market for wheat and maize. The US has a share of
more than half of global production of maize. The EU is a key player only for the wheat
market (with current exports about 16 million tonnes), while it is quite marginal for the
maize market (it is even a net importer). Table 9.1 shows that the main exporters are not
necessarily the main producers, with the exception of the US.

The main importers include three categories:

     Developed countries with a relatively low share of arable land (e.g. Japan, South
      Korea, Spain, Italy). The rationale to import cereals is simply to compensate for an
      absence of natural assets with other traded products outside the agricultural sector.
     Developing countries with population density that is simply too high compared to the
      producing capacity. Egypt is the most obvious example in this case, with only 3% of its
      territory being used for arable production. Algeria also falls in this category.
     Intermediate countries in the process of development are adopting a comparative
      advantage strategy. Although they would be able to produce cereals, they have chosen
      to allocate their agricultural production factors to other commodities.


Four Member States dominate cereals production in the EU (Table 9.2). In addition to
France and Germany, also Poland and Italy are large producers and in total they represent
54% of production in the EU. France alone has a share of about 22%.
     Figures 9.1, 9.2 and 9.3 provide an overview of the evolution of cereals production at
EU Member State level. Production tends to fluctuate over time (notably for Hungary), but
the figures show no clear trends.
     Cereals are the basis of the human diet in many ways: directly consumed (e.g. products
like bread, pastas and processed food based on starch) or indirectly consumed through
livestock products (i.e. humans eating animals fed under a cereal diet i.e. granivore animals
such as pigs, poultry). About two thirds of EU cereals production is used for feed, be it by
farmers (on farm feed mixing, 36%) or indirectly via the compound feed sector. About one
quarter of EU cereal production is used for human consumption.

In the EU, about 65% of the cereals is used for animal food. Cereals are a dry commodity,
and are rather easily transported across Europe. Thus, the industries of cereals are often
based near the main consumption areas, which are the industrialized livestock areas in
Europe. This explains why the main importers are also large livestock producers (i.e. Spain,
Italy and the Netherlands).

Arable farming and wheat production in the EU

Cereals are part of a wider mix of crops produced by EU arable farms. Table 9.3 provides
an overview of the hectares of wheat grown per specialized arable farm in the EU-27. There
is considerable variation across Member States, which reflects among others differences in
scale of farming. Large scale cereal farms can be found in Czech Republic, Slovakia, the
UK, Germany and France. A significant increase in the hectares of cereals is observed over
time in most countries. As can be understood when combining information from Table 9.4
with Table 9.3 this also reflects farm scale increase.
     Table 9.4 represents the share of cereals in the total utilizable agricultural area of
specialized arable farms in the EU. As the table shows these shares tend to be rather stable
over time. Table 9.4 provides an indication of the degree of specialization in cereals in the
specialized arable farm sector by Member State. Note that for most Member States the share
of cereals is higher than 50%, which indicates the key importance of cereals for the EU
arable sector as well as for nearly all EU Member States. Member States showing a
relatively low share of cereals in their crop mix are Belgium, The Netherlands, and
Portugal.
     Figure 9.4 provides the crop specific costs (€/ha) for selected Member States. Costs are
related to the whole crop mix, including non-cereal crops. Here, specific costs for crops
distinguish between:

   seeds and seedlings, purchased and produced on the farm;
   fertilizers, soil improvers (lime, compost, peat, manure) and crop protection products;
    and
   other specific crop costs that are the general costs directly connected with crop
    production (packing and binding materials, soil analysis, etc.).
154     Good agricultural and environmental conditions in the EU

Specific costs tend to vary between €200 and €400 per hectare. Belgium, and even more so
The Netherlands have very high specific costs per hectare and are mainly suitable to grow
arable crops with a high gross margin and high yields.

Production costs in the EU and other exporting countries

Figure 9.5 provides the cost structure for wheat production for selected EU Member States
as a percentage of the output value (Kleinhanss et al., 2011). The costs of soft wheat
production (durum wheat for Italy) are calculated for three periods, i.e. 1999-2003, 2004-
2006 and 2007 (conditional on a sufficient number of reliable observations being available).
Distinguished cost categories are crop specific costs, energy, other specific costs,
overheads, and depreciation and other fixed costs. When looking to the absolute levels,
Spain, Poland and Hungary turn out to be relative low cost producers. For the other
Member States the costs of soft wheat production vary between €600-€900 per hectare, with
156     Good agricultural and environmental conditions in the EU

the UK being a high-cost producer. Costs of energy vary between €50 and €90 per hectare.
Costs of depreciation amount to about €150 per hectare, and are as such an important cost
item.


Figure 9.4 Crop specific costs in € per hectare for selected EU Member States (2003-2006 average)




Source: EU-FADN



Yields show wide variation across Member States, and costs per unit of production are more
appropriate for an EU-wide comparison than costs per hectare. Production costs vary
between €80/ton and €130/ton, with the average being close to €110/ton. Year 2007 is a
special year, with a strong increase in the output price of wheat, as well as strong increases
in costs (Kleinhanss et al., 2011, p. 11).
     France is a key producer of cereals with surplus production. Moreover, Kleinhanss et
al. (2011) and Thorne (2005) indicate that costs of production in France are comparable to
other major cereal producing countries in the EU. It was therefore decided to focus on
France as the representative case for the competitiveness analysis of the environmental
standards. The results are used to generalize them to other Member States (taking into
account their specific compliance rates and cost-revenue structure).
     The main producing and exporting countries have quite different yields (Table 9.6).
This is largely due to natural and socio-economic conditions. The EU and its competitors
seem to opt for opposite strategies. While the EU‟s competitiveness is based on maximizing
production per hectare – due to a relative scarcity of agricultural land per inhabitant – the
Australian, Argentine, US and Canadian competitiveness is rather based on minimizing
costs per hectare.
     As these countries have different yields per hectare, the relevant data is not the cost per
hectare, but rather the costs per unit of product (€/quintal), which are provided in Figure
9.6. The costs are calculated using a standardized procedure in order to ensure
comparability over countries (as compared to Figure 9.5, in Figure 9.6 the costs of land,
capital and labour are accounted for).
