At lower end, Australia is the most competitive country (for the year indicated), with 10.6
€/q while the other countries range between 13 and 15 €/q. The figure shows that the
relatively similar costs are reached through different costs structures. In the US, for
example, the land costs are relatively high (circa 25% of all costs), due to a high
„consumption‟ of land per quintal produced (another way to label the low yields) while,
symmetrically, they are relatively low in France (12%). Reciprocally, the operational costs
are higher in France (±30% of all costs) than in the US (< 20%).
     Meanwhile, the machinery and labour costs levels are rather similar across the countries
studied, expressed in €/quintal. While the breakdown between the two items is not
available, one can only make assumptions on how this result is obtained. While the French
cereals will stand on more intensified cropping patterns, the higher costs are in general
compensated by higher yields. Said in other words, the machinery and labour costs are more
or less fully proportionate to the yield. Differences in cost structure makes the different
systems differently sensitive to variations in yields and prices.
                                 Good agricultural and environmental conditions in the EU   159

Environmental standards and their costs

This section starts with a short discussion of which standards were finally selected for a
quantitative trade-impact analysis. Thereafter it provides background about the calculated
sector percentage cost of production increases. Moreover some cases are mentioned to
provide some further farm level insights. The farms involved in cereal production will be
potentially affected by the following standards or directives:

   „general‟: groundwater, sewage sludge, placing of plant protection products, food law,
    & procedures in matters of food safety; and
   „zoned‟: Nitrates Directive (NVZ), Birds Directive (SPA) and Habitat Directive (SCI);
    the latter two are in the N2000 network.

Livestock production is more affected by environmental standards than cereal production.
Cereal production is potentially affected by the Nitrates Directive, in particular the
requirement it imposes on achieving a certain degree of soil cover during winter time. More
important for the arable sector are the requirements for Good Agricultural and
Environmental Conditions (GAECs) (see Table 9.7). GAEC standards principally concern
limitation of soil erosion, buffer strips, diversity of cropping pattern, the ban on straw
burning, regulation of irrigation. Cereals and crop systems are, in principle, mainly affected
by the GAECs although some dispensations may weaken the requirements because
individual Member States had some freedom in determining the GAEC standards and could
tailor them to their national situation.
     In order to evaluate the impact on competitiveness an estimate was made about the
sectoral percentage cost increase of the GAEC regulations on cereals. Based on previous
country studies the costs of the GAECs for cereals are estimated to be €35/ha, which
includes the estimated revenue-reduction due to the need to retain more straw on the fields
(organic matter) and the additional costs (tillage, ploughing, etc.). A much higher cost is
taken into account for the proper management of idled land, which is estimated to create a
cost of €400/ha. This latter estimate includes the costs of adequate soil cover, tillage, fuel
and labour costs.
     Since the estimated costs associated with the GAECs for cultivated and idled land are
different, the use of set-aside land (e.g. idled or used for energy crops) and the rate of set-
aside (10% for year 2005, but currently reduced to 0%) are likely to affect the cost increase
associated with the GAEC standard. For this reason it was decided to simulate two
scenarios: One scenario assumes the set-aside rate to be 10% and all set-aside land to be
used as idled land. This scenario can be considered to be a worst case scenario with
relatively high costs. The alternative scenario assumes either a 0% set-aside rate, or a
situation in which all set-aside land is cultivated with specific crops rather than left idled.
Since the relative costs for cultivated land are lower than for idled land, this scenario leads
to a relatively low cost of GAEC standard estimate. Together these scenarios provide an
idea of the upper and lower bounds of GAEC costs for cereals.
     Table 9.8 provides a brief overview of the results, with GAEC-1 the cost increase
associated with a 10% set-aside rate being in place, whereas GAEC-2 accounts for the case
where the set-aside rate is set to zero. As the relative cost per hectare of land cropped with
cereals is much lower than that of set-aside or idled land, the cost increase for GAEC-2 is
much (almost 50%) lower than in GAEC-1. For France the buffer strips, which were
160     Good agricultural and environmental conditions in the EU

previously „included‟ in the set-aside requirement, in GAEC-2 become an explicit
constraint, precluding the French effective set-aside to go to zero, although the formal rate
would allow for this.


Table 9.7 GAEC requirements (French example)

1. Soil erosion: protect soil through             Set-aside of farmland (3%) = buffer strips
appropriate measures                              Obligation of land maintenance (set-aside land, grassland,
                                                  non-productive land)
2. Soil organic matter: maintain soil             Ban on burning straw
organic matter levels through appropriate         Diversity of cropping pattern
practices
3. Soil structure: maintain soil structure        Regulation of irrigation
through appropriate measures                      Diversity of cropping pattern

The final sectoral (additional) costs estimates as presented in Table 9.8 are dependent on
the best estimates of compliance (the upper row of the table). These estimated compliance
rates take into account the extent to which GAEC requirements reflect pre-existing national
legislation (see Chapter 2 for more details).
     Table 9.8 draws from FADN data taking into account several farm types (e.g. general
field cropping, mixed crops, etc.). Differences in cost increases partly depend on farm
structure. At sectoral level, costs increase by less than 1%. However, at farm level costs
might increase by up to 4%, depending on the degree of compliance.

In Canada, management practices similar to the GAEC standards in the EU are implemented
through various cost share programmes. These agricultural practices are known as
Beneficial Management Practices (BMPs). Agriculture and Agri-Food Canada (AAFC)
(2006) defines BMPs as „farm management practices that: minimize and mitigate impacts
                                 Good agricultural and environmental conditions in the EU   161

and risks to the environment, by maintaining or improving the quality of soil, water, air and
biodiversity; ensure the long term health and sustainability of natural resources used for
agricultural production; and, support the long-term economic and environmental viability of
the agriculture industry‟.
     In order to help agricultural producers develop and implement BMPs, the Government
of Canada promoted provincially delivered Environmental Farm Plan (EFP) programmes.
Such programmes support the agricultural sector to better identify its impacts on the
environment; and promote the growth of stewardship activities within the agriculture
industry. As part of the programme, Canadian farmers attend an Environmental Farm Plan
workshop and complete a workbook designed to assess the current state of the farm and
identify areas of concern. Then farmers develop an action plan for addressing the areas of
concern. The action plan is confidentially reviewed by a group of locally appointed farmers.
Once the Peer Review Committee approves the Action Plan, a farmer can participate in the
EFP Cost-Share Program that helps cover a portion of the costs of implementing eligible
projects from the action plan (OMAFRA, 2006). Producers are eligible to apply for cost-
share incentives through the Canada Farm Stewardship Program, Green-Cover Canada, and
the Canada Water Supply Expansion Program.
     There are 36 Best Management Practice categories, each containing several practices
eligible for funding. Federal government covers up to 60% of the cost of implementing
eligible practices. Many practices covered through federal cost share programmes are also
eligible for funding under different provincial cost share programmes. As a result up to 90%
of the total project cost can be covered by combining federal and provincial funds.
However, the coverage varies depending on farmer eligibility, province and type of BMP.
In Ontario, funding is available through the Nutrient Management Financial Assistance
Program, Wetland Farm Stewardship Incentive Program, Oak Ridges Moraine
Environmental Enhancement Program and Greenbelt Farm Stewardship Program.
     Although implementation of BMPs is not mandatory, there has been a relatively high
degree of participation. For example, between 2005 and 2007, more than 11,000 of 57,211
Ontario farmers implemented or were in the process of implementation of BMPs. Even
though their implementation is partly subsidized by the federal and provincial governments,
there is a price to the farmers as well. As an example, Ontario farmers bore about a third of
the cost of implementation of the management practices eligible for funding. Net costs for
participating farmers could amount to about €1000 per farm. Because participation is
voluntary, it is assumed that farmers will only participate if the net costs they have to make
are offset by gains achieved elsewhere in the farm operation. For this reason the estimated
percentage cost increase is assumed to be zero.

Standards and competitiveness

For the GAECs, only scenarios where the EU imposes standards are evaluated. It was
estimated that the net implications on costs are effectively zero, or very close to zero.
     The simulated trade impacts for both GAEC scenarios are presented in Table 9.9
below. For both scenarios 1 and 2 it is assumed that compliance with the regulation
improves from the level as estimated to prevail early 2005 to full compliance. For the
GAEC 1 scenario EU exports decline by about 2% whereas imports increase by a similar
percentage. In terms of volume (assuming the EU wheat exports are about 16 million tons)
this implies a reduction in EU exports of about 300 thousand tons for scenario 1.
162     Good agricultural and environmental conditions in the EU

     For scenario GAEC-2, which has no set-aside obligation, EU exports decline and
import increase are respectively about 30% and 40% lower as compared to scenario 1. The
impact on total world cereals trade is negligible. Scenario 3 is similar to scenario 2, but
presents the impacts, taking into account that in all other sectors (dairy, beef, pigs and
poultry) considered, simultaneously the analysed selected standards are fully complied with.
As can be seen this only marginally changes results, suggesting a very limited impact of
spill-overs and feedback effects from other sectors.


Table 9.9 Percentage change in trade due to compliance with GAECs

              Scenario                EU      EU           Exports of which to…         Total
                                    Import   Export              South        Rest of   world
                                                      Japan      Korea        OECD      trade
1     GAEC-1 (10% set-aside)         2.2      -1.8     -1.3        -1.3        -1.4      0.02
2     GAEC-2 (0% set-aside)          1.3      -1.1     -0.7        -0.7        -0.8      0.01

The US, Argentina and Australia fill the gap caused by the reduction in net exports from the
EU. The impact of the evaluated GAEC constraints on cereal prices is negligible. As such
other „shocks‟ to the sector (like for example the price increases caused by the new demands
for biofuels) easily outpace and dominate the impact of the GAEC standard, irrespective of
whether one uses conservative or progressive estimates of the standard impacts.

Conclusion

Cereals are a key crop for the EU arable sector as is reflected in its share in the total
utilizable agricultural area of specialized arable farms, which for most Member States is
over 50%. The EU is a net exporter of cereals (in particular France is important as a net
exporting country). Its key competitors in the world market include the US and Argentina.
     Environmental and biodiversity standards affect EU cereal production mainly via the
good agricultural and environmental practices (GAEC) requirements. Concluding, it can be
noted that the increase in production costs of cereals associated with the GAECs remain less
than 1%. Several factors explain this result. First, the additional costs per hectare are
generally low, with an exception being the costs associated with keeping idled land in good
agricultural condition. Second, the best estimates of the current degree of compliance are
rather high. Farmers have already included a number of GAEC requirements into their
existing farming practices. Farmers benefit from its implementation since they reduce the
risk of soil erosion and maintain soil condition.
     Farmers may participate voluntarily in programmes reducing soil erosion and are
prepared to accept some costs. This is also confirmed in the case of Canada. Rather than
following a command-and-control approach a voluntary approach might be effective, in
particular when government is prepared to provide education and training, technical
assistance and cost-offsets. Since in Canada participation is voluntary farmers are not likely
to be faced with net cost increases affecting their competitive position.
                                   Good agricultural and environmental conditions in the EU     163

     The use of set-aside land and/or the rate of set-aside will affect the estimated
percentage cost increases (see differences between GAEC-1 and GAEC-2 scenarios): using
more set-aside land for cultivating special crops (e.g. bioenergy production) or having lower
set-aside rates (e.g. due to low buffer zone-requirements) lead to lower costs. With the set-
aside rate currently set to zero, the calculated percentage cost increases more or less halved
as compared to a 10% set-aside scenario (GAEC-1). The 3% buffer strip requirement in
France, whereas previously accounted for within the 10% set-aside requirement, can be
interpreted as an 3% minimum effective set-aside requirement (which holds even when the
formal rate goes down to zero).
     The impact of the GAECs on the EU‟s external competitiveness varied from a 1.8%
reduction in exports in GAEC-1 (set-aside rate 10%) to a reduction of 1.1% (set-aside rate
0%). EU imports increase with approximately a similar percentage as exports decline. Total
world trade is hardly affected by the impact of the GAEC standard. (However, trade is
likely to be affected by changes in the set-aside policy (changes in land base) that are not
separately evaluated).

Chapter 10

Trade effects of environmental and animal
welfare standards in the hog sectors of the EU,
US and Canada

Kees de Roest, Jonathan Winsten, Predreg Rajsic and Koen
Dillen



Introduction

The hog sector is one of the most intensive livestock production activities in developed
country agricultures. Compared to the number of hogs they raise, these farms often have a
limited land area. Typically hog farms have to cope with severe manure disposal problems,
in particular when this activity is concentrated in certain parts of a country. In order to
reduce water and air pollution caused by hog farms, the EU, the US and Canada have issued
environmental regulations. The type and strength of constraints imposed on the hog farms in
these three countries depend on the severity of the environmental problems they cause. It is
therefore of interest to analyse the differences in farm environmental compliance costs
attributable to these regulations and to investigate the effects these cost impacts may have
on trade in swine meat between the EU, the US and Canada and on the world market in
general.
     This chapter focuses on regulations related to reducing water pollution from excess
nitrogen. In the EU, these regulations are embodied in the Nitrates Directive. In the US, the
farm cost impact of the Clean Water Act will be assessed and for Canada an analysis will be
presented of the economic consequences for hog farms of the Nutrient Management Acts of
Ontario and Manitoba. The compliance costs will be assessed by using typical hog farms.
     A separate analysis will be dedicated to the animal welfare regulations in the EU, which
for hog farmers may potentially have a cost impact. Again the typical farm approach has
been followed for calculating the extra costs inherent to comply with the specific obligation
to raise sows in groups.
     The enforcement of environmental and animal welfare regulations may have
implications for the competitiveness of the swine sector, especially when the constraints in
one country are much stronger than in another. The calculation of the farm compliance costs
will be therefore followed by an analysis dedicated to the possible trade effects of the
environmental compliance for the hog sectors in the EU, the US and Canada, which will be
carried out using the GTAP model as has been described in Chapter 1. The simulation will


©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         165
166     Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors

be presented both under the hypothesis of unilateral implementation in the EU as well as
multilateral enforcement of the environmental regulations in all three commercial blocs.

Standards for nitrate pollution control and its cost implications

The main requirement established by the Nitrates Directive is to limit nitrogen application
from manure to 170 kg N/ha per year (or 250 kg N/ha of manure on grasslands in Germany
and the Netherlands), which may generate extra costs for manure disposal. In Nitrate
Vulnerable Zones (NVZ) farms exceeding the maximum limit have to find extra land to
spread excess manure, either by buying or renting land or paying a licence to spread manure
on land of nearby farmers. Moreover, they are obliged to invest in manure storage facilities
due to the prohibition on spreading manure in winter time.
     An evaluation of the impact of compliance with the Nitrates Directive requires an
analysis of the structure of swine farming systems. Such information is essential to identify
the share of total number of enterprises as well as the share of the number of animals
affected by the directive. These figure differ significantly among European countries. In
order to assess how many pig farms potentially are affected by the Nitrates Directive the
following stepwise procedure has been followed:

     Estimate the number of farms which have a stocking rate per hectare exceeding the
      limit of 170 kg N per hectare for the main producing countries in the EU.
     Estimate the number of pigs raised within Nitrate Vulnerable Zones.

Then, a literature review was used to establish the cost increase related to compliance with
the Nitrates Directive for a typical hog farm in each of the selected countries. An analysis of
the degree of compliance has allowed the estimation of the percentage cost increase at the
sector level.

Affected pig production

In order to estimate the number of pig farms which are potentially affected by the Nitrates
Directive the most important Member States in pigmeat production have been chosen:
Germany, Denmark, Belgium, Spain, France, Italy, Netherlands and the UK. These eight
countries represent 91% of the pig population in the EU-15 (see Table 10.1).
     For the first step of the analysis, single country data were obtained from the Farm
Structure Survey (FSS). These data were used to calculate the share of pig population raised
in farms whose stocking rate is higher than 17 pigs per hectare, corresponding to the limit of
170 kg N/ha that is established for the NVZ. The assumption that the whole country is
defined as NVZ is valid only for Germany, the Netherlands and Denmark while in the other
five Member States only part of the territory has been declared as vulnerable. Therefore, in
the second step the number of pigs raised in the NVZs of these countries was estimated by
overlaying the NVZ map over the NUTS 3 regions using the REGIO databank of
EUROSTAT. The final results of this analysis are reported in Table 10.1.
     In the Netherlands and Belgium, the majority of hogs are raised in NVZs and on farms
exceeding the 170 kg/ha per year N limit. Although Denmark is designated completely as
vulnerable, only 11% of pigs are raised on farms with a stocking rate above the limits
indicated, owing to the spatial distribution of pig farms and to the strict application of the
             Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors         167

Nitrates Directive in that country. Denmark has required pig farms to own or rent the land
on which the slurry is to be spread. Spain also has a low share of pigs affected, but this is
due to the scattered pattern of NVZs.

Impact of improved compliance with the Nitrates Directive on EU pig farms’ costs

Compliance costs for hog farms have been reported in the literature for several countries
which are subject to the Nitrates Directive. A literature review identified compliance costs
for the Nitrates Directive for each of the eight countries (Christiansen, 2007; Gourmelen
and Rieu, 2006; De Roest et al., 2007; De Hoop et al., 2004; Penlington, 2007). The
percentage increase per kg pigmeat has been estimated aggregating these cost estimates to
the pig production cost calculations carried out by Interpig, a network of European research
institutes coordinated by the Meat and Livestock Commission and the British Pig Executive
(see also Table 10.2).
     The estimated cost increases represent future additional costs for those pig farms which
do not yet comply with the Nitrates Directive. For farms already in compliance, these costs
have already been incurred. The degree of compliance among the Member States differs
significantly. In Italy, where only recently an acceleration of the implementation is taking
place the degree of compliance can be estimated in only 20%. In contrast, in Denmark
where a rather strict application has been imposed on livestock farmers for some time, the
degree of compliance is very high. According to the assessments carried out in the country
reports, other countries reach degrees of compliance ranging between these two extreme
values.
     In order to obtain a percentage cost increase at sector level for each country, two
crucial variables have been considered:

    The degree of compliance with the Nitrates Directive.
    The share of affected pigs as a percentage of the total number of pigs in the country.
168     Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors

It turns out that Italy, the Netherlands and Belgium will still have to face a cost increase
which varies from 1.1% to 2.5% (Table 10.3). Other countries show limited cost increases,
either because of a limited number of affected pigs (Denmark), or because of a relatively
small number of pigs present in NVZs (Spain) or due to a high degree of compliance
(United Kingdom).

Assuming no cost increase for the remaining Member States, the EU pig sector as a whole
faces an additional compliance cost of 0.55% in order to reach full compliance with the
Nitrates Directive. If we would assume that all farms having a pig density of more than 170
kg N/ha still have to adjust to the Nitrates Directive, i.e. the compliance rate is zero, then
the total extra compliance cost would imply a 4.8% increase in pig production costs in the
EU.
            Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors   169

Compliance costs for US hog farms under the Clean Water Act

Under US Clean Water Act, which has been described in detail in Chapter 8, hog farmers
are required to obtain a permit under the National Pollution Discharge Elimination System
(NPDES). This involves the implementation of a comprehensive nutrient management plan
(CNMP). The CNMP must be focused on the control of nitrogen (N) or phosphorus (P),
based on state or local priorities. The implementation of CNMPs often results in operations
seeking additional land on which to spread manure and/or the use of alternative nutrient
control strategies for manure. It is important to note that the CAFO regulations currently
affect a small percentage of livestock operations, but potentially a large part of the US pig
herd. The US Department of Agriculture (USDA) inventory for 2006 shows that farms with
more than 2,000 head are only 7.8% of all pig operations in the US, but account for 80% of
the total pig population.
     In the US, 90% of the pig herds are raised on farms having more than 1,000 pigs, and
54% are raised on operations with more than 5,000 head. The greatest concentration of pigs
exists in Iowa, Minnesota and North Carolina.
     The present analysis has estimated the costs of compliance with the Clean Water Act
(CWA) for pig farms typical of Iowa. This state was chosen because it is the leading hog
producing state in the US. The average hog operation size in this state is approximately
4,300 head, well above the threshold size to be considered a Concentrated Animal Feeding
Operation (CAFO) according to the Clean Water Act.
     The USDA has estimated the costs for implementing a successful CNMP. The CNMP
costs associated with swine operations are an average cost of all swine farms in the US and
are presented per animal unit (AU), which is equivalent to 454 kg of live weight.
     The national estimates for the CNMP were adjusted from a per AU basis (US$44) to a
per 45.4 kg of gain basis to be consistent with the available financial data for pig farms (see
Table 10.4). Using this national average cost per AU of US$44, the average cost per 45.4
kg of gain is estimated to be US$1.65. This is calculated by multiplying the average weight
gain per pig of 90.9 kg by 6.67 pigs by 2 turns per year, which results in a weight gain per
pig of 1,210 kg of gain per AU. The final result is that the cost of compliance translates into
a 3.54% increase in total production costs for farms affected by the CWA. As 54% of the
US pig population is raised on farms with more than 5,000 pigs and the degree of non-
compliance equals 56.4% the total sector cost increase for pigs in the USA to be attributed
to the CWA is 1.08%. If we compare this to the additional cost increase of full compliance
with the Nitrates Directive in the EU which has been calculated as 0.55%, we notice that the
CWA obligations in the US have a stronger economic impact on the pig sector, in particular
due to the lower rate of compliance with the CWA. If we compare the EU and the US using
a zero compliance rate in both countries the cost increase in the EU would be 4.8% as
against 3.54% in the US. The higher total compliance costs in the EU are due to the higher
share of farms which has to invest in order to comply with the Nitrates Directive as
compared to US pig farms affected by the CWA.

Nutrient management regulations in Canada: Cases of Ontario and Manitoba

While all Canadian provinces have legislation related to management of livestock
production systems, it is beyond the scope of this chapter to examine each province‟s
legislation in detail. According to the 2006 Census of Agriculture, Ontario and Manitoba
170     Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors

produce about half of the Canadian pork output, each contributing roughly equal shares.
Ontario farms are representative of the eastern Canada hog production systems, found also
in Quebec and to a lesser extent in the Maritimes. These are farms with smaller land
holdings and higher input intensity compared to the western Canada farms, found in
Manitoba, Alberta and Saskatchewan. Consequently, estimates of environmental
compliance costs in Ontario and Manitoba would likely be representative at the national
level as well. The next two sections assess the nutrient management regulations in the two
provinces and the effect on costs at the farm and industry level.

Ontario Nutrient Management Act (2009) sets standards for different aspects of nutrient
management: land application standards, siting and construction standards, requirements for
farm preparations of farm level nutrient management plans and strategies. It uses a
combined nitrogen-phosphorus limitation (total of 224 kg/ha per year for nitrogen and not
more than 78 kg/ha per year in excess of phosphorus balance). The main targets of the
regulation are the existing farms that generate more than 300 Livestock Units (LU),
operations expanding to and beyond 300 LU and new operations that generate 150 LU or
more.
     As Salvano et al. (2006) elaborated, the new nutrient regulations in Manitoba, based on
phosphorus soil test require farms with soils that contain more than 120 parts per million
(ppm) of phosphorus to apply manure in the amount that does not exceed crop phosphorus
removal capacity. Farms whose soils contain between 60 and 120 ppm of phosphorus can
apply up to 2 times the crop phosphorus removal rate, while those with soil test indicating
less than 60 ppm of phosphorus present in the soil can apply manure based on the crop
nitrogen requirement. In addition, new regulations include stipulations regarding
establishment of buffer strips, prohibition of winter application, incorporation of fall applied
manure, and building new storage on land adjacent to watercourses and in Red River
Valley, termed as Special Management Areas (SMAs).
            Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors   171

Affected pig production

Western Canada: Manitoba

Manitoba‟s nutrient regulations are based on soil phosphorus content. Farms in which soil
phosphorus content exceeds 120 ppm or where phosphorus is being accumulated are subject
to the new phosphorus-based regulations. In order to get a measure of how much of the
provincial swine production would be affected, we used the 2006 Census of Agriculture
data to calculate the agricultural land nutrient balance for each township in Manitoba by
subtracting nutrient removal by crops and pasture from nutrient input from manure. The
underlying assumption is that the soil in townships with currently positive phosphorus
balance is either accumulating phosphorus or is already saturated. Thus, farms in these
townships would be subject to the new regulation.
     The calculated nutrient removal varied across townships, depending on the ratio of
cropland to pasture. The cropland nutrient removal coefficient was calculated using the
Potash and Phosphate Institute (2001) estimates for North American wheat, barley, canola
and peas. The typical crop mix for the province was 40% wheat, 20% barley, 30% canola
and 10% peas. The pasture removal coefficient was 5 kg of phosphorus per hectare, based
on Hattey et al. (2003). Phosphorus output is a product of the township-level number of
pigs and the respective coefficients for phosphorus content of swine manure, found in
Moore and Gamroth (1993). The phosphorus output in the townships with a positive
phosphorus balance was much higher (2 to 4 times) than the provincial average. The
calculated phosphorus balance was positive in 14 townships. There were 336 swine farms in
the 14 townships, accounting for 28% of all swine farms in the province and 58% of
Manitoba‟s hog inventory. Thus, more than half of the provincial production could be
affected by the new phosphorus-based nutrient regulations.

Eastern Canada: Ontario

The main targets of the Ontario nutrient management regulations are the existing farms that
generate more than 300 LU and any expanding operations. In order to produce an estimate
of the share of the provincial swine production affected by the Ontario Nutrient
Management Act, we used the 2006 Census of Agriculture data to calculate the average per
farm number of swine livestock units for all townships in Ontario. The livestock units for
different types of swine animals units were aggregated using coefficients found in the
Minnesota Department of Agriculture Animal Unit Calculation Worksheet. Sows and gilts
for breeding were assigned 0.3 LU per animal, boars 0.4 LU, nursing and weaner pigs 0.05
LU, and 0.3 LU per animal were assigned to grower and finishing pigs.
     There were 950 farms located in townships for which the average hog farm size
exceeded 300 LU. Even though these farms account for about 23% of all Ontario swine
farms, they house almost half of Ontario‟s pig inventory (47%). It should be noted that this
estimate does not include the expanding operations with less than 300 LU, which are also
subject to the nutrient management regulations. This indicates that any cost effect resulting
from the Nutrient Management Act would affect at least 50% of the province‟s pork supply.
172     Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors

Economic cost of nutrient regulations

Oginskyy (2005) estimated the opportunity cost of the 2002 Ontario Nutrient Management
Act under different swine dietary options using a mathematical programming model of a
typical swine farrow-to-finish operation in western Ontario - a mixed hog-crop production-
type system, with hogs as the main market product and crops used as feed or for sale
depending on market conditions. Herd and land size parameters were varied in sensitivity
analysis. The estimated monetary opportunity cost of compliance, if expressed as a
percentage of total production cost, ranged from 0 to 8.1% of total costs for year 2007,
depending on the selected level of nutrient standard, pig diet and farm size. As expected,
less stringent nutrient level standards involve lower compliance costs. Similarly, adding
lysine and phytase to the pig diet reduces compliance costs.
     Salvano et al. (2006) estimated the economic effect of the new phosphorus-based
regulations on swine operations in two most swine-intensive townships in Manitoba: La
Broquerie and Hanover. The baseline model assumed either having enough owned land to
comply with the nitrogen-based manure application standard or being able to lease from
neighbours. For the farms with sufficient land area, the additional costs were associated
with spreading manure over a larger area. Those operations with insufficient land would
have an option to transport manure to another farm or to reduce manure nutrient content by
chemical treatment. The change in monetary costs between nitrogen-based and phosphorus-
based scenarios were estimated. The estimated change in costs corresponded to a range
from 0.04% to 4.94% of total production cost for 2007, depending on land area available,
manure output and compliance strategy.
     Table 10.5 summarizes the cost structure of Ontario and Manitoba swine production in
Canadian Dollars and Euros, using 1.5 Canadian Dollars per Euro exchange rate. The Table
also shows nutrient management compliance costs as a percentage of total costs.

    The average compliance costs were estimated at $0.063 and $0.035 per kg of live
weight sold in Ontario and Manitoba respectively. This corresponds to about 4.1% of total
production costs in Ontario and 2.5% in Manitoba. Assuming each province is
representative of nutrient management compliance costs for its respective region, Ontario
for eastern and Manitoba for western Canada, the national farm-level compliance costs
would be somewhere in the range of 2.5 to 4.1% of total production costs.

Animal welfare: evaluated standards and cost implications

Increasingly the protection of animal welfare has become a relevant issue and hog farms in
the EU have to adjust their management plans and housing systems to the new requirements
imposed by EU regulations. Actually, the welfare of hogs in the EU is ruled by the
directives 91/630/EEC, 2001/88/EC and 2001/93/EC. The main aspects of these directives
provide for a:

    Prohibition of tying sows and gilts.
    Prohibition of slatted floors for sows and gilts.
    Prohibition of isolation of sows during the period between 4 weeks after insemination
     and the week before farrowing with a minimum space allowance of 2.25 per m2 for
     sows and 1.64 m2 for gilts.
    Maximum stocking rates for different pig categories.
    Minimum standards for slatted floors.

Of all above mentioned measures, the most important pending cost increase likely arises
from the requirement for group housing of sows, since the other measures already have a
high rate of compliance in the EU (Enting et al., 2006). Therefore, the following analysis
focuses on the cost increase per kg of pig meat associated with the switch from individual
crates for sows to group housing, taking also into account the minimum space per head
required by the EU regulation.

Impact of improved compliance with animal welfare on EU pig farm costs

Two practical examples illustrate the consequences for a closed cycle pig farm, where
breeding sows and gilts are raised together with fattening pigs. No piglets are purchased
174     Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors

from other pig farms as they are produced within the closed cycle farm. The examples
include:

     a comparison between the old conventional housing system with sows housed in
      individual crates with an investment in a new pig farm where pigs are housed in groups
      4 weeks after insemination; and
     an adjustment of an existing pig farm to the new housing requirements for sows.

The new requirements would cause an increase in investment in the two pregnancy phases
of the sows of 12% per sow place, but as these two phases represents only 10.8% of the
total investment of a new closed cycle pig farm the total investment cost increases by only
1.3%. Using an amortization rate of 3% on buildings and 12% on equipment, the expected
increase in total production costs (per kg pig meat) is limited to 0.1%.

An adjustment of an existing pig farm to the new requirements has a more significant impact
on the economy of the farm, as the end solution is not as optimal compared to a new

     Although the cost increase is higher for the adjustment of an existing farm, the cost

directives do not appear to have a significant impact on production costs. It should be stated
however that the farms that have to adjust to group housing do face a financial problem
related to the search for resources to finance the investment, which for small farms can be a
significant burden. Considering the pig production cost calculations carried out by Interpig,
and the different degrees of compliance among the main EU producer countries, the
adjustment of existing individual crate pig farms to pig farms with group housed sows
would generate a 0.11% increase for the EU pig sector (Table 10.10).

176     Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors

Standards and external competitiveness
Expected impact of the full compliance with animal welfare rules and the Nitrates Directive
on pigmeat trade flows

Increasing costs induced by a full Nitrates Directive compliance in the EU, certainly may
affect international trade flows in pig meat and the market shares held by the main exporter
countries. These effects have been simulated with the GTAP model. The standard GTAP 6
database does not differentiate between pig and poultry meat. As this is essential in the
competitiveness analysis, the differentiation is introduced through the Splitcom application.
This application allows dividing existing GTAP sectors, i.e.: meat products, into new
sectors based on secondary production and trade data. Based on FAOSTAT data we used
Splitcom to introduce the commodity „pig meat‟ in the GTAP model.
     Full compliance with the Nitrates Directive, assuming no compliance costs in either the
US or Canada, leads to a 3% decrease of both market share and exported volumes of the
EU. The effect on the level of production could cause a general increase of the EU imports
of pigmeat by 4.4% higher demand for imported pigmeat (Table 10.11).

In the Japanese market, which is the EU‟s largest export market, the loss of about 1.15% in
the quantities traded by EU would stimulate an increase of the demand for pigmeat coming
from Brazil, the US, Canada and other countries at a rate of 0.27% each. In the other OECD
countries the decrease in EU exports has been estimated as 3.8%, which favours the
competitive position of Brazil, Canada and the US by about 1% each. Brazil‟s export share
gain would be higher than those of Canada and the US. The gains of the three top exporters
range from a maximum of 1% in the case of Brazil to a minimum of 0.3% for Canada.
     Animal welfare regulations should affect EU competitiveness to a lesser extent since its
effect on pig farm production cost has been estimated to be limited to only a 0.1% increase.
This would entail a 0.8% growth of EU imports and a decrease of exports of 0.7% (Table
10.12).
             Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors           177

     Summing up the effects (see Table 10.13) of both standards‟ requirements would result
in an overall decrease of EU exports by 3.7%. The loss in quantity on the Japanese market
would be equal to 1.5% while exports towards other OECD would decrease by 4.5%. As far
as imports are concerned, a 5.24% growth in imports is expected in large part due to the
higher imports from Brazil (+5.2%). Brazil would gain 1.2% of its global market share,
while a 0.4 and 0.8% increase in exports on the global market is to be expected for Canada
and the US.

Expected impact of the full compliance with CWA requirements in the US

Full implementation of the Clear Water Act requirements for the US pig sector would
produce higher impacts than those expected for the EU Nitrates Directive. This is due firstly
to the high share of the US pig herd raised in farms that are eligible to be classified as
178     Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors

CAFOs and secondly, because of the relatively recent implementation of the CWA, which
explains a compliance rate close to zero.
    The high costs of full compliance with CWA regulations by most intensive pig farms,
would affect the competitiveness of the US on the pig world market entailing a 7.3%
decrease in US pig meat exports. This gap left will be covered by the EU (+1.85%) and
Brazil (+1.18%), which would both gain market share, in particular on the Japanese market.
Higher gains in market share are foreseen in the case of Canada, whose exports would be
stimulated by the increasing demand coming from USA (Table 10.14).

Conclusions
As the pig sector is one of the most intensive livestock production activities in the EU it
would be expected that it faces significant impacts from the Nitrates Directive. The extent to
which the Nitrates Directive may create extra costs to the pig sector depends on the pig
density per hectare in each Member State, on the percentage of pigs present in NVZs and on
the degree of compliance of pig farmers to the Nitrates Directive. These three data differ
very much from country to country and explain primarily the very different sector cost
increases for the pig sectors of EU Member States. The overall EU additional cost increase
to be attributed to the pig sector due to full compliance with the Nitrates Directive has been
estimated at 0.55%. If we assume however a zero compliance rate the total compliance costs
of the EU with the Nitrates Directive would be 4.8%.
     Such a cost increase has a significant impact on the EU trade balance of pigmeat. Total
EU exports will decline by 3% and imports will increase by 4.4%. This decline in EU
competitiveness will favour the exports of Brazil (+1%), the US (+0.7%) and Canada
(+0.3%) on the world market. EU imports will increase in particular from Brazil (+4.3%),
Canada (+4.1%) and the US (4.1%).
     From a comparison with the impact of the CWA in the US it turns out that this act
raises the cost for the American pig sector by 1.08%, almost twice the comparable cost
effect that we attribute to the Nitrates Directive in the EU. The reason for this substantial
rise of costs has to be attributed to the large percentage of pigs affected by this measure and
             Trade effects of environmental and animal welfare standards in the hog sectors     179

its rather recent application to US pig farms, which still implies a rather low degree of
compliance. This rise in costs will cause a reduction in US exports of pigmeat of 7.3% and a
decline of exports of 4.5%. Canada would gain the most from this situation increasing its
exports by 4% on the world market, of which 4.5% more to the US and a 2.1% increase on
the Japanese market.
     Our calculation of competitiveness effects of animal welfare regulations for pig farmers
in the EU shows that the cost increase is very limited. The reasons for this minor cost
impact are an already high degree of compliance with the standards and the limited rise in
costs for farmers which still have to adapt their farm to the new legislation. At farm level
the cost increase is well below 1% and this generates a rise of costs at sector level of 0.11%.
This cost impact evidently causes a growth of imports of only 0.8% and a decline of exports
of 0.7%.
     As has been expected the Nitrates Directive for the pig farms creates the most
substantial burden of costs, in particular in EU Member States with a high pig density and a
low degree of compliance.

Chapter 11

Environmental standards in the fruits and
vegetables sector of Spain1

Consuelo Varela-Ortega and Paloma Esteve



Introduction
Spain is one of the main producers and exporters of fruits and vegetables in the EU. This
chapter gives a general overview of Spain‟s fruit and vegetable sector and examines the
impacts of environmental standards, including Good Agricultural and Environmental
Conditions (GAECs) on the competitiveness of the fruits and vegetables sector of Spain.
The application of the Single Farm Payment system as well as cross compliance regulations
is uneven across industries in the fruit and vegetable sector. However, the last reform of the
Common Market Organization for Fruits and Vegetables (CMO F&V) and the 2003 CAP
reform (the CAP Health Check) are expected to lead to substantial changes in this sector.
     The chapter is organized into five sections. Following the introduction, the second
section describes production and trade for the fruit and vegetable sector. The third section
describes the EU policy framework for fruits and vegetables. The effects of the 2003 reform
of the Common Agricultural Policy (CAP) on land use and irrigated crops in Spain are used
to illustrate the implications of this policy framework. The fourth section presents case
studies of selected crops, regions and the effects of specific policy measures on
competitiveness. The last section of the chapter contains a summary of the main
conclusions.

The global dimension of fruits and vegetables

International trade in fresh fruits and vegetables is complex and dynamic. World production
of fruit and vegetables in 2008 was over 1,560 million metric tons (FAOSTAT, 2011a) in
which fruits represent around 37% and vegetables around 63%. In aggregated terms, Asia is
the leading production region with a share of 66%, followed by Africa (9%), the EU (8%),
North and Central America (7%) and South America (6%) (Table 11.1).

In the EU, fruits and vegetables account for about a sixth of the value of EU agricultural
production, making the EU the third largest producer in the world. It is also the largest
importer. Consumption of fresh fruit and vegetables in the EU is generally stable with 43
million metric tons and 46 million metric tons (respectively) consumed within the EU. Total
vegetable production in the EU-27 was about 64 million metric tons in 2008. The leading
vegetable producing Member States are Italy and Spain (with 13.7 and 12.67 million metric
tons respectively), followed by Poland (5.4 million metric tons) and France (with 5 million
metric tons). Fresh fruit production in the EU in 2008 was 61 million metric tons. Italy was
the leading Member State (17.6 million metric tons), followed by Spain (16.2 million metric
tons) and France (8.5 million metric tons).
     Tomatoes are the leading vegetable product in the EU, in terms of volume, with a total
16.4 million metric tons produced in 2008. Of this volume, almost 6 million metric tons are
produced in Italy, 4 million metric tons in Spain, 1.3 million metric tons in Greece and 1.1
million metric tons in Portugal. Other important vegetables crops in 2008 in the EU were
cabbages (5.5 million tonnes), dry onions (5.5 million metric tons), carrots (5.3 million
metric tons) and lettuce (3.3 million metric tons). Regarding fruits, with the exception of
grapes, apples is the most important fruit product in the EU (in terms of production), with a
total of over 12 million metric tons produced in 2008. Apple production takes place
primarily in Poland (2.8 million metric tons), Italy (2.2 million metric tons), France (1.9
million metric tons) and Germany (1 million metric tons) (FAOSTAT, 2011a). Total citrus
fruit production in the EU was 11.3 million metric tons in 2008. Oranges accounted for 6.6
million metric tons and small citrus fruits (tangerines, mandarins and clementines) for 3.2
million metric tons. Spain is the main producer of citrus fruit with 6.4 million metric tons in
2008 (FAOSTAT, 2011a), followed by Italy (3.5 million metric tons) and Greece (1 million
                         Environmental standards in the fruits and vegetables sector of Spain   183

metric tons). Production of peaches and nectarines was 4 million metric tons and production
of pears was 2.4 million metric tons.
     The Netherlands, the US and Spain are the three leading fruit and vegetable exporters
in the world, by revenue, with a total value around US$15.7, 15.6 and 15.3 billion
respectively in 2008 (see Figure 11.1), which accounts for 10% of global exports, followed
by China with an export value of fruits and vegetables of US$12.6 billion. China is the
largest fruit and vegetable producer in the world (Table 11.1). Developing countries such as
China, India, Indonesia and Turkey are important potential competitors for the EU.
     The EU is the world‟s largest importer of fruits and vegetables, when the region is
considered in aggregate (FAOSTAT, 2011b). Together, the EU and the US have a share of
64% of world fruit and vegetable imports. The world‟s largest importing country in 2008
was Germany. The US, the UK, France and the Netherlands, in that order, were the next
largest importing countries in the world. Latin American countries are the main suppliers of
fruits and vegetables for Europe, supplying 27.4% of EU imports. The Mercosur countries
(Argentina, Brazil, Paraguay and Uruguay) supply some 20% of EU imports. Important
products are Chilean apples and Argentinean lemons. Mediterranean countries supply
14.7% of the total fruit and vegetable imports to the EU (Turkey, 30.7% and Morocco
27.6%). The main products imported from Morocco are citrus and tomatoes which
constitute 70% of the total. Turkey supplies mainly citrus and dried fruits.

184    Environmental standards in the fruits and vegetables sector of Spain

     Fruits and vegetables constitute an important part of total agricultural production of
Spain. In terms of value of production, fruits and vegetables account for 56% of the value of
national agricultural production in 2008 (MARM, 2010). The largest products, in terms of
volume, are citrus accounting for more than 6.3 million metric tons, including oranges at 3.4
million metric tons, and tomatoes, with more than four million metric tons. Valencia is the
most important region for citrus production in Spain, accounting for 70% of total national
production. Castilla-La Mancha is another important region for fruit and vegetable
production. Water scarcity in the area is the limiting factor for expanding production of
vegetables and therefore most farms have to rely on mixed cropping patterns combining
vegetable production with less water intensive crops like winter cereals. Vineyard is the
main crop in terms of area and, very often, it is combined with vegetables as well.

Reform of the fruit and vegetables regime in the EU
The CMO F&V differs from CAP regulations for other agricultural products. Established in
1962, the F&V CMO, institutes a minimum price for growers. A system of Community aid
granted to certain citrus fruits was laid down in 1996. The CMO F&V was reformed in
2007 in the Council Regulation 1182/2007 (CEC 2007a, 2007b, 2008). This reform was
intended to bring the fruit and vegetable sector closer to the rest of the reformed CAP
framework. However, the new CMO continues to use Operation Programs (OPs) as the
main instrument to support Producers‟ Organizations (POs). Through the OPs the POs can
receive a grant of up to 4.6% of the total value of the marketed production. This grant is
50% co-financed by the POs (40% in case of organic production) and it should be invested
in the related OPs of each PO. The new scheme includes specific measures for the
prevention and management of production-related problems. The former crop harvest set-
aside was abolished, as well as the obligation to dedicate a minimum 10% of the program‟s
budget to environmental measures. The new regulation seeks to comply with the 2003 CAP
reform in several respects. All fruit and vegetable areas will be eligible under the Single
Farm Payment (SFP) scheme. Member States can consider exemptions to these entitlements
and determine the period of reference and the payment amount for each vegetable or fruit
product. A transitional stage was implemented to facilitate growers‟ adaptation to the new
scheme. With this reform, all agricultural area that had fruit and vegetables during the
reference period, including permanent crops and land under table potatoes, is eligible in all
27 Member States.
     The introduction of fruit and vegetable areas in the Single Payment Scheme means that
the cross compliance rules will apply to all fruit and vegetables producers receiving direct
payments, which are decoupled. Support payments for fruits and vegetables have been
increased for certain processed products (such as tomatoes, citrus, peaches, Williams and
Rocha pears, raisins, dried figs and prunes). The aid scheme is based on direct aid to
producers according to national thresholds. Penalties apply if processed volumes increase
beyond fixed limits. The new CMO abolishes this type of support aid which is substituted
by a consolidated national production „envelope‟ under the SFP scheme. Under this
modality, each member state can decide how to manage the SFP funds. These can be
distributed among the former producers of processed fruits and vegetables for an equivalent
payment of the amount they received under the previous regime or, alternatively,
redistribute the new payment among all fruit and vegetable producers.
                       Environmental standards in the fruits and vegetables sector of Spain   185

     The general CAP reform approach allows Member States more flexibility in their
application of CAP regulations. In Spain, the new fruit and vegetable regime has been
applied under the National Strategy for sustainable OPs (MARM, 2008) and the Royal
Decrees 262/2008 (BOE 2008a), 864/2008 (BOE, 2008b) and 1972/2008 (BOE 2008c).
These national statutes define a transitory period so that fruit and vegetable land was not
eligible for the SFP until December 31 2010. This limitation responds to the concerns
among producers on the effects of the decoupled aid, mainly for flexible crops that can be
consumed fresh or processed, like oranges and tomatoes. Moreover, the new possibility to
produce fruits and vegetables on areas eligible for the SFP has raised serious concerns in
relation to competitiveness. A three year transition period (2008, 2009 and 2010) was
established for processed tomatoes with a 50% partial decoupling scheme. Following this
transition period, a full decoupled scheme applies based on a reference period of years
2004/05, 2005/06 and 2006/07.
     For citrus production, support payments were area based (per ha) fully coupled to
production for a two-year transition period (2008 and 2009). Following this period, support
payments were fully decoupled and transformed into a SFP to all farms in operation by
September 30, 2006, regardless of the area dedicated to processed citrus crops. For all other
fruits and vegetables, full decoupling of aid is not subject to a transition period, establishing
a reference period of years 2004/05, 2005/06 and 2006/07, except for dried figs, peaches
and Williams and Rocha pears for which the reference period is 2003/04, 2005/06 and
2006/07.
     The main trade measure outlined in the reform proposal was the abolition of export
refunds. The rest of the provisions related to trade with third countries remain unchanged
until the outcome of the WTO negotiations, including entry prices, special safeguard clause
and the general safeguard clause (USDA, 2007). Entry prices in the CMO F&V account for
almost half of the total amber box subsidies declared by the EU in the WTO.

The impact of the CAP reform on land use

Historically, the CAP encouraged intensification of agricultural production across the EU.
This has been more apparent in the Spanish central regions and in the Mediterranean
Andalucía that grow significant volumes of CAP supported crops. Over the years, the CAP
has evolved to respond to the international trade agreements and to include environmental
requirements. Under the 2003 CAP reform, direct payments have been replaced by a Single
Farm Payment, almost fully decoupled from production in Spain and tied to the „cross
compliance‟ of specific environmental regulations (among others). The last CAP reform of
2010, the so-called CAP „Health check‟ (CEC 2009 a,b,c) includes stronger and simplified
cross compliance provisions for the protection of ecosystems, including the requirement to
respond to the new challenges of climate change and water management by developing
adaptation strategies in the agricultural systems. For the first time, the new CAP specifically
foresees cross compliance measures for protecting water courses and for achieving better
water management practices in irrigated agriculture and it gives a further impetus to the
integration of water policies and agricultural policies. The analysis that we have carried out
in section 4 to assess the cost of compliance of irrigated farms reflects this emerging policy
reality.
     The CAP has sought to promote a multifunctional environmentally-oriented sustainable
agriculture in the EU. Several authors have studied the potential of CAP policy instruments
186    Environmental standards in the fruits and vegetables sector of Spain

to attain the dual objective of agricultural production and water resources conservation in
irrigated agriculture (Brouwer, 2004; Varela-Ortega et al., 2002; Varela-Ortega, 2007;
Varela-Ortega et al., 2006).
     We have compared the distribution of irrigated crops in a pre-reform year (2004) and
post-reform year (2008) at the regional level. At the national level, the new decoupled SFP
induces a crop shift away from intensively irrigated crops, such as maize and legumes, in
favour of other low water-consumption crops, such as winter cereals. Maize no longer
benefits from the higher yield-based subsidies of the previous CAP programmes. Land
cropped for maize has diminished by 23% and there has been a 17% increase in winter
cereals area. These are low water demanding crops that have an equivalent subsidy under
the new CAP. There has also been a sharp increase in lands used for olive (also under CAP
subsidies) and vine (not a CAP crop) (38% and 26% respectively) production. The
expansion of these crops responds to the higher profits obtained in specialized farming well
adapted to modern-technology drip irrigation and market opportunities.
     The effects of CAP reform vary significantly across regions in Spain. CAP-dependent
crops are grown predominantly in the central inland areas and in Andalucía. Andalucía, in
the Mediterranean south, grows a variety of crops. The continental inland regions, with
more limited cropping potential, grow predominantly continental CAP supported crops. In
all regions, the maize cropping land has fallen in favour of winter cereals. However, in
Andalucía, the larger than national average reduction in maize area (46%) has coincided
with a sharp increase in intensive olive production (35%).

Costs of compliance with good agricultural and environmental
conditions
The GTAP model used elsewhere in this book does not include the fruit and vegetable
sector, so a case study representative farm level approach is used in this chapter to study the
effects of compliance costs with the Good Agricultural and Environmental Conditions
(GAECs) in the Spanish fruit and vegetable sector. For the fruit sector, we focus on citrus
farms, because of the important role of citrus both in production and in export value. The
main Spanish region in citrus production is Valencia, so that the case study area for the
citrus farms is in that region. For the vegetable sector, the region selected for the case study
is Castilla-La Mancha. This region is one of the most important nitrate vulnerable areas in
Spain. In addition, aquifer overexploitation is an important issue in the area, so that the
GAEC requirement regarding overexploited aquifers is also relevant here. The case study
will focus on farm systems located on the Western La Mancha Aquifer, in the Upper
Guadiana river basin, which combine problems of nitrate pollution and overexploitation,
where the Water Abstraction Plan (WAP) establishes water quotas for the farms in the
aquifer. The surface of the aquifer is 5,500 km2 and irrigated agriculture in the aquifer
represents a surface of 140,000 hectares. General characteristics of the 5 farm types
included in the case studies are reported in Table 11.2. For the citrus fruit farms in the
region of Valencia, compliance costs with pesticide regulations and the Nitrates Directive,
and the impact of compliance on farm gross margins were evaluated. Data and information
were obtained from interviews with experts and data from the Ministry of Agriculture and
the Regional Government of Valencia as well as several studies that were available.
     For the mixed vegetable farms of the region of Castilla-La Mancha, the analysis of the
cost of compliance has been carried out using an integrated economic-agronomic simulation
                         Environmental standards in the fruits and vegetables sector of Spain                      187

model developed for this area. The structure of this model is described in Figure 11.2. The
products analysed include melons, peppers, potatoes and garlic, and the selected policy
measures for this area are GAEC to control water use and the Nitrates Directive,
considering that this area is a nitrate vulnerable zone. For the GAECs we have selected a
specific measure that Spain has introduced - water use limitations in areas of overexploited
aquifers as is the case in the region. Both measures are of major importance in
Mediterranean countries and regions where groundwater is the major source of water for
irrigated agriculture. In this case, the cost of compliance with the Nitrates Directive and
with the GAECs water use control, is not a direct cost to farmers but rather a reduction in
yields, due to a limitation in the use of nitrate fertilizers and water, and consequently a
reduction in farm gross margins.
     The basic analytical framework developed to analyse fruit and vegetables sector can be
summarized as follows:

(i)      Selection of the regions, sub-regions, agronomic areas and municipalities
         (counties) that represent the agricultural production systems of the products
         considered.
(ii)     Selection of the most relevant policy measures.
(iii)    Selection of representative farms within the study regions that will allow the
         analysis of cost structure at farm level.
(iv)     Assessment of compliance costs in the pre and post establishment of the selected
         measures.
(v)      Development of a model-based analysis integrating an economic (mathematical
         programming model) and agronomic model (CropSyst) for policy simulations and
         assessment of the cost of selected measures (Nitrates Directive and water use) (for
         the case study of Castilla-La Mancha).
(vi)     Analysis of competitiveness and policy standards at farm level.

Cost of compliance in citrus farms in Valencia

Two aspects of compliance costs were analysed: (i) the cost reduction due to the required
decrease of specific inputs use, and (ii) the impact on yields of input use reductions and of
other specific conservation practices. Citrus orchards in the Valencia region apply nitrogen
at an average rate of 280 kg N/ha/year. The application level allowed under the Nitrates
Directive in citrus production in Valencia is 240 kg N per ha per year. A reduction of 40 kg
N per ha might be the maximum amount of N that growers should decrease in order to
comply with the Nitrates Directive. As such this generates an average reduction in fertilizer
expenditures of €18.76 per hectare per year. Reductions in costs for pesticides uses for this
region are not easy to estimate for different reasons. The most important changes on
pesticides use during the last year have been related to insecticides, based on concerns
about the effects of active ingredients on non-target organisms and human health. Thus, a
variety of alternative products have appeared during the last years. For our case study on
citrus, we have compared the certification method EUREPGAP with the conventional citrus
orchard production in the region of Valencia.
     The effects of compliance on citrus production cannot be defined as a cost of
compliance since a reduction in inputs could not necessarily lead to a yield decrease in a
clear manner. There is no evidence of yield decrease because of reductions in the average
amounts of pesticides used or nitrogen application rates. Pesticide regulations and nitrogen
application rate restrictions do not clearly modify revenue in citrus production first because
the nitrogen dosages or pesticides application are currently many times above the optimal
level and thus a reduction could even benefit the crop, and second, because there is a large
variety of production protocols and techniques that allow producers to chose acceptable
alternatives which minimize the impact on crop profitability.
     According to information of local experts, the risk of control of the nitrates and erosion
measures perceived by local producers in Valencia is not high, and even if the main citrus
production areas belong to the nitrates vulnerable zone in the region of Valencia,
requirements related to the GAEC and Nitrates Directive seem to be easy to comply with for
most citrus farmers. Therefore, these measures might not imply a noticeable change in the
costs at the farm level.
                      Environmental standards in the fruits and vegetables sector of Spain   189

Cost of compliance of vegetable farms in Castilla-La Mancha

This portion of the analysis focuses on the impact of compliance with the Nitrates Directive
and with water consumption measures for recovering overexploited aquifers. This specific
legal requirement is part of the GAECs defined in the Spanish transposition of the EU cross
compliance legislation (MAPA, 2004). There are six nitrate vulnerable zones in this region.
The action programme (DOCM, 2001) establishes the maximum nitrogen application
allowed for each crop in these vulnerable zones and specifies the agricultural practices
required in order to minimize nitrogen leaching. The use of groundwater in the western La
Mancha aquifer allowed the development of the irrigated agriculture during the 1960‟s and
1970‟s. The joint effect of the CAP and of Spanish rural development policies, which
encouraged irrigation intensification, together with technological development, which
reduced water pumping and abstraction costs, produced a huge increase of water abstraction
from the aquifer, which led to a provisional declaration of overexploitation in 1987 and a
permanent declaration in 1991. According to the article 54 of the Spanish Water Law of
1985, the river basin authority established a regional water plan with a compulsory water
quota system (Water Abstraction Plan). The Water Abstraction Plan is updated every year
and it is based on farm size, setting higher restrictions for larger farms. This water
conservation policy produced important social and economic impacts, but has not
succeeded to achieve the aquifer recovery. Agriculture is the main cause of overexploitation
and of wetland degradation in the region and, therefore, its inclusion among the GAECs is
highly relevant.
     In accordance with the GAEC with water conservation requirements, farms with
irrigated area in overexploited aquifer zones are required to have water abstraction permits
and the installation of water flow meters is compulsory for every well on every farm.
     The simulation modelling approach used for analysis for farms in this region consisted
of an agronomic model CropSyst, calibrated for this area by Azaña (2007). This model
calculated changes in yields for each crop type for different levels of nitrogen application.
These results are then used as an input in the economic model that calculates changes in the
farm‟s gross margin and cropping pattern. The economic model used is a farm-based non-
linear single-period mathematical programming model of constrained optimization,
developed by Varela-Ortega et al., (2006). The model describes the behaviour of the
representative farmers selected confronted by different policy scenarios. Following previous
work in the area of study (Varela-Ortega et al., 1998, 2002), the model incorporates new
risk parameters and maximizes a utility function (U) subject to technical, economic and
policy constraints (g). The utility function is defined by a gross margin (Z) and a risk vector
(R) that takes into account climate as well as market price variability. The model can be
summarized as follows:

Maximize U = f (x) , f (x) = Z - R
    Subject to the following constraints       g ( x)  S1 ,
                                                x  S2
    Where „x‟ is the vector of the decision-making variables or vector of the activities
defined by a given crop-growing area and by an associated production technique, irrigation
method and soil type (S). The model is solved using GAMS (General Algebraic Modeling
System). The technical coefficients and parameters of the model were obtained from field
190    Environmental standards in the fruits and vegetables sector of Spain

work carried out during 2006 and 2007 (Varela-Ortega et al., 2007) in the study area,
consisting of surveys and interviews with farmers, irrigation community representatives,
technical experts, river basin managers, and regional government officials. The model was
calibrated and validated, using the risk aversion coefficient as calibration parameter and the
comparative data on crop distribution, land and labour parameters in the study area (Varela-
Ortega, 2007).
      Model results showed that the impact of the Nitrates Directive varies considerably
among different crops and therefore the impact of nitrogen reductions at the farm level
changes depend on the farm‟s cropping pattern. The necessary nitrogen application rate
reductions are considerably larger for some intensive crops such as peas, potatoes, vines or
maize (65%, 62%, 56% and 42% respectively) and smaller for melons (28%) and wheat
(31%). The results of the agronomic model show that the impact on crop yields is high for
sugar beet (23%), sunflower (20%) and barley (19%). Among the horticultural crops,
potatoes and garlic are the most sensitive crops (yield decreases about 10% and 6%
respectively).
     The results of the economic model show that, given the farmer‟s capacity to change
cropping patterns as a means of adapting to regulations, those changes can minimize the
impact of the Nitrates Directive. Compliance with this directive does not inflict any income
loss to farms F2 and F4 (with flexible cropping patterns), results in a slight reduction in
income for farm F3 (3%) and a considerable reduction for farm F5 (15%), due to a lower
capacity to adjust its cropping pattern, since it has a high proportion of permanent crops.
     The impact of the GAEC‟s water conservation measure is studied based on the
application of the region‟s Water Abstraction Plan (WAP), a strict water quota system for
recovering the overexploited Western La Mancha aquifer. Water abstractions are reduced
from an average volume of 4,270 m3/ha (historical water rights) to an average volume of
1,700 m3 per ha ranging from a maximum of 2,640 m3 per ha (for farms under 30 ha) to
1,000 m3 per ha (for vineyards). This water policy produces a shift in cropping patterns
from high water demanding crops (maize, sugar beet) to low water demanding crops (barley
and sunflower). Horticulture crops are produced using modern irrigation techniques
(sprinklers and drip irrigation) that have increased water use efficiency in the area.
     Figure 11.3 shows that farms are more vulnerable to reductions in water availability
than to nitrogen fertilizer restrictions. Compliance with the Water Abstraction Plan results
in an average reduction of farm income of 20% as compared to a 3% reduction in the case
of compliance with the Nitrates Directive. Compliance with the WAP is comparatively more
costly to farms that crop water intensive crops, such as F3 and F5 (up to 40% and 30% of
their initial income respectively).

Competitiveness at the farm level

The assessment of short-run competitiveness at the farm level is based on the relative
changes in gross margin (GM). Aggregate results are calculated as a weighted average
considering the relative weight of each farm type in the area. The aggregate results for the
irrigation zone of the Western La Mancha aquifer (the main nitrates vulnerable area in the
region of Castilla-La Mancha) show an average gross margin loss of 21€ per ha, which is
equivalent to a reduction of about 3% (see Table 11.3). Average competitiveness index is
0.97 varying from 0.84 for the farm with a low adaptation capacity to 1.11 for the farm with
                          Environmental standards in the fruits and vegetables sector of Spain          191

a flexible cropping pattern and high adaptation capacity. This shows the potential benefits
of compliance in some farms in this region in Spain.

The same approach was used to analyse the effect of compliance with the water policy
measures in the region of Castilla-La Mancha. Table 11.4 compares the pre and post
