Conclusions
The results of this chapter suggest that, in general terms, the cost of compliance with the
environmental regulations for the fruits and vegetables sector in Spain would probably be
slight (or even zero or beneficial in some specific cases). It has to be noted, however, that
due to the limitations of the study, not all the fruits and vegetables production regions have
been considered and, therefore, these general conclusions may differ across regions,
farming systems and types of farms. In highly productive areas of single-crop production,
such as the citrus groves of the region of Valencia, the assessment of the cost of compliance
for pesticides and nitrates show that benefits of compliance might be possible. For these
farms, many practices banned by European or Spanish policies have an alternative method
that is economically attractive. Methods developed recently for pest management show clear
improvements in pest control efficiency almost without any extra cost.
     The impact of compliance with the Nitrates Directive on vegetable crops in the region
of Castilla-La Mancha showed that the cost of compliance at the farm level varies with farm
type and also depends on the opportunities that farmers have to change the mix of products
that they grow. When a farmer‟s adaptation ability is low, the most vulnerable farmers
would incur income losses of around 15%. When farmers adjust the cropping patterns to
                       Environmental standards in the fruits and vegetables sector of Spain   193

comply with the Nitrates Directive by changing to less nitrate-demanding crops and
eliminating over-fertilization, the income loss is low and, in some cases, incomes may even
increase.
     Competitiveness of citrus production might be unaffected or even improved if changes
in production practices result in the same yields with lower applications of inputs, so there
could be a farm level benefit of compliance with standards. Growers tend to use nitrate
fertilizers exceeding the maximum permitted dosage. Nevertheless, there is no evidence of
yield decreases when fertilization is reduced to the directive‟s limits. In consequence,
production costs decrease and gross margins in citrus production might increase slightly.
     For mixed vegetable farms of Castilla-La Mancha, the effects of the application of the
Nitrates Directive on the overall competitiveness of the region‟s farms also varies across
farm types. Farms with a fixed cropping pattern have a lower capacity to adjust and
therefore face higher compliance costs. Conversely, larger farms cultivating annual cash
crops can adjust more readily to environmental regulation by changing their cropping mix.
There is some evidence that compliance can have beneficial effects on certain types of
farms in Spain. Water quotas established in the region have a significantly larger impact on
farm profitability than the Nitrates Directive. However, there are synergies between water
and agricultural policies, since water use limitations are promoting the substitution of water
demanding crops with less water intensive crops which require also lower nitrogen
applications. On the other hand, as the land cropped under fruits and vegetables will be
eligible for the Single Farm Payment, it is expected that mixed-production farms that
combine vegetable production will increase the land area dedicated to vegetables.
     The fruit and vegetable sector in the EU faces important challenges in international
trade from input use restrictions, such as the Nitrates Directive as well as pesticide
registration and use regulations. On the other hand, certification schemes have the potential
to improve the competitiveness of this sector through improved market access, and
occasionally from decreases in costs and even premium prices. But competition from
exports from less developed countries which may have production cost advantages,
including different environmental compliance costs, will continue to be an important
challenge for growers in Spain.

Chapter 12


Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving
trade disputes involving non-tariff measures:
four cases involving Brazil

Sílvia Helena Galvão de Miranda



Introduction
The World Trade Organization (WTO) system is not completely suitable if developing
country expectations in terms of solving international trade controversies, particularly those
related to sanitary and technical issues, are to be met. The situation is even worse if the
environmental requirements currently affecting trade flows are considered. The Technical
Barriers to Trade (TBT) Agreement and the Sanitary and Phytosanitary (SPS) Agreement
are currently being used to address international sanitary, technical and even environmental
trade issues and will probably continue to be used in this respect. It is expected that the
WTO and the Multilateral Environmental Agreements (MEAs) will eventually make an
improved regulatory system available to deal with trade and environmental disputes.
     This chapter is intended to provide insight into the role of the WTO in the solution of
SPS and TBT trade disputes from the perspective of developing or middle-income
countries, in contrast to the developed country perspective offered by the other chapters of
this book.
     Sanitary and technical barriers1 to commerce have become one of the most challenging
issues in the field of international economics, not only because they can restrict trade, but

1
  There are several arguments about the understanding of the word „barrier‟ as it applies to trade. Some people prefer the word
„obstacle.‟ This discussion is still alive, particularly by the Technical Barriers to Trade Committee at the WTO. One of the
arguments is that when one refers to a sanitary or technical barrier, it is assumed that this measure makes trade impossible. On the
other hand, the word obstacle means that the measure makes trade difficult while not necessarily halting trade flow. For the private
sector, as highlighted in Miranda and Barros (2005), a barrier is any kind of sanitary or technical requisite that jeopardizes business
(increases costs of compliance and/or reduces trade flows or prices). From this perspective the barrier does not have to be a
regulation, it can also be a private standard. However governments do not consider private standards to be barriers because they do
not have the force of law. A second feature essential to understanding the discussion in this chapter relates to the recognition of a
sanitary or technical requirement as a barrier or obstacle, and how this is connected with the legitimacy issue. A barrier is legitimate
if it is non-discriminatory, is supported by scientific evidence and enhances food safety, the environment or animal, human and plant
health. If the purpose of the regulation is provided for by an agreement's provisions and procedures, based on science and legitimate
goals, even though this measure impacts trade, it will not be considered an illegitimate barrier. On the other hand, if a regulation's
requirement is based on protectionism and distorts trade, then it is considered an illegitimate barrier and the domestic regulation will
probably face criticisms and disputes between the enacting country and its trade partners. The determination of a regulation's
legitimacy is complex, and each regulation or standard should be considered on a case-by-case basis. This chapter will not address
the legitimacy issue in depth. A more detailed discussion is provided by Miljkovic (2005) and Miranda and Barros (2005); the main
legitimacy criteria come from GATT, SPS and TBT Agreements (www.wto.org).

©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         197
198        Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures

because economic agents, particularly consumers, are increasingly sensitive to the concerns
they address. Several of these measures raise costs and reduce competitiveness while others
exclude goods from the market. This rather negative view of the effect of sanitary and
technical measures often prevails when the topic is discussed in developing countries;
however, authors such as Roberts and Unnevehr (2005) have recently emphasized the
possible beneficial effects on welfare generated by the international regulation of sanitary
and technical standards.
     Surveys taken in several countries have shown that a significant number of recently
established sanitary and technical requirements are private voluntary standards rather than
government regulations and that there is an increasing demand for even stricter sanitary and
technical regulation. New, stricter standards seem to benefit all consumers; however, these
stricter standards create more difficulties in the developing world than in the developed
world.
     The developing world is impacted by financial limitations, technological and workplace
restrictions, cultural and religious factors, and infrastructure deficiencies less evident in
developed countries. These handicaps impede implementation of international sanitary and
technical regulations. Even within each developing economy, compliance is unequal. In
Brazil, for example, many fruit producers are adopting the Fruit Integrated Production2
(PIF) label, a national quality certification stamp; but in some regions and sectors the
process of adjustment has lagged.
     Part of the controversy surrounding international regulation of sanitary and technical
practices stems from difficulty encountered when attempting to identify this type of
regulation‟s economic and social costs and benefits. Measurement of the effects of sanitary
and technical regulation is exceedingly complex as it involves public health, food safety,
and protection of environment from new pests and diseases rather than the logistics of
international trade. Adding to the controversy is the problem encountered trying to
disentangle legitimate regulation from protectionism, a caveat that justifies the following
section‟s brief discussion of the legitimacy issue.
     Table 12.1 gives some examples that illustrate the importance of trade disputes arising
from the application of sanitary and technical measures. The examples come from a review
of relevant literature, press releases, and WTO Committee releases.

Conceptual and legal aspects

The legal framework for analysing sanitary and technical trade measures is basically
contained in the SPS and TBT Agreements, both within the scope of the WTO. The two
agreements establish provisions that guide negotiation and regulation of sanitary and
technical measures. These provisions are reviewed in works by Roberts et al. (1999) and
Burnquist et al. (2005).

The basic difference between the SPS and the TBT agreements is in their scope. The SPS
Agreement covers all measures intended to protect

       human or animal health from food-borne risks;
       human health from animal or plant-carried diseases; and

2
    In the original Portuguese, Produção Integrada de Frutas.
      Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures         199

    animals and plants from pests or diseases, whether or not these are technical
     requirements.


Table 12.1 Examples of disputes related to sanitary and technical issues

management procedures imposed to

The TBT Agreement covers all technical requirements, consumer information (e.g.
labelling), voluntary standards and the procedures to ensure that these requirements and
standards are met (conformity assessment procedures), except when these are measures
specifically covered by the SPS Agreement.
     The SPS Agreement commits signatories to implement sanitary and phytosanitary
measures based on internationally established guidelines and risk assessment procedures.
The international guidelines are established by three scientific organizations: the Codex
Alimentarius (Codex) for food safety measures, the World Organization for Animal Health
(OIE), and the International Plant Protection Convention (IPPC). According to Roberts
(1998), these international organizations are taken as providers of benchmark or „safe
harbour‟ standards.
     Implementation of multiple, divergent SPS measures to regulate both processes and
limits has caused the costs of trade to increase substantially and led to attempts at
harmonization. After analysis of Maximum Residue Limits regulations, Telteboim et al.
(2007) found that despite the WTO efforts to harmonize technical and sanitary standards,
individual countries continue to set and apply their own limits. These country limits, if they
significantly diverge from an accepted norm, cause additional costs and other difficulties for
exporters of Brazilian fruits.
     However, a positive effect of the SPS Agreement‟s adoption has been the application of
the „regionalization‟ principle (article 6 - Adaption to Regional Conditions, Including Pest-
or Disease-Free Areas and Areas of Low Pest or Disease Prevalence). Applying this
principle, if a country can demonstrate that all or at least part of its territory is free from a
hazard, it can circumvent sanitary and technical restrictions blocking all their exports of the
affected product. For instance, Brazil established the Cattle Circuits process by which
200    Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures

regions free of foot and mouth disease (FMD) were differentiated from infected regions.
Miranda (2001) and Miranda and Barros (2009) reported that this tool allowed an increase
of Brazilian beef exports to the EU (EU) from regions free of FMD with vaccination.
     The TBT Agreement states that countries should prevent the adoption of technical
regulations if they create unnecessary obstacles to trade. Conflicts generated by technical
obstacles are related to labelling requirements, conformity assessment procedures,
traceability, content of products, testing and sampling requirements, and other issues that
are sometimes referred to as sanitary issues. According to the Agreement, countries are not
supposed to adopt technical regulations that are more trade-restrictive than needed to fulfil a
legitimate goal, which can be to meet national security requirements, to prevent deceptive
practices, to protect human health or safety, to preserve animal and plant health, or to
protect the environment.
     The Agreement states that it „deals only with technical regulations, standards and
conformity assessment procedures related to products or processes and production methods.
Standards as defined by ISO/IEC Guide 2 may be mandatory or voluntary. For the purpose
of this Agreement standards are defined as voluntary and technical regulations as mandatory
documents. Standards prepared by the international standardization community are based on
consensus. This Agreement covers also documents that are not based on consensus‟ (TBT
Agreement/WTO).
     The WTO‟s main tool to guarantee transparency is Notification, due whenever a
government proposes a new regulation or modifies an already existing one if it differs from
an international standard or if it potentially might affect trade. Once notified, the SPS or
TBT Secretariat is responsible for forwarding the notification to other WTO members to
assure them an opportunity to comment on that regulation.
     A remarkable difference between the SPS and TBT approaches, pointed out by Popper
et al. (2004), is that unlike the SPS Agreement, the TBT Agreement treats „sound science‟
as an element for consideration but not a fundamental basis for establishing policy. „The
absence of more rigorous requirements for risk assessment under the TBT Agreement
leaves considerable room for manoeuvre when determining the acceptability of regulations,
standards, and conformity-assessment procedures, including necessity.‟
     Even though international agreements set out to prevent countries from using technical
and sanitary regulations to restrict trade or protect domestic suppliers from foreign
competition, this goal is not always achieved. Many authors have suggested that the
problems of protectionism and unjustified trade restriction could be ameliorated if more
light were shed on unilaterally adopted sanitary and technical regulations.
     Roberts et al. (1999) proposed a system that classifies technical measures by policy
instrument and scope first and then by regulatory goal, which indicated how and why
domestic demand and supply would change as a result of the success or failure of a specific
technical measure. This system would allow the determination of whether a regulation is
welfare-reducing or welfare-enhancing and if it arises from a risk-reducing or non-risk
reducing policy. Josling et al. (2004) updated that methodology so that it first identified the
regulatory agents, then the dimensions of the measure, and then the tools to be employed by
the regulatory measures. In a study of Brazilian beef trade focused on regulations related to
foot and mouth disease and BSE, Machado (2007) adapted these methodologies to address
sanitary measures.
     Popper et al. (2004) also focused on the classification of technical measures due to
their concern about these measures‟ consistency with TBT provisions. The authors prepared
     Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures   201

a report to the US National Institute of Standards & Technology (NIST) proposing criteria
for specifically identifying TBT-like practices. They determined that in practice it is often
difficult to discern WTO inconsistency when addressing TBT measures and concluded that
the determination of legitimacy is possible only on a case-by-case basis.
     More recently, Miranda and Barros (2005) developed a procedure to qualitatively and
quantitatively evaluate the level of legitimacy of a sanitary or technical requirement. They
proposed a checklist encompassing three evaluative steps:

   preparation of an economic impact analysis;
   carrying out an analysis of the requirement‟s legality in respect to compliance with the
    provisions of international agreements; and
   analysing the sanitary or technical regulation‟s scientific justification.

In final form, their checklist, re-titled the Relevance Index, contains two components: an
economic impact analysis and a legitimacy analysis, which is a combination of the earlier
checklist‟s legality and scientific analyses (Miranda and Barros, 2005).
     Advances in the methodology and data collection used for this legitimacy analysis will
probably turn the legislative adoption of a sanitary or technical measure into a more
comprehensive process that includes a discussion on the measure‟s potential benefits and
costs.

The application of SPS and TBT agreements in developing countries

This section examines implementation of the SPS and TBT Agreements in developing
countries, specifically focusing on the caveats those countries face meeting SPS and TBT
requirements and highlighting the effects of these measures‟ imposition by countries or
firms (private standards) on trade and welfare.
     Burnquist et al. (2005) analysed notifications of sanitary regulations to the SPS
Agreement Committee. Their analysis included 34 countries in the Western Hemisphere and
covered the period from January 1995 to December 2001. About 47% of the 34 nations did
not notify the SPS Committee of any SPS regulation changes. The authors noted that
sanitary regulation might have been underreported, possibly due an individual country‟s
determination that the regulation or its change would not impact trade. Moreover, most
Western Hemisphere countries would have faced difficulties meeting Enquiry-Point
requirements to take part in the WTO‟s transparency process.
     Burnquist et al. (2005) noted a pattern in the notification process when they correlated
notifications by Western Hemisphere country with that country‟s World Bank income
classification. They found that the hemisphere‟s higher-income countries (US and Canada)
submitted SPS notifications much more frequently than lower income countries.
     Henson et al. (1999) conducted ten case studies of developing countries to identify the
relative importance of several factors in respect to their ability to meet SPS requirements
when exporting agricultural and food products to the European Community. The studies
were based on responses to questionnaires. The most important complaints coming from the
respondent countries were „insufficient access to technical and scientific expertise‟ and
„incompatibility of SPS requirements with domestic production/marketing methods.‟ Less
frequently cited problems were „poor awareness of SPS requirements within the agricultural
and food industries‟ and „poor access to information on SPS requirements‟.
202    Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures

     Several authors have studied the effect of the SPS Agreement‟s implementation on
trade. Stanton (1999) noted that the purpose of the Agreement is to promote international
trade by limiting the use of SPS regulation as disguised barriers to trade. This purpose is
explicit in the TBT Agreement, albeit not so clearly in the SPS Agreement.
     In order to examine how the SPS Agreement affects a country‟s sanitary policies, one
must have an understanding of how international standards are established and identify the
exceptions that allow a country to deviate from those standards. An exception to
harmonization occurs whenever nations adopt SPS measures less stringent than current
international measures. Victor (2000) holds that the weakening of existing criteria could be
thought to be much less of a threat to trade than strong measures but that the problem of
conflict between the SPS Agreement‟s stipulations and the weakening of SPS criteria has
probably not yet arisen because SPS criteria is most likely to be lowered by developing
countries; and these countries have not yet fully implemented the Agreement.
     Numerous researchers have studied the effect of TBT requirements on importation.
Ferraz Filho (1997) took a survey of Brazilian exporting companies in the 1990s. The
author found that slaughterhouses and electrical equipment industries were most directly
affected by technical requirements and considered these requirements a very important
obstacle to expansion in the international marketplace.
     The World Bank conducted a TBT survey that included 689 firms, 20 industrial
sectors, and 17 developing countries (Wilson and Otsuki, 2004). About 70% of these firms
reported that they faced technical regulations in their export markets, with EU and US
regulations generally considered to be the most important. The study showed that to meet
EU and US standards, the firms invested in additional plant and equipment, redesigned their
products, and hired additional labour for production, testing, and certification. It is clear
that technical requirements increase production costs and certainly complicate bringing a
product to market.
     In Brazil, Faria and Burnquist (2006) evaluated the effect of technical requirements on
Brazilian exports based on a survey applied to 77 exporting firms. The study variables
represent 6 types of technical measures that affect exported products and are distinguished
by the measure‟s sphere of concern: packaging, the production process, labeling,
conformity, quality, and environmental effect. About 55% of the total companies sampled
were domestic, most of them supplying both developing and developed countries.
Adherence to quality requirements had the greatest effect on Brazilian exports, followed by
conformity testing and processing requisites. Labeling was found to be of lesser importance
(Faria and Burnquist, 2006). Similar results were verified by a World Bank study of the
effects of technical measures on the exports of several developing countries at the end of the
1990s.
     The many factors affecting developing countries as they try to comply with sanitary and
technical measures has led to renewed effort in the difficult task of correctly evaluating the
current and potential economic and social impacts of this type of regulation.
     Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures    203

SPS and TBT disputes - Brazilian background

This section presents four trade cases involving Brazil and its trading partners. The cases
highlight differences between developing and developed countries in respect to sanitary and
technical requirements.

Risk assessment and risk management procedures imposed by Brazil on wheat
imports from the US (1997)

In 1997, the US questioned new Brazilian requirements that essentially stopped the
importation of US wheat into the country. The new standards were established to prevent
introduction of the fungus Tilletia controversa (TCK bunt or Dwarf bunt) and as part of a
harmonization effort within the MERCOSUR regional trade bloc.
     The US complaint became a Specific Trade Concern (STC) under the SPS Technical
Committee‟s scope. The US contended that a 1996 bilateral agreement between the US and
Brazil contained an understanding that the fungus could not be established in Brazil and that
this understanding had never been scientifically disproved. Brazil countered that they had
only agreed that the fungus would probably not enter but that new Brazilian risk assessment
legislation necessitated the new requirement and that the bilateral agreement did not
preclude Brazil from applying internal risk assessment legislation (WTO, 2007).
     This STC was finally resolved in July 2001 when the US reported that after several
technical consultations, Brazil had issued new import instructions allowing the importation
of certain classes of US wheat. At that time, the dispute was considered settled.
     In March 2001, the Brazilian government did withdraw its ban on the importation of
Hard Red Winter, Hard Red Spring and Soft Red Winter wheat grown in the US. Due to
phytosanitary concerns, the ban remained in force for durum wheat and all wheat produced
along the US West Coast (FAO, 2004), which remains a contentious issue. In August 2001,
Brazil decided to ban the importation of wheat with more than 2 percent dockage. This
wheat had previously been allowed to enter the country as „out of standard‟ (substandard).
     Wheat was again the focus of new debate in early 2002 when new Brazilian regulations
on imported cereals and grains were announced. The new regulations required that all
countries exporting to Brazil, except MERCOSUR3 member countries, submit a Pest Risk
Assessment within 180 days from the import date (FAO, 2004). Legislation requiring this
type of analysis is a milestone in the organization of phytosanitary requirements and had
been recommended in the SPS Agreement and by the IPPC.
     Brazil is one of the world‟s largest importers of wheat. Storz et al. (2005) note that
despite Argentina‟s advantages, there are significant opportunities for US wheat in the
Brazilian market, particularly from May through September. The authors point out that in
1996, the US exported roughly 760,000 metric tons of wheat to Brazil with a value of
US$174 million. In 2002, US wheat exports to Brazil had fallen to US$96 million; and by
2007 the volume exported to Brazil had declined to only 354,065 metric tons.
     There have been no other disputes between Brazil and the US involving sanitary and
technical measures to the importation of wheat brought to the attention of the WTO.
However, by granting preferential tariffs to MERCOSUR members, Brazil has opened the
door to future trade disputes with non-MERCOSUR affiliated countries.

3
 The MERCOSUR agreement had been in force since 1995. MERCOSUR members have become the most
important suppliers of wheat to Brazil, and wheat is the main Brazilian agricultural import.
204     Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures

     From the economic perspective, the wheat case is a prime example of the impact of
phytosanitary measures when they become a trade measure. While the volume of wheat
imported into Brazil is huge, the US, a major international wheat exporter, has a very small
share of its market. From the legal perspective, it appears that Brazil‟s phytosanitary
requirements met the SPS agreement‟s transparency provisions, in that the country
consulted its trading partners before implementing the new regulations. By requiring that
exporters provide a pest risk analysis, Brazil has met the agreement‟s „scientific basis‟
requirement for the implementation of new phytosanitary regulations.
     The wheat dispute offers a better understanding of the importance of phytosanitary
requirements in international trade and indicates that the measures they erect can be
negotiated. While phytosanitary measures may not be the most important obstacles to
international trade, each new regulation of this type must be subjected to detailed study to
determine its relevance and legitimacy.

Requirements on Brazilian mango exports to Japan

According to Brasil (1999), Japanese legislation requires that a phytosanitary certificate
accompany imported plants and plant products. The certificate should be issued by a
competent authority from the exporting country.
    Essentially, Japan divides agricultural imports into three categories:

     forbidden products;
     products that require quarantine procedures; and
     products without quarantine requirements.

     Because they are grown in regions that contain the Mediterranean fruit-fly, a pest not
found in Japan, mangoes fall into the first category. The fruit-fly also causes a restriction on
the importation of other Brazilian fruits.
     A Brazilian report on export measures notes the Japanese pest protection policy
requires that a different test be performed on each agricultural product type, even if the
products are just different varieties of the same species. The testing is a very expensive,
time-consuming procedure (Buainain and Batalha, 2007). Japanese authorities have ignored
evidence showing that the same test can be easily extended to different varieties of the same
species.
     The US also argued against this regulation under the scope of the SPS Agreement
through the WTO in April 1997. Brazil supported the US in this dispute, also raising
concerns about the Japanese varietals test and accompanying quarantine requirements. The
WTO panel affirmed the US position in 1998. The decision recommended that Japanese
authorities modify their varietal test requirements for several fruits, as they were
inconsistent with SPS Agreement provisions. The decision was corroborated by the WTO‟s
Appellate Body in February 1999.
     A report issued by Brasil (1999) noted a lack of transparency in the Japanese
fumigation requirements. The provision requires the fumigation of fresh horticultural and
fruit products whenever live insects are encountered, even if that insect is present in Japan.
This system increases all costs and negatively affects fresh fruit and vegetable quality by
delaying delivery. The strict exigencies Japan has been imposing on imported Brazilian
mangoes arise, in most part, from Japan‟s effort to prevent infestation by the Mediterranean
     Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures   205

fruit-fly. In 2003, Brazil and India complained against the Japanese fumigation
requirements during a SPS technical committee meeting.
     According to the Brazilian complaint, for 18 years the country had been trying to get
the Japanese to ease entry restrictions on mango importation, which entailed an exporter
provided steam treatment in addition to other technical measures taken by Brazil to remove
live Mediterranean flies from the fruit. Brazil alleged that treatments already provided by
the growers gave results that were equivalent to those from steam treatment. Brazil also
claimed that Japan had hindered negotiations with their demands for more information
while ignoring results from prior scientific studies. Brazil considered that Japan‟s
requirements were inconsistent with the SPS Agreement‟s equivalence, regionalization and
technical cooperation provisions (WTO, 2008).
     Before taking this issue to the SPS Committee, Japan and Brazil negotiated bilateral
understandings and a commitment to technical cooperation. In 1986, Brazil requested
technical assistance, and Japan agreed to the request. Brazil discontinued the assistance
programme in 1990 as part of a push to develop its own hot-water based treatment. The new
treatment was preliminarily approved by Japanese authorities in 1998. Final data was
submitted by both Japan and Brazil in 2001; however, Japanese authorities requested
additional information before they could conclude their technical studies of the equivalence
of the two pest eradication methods.
     Specific Trade Concerns documents from October 2003 describe a complaint by Brazil
alleging that Japanese new restrictions on mango imports were unjustified, arguing that the
fruit in question had been produced in an area 2,000 km from where fruit flies had been
found. Japan replied that authorities were reviewing fruit-fly trapping information that had
recently been received from Brazil. In March 2004, Brazil notified the WTO that the
Japanese authorities had had a favorable reaction to technical data provided by Brazil in
2003, and a solution was expected. The solution was to include a protocol addressing the
packing, storage and transportation of mangoes destined for Japan.
     A Japanese technical evaluation of the Mediterranean fruit-fly problem was completed
in 2004, and a pest risk assessment process was developed. At that time, a bilateral meeting
was held to coordinate plant quarantine efforts and to formalize the requirements for hot
water dipping (WTO, 2008). In March 2005, Brazil informed the SPS Committee that in
September 2004, Japan had modified its phytosanitary regulations and established specific
norms for the import of mangoes from Brazil. In January 2005, the first shipment of
Brazilian Tommy Atkins mangoes was exported to Japan.
     Currently, Brazil is negotiating with the Japanese government about the possibility of
exporting other mango varieties to Japan, as the Tommy Atkins variety was not well
accepted by Japanese consumers. The current negotiations may be as difficult to resolve as
the earlier ones, which took almost 10 years to settle.
     One point to take away from this discussion is that it takes a considerable amount of
time to resolve disputes over phytosanitary regulation. Risk analysis, document submission
and consideration, and decision making on the international level is not something that is
rushed, consequently the economic impact of this type of dispute on the exporting country
can be severe. WTO transparency and criteria assessment procedures regarding equivalence
process are still under-regulated by the WTO, which should be of concern since
„equivalence‟ was proposed as a method of simplifying and expediting harmonization.
206    Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures

European requirements for Brazil nut aflatoxin levels

The dispute between the EU and Bolivia, Brazil and other countries over the aflatoxins
content of foodstuffs is a case of a phytosanitary obstacle to trade based upon questionable
technical requirements. In 1987, the Codex Alimentarius concluded that there was not
enough information to establish an aflatoxin tolerance level. Although the Committee did
set standards for peanuts in 1999, no level was set for other foodstuffs. The Codex
Alimentarius is still working on the issue, and there is still no international standard for
aflatoxin levels (WTO, 2009).
     In March 1998, Bolivia, Brazil and 10 other countries raised a dispute before the SPS
technical committee complaining that European regulations setting a new maximum level
for aflatoxin contamination in foodstuffs would severely restrict trade without resulting in a
significant reduction in consumer health risks. Additionally, the same countries contended
that the new requirements were established without a proper risk assessment study and that
the sampling procedure that exporters were required to employ represented an unjustifiably
large expense. The complainants urged the EU to review its requirements.
     The EU replied that its requirements reflected the level of protection required by its
member countries. The EU also asserted that the complainant‟s less expensive sampling
procedures were flawed in that the sample size was too small. The WTO‟s solution to this
dispute was reported in March 2004. At that time, the WTO determined that some European
sampling procedures and maximum aflatoxin levels should be revised.
     After evaluating all the initial comments received through the WTO‟s consultancy
process, the European Community reported that it had forwarded a revised proposal to its
member country representatives in June 1998. This first revised proposal reduced some
maximum aflatoxin levels and extended the timetable for introduction of the new
requirements until January 1999. However, in September 1998, before the revised
requirements were introduced, Bolivia informed the SPS Committee that the EU‟s revised
proposal would severely, negatively affect its Brazil nut exports. The Bolivian complaint
led to technical consultation between Bolivia and the EU. After consultation, Bolivia
presented a plan to improve the quality of its Brazil nuts and requested application of the
WTO‟s developing country „special and differential treatment‟ provision. As a result, an
assistance programme between Bolivia and EU was set up. In 2002, an accreditation and
certification procedure was successfully negotiated.
     In June 2002, it was determined that the largest Bolivian exporters were able to meet
the EC requirements. In June 2003, Bolivia informed the WTO that a bilateral meeting had
had a favourable outcome and that Bolivia should soon receive permission for the export of
whole Brazil nuts to the EU. In March 2004, bilateral consultations between Bolivian and
EU representatives led to the scheduling of an assessment visit by EU authorities to finalize
the procedure for EU certification of Bolivian Brazil nuts for export to the EU. Over recent
years, the volume and value of Bolivian Brazil nut exports to the EU have increased;
although, the considerable expense involved in successfully completing the EU certification
process forced smaller Bolivian Brazil nut exporters out of the EU market.
     Despite understandings reached between Bolivia and the EU, there remained several
other countries‟ unresolved complaints against the EU‟s aflatoxin regulations. One of the
main points underlying the complaints was that the EU had not presented a risk assessment
to support the need for its restrictive aflatoxin requirements.
      Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures                  207

     As Bolivian exports of whole Brazil nuts and almonds to the EU grew, the export of
Brazilian whole Brazil nuts to the EU dropped drastically. Silveira et al. (2007) showed that
the European‟s maximum levels for aflatoxin contamination negatively affects Brazilian
Brazil nut exports, with a 1% reduction of the European‟s maximum limit reducing Brazil‟s
Brazil nut exports to the EU by 1.22%, and that difficulties encountered attempting to test
for these miniscule levels excessively and unwarrantedly increase producer costs. Their
study concluded that regulation of maximum aflatoxin levels needs to be harmonized
globally and that the Brazilian government should make an effort to improve production,
harvest, storage and transport conditions in their country to reduce mycotoxin
contamination.
     Several Brazilian Brazil nut shipments to the EU and US were barred at the border
because of aflatoxin levels above regulatory limits. In July 2003, the EU suspended the
importation of Brazilian Brazil nuts and requested that government authorities impose
special conditions on import of Brazil nuts originating in or consigned from Brazil, for
instance: official Brazilian aflatoxin certificates must accompany consignments;
consignments may only enter the EU at specified points; and consignments be subjected to
sampling and analysis and can be detained for a maximum of 15 working days from the
point of entry into the Community before release onto the market (EC decision #493).
     Results from scientific studies evaluating damage to the human organism from different
aflatoxin levels have not been conclusive, which has led to great diversity among individual
country aflatoxin regulatory requirements. By 2003, 61 countries had established their own
specific limits on the amount of aflatoxin B1 that can be contained in human foodstuffs and
76 had specific limits to total aflatoxins: B1+B2+G1+G2 (FAO, 2004). Some countries
have succeeded in harmonizing their regulation within trade blocs (Australia/New Zealand,
EU, MERCOSUR) (Silveira et al., 2007). Over time, aflatoxin limits have become lower as
the sensitivity of analyses improves and knowledge about the effects of aflatoxins advances
while divergence between producers and consumers has grown over what aflatoxin level
effectively guarantees food safety.
     In 1987, the European Commission‟s Codex Alimentarius committee concluded that
there was not enough information to establish an aflatoxin tolerance level and recommended
that the level of aflatoxin in food should be reduced „as low as reasonably achievable‟ –
ALARA. In 1996 and 1997 JECFA4 conducted quantitative risk assessments to revaluate
this issue and concluded that in regards to human health risks there was not a significant
difference between assuming a maximum level of 10 µg/kg or 20 µg/kg of aflatoxin B1
(Boutrif, 1998). In July 1999, the Codex Alimentarius revised its proposal for the maximum
tolerable total aflatoxins limit in peanuts and adopted 15 µg/kg as the international
reference. No other limits were adopted at that time.
     There is not a specific Brazilian limit defined for all aflatoxins specific to Brazil nuts.
In 1977, the Brazilian Health Ministry passed Resolution no. 34/76 for Food, which
established a maximum B1 + G1 aflatoxins limit of 30ppb (µg/kg) in foods for human
consumption (Silveira et al., 2007).
     The European Brazil nut aflatoxin contamination limit of no more than 4 ppb of
aflatoxin (2 ppb for aflatoxin type B1) is a very low limit compared to the Codex and to US

4
 Expert Committee on Food Additives (JECFA), a committee that involves the Food and Agriculture
Organization (FAO) and the World Health Organization (WHO) and is the consultative group responsible for
evaluation of toxic substance safety limits. JECFA provides scientific recommendations that are the basis for the
WTO‟s Codex Alimentarius‟s development of food safety standards.
208    Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures

referents (US maximum aflatoxin content <10 ppb). Silveira et al. (2007) reviewed some of
the criticisms of the imposition of such low aflatoxins limits: there has been no
determination of what actually is a safe daily aflatoxin consumption limit; there has been
too little discussion in a competent international forum of what this limit should be; daily
Brazil nut consumption is unknown, other than it is much lower than the consumption of
other foodstuffs containing aflatoxin; and the European aflatoxin limit is only very rarely, if
ever, achieved through supplier efforts. A number of technicians believe that the acceptable
level is more a political than a scientific determination, as there is no scientific evidence to
support the European establishment‟s determination of the safe aflatoxin level.
     At this time, the European aflatoxin limit on Brazil nuts appears to be unjustifiable on
either a scientific or a consumer health basis. Silveira et al. (2007) concluded that the EU‟s
Brazil nut aflatoxin specifications override market forces and do not allow the market to
balance supply and demand via prices.
     To open the European market, Brazil‟s government implemented initiatives to control
aflatoxin contamination in the peanut and Brazil nut chains. The initiatives included the
Good Manufacturing Practices resolution and the Ministry of Agriculture‟s Emergency
Work Plan for Brazil Nuts and involved the productive sector, their representatives, and the
country‟s exporting association. In May 2004, a new federal regulation providing rules for
the sanitary certification of Brazil nuts was issued. The regulation was intended to improve
Brazil nut quality in the domestic market and to meet some requirements of European
Commission decision no. 493.
     Brazil exports Brazil nuts to the major world markets, but the volume of these exports
has been decreasing since 2003, particularly to the EU. In 2002, European imports of
Brazilian Brazil nuts were 3,400 metric tons. Because of the restrictiveness of EU‟s
technical requirements, these imports fell to only 16 metric tons in 2005. Brazil did succeed
in re-directing some Brazil nut exports to other countries, like Bolivia, Canada, Norway and
the US. In 2002, Brazil exported no Brazil nuts to Bolivia; in 2004 Brazil exported 6,300
metric tons of Brazil nuts to Bolivia.
     Based on interviews and a review of relevant literature, Silveira et al. (2007) concluded
that apparently Brazil did not choose to comply with European Commission‟s Brazil nut
regulations. More to the point, Brazilian programs implemented to improve production
chain procedures did not achieve their intended goals in terms of product quality. Brazil has
since revised its nut export strategy, shifting the focus of exportation to shelled Brazil nuts
rather than whole Brazil nuts, because shelled nuts present a lower level of aflatoxin, and
focusing Brazilian nut exportation to markets that have enacted less stringent aflatoxin
regulations.

Foot and mouth disease in bovines: the regionalization provision and its acceptance

Foot and mouth disease, and the trade restrictions it has brought about, has severely affected
international bovine meat trade in recent years. Indeed, this is the iconic example of a
sanitary dispute involving international markets and Brazil.
     Within the Western Hemisphere, FMD-free countries (Canada, US and Mexico) restrict
meat imports from countries that have not been able to completely eradicate FMD, such as
Brazil. Due to fear of FMD contamination, the US only imports processed Brazilian beef.
     Other Latin American countries have been impacted by FMD, but the impacts have
been limited. Argentina reached FMD free status in May 1997 (OIE) by completing a
     Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures   209

vaccination programme. Although the country experienced a FMD outbreak in 2001, it still
was able to export 20 thousand metric tons of fresh beef to the US that year, meeting its
export quota.
     The US also provided a 20 thousand metric ton quota to Uruguayan beef exporters
(Miranda, 2001). Of course, every time there is a sanitary outbreak in MERCOSUR,
Uruguayan and Argentinean beef exportation to the US is temporarily interrupted. After the
outbreak is controlled, negotiations begin to re-start the process and exportation eventually
resumes.
     Lima et al. (2005) found that bans on Brazilian beef due to its FMD disease status cost
the country US$7.5 billion in 2004. The non-tariff measures were for the most part erected
by Pacific Rim countries: US, Canada, South Korea, Japan, Australia and New Zealand; all
countries that, in general terms, do not accept „regionalization‟ in respect to FMD.
     The US, one of the world‟s major meat traders, has been considered FMD-free since it
last had a FMD outbreak in 1929. The country has maintained a strict regulatory position in
the commerce of beef and pork. In 2002, a US Department of Agriculture (USDA) risk
analysis of beef importation from Uruguay found that if FMD was reintroduced into the US,
the country would have suffered export losses of over US$3 billion in 2001 and total losses
of between US$37 and US$44 billion (USDA, 2002). At the March 2004 Continental
Conference for FMD Eradication in the western hemisphere held in Houston, North
American officials estimated a total cost of US$52 billion if FMD was reintroduced,
inclusive of the effects on grains markets (Lima et al., 2005).
     Kassum and Morgan (2002) estimated that world trade in animals and meat products
was deeply affected by FMD and BSE in 2000 and 2001, when most countries closed their
markets to at least 25% of all beef exports and almost 40% of all whole pork exports. Bans
included stricter border control measures that affected even countries officially free of
animal diseases. According to the authors, SPS/WTO FMD notifications led to 113 bans
and serious restrictions on imports between 1995 and 2001. However, the authors are
positive that the SPS Agreement, with its notification requirement, achieved its goal by
providing the transparency needed to prevent some outbreaks and limit others.
     The OIE has multiple objectives. It is charged with:

   improving animal health worldwide based upon the development of sanitary standards;
   collecting, analysing and disseminating scientific information on animal medicine;
   guaranteeing the transparency of country status in relation to animal diseases; and
   providing technical assistance and promoting international cooperation to control
    animal diseases.

The WTO and the OIE are linked: the SPS Agreement is enforced by the WTO, and
provides the means to guarantee transparency in regards to sanitary measures implemented
to protect animals from diseases and zoonoses, as far as these diseases affect human health;
and the OIE has the technical knowledge needed to prevent and control these diseases.
     In the case of FMD, OIE is responsible for the official recognition of disease-free areas,
which are then differentiated into disease-free areas and disease-free with vaccination areas.
This differentiation is essential to trade, since some nations (Japan and South Korea) have
an aversion to imported beef from areas that have not eradicated the FMD yet.
     Brazil is made up of more than one region, and there can be a difference in status
between regions in regards to animal disease. One single case of FMD impedes the
210    Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures

recognition of that area as FMD-free for at least two years. The same requirement is used to
classify a region or country as FMD-free with vaccination, i.e. no cases during the last two
years and no virus in the last 12 months.
     FMD was first detected in Brazil in 1895 and was considered endemic until 1980.
Since the 1970s, the Brazilian focus has been on quality vaccines and infected animal transit
control to lead the advance against this disease. In 1992, Brazil‟s National Program to
Eradicate FMD (PNEFA) was launched, and the country was divided into different sanitary
regions (regionalization) based on FMD prevalence and the disease‟s effect on trade flow.
In 1996, new criteria were added into the regionalization mix: risk of FMD transmission
and possibility of FMD control. Over the years, PNEFA has succeeded in reducing
Brazilian FMD outbreaks and improved the country‟s sanitary status.
     The principle of regionalization, a non-binding WTO guideline, holds that restrictions
on products from areas with disease do not apply necessarily to whole countries. This
principle has not been recognized by all countries, with some still applying their FMD
measures on a by-country basis alone. The US does not apply the regionalization principle
in relation to Brazil.
     The first Brazilian request to export beef to the US was submitted to the US Animal
and Plant Health Inspection Service (APHIS) in 1999, just after the Brazilian states of Santa
Catarina and Rio Grande do Sul were certificated FMD-free with vaccination by the OIE. In
August 2000, there was a case of FMD in Rio Grande do Sul; and the certification process
was suspended at Brazil‟s request. In 2002, the certification process was restarted. In 2003,
a sanitary mission from the US, Canada and Mexico visited Brazil. It was concluded that the
importation of fresh Brazilian beef into the US will not be permitted until APHIS conducts
a risk assessment of Brazil‟s sanitary status, taking regionalization into account, and
provides a health certificate for importation. Currently, exporters cannot ship unprocessed
fresh and frozen Brazilian beef to the US.
     Uruguay had to go through the same APHIS risk assessment process after FMD
outbreaks in 2000 and 2001, but exportation has resumed (Lima et al., 2005). From this
perspective, the release of Brazilian beef exports to the US is taking an inordinately long
time, perhaps due to unclear application of the regionalization principle.
     The EU accepts regionalization and has recognized the OIE designated sanitary status
of Brazil‟s regions in relation to FMD. Russia was the second largest importer of Brazilian
beef by volume and the largest for pork. Unfortunately, Russian actions in the international
meat market are frequently inexplicable. Although Russia claims to recognize the
regionalization principle, it banned all meat imports from Brazil after FMD outbreaks in the
Brazilian states of Pará and Amazonas (Brazil‟s North Region) in 2004.
     Lima et al. (2005) reported that the EU has complained about the lack of transparency
and coherence in the North American policy on regionalization, particularly during the
sanitary crisis in 2001 when some FMD cases were reported in several EU Member States.
The EU has also argued against the US policy on regionalization during and after an
outbreak of swine fever.
     One great value of bilateral sanitary agreements is that they can be used to establish and
recognize process „equivalence‟ as provided for in the SPS Agreement‟s Article 4. When
countries reach a consensus that different sanitary or phytosanitary measures offer the same
level of health protection, product flow hindered by sanitary or technical measures improves
and trade tends to liberalize and expand. The process of negotiating a bilateral trade
agreement provides the transparency and flexibility needed to reach this consensus. An
     Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures    211

equivalence accord as part of a bilateral sanitary agreement should lead to more efficient
regulation of specific sanitary concerns. Although equivalence accords are useful, there is
room to advance the debate about how to better implement their provisions.
     Bovine meat regulations account for most SPS notifications. Machado (2007)
discovered that FMD concerns caused the second most notifications, just after BSE
notifications. From 1997 through 2005, FMD was the motive for 22 Specific Trade
Concerns (STCs) registered with SPS/WTO, most of them occurring just after a sanitary
crisis in the exporting country. Analysing the frequency and nature of STC registrations,
Machado found that the majority of concerns were raised against the world‟s main beef
importers, confirming the belief that major exporters strongly react to importer regulation.
The author (2007) emphasizes that importers have had a more important role than exporters
in determining sanitary control regulations and that importer regulations have a high
probability of becoming world trade references.

Final remarks: challenges to developing countries and the role of WTO
institutions

A review of pertinent literature shows that there is consensus in the conviction that non-
tariff measures, which comprise sanitary and technical requirements, have a
disproportionate effect on international trade. The United Nations Conference on Trade and
Development database (TRAINS) shows that, on average, 5,620 tariff lines per country
were subjected to some type of non-tariff measure in 2004, with technical requirements
accounting for 58.5% of this total. It is important to reiterate that regulation in itself is not a
barrier to trade, but some regulations have become obstacles to trade.
     The harmonization of divergent sanitary and technical measures is a time consuming,
often technologically complicated, human capital intensive, and legally convoluted process
that may raise additional trade concerns and excessively burden undercapitalized businesses
and countries. Examples discussed in this chapter have illustrated some of the inefficiencies
and intricacies of this process.
     Adding to this tangle, each country has a propensity to establish its own sanitary
regulations according to their technological infrastructure, human-capital availability,
sanitary status, and political priorities. Above all, economic interests and preferential trade
agreements (formally or not formally agreed upon) may drastically affect each country‟s
policy decisions.
     Sanitary and technical requirements aligned with international scientific guidelines and
non-discriminatory by region, country, or supplier are generally not assumed to be trade
measures. Use of the SPS‟s notification requirement, a relatively unambiguous tool to meet
the WTO‟s transparency test and facilitate analysis of any regulation‟s scientific basis or
possible bias, helps insure that trade measures are not intrusive actions of a political or
otherwise unwarranted nature.
     Developing countries and their exporting industries are right to have concerns about
their ability to comply with sanitary and technical requirements imposed in the international
market. Meeting regulatory limits established by governments and voluntary standards
demanded by firms and consumers, even if they are related to quality, is often difficult in
developed countries and can be an unattainable goal in the developing world, aptly
illustrated by the difficulties Brazil encountered in trying to comply with EU limits on its
Brazil nut exports.
212     Evaluating WTO institutions for resolving trade disputes involving non-tariff measures

     Henson et al. (1999) suggest that the effects of SPS measures are more expressive for
developing countries than for developed countries due to the relative importance of
developing country agricultural and food exportation sectors and the same countries‟
technological deficiencies. Successful implementation of SPS and TBT Agreement
requirements, especially conformity and risk assessment requirements, also may require a
disproportionately large infrastructure investment by developing country governments.
     A book edited by Milan et al. (2007) shows the results of a research project to identify
the levels of quality in Brazilian agribusiness chains and the challenges that must be
overcome to improve these levels to those demanded by the international market. One of the
greatest but least recognized obstacles confronting any attempt to improve Brazilian
agricultural product quality is the significant asymmetry between regional laboratories and
available human capital. To stay competitive, Brazilian industry and government must
invest in laboratory facilities and trained personnel. This investment would significantly
improve the likelihood that Brazilian agricultural products will comply with international
quality, environmental protection, and sanitary standards.
     It is remarkable that Brazilian exporting companies (many of them multinational) are
pressured more by the certification processes (voluntary rules) than by government
technical regulations. In light of this, quality standards may be more likely met if a
voluntary rather than a mandatory process establishes them.
     Over the mid-term, a better result for developing countries in the world trade arena
would be achieved by increasing their participation in relevant international forums
discussing sanitary and technical regulation and standardization; however, more active
participation requires more investment. The current low level of developing country
participation in this type of discussion gives undue weight to developed country scientific
and political interests when sanitary and technical guidelines are debated. It is worth noting
that political interests, more than science, guide international negotiations.
     Another way to improved rates of sanitary and technical regulation compliance is to
include a discussion of these regulations during regional integration and bilateral
partnership negotiations. These more focused gatherings have been shown to provide the
best agenda for dealing with sanitary and technical regulation at this time. During
negotiations leading to a bilateral agreement, there is room for compromise over specific
issues, thereby improving the chance of reaching an understanding. These negotiations also
create an opening to apply for technical assistance. Whatever the forum, the harmonization
of sanitary and technical requirements among countries that are at extremely different levels
of development is very difficult.
