Chapter 13

Meeting standards in EU agriculture:
the changing policy context

Vicki Swales



Introduction

The food we eat and, more importantly, how it is produced, is under greater public scrutiny
than ever before. Increasingly, consumers demand not only that food is safe to eat but that
its production has not harmed the environment and, where livestock products are concerned,
that animals have been treated humanely. As a result, agricultural production is subject to an
increasingly wide array of standards, introduced to ensure that public and consumer
expectations are met.
     The application of standards is a means to achieve a level of safety, quality or
consistency in the products and processes that affect our lives. Standards are also vital tools
of industry and commerce and often provide the basis for buyer-seller transactions. The
level at which a standard is set depends on the desired outcomes. Minimum or baseline
standards usually reflect „socially accepted norms‟ and frequently seek to prevent negative
outcomes that would generally be considered as detrimental to society, such as unsafe food
or the pollution of water. Higher standards, by their nature, go beyond accepted norms and
are generally adopted to secure positive outcomes that confer additional benefits; such
standards are often used in order to achieve market advantage.
     Standards relating to agricultural production are found most frequently in the fields of
environment, public health, animal and plant health and animal welfare. Such standards may
derive from legislation, be defined by market requirements or represent industry best
practice. This suggests that different approaches can be taken to achieving standards in
agricultural production, from public policy intervention on the one hand through to market
led initiatives on the other. The approaches adopted by a particular country or region to
setting, promoting and achieving compliance with standards are likely to be determined by a
range of factors including: the levels of awareness and understanding of problems arising
from agricultural production and the nature of the problems themselves; the availability and
costs of technical solutions to those problems; administrative and delivery structures; and,
the prevailing politics and culture.
     The role of public policy in setting, promoting and achieving compliance with
minimum or baseline standards is of particular interest in the context of EU agriculture and
the environment. The introduction of cross compliance in the EU – discussed in Chapter 2 –
established a link between some CAP payments and compliance with certain legislative

©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         215
216    Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context

requirements in order to promote more sustainable agriculture. The 2008 CAP Health
Check resulted in a number of reforms to the CAP, including revisions to cross compliance,
and further CAP reform is anticipated from 2013 in response to the EU Budget Review.
Whilst any future CAP reforms are being driven primarily by budget considerations rather
than environmental ones, it is also clear that the EU (as well as its competitor countries)
needs to address some key environmental challenges including issues such as climate
change, water quality and quantity and biodiversity loss. A growing world population means
that these environmental challenges will need to be met whilst increasing food production
and responding to consumer demands. Given these political, environmental and societal
pressures, the future prospects for a policy such as cross compliance and the issue of how
best to meet standards in EU agriculture more generally, warrant further consideration. The
experiences and approaches of some of the EU‟s agricultural competitors, namely the US,
Canada and New Zealand, to meeting standards, as discussed in Chapters 3, 4 and 5
respectively, are relevant here.
     With these issues in mind, this chapter explores the role of, and need for, policy
interventions to promote standards in relation to agriculture and environmental protection in
the EU. It considers different policy approaches to meeting baseline standards, contrasting
the EU approach with approaches in the US, Canada and New Zealand, and identifies what
lessons can be learned. Finally, recognising that EU agriculture policy is likely to face
reforms in future, this chapter considers the possible direction of such reforms and the likely
implications for meeting standards in the agriculture sector.

Why the need for standards?

The modern day farm level processes that produce the food we eat give rise to a wide range
of externalities. Whilst many of these externalities are positive or beneficial to society, such
as the maintenance of attractive landscapes or the regulation of soil and water resources,
many are negative and potentially harmful to society, such as pesticide residues in food or
the pollution of drinking water. By setting standards, public institutions seek to prevent the
negative externalities of food production and promote the positive ones.
     Whilst food safety, animal and plant health and animal welfare are all issues which
demand responses from policy makers, particular attention has been given in recent years to
the need for environmental protection measures in the agricultural sphere. Some of the main
environmental impacts that public policy is currently trying to address through standards in
the agriculture sector are discussed below. The focus is on the EU with three of its main
agricultural competitors, namely the US, Canada and New Zealand, examined as
comparisons (see Chapters 2, 3, 4 and 5 for further discussion of the development of
environmental legislation in the EU, US, Canada and New Zealand respectively).

Agricultural externalities in the EU and competitor countries

The relationship between farming and the environment is complex. Unlike some other
economic activities, farming forms part of an ecosystem rather than being external to it.
Agricultural commodities are produced by employing land, labour and capital, introducing
inputs such as seeds, pesticides and fertilizers and by manipulating the environment through
a range of different practices such as land drainage, tilling of soil, diverting natural water
sources and grazing livestock. As a result, these practices can impact – both positively and
                         Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context   217

negatively - on soil, air and water resources and on the physical landscape and the
biodiversity found there. In many countries, agricultural activity has also been an important
factor in the creation of valued landscapes and many species and habitats have become
adapted to, and dependent on, certain forms of agricultural management. Increasingly,
agriculture‟s role in relation to climate change is also acknowledged as being both a source
of greenhouse gases, particularly methane from livestock production and through the use of
fossil fuels (to run machinery and for the production of fertilizers), and a potential sink for
gases such as carbon dioxide. Increasing amounts of agricultural waste are also of concern.
     The OECD (2001) characterized the main environmental impacts of agriculture in
relation to their contribution to:

   Soil quality (erosion, nutrient supply, moisture balance, salinity).
   Land quantity (area of ecological management of agricultural land).
   Water quality (nutrient, pesticide and sediment runoff and leaching, salinity).
   Water quantity (irrigation consumption, use efficiency, water retention capacity,
    flood prevention).
   Air quality (emissions of dust, odours, ammonia and greenhouse gas, absorption of
    carbon dioxide).
   Biodiversity (farm and indigenous animal and plant diversity).
   Wildlife and semi-natural habitats (diversity of animal and plant habitats
    associated with farming).
   Rural landscape (environmental features of areas shaped by farming, including
    those associated with historic buildings and landmarks).

The environmental impacts of agriculture in the EU are increasingly understood and
documented. For much of its agrarian history, Europe has been dominated by relatively
extensive, low input systems of farming which gave rise to a wide diversity of species and
habitats and managed critical natural resources sustainably (Beaufoy et al., 1994). Farmland
which continues to be managed by such extensive farming systems today has been defined
as High Nature Value (HNV) but is threatened by the opposing trends of intensification and
specialization on the one hand and land abandonment on the other (EEA, 2004; IEEP,
2007a). Work is in progress to measure the full extent of HNV farmland but estimates
suggest 30% or more of EU farmland may be HNV. Elsewhere, over the past fifty years or
more, agriculture has become increasingly intensive and specialized with an emphasis on
high input-high output systems which have resulted in a range of negative environmental
impacts (EEA, 2005; Dwyer, 2007) summarized in Table 13.1.
     Policy responses have resulted in some improvements in the quality of Europe‟s
environment over the past thirty years but many serious environmental problems remain.
Measures are needed to address past environmental damage and to protect, maintain and
enhance existing environmental resources through agricultural management. Environmental
standards have a critical role to play in this regard.
     Are Europe‟s agricultural competitors experiencing the same or similar environmental
problems as a result of the agricultural systems and practices that predominate there?
Analysis by the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) of the
relationship between agriculture and the environment (OECD, 2004; OECD, 2008)
illustrates that whilst there are many similarities there are also some notable differences
which may have a bearing on the policy responses adopted and standards applied.
218      Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context

Table 13.1 Summary of the main impacts of agriculture on the environment in the EU

Environmental attribute        Main environmental impacts resulting from agriculture

Soil                           Soil erosion by water and wind mainly in southern and eastern Member
                               States. Soil compaction, contamination and acidification have lessened in
                               New Member States in recent years but may increase with anticipated
                               intensification.
Air                            Air pollution from ammonia (leading to acidification especially in northern
                               Member States) and odour from intensive livestock production. Declining
                               emissions of methane and nitrous oxide since 1990 in EU 15 but picture
                               more variable in new Member States.
Water                          Water pollution by nutrients and sediment (affecting both ground and
                               surface waters). Unsustainable water abstraction especially in southern
                               States with 50% of population living in water stressed areas and expected
                               to increase as a result of climate change.
Biodiversity                   Habitat loss, degradation and fragmentation which has resulted in declines
                               in the number and distribution of some species, most notably farmland
                               birds and butterflies. New Member States retain large areas of undisturbed
                               semi-natural habitats and HNV farmland but these are threatened by
                               intensification and abandonment.
Landscape                      Declines in landscape character and quality due to homogenization of land
                               use activities and loss/degradation of landscape features.
Climate change                 Production of greenhouse gases such as methane from livestock and
                               carbon dioxide released from soils under certain management practices.
                               Carbon storage and biofuels can help to mitigate climate change.

Source: Summarized from EEA (2006) and Dwyer (2007)



In both EU countries and their competitors (US, Canada and New Zealand) the general
trend since 1990 has been one of increasing production on a reduced or near stable area of
land achieved by intensifying production. However, the overall intensity of production is
lower in the US, Canada and New Zealand than in most other OECD countries (but
increasing in some cases) whilst the intensity of production in most of the EU 15 is above
the OECD average (OECD 2004 and 2008)1.
     Soil erosion caused by both wind and water is increasingly problematic in both the US
and Canadian wheat belt whilst water-related erosion problems are found in New Zealand,
especially on steep land that has been cleared of natural vegetation.
     Excess nutrients appear to be an increasing problem in the EU competitor countries
with increases in both fertilizer use and livestock numbers resulting in overall nutrient
surpluses. Water pollution is a particular problem in some regions of the US and at local
levels in Canada and New Zealand. Greenpeace (2008) publicized record algal blooms
caused by fertilizer run-off in 2007 in Quebec and on Lake Winnipeg, Canada whilst
communities in New Brunswick and Quebec have been campaigning against Intensive
Livestock Operations (mainly hogs) due to problems of odour and water pollution. Similar
concerns have arisen from Concentrated Animal Feeding Operations (CAFOs) in parts of
the US. Such intensive livestock systems are also significant contributors to air pollution
1
 Some care must be taken when comparing average intensities. Production can be concentrated in certain regions
and be relatively intensive even in countries where the national average intensity of production is low e.g. France,
US, Canada.
                              Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context                 219

problems giving rise to odours and release of ammonia, leading to acidification, as well as
releasing greenhouse gases such as methane. Other water issues include excessive
groundwater extraction levels for irrigation particularly in some parts of the US.
     Strong similarities can therefore be identified between the EU and its competitors in
relation to agriculture‟s impact on natural resources of soil, air and water. But when it
comes to biodiversity and landscapes, it appears there are notable differences between the
two. While there appears to be an intrinsic relationship between farming, biodiversity and
landscapes in Europe, with many species and habitats having co-evolved with traditional
agricultural practices over time, in New Zealand and North America much of the
biodiversity value is associated with natural areas such as grasslands, wetlands, native
forests and bush. In other words, there appears to be a much stronger distinction between
land occupied by farming and „wild‟ or unmanaged land of high nature value. There is
however evidence of these natural habitats being threatened and declining as a result of
agricultural practices such as the conversion of natural grasslands and wetlands to cropland
(OECD, 2004). Similarly, whilst traditional farming practices in Europe have helped to
mould and shape the landscape over time giving rise to highly valued cultural landscapes,
the same cannot largely be said of the US, Canada and New Zealand.
     These brief insights suggest that policy makers in EU competitor countries are facing
some similar challenges to those in the EU when it comes to dealing with the impacts of
agriculture on the environment but also some distinctly different issues. So how are policy
makers in the EU and competitor countries addressing these problems? Are similar or
different policy mechanisms being used to meet both baseline and higher standards in
agriculture?

Approaches to meeting standards

The EU‟s response to the environmental problems resulting from increasingly intensive and
specialized production has been to introduce, over time, a series of measures designed to
prevent environmental damage and degradation on the one hand and incentivize positive
environmental action on the other, as discussed in Chapter 2. Environmental protection has
largely been the remit of regulation whilst maintaining existing environmental assets and
encouraging their enhancement has fallen mainly to incentives such as those offered under
agri-environment agreements. Cross compliance, as introduced by the 2003 CAP reforms, is
distinctive in creating a link between compliance with (mainly pre-existing) regulatory
standards and some CAP payments (all direct payments under Pillar I of the CAP and
payments under eight measures within Pillar II2). Non-compliance with standards can result
in the withdrawal of a proportion of or all a recipient‟s CAP payments. The objective of
establishing such a link between standards and payments is to promote sustainable
agriculture and help to prevent land abandonment. Cross compliance also establishes the
baseline standards that must be met before farmers can receive other incentive based
payments, such as agri-environment payments. It therefore marks an important boundary or
reference level between baseline environmental protection standards and higher standards
designed to achieve more demanding environmental outcomes.

2
  The CAP is comprised of two distinct Pillars. Pillar I consists of market measures and direct aids to farmers,
including the Single Farm Payment, and accounts for the largest share of the CAP budget. Pillar II consists of a
series of measures under the umbrella of the European Agricultural Fund for Rural Development (EAFRD)
including the agri-environment measure and the Less Favoured Area measure.
220      Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context

     The EU‟s choice of policy measures reflects two important principles that have become
enshrined in policy development – the „polluter pays principle‟ and „the provider gets
principle‟. Together, these principles help to explain the EU‟s approach to meeting
environmental standards in the agriculture sector. The former principle underpins much EU
environmental law and makes the case that a polluting party must pay for the damage done
to the natural environment or bear the costs associated with avoiding such damage. The
„provider gets principle‟ recognises that agriculture produces a range of environmental
goods and services that benefit society as a whole but which are insufficiently rewarded by
the market and hence underprovided. A desire to correct such market failure in relation to
the provision of environmental goods and services, many of which can be defined as „public
goods‟3, increasingly underpins the rationale for public intervention in the agriculture
sector. The „provider gets principle‟ is essentially the justification for payment of a wide
range of land management incentives today. Whilst both principles are not without their
problems, for example it is often difficult in practice to ensure that the polluter actually pays
rather than passing on costs to consumers or to determine the value of public goods and
therefore what the provider should receive for providing them, they are increasingly used to
determine the nature and manner of public sector interventions.
     The application of environmental legislation in the EU agriculture sector has evolved
over time. Directives concerning the conservation of wild birds4 and the protection of
groundwater from pollution5 date back to 1979 and 1980 respectively. In 1991, the Nitrates
Directive6 introduced restrictions regarding the application of manure in order to protect
water from pollution while more recently the Water Framework Directive 7 introduced a
range of controls in order to ensure that all water bodies achieve good ecological status by
2015. A framework Directive for Soil8 is also now under consideration but political
agreement has not yet been reached. Implementation of existing Directives by Member
States has had a somewhat chequered history requiring the European Commission, in
numerous cases, to open infringement procedures. Nonetheless, this growing body of
legislation suggests a willingness on the part of the EU institutions to regulate agricultural
activity in order to address key environmental problems. The more recent introduction of
cross compliance which, according to Regulation 1782/2003, serves to „incorporate in the
common market organizations basic standards for the environment, food safety, animal
health and welfare and good agricultural and environmental condition‟ is further evidence
of the desire by EU policy makers to achieve environmental protection and an example of
how compliance with standards can be reinforced through financial mechanisms. Indeed, the



3
  Public goods exhibit „non-rivalry‟ and „non-exclusiveness‟, meaning that one person‟s consumption of the
public good does not diminish its availability for another person (e.g. me gazing at a landscape does not stop
anyone else from doing so) and that it is not possible to restrict consumption to only those paying for it (e.g. I
don‟t have to pay to gaze at a landscape). This latter condition effectively means that there is no market
mechanism to reward producers of public goods, and so public goods are under-supplied.
4
  Council Directive 79/409/EEC of 2 April 1979 on the conservation of wild birds (OJ L103, 25.4.1979).
5
  Council Directive 80/68/EEC of 17 December 1979 on the protection of groundwater against pollution caused
by certain dangerous substances (OJ L 20, 26.1.1980).
6
  Council Directive 91/676/EEC of 12 December 1991 concerning the protection of waters against pollution
caused by nitrates from agricultural sources (OJ L 375, 31.12.1991).
7
  Directive 2000/60/EC of 23 October 2000 establishing a framework for Community action in the field of water.
OJ L327/1.
8
  COM (2006) 232 final. Proposal for a Directive establishing a framework for the protection of soil.
                           Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context           221

Commission‟s mid term review of the Sixth Community Action Programme 9 pointed to the
effectiveness of cross compliance in integrating environmental concerns into the CAP and
stated that the Commission would investigate whether this policy tool could also be applied
in other policy areas such as fisheries and transport.
     But as pressure to regulate the agriculture industry has increased so too have calls for
substantive reforms to the CAP and to increase support for public goods payments (BirdLife
International, 1997, 2007; WWF, 2008). These calls, which coincided with other internal
and external pressures for CAP reform, have led to progressive reforms to commodity
regimes (reductions in price support and the gradual decoupling of subsidies from
production) and the development of agri-environment policy. Agri-environment payments
constitute payments to farmers who undertake agri-environment commitments (which go
beyond mandatory standards) on a voluntary basis. Payments are intended to cover the
additional costs and any income foregone resulting from the commitment made. The first
EU wide agri-environment measures were introduced in 1986 as support for
environmentally sensitive areas and progressed to Regulation 2078/92 „the agri-
environment regulation‟ in 1992 as one of four „accompanying measures‟ to the MacSharry
CAP reform. Regulation 2078/92 later became part of a wider Rural Development
Regulation in 1999 and today, agri-environment payments are one component of the
European Agricultural Fund for Rural Development (EAFRD). This Fund also provides
support to improve the competitiveness of agriculture, the quality of life in rural areas and
the diversification of the rural economy. Agri-environment payments are not the only
environmental payments in EAFRD; other measures such as natural handicap payments in
mountain areas and Natura 2000 payments seek to encourage sustainable land management
and achieve environmental outcomes. The priority given to such measures and the funds
allocated to them varies considerably across the EU Member States but all countries must
implement agri-environment measures and the uptake of these measures by farmers has
increased substantially since their first introduction (EEA, 2005).
     In the EU, we can see that environmental legislation, cross compliance and agri-
environment measures are the main policy instruments used to respond to environmental
issues in the agriculture sector. Together, these measures are used to achieve a range of
environmental standards starting with environmental protection and rising to environmental
enhancement. Environmental regulation operates irrespective of any payments made to
farmers but cross compliance and agri-environment measures are central components of the
CAP and linked directly to certain payments to farmers. How does this compare with
approaches to meeting standards in EU competitor countries? Do other countries adopt
similar or different approaches to achieving environmental standards?

Meeting standards in EU competitor countries

The OECD (2004) notes that all OECD countries, including the US, Canada and New
Zealand, impose regulatory requirements to address the negative impacts of agriculture on
the environment, ranging from outright prohibitions to standards and resource use
requirements. The imposition of such requirements appears to have been extended or
developed since the early 1990s. In the same way that EU Member States are required to

9
 COM (2007) Communication from the Commission to the European Parliament, the Council, the European
Economic and Social Committee and the Committee of the Regions on the mid-term review of the Sixth
Community Environment Action Programme.
222    Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context

transpose and implement EU wide legislation at national and regional level, OECD states
that, „An increasing number of regulatory requirements derive from state, provincial,
regional or local measures under the framework of national umbrella legislation, in order to
accommodate the local nature of many environmental concerns.‟ The Nutrient Management
Act in Ontario and the Water Protection Plan in Manitoba, which set targets for N and P
levels and regulate some activities such as the timing of manure spreading to land, are two
examples of regulatory approaches applied at Provincial level in Canada to control water
pollution from intensive livestock operations (see Chapter 4 for further discussion of
regulatory approaches in Canada). In the US, the Clean Water Act requires CAFOs to have
pollution permits and implement nutrient management plans (see Chapter 3 for further
discussion). In New Zealand, water protection is driven by the Resource Management Act;
farmers themselves remain responsible for resource management and authorities charge
farmers in order to recover the costs associated with programs and applications (see Chapter
5 for further discussion).
     Beyond regulation, a range of different approaches to meeting environmental standards
are found in use in EU competitor countries (OECD, 2008). In Canada, the 2003-2008
Agriculture Policy Framework (APF) provides support to the farm sector under a range of
different programs. The APF consisted of five pillars, namely: business risk management,
food safety and quality, science and innovation, environment, and the renewal of the
agricultural sector. Under each of these, various financial instruments were made available
to support the agriculture sector. These included: income support under the Canadian
Agricultural Income Stabilization (CAIS) scheme; production insurance; the Canadian Food
Safety and Quality Program (CFSQP), under which it helped stakeholders to develop and
implement food safety, quality and traceability systems throughout the food chain; a science
and innovation programme including funding the sunrise industries engaged in the
production of new commodities and bio-products such as bio-fuels and bio-materials; and, a
number of environmental programs. Most APF agri-environmental programs are cost-shared
between the Federal, Provincial and Territorial Governments. Programs under the
environment chapter of the APF provide producers with assistance to improve their
environmental management of soil, water, land and biodiversity by cost-sharing activities
such as technical assistance, extension, research and demonstration activities.
     In the US, agri-environment programs – also based on cost-sharing – form a growing
component of agriculture policy. Measures such as the Conservation Reserve Program
(CRP) and Wetlands Reserve Program (WRP) help farmers to remove highly erodable land
(HEL) from production and protect and restore wetlands respectively. To achieve this, the
programs fund the purchase of long-term agreements or conservation easements. A
conservation easement is a legal agreement by which landowners convey certain property
rights to a qualified entity for safekeeping, usually for a set period of time or sometimes in
perpetuity. The easement essentially prohibits certain activities such as development, cutting
down forests or converting wetlands to cropland. In recent years, greater emphasis has been
given to supporting conservation practices. The Conservation Security Program (CSP)
introduced in 2002 – renamed as the Conservation Stewardship Program by the 2008 Farm
Bill – pays farmers who have met a high standard for environmental performance to adopt
or maintain practices to further enhance environmental performance, such as improving soil
and water quality or wildlife habitat. Meanwhile, the Grassland Reserve Program (GRP)
introduced in 2003 aims to preserve and improve native grass species. Technical advice and
research to support sustainable farming is also funded under the Farm Bill. Cross
                        Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context   223

compliance is also in evidence in the US; the basic principle of making a link „across
programs‟ originated there during the late 1970s as a way of enforcing set-aside
requirements (Baldock and Mitchell, 1995). Today, in order to receive payments under
commodity programs such as price support loans and income support payments farmers
must implement soil conservation systems („sodbuster‟) on HEL and refrain from draining
wetlands („swampbuster‟). In the same way that EU farmers can have payments under the
Single Payment Scheme withheld for non-compliance, violations of either sod- or
swampbuster result in the loss of all USDA programme payments for that year and for the
years that follow.
     New Zealand differs most among the countries under consideration here possibly as a
result of having moved furthest towards a fully liberalized agriculture sector. Producer
support represented just 2% of agriculture receipts as calculated by the OECD Producer
Support Estimate (PSE) compared to an OECD average of 30% of receipts. Of the support
given to agriculture, expenditure on agri-environment measures accounted for 15% in 2004
and 90% of this was spent on research and education (OECD, 2008). Consultation, outreach
and education appear to be having an impact although significant environmental problems
remain – especially soil erosion and water pollution – and new approaches are now being
tested. Nutrient flows from agriculture into Lake Taupo were such that in 2004 national and
local governments agreed to fund a total package of nearly NZ$82 (US$54) million to limit
nutrient flows through restrictions on land use and allowing nitrogen trading to occur.
Similar to easements, conservation covenants are used in New Zealand to protect a variety
of open spaces, including forest remnants, wetlands, lakes, peat lakes, coastline, tussock
grasslands, from damaging agricultural practices. Some farmers have entered into open
space covenant agreements through the QE II National Trust, a statutory body with a budget
of NZ$4 million. The Trust provides limited support to protect certain areas of farmers‟
land while they retain ownership. Community based approaches – known as Landcare or
conservation clubs – are also found in New Zealand as they are increasingly in Canada and
the US. Such approaches which encourage collective action to solve environmental
problems, „…tend to take advantage of farmers‟ self-interest in environmental conservation
and make use of local expertise in solving environmental problems‟ (OECD, 2004).

Lessons learned from EU competitor countries

This brief analysis suggests a number of similarities in approaches to achieving
environmental standards in the EU and some of its competitor countries. Environmental
regulation is in evidence in all countries although there may well be differences in the
intensity of regulation reflecting, amongst other factors, differences in the intensity of
agriculture. Forms of cross compliance can be found in both the US and EU with the threat
of payment withdrawal being used as leverage to secure compliance with standards. There
also appears to have been an expansion over the last decade or more of agri-environment
type measures in both EU and non-EU countries although the exact nature of these varies
from place to place. There are, however, some notable differences between the EU and the
countries examined. In the US, the buying out of property rights through the extensive use
of conservation easements is notable. OECD (2001) suggests that conservation easements
may be attractive policy instruments because, „…purchasing a partial interest in a parcel of
land is less costly than acquiring full ownership and they involve voluntary transaction.
Moreover, compared with conservation incentives (taxes or annual payments) or tradeable
224      Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context

development rights, easements do not entail large administrative burdens.‟ Cost-sharing
programs and technical assistance are much more prevalent in the likes of the US, Canada
and New Zealand whilst in the latter, the low level of agricultural support generally appears
to have resulted in much greater emphasis on research, education and technology transfer.
However, growing concerns regarding water pollution in New Zealand is leading to
innovative approaches such as the introduction of tradeable nitrogen permits. Community
based approaches are also increasingly predominant in the non-EU countries examined.
     Examining policy approaches used in other countries can often be instructive; new
ideas can be gained and lessons can be learned from experiences elsewhere – although the
scope for transferring approaches needs to be considered carefully given the context-
specific nature of environmental problems and solutions (Bemelmans-Videc et al., 2003;
Dolowitz and Marsh, 2000; Evans, 2006). In principle, some of the approaches used to help
meet environmental standards in other countries could be relevant to the EU agriculture
sector. The greater emphasis in other countries on research, education and technology
transfer as well as cost-sharing programs and the use of easements to buy out property rights
is of particular interest. Such approaches may warrant further consideration in an EU
context, especially if reforms to the EU Budget and the CAP constrain the use of existing
policy mechanisms. It is to these issues that we turn next.

Future prospects for meeting standards in EU agriculture

The CAP faces adjustments and reforms urged on by a combination of both internal
pressures (such as consumer demands and budget constraints) and external ones (such as
World Trade Organization (WTO) negotiations and world food demand). Such reforms may
have wider implications for how environmental standards are achieved in the agricultural
sector in future. With this is mind, this section reviews the main drivers of, and
opportunities for, CAP reform, the possible policy outcomes that might emerge and their
implications for meeting environmental standards. The extent to which the EU could adopt
approaches to meeting standards used elsewhere is also considered briefly.

‘New challenges’ for EU agriculture

The CAP Health Check was only the most recent in a long line of reforms to EU agriculture
policy. Launched by the European Commission on 20 November 200710, and concluded
with an agreement on 20 November 200811, part of the justification for the reform proposals
was explained as the need to meet „new challenges‟ including: climate change; bioenergy;
water management and biodiversity. Agriculture was noted as having the potential to help
mitigate climate change but also being „highly exposed‟ to it. Equally, it was suggested that
without sustainable water management, „…the pressure on the quantity and quality of water
for agriculture will increase considerably.‟ Agriculture‟s key role in the protection of
biodiversity was also highlighted as was the EU‟s likely failure to meet a commitment to
halt declines in biodiversity by 2010. The strengthening of rural development policy
(EAFRD) and cross compliance were both agreed as responses to these challenges, through
increased funding for EAFRD and changes to Good Agricultural and Environmental
10
   COM (2007) 722 Communication from the Commission to the Council and Parliament „Preparing for the
“Health Check” of the CAP reform‟
11
   See http://ec.europa.eu/agriculture/healthcheck/index_en.htm for further details of the reform agreement
                           Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context         225

Condition (GAEC) within cross compliance. In other words, both regulatory and incentive
based approaches were proposed, until 2013 at least, as part of a strategic approach to
meeting standards in agriculture and promoting sustainable agriculture. Whilst this reform is
likely to make a valuable contribution to addressing the environmental challenges identified
by the Commission much remains to be done. Issues such as climate change and better
water management are likely to continue as key drivers of future policy developments.
     Environmental challenges are not the only ones likely to drive EU agriculture policy
and production standards in future. The prospects of a growing world population and
increasing demand for food have led to growing concerns about food security and a so-
called „food challenge‟. FAO (2002) estimates that global food production needs to rise by
50% by 2030, and to double by 2050, to meet the demand of a rising world population and
supply the eating habits of the growing middle classes in emerging economies. ELO (2008)
suggests that improving productivity to meet these demands and, at the same time,
improving the condition of the natural environment, is the „Great 21st Century Land
Challenge‟ and continues:

          „During the next half Century the world faces fundamentally two challenges – to
          produce enough food for the growing numbers of, on average, better-off and longer
          living people, and at the same time to preserve natural resources and the
          environment. The two challenges are closely interrelated, and each is heightened
          by climate change. The EU is of such a scale that we must play a responsible role
          in rising to these two challenges. Furthermore, food and environment are core
          competences of the EU.‟

The EU budget review

While the Health Check dealt with some rather specific issues which required adjustment to
the CAP in the short term, a much broader debate about the future of the CAP as a whole is
already in train. A key process driving this debate is the EU Budget Review which was
initiated with the launch of a document entitled, „Reforming the budget, changing Europe‟ 12
and the opening of a public consultation which ran until April 2008.
     The Budget Review owes its origins to heated discussions at the EU Summit in
December 2005 when the last EU financial framework for 2007-2013 was agreed. Tony
Blair, the then UK Prime Minister, raised fundamental questions about EU spending
priorities and attacked the CAP in particular, much to the displeasure of France, historically
a staunch defender of the policy. As a result, in May 2006, an Inter-institutional Agreement
on budgetary discipline and sound financial management between the European Parliament,
the Council and the Commission invited the Commission:

           „…to undertake a full, wide ranging review covering all aspects of EU spending,
          including the CAP, and of resources, including the UK rebate, to report in 2008/9‟.

     The Budget Review asks some fundamental questions about European spending and, in
particular, the effectiveness, efficiency and transparency of budget delivery. The outcomes
of this Review are likely to determine the next EU financial framework for 2014 onwards.
12
   Communication from the Commission, reforming the Budget, Changing Europe: A Public Consultation Paper
in View of the 2008/2009 Budget Review, SEC (2007) 1188, 12.9.2007
226     Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context

     Discussions about the CAP, within the context of the budget, may well be far reaching
with questions asked about the overall purpose of the CAP and the benefits achieved by
currently spending 47% of the total EU budget on agriculture. This expenditure finds itself
increasingly in competition with other demands on the EU budget. Some commentators
(IEEP, 2007b) consider it is almost a given that the CAP budget will be cut in real terms in
2013. If this is the case, the CAP of the future may look rather different to the CAP of
today. One of the strongest arguments for the continuation of CAP funding and for public
payments to farmers is that it is needed to ensure the provision of public goods such as the
maintenance of wildlife species and habitats and the management of landscapes.
Responding to the „new challenges‟, such as climate change, is also seen as a legitimate
reason for providing certain kinds of support to the agriculture sector. Equally, there may be
arguments for support to achieve social objectives within rural areas, although not
necessarily targeted at the agricultural community as has largely been the case in the past.
As the objectives of the CAP are debated so too will be the means of achieving such
objectives. The extent to which objectives (and standards) can be achieved through the
current framework of CAP Pillar I versus Pillar II payments, or whether alternative
approaches are needed, are likely to be the focus of much discussion. Possible prospects for
the CAP beyond 2013 are discussed below.

The WTO negotiations

The future direction of the CAP, as well as being determined by internal processes such as
the Budget Review, may also be influenced in the longer term by external processes such as
the WTO negotiations.
     A new round of trade negotiations began in November 2001 – the so-called Doha
Development Agenda. The Doha Declaration defines the basis for these negotiations and
includes commitments to:

     Substantial reductions in trade-distorting domestic support.
     Substantial improvements in market access.
     Reductions of, with a view to phasing out, all forms of export subsidies.

     In August 2004, a Framework Agreement was reached that committed WTO members
to eliminate „…by a credible end date, all forms of export subsidisation and for the
progressive reductions of tariffs and domestic support‟. Negotiations to define the necessary
details of such an agreement have been protracted. Negotiations were suspended in July
2006 and later resumed, only to end again without agreement in July 2008. In July 2009,
WTO agriculture negotiators began technical work to identify what data will be needed and
how to present it when members eventually draft their commitments to cut agricultural
tariffs, open up quotas, reduce domestic support and scrap export subsidies. For a large
majority of the WTO‟s 151 members, major reductions to trade-distorting farm support in
rich countries remains central to delivering on the development dimension of the round.
     The outcome of these negotiations could yet have implications for future EU
agriculture policy and may have some bearing on any CAP reform decisions beyond 2013.
Potter (2007) has suggested that, in order to satisfy Green Box requirements (for non-trade
distorting subsidies), CAP Pillar I payments may need to become more like basic agri-
environment type payments justified on the grounds of delivering public goods rather than
                              Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context                227

income support. Potter has also suggested that with an agreement in place, and assuming the
EU secured recognition for Geographical Indications (GIs)13, Europe‟s farmers, or at least
some of them, are likely to focus on producing high-value products rather than
undifferentiated commodities. In this context, production standards would be of particular
importance, albeit many of them driven by market requirements rather than legislation.
Under such a scenario, Potter suggests cross compliance could be expected to migrate to a
limited system underpinning other types of payment.

The CAP beyond 2013: future prospects for meeting standards in
agriculture

New food and environmental challenges, pressure on the EU budget and a possible new
global trade deal all suggest that further reform of the CAP is inevitable. Whilst the nature
of any reform is, at this stage, difficult to judge, numerous ideas and proposals have been
put forward by farming and environmental stakeholders, some EU Member States, think
tanks and academics (see for example BirdLife International, 2007; COPA-COGECA,
2008; Ministry of Agriculture, Nature and Food Quality, 2008; Notre Europe, 2008;
Swinnen, 2009). Ideas range from maintaining the existing CAP structure but „greening‟
Pillar I income support by adding new environmental conditions to phasing out such support
over time and substantially increasing support under Pillar II, with numerous other
variations. The European Commission is expected to issue a communication in mid 2010
setting out some possible options for CAP reform beyond 2013.
     The future prospects for meeting environmental standards in agriculture are therefore
rather uncertain at this stage. It is expected however that the CAP budget will be reduced
and this will have a significant influence on future funding streams under Pillars I and II and
what can be achieved through those Pillars in terms of environmental standards. These
issues can be helpfully explored by considering possible alternative directions for a future
common EU agriculture policy14. What follows are three possible alternatives for CAP
reform based on a combination of changes to the budget and to Pillars I and II. For each of
these, the implications for meeting standards and addressing environmental challenges are
considered. The suggestions put forward are described as „reform options‟ but are intended
to be illustrative only and to help think through CAP reform implications.

CAP reform option 1

-    The Pillar I budget is reduced moderately e.g. by no more than 20-30%.

13
   Geographical indications are place names (in some countries also words associated with a place) used to
identify the origin and quality, reputation or other characteristics of products (for example, „Champagne‟,
„Tequila‟ or „Roquefort‟). Only products produced in the defined areas and with the relevant characteristics can be
labelled accordingly and the measure effectively protects products from unfair competition. There is extensive
debate within the WTO regarding GIs and the level of protection afforded to such products with the EU arguing
that registered GIs must be protected in other WTO member countries.
14
   The ideas discussed here draw on previous work on policy options for cross compliance. See: Farmer, M. and
Swales, V. (2007), „Future Policy Options for Cross Compliance‟. Background paper 26 April 2007 Cross
Compliance Policy Seminar Brussels. D23 of the Cross Compliance Network. IEEP, London; Farmer, M. and
Swales, V (2007) Facilitating the CAP Reform: compliance and competitiveness of European agriculture. Project
No SSPE-CT-2005-006489. D15: Guidelines for policy: A Policy Perspective on Cross Compliance and
Competitiveness.
228     Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context

-     All direct payments are fully decoupled from production and paid as a flat-rate area
      payment.
-     As a result of these changes, the value of the direct payment is less than at present.
-     Pillar II continues largely as at present in terms of content and budget allocation.

Under this option, income support payments in the form of the SPS are reduced but remain
significant. With no expansion of Pillar II and hence constraints on how far „good‟
environmental behaviour can be incentivised, EU policy makers may look to strengthened
regulation and enhanced conditionality of Pillar I in order to address key environmental
problems. This is essentially a „greening‟ of Pillar I. Cross compliance therefore remains a
key policy tool for achieving environmental standards under this option.
     Possible options for enhancing cross compliance could include the addition of new
items of EU environmental legislation, such as the Water Framework Directive (WFD) to
the SMRs under cross compliance. GAEC, in particular, could be broadened with new
standards added, such as those that are normally associated with agri-environment schemes
e.g. requirements for farmers to set-aside a proportion of their land as „ecological
compensation areas‟. In such areas conservation land uses, including buffer strips alongside
features, would be required. This could be a simple way to manage environmental pressures
such as soil erosion, diffuse agricultural pollution and biodiversity conservation. This could
provide a replacement for compulsory set-aside which was abolished following the CAP
Health Check. Issues such as climate change and better water management could also be
further incorporated in GAEC.

CAP reform option 2

-     The Pillar I budget at EU level is reduced substantially e.g. by about 60%. Direct
      payments that remain are targeted to farms in marginal areas where land abandonment
      would result in detrimental impacts to the environment.
-     The Pillar II budget is increased substantially from savings made by reducing the Pillar
      I budget (but Pillar II does not receive a full transfer of funds allowing some reduction
      in the overall CAP budget).
-     Rural development measures are expanded to combat climate change, achieve better
      water management and reverse declines in biodiversity.

Under this option, Pillar I is reduced substantially. Only farming systems located in High
Nature Value or more marginal farming areas, where the consequences of land
abandonment would result in significant loss of public goods, receive any basic income
support. Farmers in receipt of targeted direct payments must comply with the requirements
of EU legislation (as must all farmers) and adhere to some basic environmental standards
appropriate to the type of farming systems being supported (e.g. in relation to appropriate
stocking densities). Baseline standards must also be met by farmers who wish to claim Pillar
II payments. Whilst the rationale for CAP support may be easier to justify under this option,
some potential problems are likely to arise. Cross compliance no longer becomes an
effective mechanism since relatively few farmers qualify for the direct payment and Pillar II
measures are voluntary. The leverage cross compliance offered to encourage compliance
with standards over a large area of farmland therefore declines requiring policy makers to
use alternative mechanisms. The enforcement of legislation by relevant inspection bodies
                        Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context   229

and the application of fines become increasingly important under this Scenario. As in EU
competitor countries such as the US, Canada and New Zealand, greater emphasis on
research, extension and advice could be used to help to support compliance with legislation,
for example helping to identify best and least cost management practices. There could also
be a case for the introduction of cost-sharing programs where the burden of legislation was
felt to be too great in some situations. Such programs could potentially be funded from the
Pillar I budget. Options such as tradeable permits for nitrogen use could also be explored.

CAP reform option 3

-   The CAP budget is reduced by 30-50%; the CAP now consists of only one Pillar and is
    re-named the EU‟s Common Rural and Environment Policy (CREP) or similar.
-   The CREP does not provide income support for farmers, beyond providing a minimum
    safety net mechanism, supplemented by private risk insurance.
-   CREP payments are designed to deliver specific public benefits and support the
    sustainable development of rural areas.

Under this option, the rationale for public payments to the agriculture sector is one of
delivering public goods. Payments can only be made for activities that go beyond
mandatory requirements set by legislation. The emphasis of payments is likely to be on
maintaining existing environmental assets and achieving environmental enhancement
beyond the baseline. Particular emphasis would be given to addressing key environmental
challenges such as climate change, bioenergy, better water management and biodiversity. As
for Option 2, the enforcement of baseline legislation would rely on systematic inspection
and the application of fines. With no Pillar I or cross compliance mechanism to draw on,
standards are achieved through application of the polluter pays principle on the one hand
and the provider gets principle on the other. However, additional support to improve
compliance with legislation and promote best practice, e.g. through advice and training,
could be provided through CREP. Environmental taxation, e.g. a nitrogen or pesticide tax,
could also be considered as a means of achieving environmental protection. Community
based approaches to land management could also be explored, possibly supported and
facilitated through CREP funding.
     What these three options help to demonstrate is that different budgetary outcomes and
changes to the architecture of the CAP Pillars have implications for the choice of public
policy instruments employed to help meet environmental and other standards in the EU
agriculture sector. Environmental regulation is likely to remain important in all cases, and
may need to be strengthened, but the scope for use of cross compliance and incentive
payments varies depending on the outcomes of any reforms regarding Pillars I and II. The
application of other policy instruments such as information provision (including research,
extension and advice), capacity building (including training and facilitating community
based land management), alternative market incentives (such as tax breaks, taxes, user
charges and conservation easements) and market based instruments (such as tradeable
quotas and permits) may become increasingly necessary. Instruments and policy approaches
used in EU competitor countries may have some relevance in the EU and warrant further
consideration.
230    Meeting standards in EU agriculture: the changing policy context

Conclusions

The EU is not alone in needing to employ policy measures to address environmental
problems in the agriculture sector. Competitor countries such as the US, Canada and New
Zealand are also experiencing the negative impacts of intensive agriculture on soil, air,
water and biodiversity resources and face the same needs to address issues such as climate
change. Similarities in policy approaches are apparent in all countries, for example, the use
of regulation to prevent damaging activities and to set minimum standards for water or air
quality. But beyond regulation, the approaches employed appear to differ, owing something
to the underlying policy framework and the nature of support given to the agriculture sector.
In all cases, policy makers appear to rely not just on one measure but on a suite of measures
to achieve standards in agriculture, perhaps a reflection of the complex interactions between
agriculture and the environment, and of the challenge of achieving sustainable food
production.
     Within the EU, the CAP plays a dominant role in helping to achieve environmental
standards (both baseline and higher standards) within the agriculture sector through
instruments such as cross compliance and Pillar II measures. These are underpinned by a
substantive body of environmental legislation. But the CAP faces possible reform beyond
2013 as a result of budgetary pressures and the need to respond to new food and
environmental challenges. The CAP of the future could look substantially different from the
CAP of today.
     The three CAP reform options presented here show the relationship between CAP
payments and the delivery of standards is rather delicate. Changes to the CAP may
determine the extent to which policy mechanisms such as cross compliance remain relevant
or appropriate to achieving such standards in future. As long as income support payments
remain, the principle of making a link between such payments and compliance with certain
standards remains valid. Farmers have an incentive to comply with standards or face
withdrawal or loss of payments. If such payments were significantly reduced or abolished,
the link would be weakened and other means of achieving compliance with desirable
standards, such as regulation, information provision (including research, extension and
advice) and market based instruments, are likely to become more important. The level of
funding remaining for Pillar II type measures will also be critical in determining the extent
to which farmers can be incentivised to meet certain standards, especially those which go
beyond baseline regulatory requirements. Experiences in EU competitor countries, and the
policy approaches employed to meet standards, are highly relevant in the EU context and
deserve further consideration. However, the scope for transferring approaches needs to be
given careful consideration due to the context-specific nature of environmental problems
and solutions.
     As pressure on our natural resources increases – from factors such as population
growth, changing consumer demands and climate change – our response to protecting those
resources and ensuring their sustainable use must grow ever more sophisticated and
effective. Setting, promoting and achieving compliance with standards is an essential part of
the future challenge for global agriculture.
