Chapter 14

The pollution haven hypothesis and the location
of livestock production: two North American
case studies

Alfons Weersink



Introduction

The previous chapters have examined how policies targeted toward addressing
environmental concerns within agriculture have impacted various regions and commodity
sectors. This chapter builds upon those comparisons by assessing the influence of
environmental regulation on the location decisions for livestock production within a local
region (south-western Ontario, Canada) and at a national scale (US). The North American
livestock sector has experienced a geographic concentration of production in fewer regions
and a shift in production to areas with little prior livestock experience. One of the reasons
may be the increasingly important role of the processing sector and the integration of this
sector back into production. Processing plants operating under economies of size are
becoming larger and fewer, and scattered around the country with clusters of livestock
farms around them (Apland and Anderson 1996; Abdalla et al., 1995). Such clusters tend to
move to localities with better natural endowments, labour market conditions, and business
environment due to agglomeration economies or tax policies (Roe et al., 2002).
     Changes in the spatial distribution of livestock production may also be directly affected
by differences in the stringency of environmental regulations across administrative regions.
The spatial differences in environmental pressures might create „pollution havens‟ where
lenient regulations in some regions may attract livestock producers to build their facilities in
such localities. It is possible that by introducing or maintaining lax environmental
regulations relative to competing regions and allowing tardy enforcement of those
regulations, one region can lure „dirty‟ industry investments, which are important in
employment creation and regional economic development (Kunce and Shogren, 2002;
Levinson, 2000; Jafee et al., 1995). If governments really do engage in a race to the bottom,
certain regions would have an inefficiently high number of livestock operations. Because
the assimilative capacity of the environment is deliberately undervalued in a region where a
race to the bottom has occurred, the heavier concentration of livestock farms is the cost to
the local community.
     Both US and Canada allow national and sub-national governments to enact relatively
independent policies in the environmental sector. A disparity in regulatory stringency
among US states arose in the 1980s when the federal government delegated the function of
devising regulatory regimes to state authorities (Kraft and Vig, 1994; Levinson, 2000).

©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         233
234     The pollution haven hypothesis and the location of livestock production

Martin and Zering (1997) argue that the differences in regulations across states in the US
were responsible for the shift in large-scale intensive pork production to southern states
such as North Carolina and Arkansas. While there are differences in regulatory stringency
across provinces in Canada as across states in the US, there also existed differences across
lower tier governments within a province. For example, local municipalities in Ontario until
recently could directly influence the form and location of livestock barns through controls
on the land use and the issuance of building permits. This decentralized regulatory approach
resulted in a range of environmental laws with some municipalities going to the extent of
imposing caps on livestock numbers while neighbouring ones may have had few restrictions
(FitzGibbon et al., 2002). The difference in environmental pressure across jurisdictions
presents a natural experiment to test the pollution haven hypothesis in agriculture.
     This chapter reviews the results of two studies examining the factors affecting farm
location with specific emphasis on the existence of pollution havens in the livestock sector.
One study by Weersink and Eveland (2006) examines the impact of differing municipal by-
laws at the local level in south-western Ontario. The other study by Herath et al. (2004)
determines the effect of environmental regulations on changes in the spatial distribution of
production levels of hogs, dairy and fed-cattle by state in the US. The chapter begins by
discussing the conceptual framework on factors affecting farm location that underlies both
studies. The results at the local level are then presented and followed by the national
analysis. Implications for the influence of environmental pressures of farm location are then
discussed.

Conceptual framework

The local and national studies both employ a similar framework to determine what
influences farm location. In both studies, livestock intensity is assumed to be a function of
four major categories of explanatory variables:

     the degree of environmental regulations facing farmers;
     relative output and input prices;
     the attractiveness of a region for a firm considering locating in the area or business
      climate; and
     agglomeration economies and regional support for farming.

The following sub-section defines how livestock intensity is measured in each of the two
studies. The specific factors within each of the major categories of explanatory variables are
then defined along with their hypothesized effects on farm location.

Dependent variable

Spatial production changes can be captured by the number of new farms or the intensity of
production within a region. Bartik (1988) argued that aggregate measures of regional
economic activity, such as inventory levels, reflect a number of different types of economic
decisions by agents. Production levels can change due to the expansion or contraction of
existing facilities, the introduction of new facilities, or the closing of old ones. Since new
firms considering locating in a region tend to face harsher environmental constraints than
existing firms due to grandfathering arrangements, the opening up of new facilities will be
                    The pollution haven hypothesis and the location of livestock production   235

lower in a region with more stringent environmental regulations (Bartik, 1988). Measuring
farm location through new barn building or inventory change is one aspect of overall farm
structure that may include factors such as farm size distribution and marketing
arrangements.

Local analysis

The local study follows the recommendation by Bartik and uses primary data on building
permits granted to individual farmers to build an addition to an existing barn and build a
new barn. Such permits are required for all new buildings, primarily to ensure construction
codes are met for safety but increasingly as a means of enforcing environmental restrictions.
Although over 200 municipalities within 8 counties of the traditional livestock production
region of south-western Ontario were contacted, usable results were obtained for 42
municipalities. Of those municipalities that did provide data, 1,424 building permits were
issued between 1996 and 2001. Over 40% of these permits were for swine facilities with
approximately 26% for dairy barns and 19% for poultry operations. The majority of these
permits (996) were for new barns while only 30% represented additions. Over 75% of the
permits issued for the swine (428) and poultry (221) sectors were for new barns. The
majority of permits for both new facilities and additions (760) were for barns less than 100
livestock units (LU) but most of the permits issued for the swine sector were for facilities
larger than 100 LU. Further descriptive statistics on the barn permits is in Eveland et al.
(2005).
     The focus of the analysis is on the number of permits that were granted per 10,000 ha
of farmland by each municipality. The average value is 11.5 permits per 10,000 ha with the
high density building areas tending to be located in the traditional livestock producing
municipalities of Perth and Huron counties. In addition to the density of total barn building
permits, two other dependent variables were considered in the local study. One measured
the density of permits for new facilities and the other counted the density of permits (new
and additions) for swine barns. The former is used to assess whether environmental
regulations are tougher for new operations which also tend to be more mobile and thus more
sensitive to such regulations. The density of permits for swine operations is used to
determine whether differing municipal by-laws are particularly effective in altering location
decisions of hog farms for which public opposition is most vocal.

National analysis

While the number of new livestock operations may be the best measure of regional
production changes due to environmental laws, it is not available for an extended period of
time at a larger, aggregate level. Thus, livestock inventory is used as the measure of spatial
production in the national US study. Data on hog, dairy and fed-cattle production levels
from 1975 to 2000 were collected for each of the 48 contiguous states. In order to control
for cyclical fluctuations of livestock inventories, annual inventory shares for each of the
three livestock sectors from 1976 to 2000 were collected resulting in 25 observations over
time for each state for each livestock sub-sector.
     The hog and dairy sectors in the US are increasingly siting production towards western
states and away from traditional production regions in the east. In contrast, production
levels in the fed cattle sector have increased over the last generation only in the three main
236     The pollution haven hypothesis and the location of livestock production

producing states (Texas, Kansas and North Dakota). The spatial changes in US livestock
inventory over time are described further in Herath et al. (2004).

Explanatory variables

Environmental stringency

The weakness of most previous studies examining the effect of regulatory pressures on farm
location is the measurement of environmental stringency. Both the local and national studies
have constructed unique variables on the environmental pressure facing livestock farmers.

Local analysis

Environmental stringency at the municipal level in Weersink and Eveland (2006) is proxied
by five possible explanatory variables surrounding the possible requirements that must be in
place for any proposed livestock barn to get a building permit:

     required manure storage days;
     minimum distance from waterway;
     minimum lot size;
     nutrient management plan (NMP) requirements based on density; and
     trigger for expansion.

An increase in the number of manure storage days required by new livestock facilities
allows the producer to apply waste less frequently, but it also means a greater initial
investment for new livestock facilities, which could deter farmers from starting their
business in certain areas. The minimum distance from the waterway and minimum lot size
for new barns imply the farmer must set aside a greater amount of land in construction.
Increasing the distance between livestock operations and neighbours may increase financial
costs and thus decreases the likelihood of barn construction occurring but it may also reduce
the likelihood of conflicts that represent future monetary and non-monetary costs to the
farmer. The effect of the stocking density restriction from the NMP depends on land
availability. If the constraint is binding, the cost of the NMP is the amount of land required
multiplied by the land rental rate.
     The two remaining potential environmental stringency variables in the local study relate
to the triggers at which producers must complete a nutrient management plan (NMP) as part
of receiving a building permit. A NMP indicates the sources, uses, and application
testing/methods of organic and inorganic nutrients on the farm, and represents a significant
cost to farmers, particularly smaller farms. The trigger is defined here in terms of the
density of the proposed facility expressed in animal nutrient units. The lower the trigger
value for a municipality, the greater the chance the farmer will have to file a NMP for a
given size of operation and thus the less likely they are to build within the municipality.
While it varied across municipalities, a common trigger that is currently used for the
standard provincially is 300 animal nutrient units (NU) with 6 finishing pigs representing
one NU. A final dummy variable was defined depending if the municipality had a trigger for
the requirement of a NMP.
                    The pollution haven hypothesis and the location of livestock production   237

National analysis

The national study by Herath et al. (2005) constructed a single regulatory stringency
measure for each state over time rather than use a one-period cross sectional stringency
measure as has been done in other studies testing the pollution haven hypothesis in
agriculture. State values of 14, one-period environmental stringency measures developed for
various years by alternative studies were gathered. Examples of such indices include the
Green Index by Hall and Kerr (1991) and per capita environmental quality control
expenditure by state from the US Dept of Commerce. A measure for the year 2000 for all
states was calculated using the approach Metcalfe (2000) employed in estimating regulatory
pressures facing farmers for a subset of states in 1994 and 1998. The presence (or absence)
of seven regulations for each of the 48 states were summed to form the 2000 stringency
index. In order to capture the temporal changes of regulatory stringency across states, the
individual state values for all the above indices were normalized by dividing through with
the arithmetic mean of the respective index with missing years estimated through
interpolation of nearby years. The normalized index value represents the position of the
state relative to the arithmetic mean of each index and allows the different indices to be
combined into a single regulatory variable with a consistent scale measure.

Relative prices

Increasing output prices or decreasing input prices can both increase relative profitability,
which is assumed to be one of the main drivers of the production expansion and spatial
distribution of livestock operations. For example, the livestock to feed price ratio has been
shown to positively affect spatial distribution of livestock production in the US. Previous
studies have also found firms are more likely to locate in regions with lower energy costs
(McConnell and Schwab, 1990) and lower labour costs (Levinson, 2000). Relative output to
feed price indices at the state level are used for the US study but are not appropriate for the
local Ontario study since producers in this relatively small geographic area face the same
output prices for their livestock as most are either marketed under a supply management
scheme (dairy and poultry) or a pooling system (swine).
     The one price used in both studies is the value of farmland. Studies assessing spatial
distribution of national livestock production have found evidence of inventories shifting to
locations with cheaper land prices and thus cheaper construction costs (Mo and Abdalla,
1998). This effect within a local area may be countered by the lower feed costs associated
with higher-yielding, and consequently, higher-priced farmland. Thus, the a priori effect of
farmland value on the density of barn permits granted is ambiguous.

General business climate

Business climate variables capture the receptiveness of the community towards a new firm
locating in the region and the attractiveness of the region for the prospective firm. The
socio-economic variables that could influence the establishment of a livestock operation
include: population, unemployment rate, education and wealth.
     Increasing the number of people and businesses can increase the amount of available
labour, increase the demand for associated products, and reduce costs by increasing the
extent of public infrastructure (Eberts and McMillen, 1999). However, the increasing role
238    The pollution haven hypothesis and the location of livestock production

of the „not in my backyard‟ (NIMBY) attitude is expected to dominate any positive
economies of urbanization so that population density is hypothesized to have a negative
effect on livestock production density.
     The level of unemployment can have an influence on farm location through labour
supply and the receptiveness of the community towards new livestock operations. A region
with a high unemployment rate is more likely to have excess labour available to work in
agriculture. In addition, areas with higher unemployment may seek livestock operations as a
means to generate economic opportunities.
     The skill set of the workforce as proxied by the education level has been found to be a
major attraction for new non-agricultural firms locating in a given region (Bartik, 1988;
McConnell and Schwab, 1990). While increasing the education level may increase the
productivity of potential farm labourers, education is expected to have a negative influence
on the density of barn permits issued. A more educated population is more likely to be
concerned about environmental quality and thus more likely to be aware about potential
negative externalities associated with livestock production.
     Another variable related to the NIMBY hypothesis is wealth. Wealth is measured in
terms of average dwelling cost and average per capita income. The environmental Kuznets
curve suggests the value placed on environmental quality increases with income. Thus,
wealthier regions will not want potentially polluting industries in their backyard and have
access to the resources to fight proposed and present livestock operations. Furthermore,
higher income regions can rely on other sources of economic growth besides livestock
production. Thus, wealth and income are assumed to be negatively related to the density of
building permits.

Livestock infrastructure and support

Agglomeration economies refer to the positive effects that a farm can experience as a result
of having a high concentration of other farms in the region (Eberts and McMillen, 1999).
For example, Roe et al. (2002) found agglomeration economies and market access to be the
significant factors influencing the number of hogs raised at the county level in the US
Midwest. Agglomeration economies were also found by Isik (2004) to be important
determinants of changes in production levels in the US dairy sector. The effects include
lower input prices from suppliers and lower transaction costs in finding those suppliers who
are more likely to be concentrated in livestock intensive regions. In addition, there is greater
access to information on production and management practices in regions with a greater
concentration of farmers.
     In the local Canadian study, infrastructure is measured by the density of hog, dairy, and
beef production in the municipality in 1996 as measured by the Canadian agricultural
census and the growth in these livestock sectors between the 1991 and 1996 census years.
The direct and indirect support for agriculture was also measured by three variables
attempting to capture the importance of farming to the municipality. One measured the
percentage of the population living in rural areas. A second is the share of all land in the
region that is allocated to agriculture and the third variable is the share of total labour force
employed in agriculture. It is hypothesized that increases in the „ruralness‟ of the region will
increase the number of barn building permits issued in the municipality.
     State farmland area is an important determinant for both general receptivity of farming
operations and assimilative capacity for land-based manure disposal. States with greater
                    The pollution haven hypothesis and the location of livestock production   239

farming areas are assumed to be more receptive to livestock operations. Gollehon et al.
(2001) found that about 72% of large livestock operations had inadequate land capacity to
utilize all the manure based organic nitrogen produced from their operations and required
alternative disposal arrangements. Thus, the costs of manure disposal are likely to be lower
in states with more available farmland.
     Production shares are likely to increase in regions where the distance to market is
smaller, since transportation and transaction costs will be lower. For example, access to
slaughtering facilities was found to be positively related to the intensity of hog production
within 15 states by Roe et al. (2002). Market access is measured in the national US study by
the number of hogs and beef slaughtered within the state. Market access for dairy
processing capacity is captured by whole milk equivalent in thousand pounds used in
manufactured dairy products at the state level.

Results
Local analysis

The regression results explaining the density of total barn building permits, new barn
permits, and permits for swine facilities in south-western Ontario are reported in Table 14.1.
The adjusted R-square values are reasonable given the cross-sectional nature of the data but
relatively few coefficients are statistically significant across the reported regression
coefficients due to the panel nature of the data and the correlation among independent
variables.
     The first, third and fifth columns of Table 14.1 list the estimated coefficients when all
explanatory variables are included with the exception of the density and growth rates for the
dairy and beef sectors which are highly correlated to the respective values for the hog
sector. The high degree of correlation between the variables representing the importance
and growth of different farm types suggests that the municipalities under consideration do
not specialize in just one type of livestock. In addition, the percentage of the population that
is rural and the share of land allocated to agriculture are highly correlated with the share of
the workforce in agriculture and so only the latter is used as a measure of ruralness. While
the variables tend to have signs consistent with the initial hypotheses, the only statistically
significant coefficient when all explanatory variables are included tends to be the density of
hog production in the period prior to the collection of building permit data. In other words,
barn building occurred primarily in the traditional production regions.
     The subsequent columns in Table 14.1 report the results of dropping additional
variables on the basis of their degree of correlation with other variables and their
insignificance in the previous regression equations. The second column for each of the three
dependent variables contains only the explanatory measures that consistently remain
statistically significant. For example, there was a high degree of correlation (if one type of
regulation is imposed on livestock farmers, other requirements are also likely to be
imposed). The variables that remain statistically significant in the case of the density of
permits for all livestock facilities are the size at which a nutrient management plan is
required, the rate of growth in population density, and the growth rate in hog density.
Increases in all three variables were found to increase the density of barn building permits
issued by a municipality.

                    The pollution haven hypothesis and the location of livestock production   241

     The threshold livestock density of the proposed farm operation at which a nutrient
management plan is required does provide some limited support to the pollution haven
hypothesis. Farms are more likely to build in municipalities where the stocking density
restriction requiring a NMP is not as strict. However, the major driver on the relative
number of barn building permits issued by a municipality appears to be livestock
infrastructure and support.
     The positive effect of hog density in the prior period provides support for the findings
of Roe et al. (2002) on the importance of livestock infrastructure. Many of the permits
issued during this period were by municipalities within Perth and Huron counties, which are
the most livestock intensive counties in the province. These regions are more likely to have
direct and indirect support for livestock farmers in the form of services such as feed and
equipment dealers. The intensity of livestock production in these regions relative to other
municipalities under consideration may also explain the significance of the other findings.
Population density is greater in these livestock intensive regions which are located closer to
major urban centres than most of the other municipalities.
     The importance of agglomeration economies is supported by the results of the other
two dependent variables. The prior density of hog production and the growth rate of hog
production in the previous period are positively related to the density of permits issued for
new facilities and swine barns. The correlation coefficient between the prior density of hog
production and the subsequent growth rate in production is 0.7 suggesting that inventory
levels have increased primarily in the hog intensive regions. These areas also tend to have
higher farmland values reflecting the higher productivity of the land for agricultural
purposes and also closer proximity to major population centres. The latter effect may
explain the unexpected positive effect of population density.
     There is limited empirical support for the pollution haven hypothesis with the two other
specifications for the dependent variable. The significant variables related to environmental
stringency all have signs inconsistent with the view that firms located where environmental
regulations are laxest. The density of building permits for new barns and swine facilities
increases with the size of manure storage requirements. Most new barns and/or swine barns
normally involve storages large enough to hold the annual waste production so the
restriction may not be a constraint in the location decisions of new hog operations. The
minimum lot size was hypothesized to have an ambiguous effect on the density of building
permits. While it can increase costs immediately, it may reduce future costs associated with
potential conflicts with neighbours. The negative and statistically significant negative
coefficient on minimum lot size implies the latter effect dominates the former in the case of
swine barns.

National analysis

The regression results explaining the temporal changes in livestock inventories across US
states are listed in Table 14.2. A fixed effects rather than a random effects model was
estimated for each of the three livestock sectors on the basis of a Hausman specification
test. The resulting coefficients explain the variability in production numbers well with the
majority of the variables statistically significant.

The coefficient for the environmental regulatory stringency is negative and statistically
significant for the hog and dairy sectors with the relative effect largest in the hog sector. The
results suggest that a state‟s regulatory stringency curtails the share of national hog and
dairy numbers, thereby supporting the pollution haven hypothesis. The relationship runs
counter to previous studies in agriculture that found livestock increases with environmental
stringency (Metcalfe, 2001; Osei and Lakshminarayan, 1996; Mo and Abdalla, 1998). The
results of those studies suggest that inventory levels increase first and regulations follow
rather than the regulations being set ex ante with production decisions constrained by those
                    The pollution haven hypothesis and the location of livestock production    243

laws. The support for the pollution haven hypothesis in this study could be due to the use of
a time varying regulatory stringency measure and the accounting for possible endogeneity
between the measure and livestock production shares.
     In contrast to the hog and dairy sectors, there is no empirical support for the pollution
haven hypothesis in the beef sector. The difference in effects across livestock types
associated with the regulatory stringency index may be due to the nature of the production
changes by sector. The increase in hog and dairy inventory has been in non-traditional
production regions where environmental laws related to livestock farming appear to have
had an effect on the location of production. In contrast, beef production increased in only
the three states that had the largest numbers a generation ago. These remain relatively non-
populated regions so that expansion may have been influenced by factors other than
environmental regulations.
     Relative prices generally have mixed effects on inventory levels depending on the
sector. The output price-feed price ratio was statistically significant for both cattle and dairy
but was the expected positive sign for only the former sector. Wage rates have the
anticipated inverse relationship with growth rates in inventory levels across all sectors but
are insignificant for fed-cattle. The price variable that has a statistically significant effect on
production levels across states is the price of farmland. It was expected that increases in the
value of farm real estate would curtail relative livestock production. The opposite result
suggests farmers bid up the price of land as part of their expansion and potential concerns
regarding land availability relative to the volume of manure generated. The result may also
be due to locating in relatively productive agricultural regions after other factors including
availability and demographics are considered.
     Livestock infrastructure has significant effects on changes in production levels for all
three livestock sectors. Hog slaughtering capacity is positively related to production
increases, which is consistent with the findings of Roe et al. (2002) for 15 states. Similarly,
beef slaughtering capacity had a statistically significant positive effect on relative cattle
inventory levels which is consistent with the view that regional shifts in production have
coincided with shifts in beef packing location. An increase in the processing capacity of the
dairy sector was also positive implying that the access to markets is an important factor for
dairy expansion as it has been hypothesized for the red meat sector. The results support the
„animal clusters‟ argument that states with a larger inventory density tend to have a greater
processing capacity (Pagano and Abdalla, 1994).
     States with a larger proportion of agricultural output in its gross state product tend to
have a larger share of national livestock production but the size of the impact from this
livestock infrastructure variable is much less than for processing capacity. This variable
proxies both the level of services available to farmers and the level of support for
agriculture within a state (and consequently the likelihood of acceptance to production
expansion). Availability of common agricultural infrastructure (veterinary services, feed
availability, other input supplies) is an important factor for livestock industry expansion.
The results for the dairy sector are consistent with Weersink et al. (1995) who found that
dairy farmers place a significant level of importance on the availability and quality of farm
support services.
     It was expected that states with a larger share of total population that is rural would be
more likely to experience increases in relative livestock inventory levels across states. This
assertion was accepted for the fed-cattle sector but the opposite effect was statistically
significant for the other two sectors. A possible explanation for this result is that the
244    The pollution haven hypothesis and the location of livestock production

likelihood for conflict between farmers and neighbours is enhanced by population levels in
rural areas when all other factors are constant including land availability. Potential nuisance
complaints from non-farm rural residents could deter the expansion of livestock production
capabilities. In contrast, cattle production only increased in three states which have a
relatively large rural population so the potential conflicts are not an issue.
     Business climate and natural endowment variables have little explanatory power. The
unemployment rate has a negative effect on inventory shares across all three sectors but the
result is statistically insignificant. Farmland area is statistically significant but has an
unexpected negative effect in both the dairy and fed-cattle sectors. The inverse relationship
is consistent with the shift of dairy farming to the western states which have less agricultural
land as a share of total area due to the mountains as compared to traditional dairy regions in
the central and north-eastern regions of the country. The unexpected negative effect of
farmland area on the fed-cattle sector suggests inventory numbers are increasing in states
with smaller areas available for expansion. However, the increases in production shares
have occurred in three traditional producing states with large amounts of agricultural land.
The increase in production shares of fed-cattle by these states has been driven by relative
prices and infrastructure support.

Conclusions

This chapter has reviewed two studies analysing the factors affecting farm location with
particular emphasis on the influence of environmental pressure. Spatial differences in
regulatory stringency can create the possibility of pollution havens in which livestock farms
locate in the regions with the lowest regulatory compliance costs. The significant variation
in the stringency of environmental regulations across municipalities within the Canadian
province of Ontario and across states within the US created a natural experiment to test for
the pollution haven hypothesis at the local and national level.
     The local study by Weersink and Eveland (2006), which used actual building permit
data rather than aggregate regional production numbers, found little support for the
pollution haven hypothesis. Instead of locating to reduce environmental compliance costs,
barns are being built largely where the livestock sector is concentrated within south-western
Ontario suggesting the existence of agglomeration economies. The lack of a pollution haven
effect could be due to the limited geographical area of analysis. There are limitations to
spatial movement due to adjustment and moving costs from a farm‟s current location.
     If the farm is considering a significant expansion or the environmental constraints are
binding, these adjustment costs may be outweighed by the advantages of a move to a region
with less restrictive environmental regulations. Examples include some movement by large
hog operations to locate in the Prairie provinces from Ontario and particularly from Quebec
where limits were placed on the expansion of pig breeding operations in June 2002. The
move may also be prompted by more open area in the Prairies, which could also explain the
differences in environmental stringency. Similarly, the move by Dutch dairy farmers to
Ontario was prompted by increasingly restrictive environmental controls within the
Netherlands (Wolleswinkel and Weersink, 2001).
     The results at the national scale for the US livestock sector do provide support for the
conjecture that the effect of environmental pressure on farm location depends on the scale.
Previous studies at a larger spatial scale have tended to reject the pollution haven hypothesis
in agriculture and instead have found that inventory levels appear to increase first and
                    The pollution haven hypothesis and the location of livestock production    245

environmental regulations follow rather than the regulations being set and production
decisions then being constrained by those laws. By developing state-specific measures of
regulatory stringency over time and accounting for the potential endogeneity between
environmental regulations and production decisions, the study by Herath et al. (2004) was
able to conduct a more robust test of the pollution haven hypothesis. The results of the
analysis suggest that regional production shares for hogs, and to a lesser extent dairy, have
increased in those regions with relatively more lenient regulatory regimes. Combining
environmental stringency at a detailed spatial level and assessing differences as conducted
in the local analysis but with the temporal changes at a national scale would represent a
contribution to the literature on the location effects of regulations.
     In both studies, the location decisions of livestock facilities are largely determined by
economic factors associated with livestock infrastructure support. Livestock clusters are
occurring in North America and these are locating in agriculturally-intensive regions with
available farmland and nearby processing capacity. These results suggest that government
regulators can directly and indirectly have a major influence on the size of the animal
industry in their jurisdiction. Tightening compliance requirements and enforcement can
increase relative abatement costs to the point that livestock farmers may shift production to
another location. Aside from this direct impact, regulators can alter the incentives faced by
the processing sector to locate in their state. The location decisions of the fewer, but
significantly larger, processors in turn have a major influence on the spatial production of
the associated livestock sector.

Chapter 15

Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement
policies in potato production: assessing policies
in Canada, the Netherlands and the US

John Baptist D. Jatoe, Alfons Weersink, B. James Deaton and
Emmanuel K. Yiridoe



Introduction

The economic and environmental consequences of agriculture-induced pollution are of
growing concern in regions with intensive agricultural production systems. Cropping
systems based on potatoes may generate a wide range of residual problems – e.g., sediment
and nitrogen pollution – because high levels of fertilizer, pesticides, and tillage are applied
to, what are often, light-textured soils. Indeed, some potato-growing regions in the
Netherlands have nitrate concentrations in excess of the European Commission‟s
recommended limit of 50 mg NO3 l-1. Moreover, erosion is a problem in the north and
south-east regions of the country (Spaan et al., 2006; Fraters et al., 1998).
     These same residual issues confront policy makers in the Canadian province of Prince
Edward Island (PEI) where potatoes are the dominant crop in most farming systems.
Recently, a provincial task force examined means to reduce nitrate concentrations in
groundwater with soil erosion generally acknowledged as the other major environmental
problem (Savard et al., 2007; Carter and Sanderson, 2001; Edwards, 1988; and Milburn et
al., 1997). These problems are also an issue in the US. For example, in the US state of
Maine, potato cropping has been associated with severe soil erosion (Griffin et al., 2007;
Griffin and Porter, 2004; Grandy et al., 2002) and with the excessive nitrate levels found in
over 25% of wells sampled in the state (Williams et al., 1987). The issue of residual runoff
is important in all three settings because these countries are major potato producers. The US
and Canada rank fourth and 13th, respectively, while the Netherlands ranks ninth among the
world's top potato producers (FAO, 2008).
     The three regions with similar potato production systems and environmental issues are
using different agri-environmental policies to deal with the residuals, thereby resulting in a
natural experiment on the effectiveness of those mechanisms. This study applies these
different policy approaches to the same watershed to simulate differences in profits, costs,
and sediment abatement levels. Our results compare the cost-effectiveness of three different
policies. (We develop the conceptual approach and empirical methods in subsequent
sections.) Our study is a departure from previous studies some of which examine the

©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         247
248       Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement policies in potato production

reasons for differences in environmental stringency and the determinants of policy success
(Watkiss et al., 2004; Dasgupta et al., 2001; Caruso, 2000; Janicke, 1992). 1 Other studies
compare compliance costs to agri-environmental policies imposed on the agricultural sector
to assess the impact of these regulations on the relative competitiveness across countries or
sectors. For example, Jongeneel et al. (2007) estimate the compliance cost associated with
the Birds and Habitats Directive of the EU, for Spain (€33 ha -1), France (€190 ha-1) and the
Netherlands (€160 ha-1) and attribute differences to farm structure, management practices
and biophysical conditions.
     In contrast to the aforementioned literature, this study examines the cost effectiveness
of actual policies employed across regions on the same watershed comprised of a number of
farms. The various policy approaches – discussed more fully in section 3 – are imposed on a
programming model that allows us to examine economic and environmental outcomes from
agricultural production within a watershed. We derive the model using information about
agricultural production, topography, and environmental characteristics using data from an
actual watershed in PEI.

Conceptual framework

Our approach is to model economic and environmental outcomes under actual policy
regimes used within three different jurisdictions. The environmental outcome of concern in
the analysis is soil erosion and the amount of soil sediment deposited into a waterway
differs by farm with a given watershed area. This difference might be due to the
conservation practices employed – e.g. row crops versus cereal crops. Alternatively, the
same practices could result in different erosion levels depending on site characteristics such
as topography, soil type, and proximity to streams. In the remainder of this section, we
characterize our method by assuming two farms, A and B. This is a simplification of our
empirical approach, which examines many farms within a given watershed.
     In an unconstrained scenario – where no policies are in place to reduce the residuals
associated with agricultural production – both farm A and B are assumed to maximize profit
by employing inputs until the marginal value product gained from an incremental increase
in the input is just equal to its marginal cost. In addition, we assume that the farms are
homogeneous with respect to production technology, input costs, and output price. The
implication is that returns are the same across farms for a given practice – this assumption is
relaxed in the empirical analysis. However, the level of residual associated with a given
technology does differ across farms. Allow, for example, Farm A to produce a greater
amount of residual, per unit of output, than Farm B. This difference may be due to different
locations relative to a river.
     In the unconstrained scenario, profits in the watershed are maximized. Indeed, by
conventional assumption any policy that alters this input choice should reduce aggregate
profits. Moreover, by assumption, each farm produces the same level of output with the
same technology. However, Farm A deposits a higher amount of sediment into the water
course per unit of output because it is located next to the river. While the unconstrained
scenario generates the highest level of return from farming within the watershed, it is not


1
 These studies identify income per capita, degree of urbanization and industrialization, security of property rights
and general administrative efficiency as the major factors influencing the likelihood and effectiveness of
environmental policy.
                    Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement policies in potato production   249

socially optimal – i.e., maximize the present value of net-benefits for society – if the
resulting residuals from agricultural production cause damages.
     One approach to this problem is to impose a set of production practices on both farms
in the watershed. In actual application, such a policy might require all farms to use the same
cropping rotation. We refer to such a policy as the „uniform policy approach‟ because it
requires uniform production practices across all farms. Compared to the unconstrained
scenario, net revenue in the watershed would decline and so would residuals under the
assumption that the imposed crop rotations were effective. While total residuals decline, the
level of abatement will differ between farms. One cost-effective measure – the one we
employ in our empirical approach – is to estimate the „policy cost‟ as the difference between
profits between the unconstrained and constrained scenarios divided by the reduction in
level of residuals across the two scenarios.
     An alternative policy approach is the „targeted approach‟. Targeted policies identify
farms associated with relatively high runoff (i.e., Farm A in our scenario) and apply
specific regulations to those farms. Following a similar logic as the above scenario, when
compared to the unconstrained scenario, there will be a decline in the profits and residuals
associated with the targeted approach. The residuals associated with production will again
differ across the two farms. However, unlike the uniform policy, the distribution of costs
associated with the policy will differ. The cost-effectiveness of the targeted approach can be
estimated following a similar logic as above: compare profits in the constrained and
unconstrained scenario and divide by the reduction in residuals.
     In the following sections, our conceptual approach is developed and applied to a single
watershed. The specific policies, detailed in the next section, are currently in force
throughout Europe and North America. Our empirical results provide one measure to assess
policy differences.

Sediment abatement policies

The general nature of the sediment abatement policies for the three regions studied are
presented in an order based on the degree of targeting. The discussion begins with Maine,
which has the most uniform policy, followed by PEI and concludes with the Netherlands,
which has the most targeted set of abatement policies. The specific practices imposed by the
policies are outlined following the presentation of the three regions.

Maine, USA

The US Federal Agricultural and Improvement Reform (FAIR) Act of 1996 expanded
conservation and environmental coverage of previous farm bills, by establishing the
Environmental Quality Incentives Program (EQIP). EQIP is a voluntary, agri-environmental
programme that provides technical, financial and educational assistance to farmers through
the Natural Resources Conservation Service (NRCS). The 2002 Farm Bill reauthorized
EQIP through 2007 with greater funding and flexibility (NRCS, 2004). National priorities
under EQIP include reduction in soil erosion and sedimentation from agricultural land, and
reduction of non-point source pollutants such as sediment, nutrients, pesticides or excess
salinity (NRCS, 2004).
     EQIP is used in conjunction with state legislation to address agri-environmental issues
at the local level. In 1997, Maine enacted the Erosion and Sediment Control Law to
250     Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement policies in potato production

establish baseline measures of erosion control for use in watersheds throughout the state
(State of Maine Department of Environmental Protection, 1998). The appropriate
conservation priorities and abatement measures by region in the state are implemented with
the aid of EQIP in Maine through 16 Local Work Groups (LWGs) (NRCS, 2007). The
LWGs, which consist of staff from NRCS and other government agencies, determine the
resource concern, eligible practices to address the concern and the cost-share rates and
limits for those practices. The funded measures are uniformly recommended for the entire
watershed without discrimination by spatial location.

Prince Edward Island, Canada

Canada‟s Agricultural Policy Framework (APF), launched in June 2002, is based on five
pillars, one of which is the environment. Several agri-environmental programmes are funded
through this pillar including Environmental Farm Planning (EFP), the National Farm
Stewardship Program and the Greencover Canada Program (AAFC, 2005). The
Environmental Farm Plan (EFP) programme is voluntary and allows farmers to set priorities
for actions that address on-farm environmental concerns while the other two programmes
provide financial incentives to farmers who have or are implementing EFPs and are eligible
for assistance to implement the practices identified in their action plans (AAFC, 2005). PEI
provides provincial funding opportunities for agri-environmental programmes under the PEI
Enhanced Environmental Farm Plan (Government of PEI, 2006). Both the Canada-PEI
Agriculture Stewardship Program and the Sustainable Resource Conservation Program
provide technical and financial incentives to farmers to implement measures or Action Plans
identified under the EFP program (AAFC, 2005).
     In addition to the cost-sharing arrangements stemming from voluntary adoption of
EFPs, PEI has imposed design-based standards on potato farmers in an attempt to maintain
and improve the quality of soil and water (both surface and ground), as well as preserve soil
productivity. The PEI Agricultural Crop Rotation (ACR) Act, which was passed in 2002,
regulates the crops grown with potatoes, and the frequency and sequence of specific crops
in such rotation systems (Government of PEI, 2002). The ACR Act also prohibits row
cropping on farmland with a slope greater than 9% and stipulates that regulated crops may
be grown in a field no more than once in three years (except with the completion of an
EFP).

The Netherlands

The influence the CAP once had on patterns of production through production related
payments and market interventions has significantly decreased, and will decrease further
over the coming years. Support within the CAP has started to place a greater emphasis on
sustainability, the environment and rural development, encouraging the provision of public
or non-marketed goods. Dutch efforts to control water erosion and sedimentation have
focused on a multi-pronged approach using environmental legislation and incentive
programmes, as well as cooperation among stakeholders. One such program dubbed
„desired grassland‟ was introduced in 2004 and pays farmers for maintaining permanent
grassland or for converting arable land into permanent grassland in environmentally
sensitive areas or critical zones. The major programmes, however, are the Erosion
Ordinance and the Farm Erosion Management Plan (FEMP), which are designed to control
                    Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement policies in potato production   251

soil erosion through changes in land use and land management practices particularly for
crops susceptible to high erosion such as potatoes, maize and sugar beet (Spaan et al.,
2006). The Erosion Ordinance measures restrict practices related to tillage, cover crop, row
length, buffer strips and crop rotation based on the degree of slope in the field. Under the
FEMP, farmers are scored on the basis of their adoption of beneficial management practices
categorized by crop/tillage, landscape infrastructure and drainage system. Those who meet
threshold scores qualify for exemptions from certain measures under the Erosion Ordinance
with the scores varying spatially. While the Dutch measures are a mixture of regulations and
voluntary cost-share that are design-based similar to PEI, the Dutch Erosion Ordinance is a
more targeted policy than the PEI ACR Act.

Empirical model
Study area

The cost-effectiveness of the soil erosion policies across the three regions were examined
for one location – the Upper Wilmot River watershed, which is a relatively small
agricultural watershed of 8,339 ha located in the potato belt of PEI (see Figure 15.1). Over
70% of the watershed is allocated to agriculture, and row-crop potato farming is the
dominant agricultural land use in the watershed (Savard et al., 2007). The watershed was
selected for study because of its current environmental problems stemming from agriculture
and the availability of reliable data on observed stream flow for SWAT model calibration
and validation.
     The watershed was subdivided, based on surface hydrology, into 45 sub-basins using a
digital elevation model on a GIS platform (Figure 15.1). The sub-basins ranged in size from
1.02 ha to 438.68 ha, with an average of 99.44 ha and contained 353 individual hydrologic
response units. Each sub-basin represents a contiguous land area that delivers its pollution
to a common outlet.
     The division of the watershed into spatial units was done using the Soil and Water
Assessment Tool (SWAT), which was developed by the Agricultural Research Service of
the US Department of Agriculture (USDA-ARS) to assist resource managers assess the
long-term effects of farming practices on sediment and chemical yields in large watersheds
and river basins. SWAT uses input data such as climate, land cover, digital elevation and
stream network location to simulate average impacts of changes in land use and
management practices on water, sediment and agricultural chemical yields in large, complex
watersheds with varying soil, land use and management conditions over long periods of
time (DiLuzio et al., 2002; Neitsch et al., 2002). SWAT model outputs include stream flow
or water yield, sediment and nutrient yields, which can be traced across the watershed.
SWAT estimated the sediment loads at the hydrological response unit for the cropping
systems imposed under each policy regime as described in the next section.

Cropping systems

A potato-corn-potato-corn-clover rotation system under conventional tillage, without
surface residue and winter cover crop management was used as the base cropping system
across the three policy regimes (see Table 15.1). This cropping system generated the highest
agricultural returns for the whole watershed across 21 possible systems, when no residual
252     Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement policies in potato production

loading constraint was imposed on agricultural activities (Jatoe, 2008). Each of the 21
cropping systems was characterized by a crop rotation, tillage system and use or otherwise
of soil cover and surface residue management. A continuous potato crop rotation was not
considered among the 21 systems for agronomic reasons despite the high potential returns.


Figure 15.1 Upper Wilmot River watershed showing delineated sub-basins
                     Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement policies in potato production    253

Table 15.1 Base profit-maximizing system and additional practices imposed by region

Scenario                        Practices imposed by agri-environmental policy
Profit-maximizing base          -potato-corn-potato-corn-clover crop rotation
                                -conventional tillage
                                -no surface residue and winter cover crop
Maine                           -surface residue (mulch) on potato and corn at planting
                                -winter cover crop planted after harvest
PEI                             -winter cover crop planted after potato
                                -conservation tillage after cereal harvest in fall
                                -only first cut hay removed
                                -no row cropping on farmland with a slope greater than 9%
Netherlands                     -surface residue (mulch) on potato and corn at planting
                                -winter cover crop planted after harvest
                                -exemption for sub-basins with a slope less than 2%
                                -limit on crop row length on area with slope greater than 9%
                                -grasslands on farmland with a slope greater than 18%



Gross margins across the potential cropping systems varied across sub-basins primarily due
to the spatial variability in crop yields. Gross margins were derived as gross revenue minus
the estimated variable cost of production for each crop and tillage/cover crop practice by
soil class. Gross revenue for a given cropping system was the product of crop yield and
respective market prices. Yield data for each crop was obtained from the PEI Department of
Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry and adjusted for differences in soil productivity, thereby
allowing yields to vary across sub-basins within the watershed. Crop output prices were the
average real prices in PEI for 1996 to 2005. Input costs were based on enterprise budgets
for the crops compiled from the PEI Department of Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry
(1995; 1994) and the Ontario Ministry of Agriculture, Food and Rural Affairs (2005).
Annual gross margins for each cropping system were calculated by adding the annual gross
margin for each crop in the rotation and dividing by the length of the rotation (5 years).
     Within a sub-basin, there is substantial variability in profitability and sediment loads
across cropping systems. There are relatively minor differences in residual levels resulting
from alterations in the crop rotation but net returns are greatest when corn is included in
rotation with potatoes followed by wheat and then barley. The intensity of tillage is
inversely related to soil erosion levels and positively related to profitability. The greatest
changes between cropping systems arise from the inclusion of cover crops after harvest of
the potato crop. While cover crop management decreases gross margins by approximately
$50 ha-1, it significantly cuts sediment level.
     The base cropping system incorporates some of the BMPs selected from the Maine
BMP manual to qualify for government payments under EQIP, including crop rotation. The
only modification to the base system for the Maine scenario is the inclusion of a winter
cover crop for all sub-basins in the watershed. The crop rotation is also acceptable under the
PEI ACR Act as no sub-basins have slopes greater than 9%, which would have imposed
stricter PEI regulations. The additional general practice added for the PEI scenario was
conservation tillage to provide the 30% surface residue after planting conventionally tilled
potato, required by the policy. Key characteristics of the Dutch Erosion Ordinance
simulated included spring tillage (which replaced tillage in autumn after harvest), a winter
cover crop and row crops seeded directly in mulch on all sub-basins with an average slope
254     Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement policies in potato production

greater than 2%. The practices imposed spatially on the model for each jurisdiction‟s
policies are summarized in Table 15.1.
     The analysis considered two main types of costs associated with the individual policies.
One was the direct cost of implementing the management practices required or prescribed
under each policy. For example, the annual cost of chopping straw and spreading as mulch
was $125 ha-1 while the inclusion of a cover crop increased costs by $100 ha -1. The other
type of cost considered was imputed cost or the value of lost output due to policy-induced
decreases in yield for each scenario. The total abatement cost of each policy or policy-
induced cost was estimated as the difference between the gross margin for the benchmark
potato-grain corn-potato-grain corn-clover rotation system using conventional tillage with
no surface residue and winter cover crop and the gross margin for the same cropping system
but incorporating adjustments to management practices as required by the particular policy.

Results
Environmental effectiveness

The soil erosion levels for each of the 45 sub-basins in the watershed under the base
cropping system and the three policy regimes are listed in Table 15.2. Under the benchmark
cropping system, total sediment load for the entire watershed was 11,170 metric tons year -1
but there was substantial spatial variability across the sub-basins in the watershed. The
difference in sediment pollution across the watershed was due largely to differences in land
and soil characteristics such as soil texture, drainage and slope and elevation, as well as
location of sub-basin relative to the watershed outlet. For example, while both sub-basins 44
and 45 have well drained sandy soils, sub-basin 45 has a sediment load that is
approximately five times higher than sub-basin 44 because it is located at the main outlet of
the watershed while sub-basin 44 does not drain directly into surface water. In contrast, sub-
basins 1 and 12 are located close within the watershed but sub-basin 1 consists primarily of
well-drained, sandy loam soils that are more prone to erosion than the heavier soils in sub-
basin 12.
     Total sediment loads under each policy regime were lower than the unconstrained base
system. The uniform use of cover crops across all sub-basins (i.e., the Maine policy)
resulted in the lowest sediment load for the entire watershed at 5229 metric tons year -1. The
more targeted but restrictive policies in PEI and the Netherlands generated higher levels of
sediment than the Maine scenario at 10,015 metric tons year -1and 7752 metric tons year-1
respectively. Annual average sediment load was 1.17 metric tons ha-1 under the policy for
Maine, 1.73 metric tons ha-1 for the Netherlands and 2.24 metric tons ha-1 for PEI. The
differences in sediment load under the policies were due to differences in frequency of use
of surface residue and winter cover crop, area covered by the surface residue and winter
cover crop when used (uniformity of coverage) and use of fall (conservation) tillage.
     The policy for Maine, with the lowest sediment load, used surface residue and winter
cover crops on the entire cropland in four out of five years in each rotation cycle. There
were no requirements for the use of conservation tillage, which is a common recommended
practice to reduce soil erosion. Instead, the focus was on the uniform use of surface residue
(mulch) after planting and winter cover crop after harvesting both potato and grain corn for
farmland in the entire watershed. In this very flat watershed, the use of these practices is
more effective in reducing sediment than tillage and crop rotation options.

     The abatement policy for PEI had the highest sediment load because it used surface
residue and winter cover crop on cropland in only two out of five years in each rotation
256     Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement policies in potato production

cycle. The same crop rotation was used as in the Maine scenario but the restriction on cover
crops was only applied to certain crops rather than uniformly to all. In addition, the PEI
policy required conservation tillage but in the fall, along with removal of straw in the
second grain year before planting potatoes in the third year of the rotation. The watershed
did not contain any farmlands with slope greater than 9% and the subsequent tighter
restrictions on practices were not imposed. While the PEI policy required a number of
specific practices depending on the crop and land quality, the characteristics of the
watershed resulted in less sediment abated than the use of cover crops regardless of crop
and land characteristics as specified in the policy for Maine.
     The Dutch Erosion Ordinance had the potential to be the most restrictive, targeted
policy. Crop row length is limited for lands with slopes greater than 5% and grassland
obligatory for slopes greater than 18%. None of the sub-basins in the Wilmot watershed is
hilly enough to meet these criteria. However, two sub-basins are flatter than 2% and thus do
not require a cover crop in the winter or row crops to be sown directly into mulch.
Consequently, the erosion levels for those two regions are higher. In addition, conservation
tillage was required on the sub-basins with farmland slopes greater than 2%.

Cost-effectiveness

The gross margin for the base scenario in which a potato-corn-potato-corn-clover rotation is
grown under conventional tillage on all 1550 ha of farmland in the watershed is $1.36
million or $877 ha-1. The abatement cost, or reduction in returns from the gross margin for
the base scenario, is listed for each policy scenario by sub-basin in Table 15.3. The
abatement costs include the direct costs of the practice prescribed by the policy as well as
the indirect costs stemming from a potential reduction in yields.
     The uniform policy of Maine imposes the same cost across all sub-basins but without
negative impact on crop yields since it allowed conventional tillage. Therefore, the total
abatement (policy-induced) cost was limited to the annual cost, estimated at $100 ha -1. The
direct costs of a cover crop, along with chopping and spreading straw as mulch, is $100 ha-1
or $155,000 for the whole watershed (Table 15.3). This abatement cost is the lowest of the
three policy scenarios. Given the Maine policy also results in the least erosion, its
conservation mechanism is the most cost effective with an annual cost per unit of sediment
abated at $77 metric ton-1 (=$100 ha-1 /(2.47 metric ton ha-1 – 1.17 metric ton ha-1)) as listed
in Table 15.4.
     The targeted approaches of PEI and the Netherlands resulted in higher abatement costs
(Table 15.3). Total abatement cost of the policy for PEI was $185,675 due largely to the
requirement of conservation tillage in three out of five years. It is a significant change to the
farming system with higher operating costs and lower yields. Given the relatively low
level of sediment reduction, the PEI program is the least cost-effective with annual cost per
unit of sediment abated at $507 metric ton-1 (=$119.79 ha-1 / (2.47 metric ton ha-1 – 2.24
metric ton ha-1).
     The average abatement cost per unit of sediment for the Netherlands is $193 metric
ton-1, which falls between the results for the other two regions (Table 15.4). The absolute
level of abatement costs is the highest among the three approaches at $221,437 for the
entire watershed. While these total abatement costs are approximately 20% higher than
under the PEI scenario, soil erosion was 30% less than the base scenario compared to 10%
less for the PEI policy. The policy for the Netherlands targeted mulch and cover crops on
                      Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement policies in potato production      257

row crops but also prescribed conservation tillage on cropland having a slope above 2%,
which resulted in yield loss. The more frequent use of surface residue and winter cover crop
under the policy for the Netherlands increased its effectiveness in sediment reduction
compared to the PEI option, while the yield loss due to conservation tillage reduced its cost-
effectiveness relative to the policy for Maine.

                     Cost effectiveness of sediment abatement policies in potato production   259

Conclusions

The study evaluated the cost-effectiveness of sediment reduction policies for potato-based
farming systems used by the US state of Maine, the Canadian province of PEI and the
Netherlands. The practices imposed by the actual policies in each jurisdiction were
simulated under a biophysical model of a representative agricultural watershed to estimate
sediment loads and under an economic model to estimate abatement cost. The Maine policy
uniformly requires the use of cover crops and mulch across all farm fields in the watershed.
The policies in PEI and the Netherlands are targeted with restrictions on cropping choice
and tillage practice depending on spatial location and land characteristics, particularly slope
of the farmland. The degree of potential targeting is greatest with the Dutch system.
     The policies used in all three jurisdictions reduced sediment loads at relatively low cost
from the benchmark system but, surprisingly, the most cost-effective choice was the uniform
policy used in Maine. The legislative approach in Maine requires all farmers to use mulch
after planting, and establish winter cover after harvesting row crops (potatoes and grain
corn). The cover crop practice is low cost without negative effects on crop yield, and
reduces erosion better than more costly alternatives such as tillage management. The
targeted policies of the Netherlands and PEI impose tighter, and more costly, erosion
control practices with spatial location, particularly farmland slope. The costs of the tighter
and targeted controls even without the monitoring and enforcement costs are greater, and
generate a higher sediment load than the uniform policy focusing on the most cost-effective
practice for the watershed.
     The paradoxical finding regarding the cost-effectiveness of the uniform over the
targeted policy is due to two primary reasons. One is the nature of the watershed analysed.
Cover crops were the best means to reduce sediment loading but other practices such as
tillage and crop rotation could be a more appropriate means to cut erosion in another
location, particularly one with rolling topography. Second is the pollutant target of the
policy. This study has assumed sediment is the residual of focus and, consequently, cover
crops are the low-cost means for cutting back on that residual. Potato farming systems,
however, generate pollutants other than soil erosion, including nitrates. The choice of
practices may reflect an attempt to balance reducing multiple pollutants rather than an
incorrect choice of a single pollutant. While cover crops effectively reduce sediment, nitrate
levels are often higher so that a policy attempting to reduce both pollutants may not require
cover crops on all locations at all times in a rotation.
