This page intentionally left blank
Chapter 16

Environmental compliance costs in developed
country agricultures: implications for trade
and competitiveness

Glenn Fox and Floor Brouwer



General themes

The research reported in this book reflects a recognition that agriculture, in the OECD
countries in particular, is increasingly subject to environmental regulation. Increased
income per caput, stronger public interest in environmental stewardship and more awareness
of the origins and the attributes of the food supply have all contributed to a policy
transformation that transcends national boundaries. At the same time, modes of agricultural
production have become more specialized and input intensive and fewer and fewer people
have direct contact with primary agricultural production. Primary agriculture still occupies a
large share of the overall land base in most OECD countries, but generates only a small
percentage of Gross Domestic Product (GDP). Farm consolidation in response to economies
of size, scale and scope, often encouraged by commodity support policies, has changed the
social matrix within which agriculture functions.
     After a long period of being treated as an exemption, agriculture is now irrevocably
engaged in the long term secular trend toward trade liberalization under the GATT/WTO
institutional structure. Patterns of regional specialization in agricultural production are
emerging as trade barriers and transportation costs fall and as processing, distribution and
retailing functions have exploited their own size, scale and scope economies and as logistics
have improved. This increasingly economically and socially integrated developed country
agricultural economy is also being weaned from a not-yet forgotten dependence on
government price supports and transfer payments. Within this evolving context, farmers are
becoming more and more aware of the competitiveness and trade implications of domestic
policies and regulations. One especially contentious topic is the cost of compliance with
more and more demanding environmental regulations. The purpose of the research
presented in this book is to provide an overview of the nature of these compliance costs for
some of the important agricultural commodities produced in the agricultural economies of
the OECD countries and to assess the magnitude of those costs and their impacts on
competiveness and trade. In the pursuit of this purpose, however, the international team of
researchers involved in this project discovered other important findings that are related to


©CAB International 2012. The Economics of Regulation in Agriculture: Compliance with Public
and Private Standards (eds F.M. Brouwer, G. Fox and R.A. Jongeneel)                         263
264        Environmental compliance costs in developed country agricultures

this primary purpose, but that were not anticipated at the outset of the project. But this is
often the case in policy research.
     Initially, this research focussed on the cost and competitiveness effects of cross
compliance policy in the EU, where adherence to Statutory Management Requirements
(SMRs) has been imposed as a condition on eligibility for the Single Farm Payments
(SFPs). However, apart from previous applications of a cross compliance in the US during
the 1980s, other OECD jurisdictions have generally not used this model. The economic
explanation for this situation is simple. For export oriented commodities, such as dairy in
New Zealand and wheat in Canada, there is no programme payment to which compliance
with environmental regulations can be easily attached. So, as our research progressed, the
focus of the international competitiveness analysis turned to the costs of compliance with
environmental regulations directed toward agriculture generally. The research reported in
this volume documented the importance of enforcement mechanisms and implementation,
subjects which have been undervalued in previous research on agricultural environmental
policies.
     The particular focus of agricultural environmental policies studied in this research has
been on measures to reduce emissions of nitrates, pesticide policies, food safety measures,
animal welfare standards and wildlife habitat protection policies. This emphasis reflects
largely an EU perspective. In North America, in many state and provincial jurisdictions,
phosphorus emissions have emerged as a more important policy priority than nitrates. Most
of the available empirical evidence on agricultural environmental compliance costs is
related to nutrient management, pesticide policies, animal welfare standards and food safety
measures. Less empirical research has been done on the costs of compliance with wildlife
and habitat protection policies, although that is starting to change.

Reference standards for environmental performance in agriculture

A common feature of environmental regulation of agriculture across the countries
considered in this book is the specification of a reference level of environmental
performance for the farming sector. Most national policies directed toward improving the
environmental performance of agriculture generally, implicitly or explicitly, invoke a type
of threshold level of stewardship that separates environmental performance that farmers are
expected to achieve at their own expense and performance that exceeds the threshold and
for which farmers are partially or fully compensated. The levels at which these thresholds
have been set seem to be the product of unique historical contexts and there is no general
consistency across commodities or jurisdictions, creating considerable tension among farm
groups, especially for traded agricultural commodities, as well as environmental
constituencies.
     Each reference level or threshold represents a particular structure of agricultural
property rights. If a farmer is required to achieve a level of environmental performance
sufficient to satisfy a threshold, that implies that someone possesses a right to enjoy that
level of environmental performance. If a farmer is compensated for environmental
performance in excess of the reference level, then this implies that no one has a right to
enjoy that enhanced level of environmental performance. But agricultural environmental
problems are not generally viewed from a consistent property rights perspective 1. There are
a number of possible explanations for the limitations of the agricultural environmental
1
    Brubaker (1995, 2007) and Bate (2001) are noteworthy exceptions.
                        Environmental compliance costs in developed country agricultures    265

policy literature on the question of property rights. One contributing factor is the generally
low level of awareness of competing property rights theories in the discipline (see Fox,
forthcoming). Agricultural and environmental economists writing on property rights issues
appeal to at least five competing theories of property rights, theories which are incompatible
on several dimensions. Clarification of property rights issues in the interaction between
agricultural production practices and environmental outcomes is needed before significant
progress can be made on normative aspects of agricultural environmental policy. And lack
of resolution on these property rights controversies can derail policy action politically, as
rural landowners organize against what they perceive to be regulatory takings of property.
Even the justification of enforcement of environmental policy measures rests on a
foundation of property rights theory. Air and water borne emissions from agricultural
production systems constitute physical invasions of the property or the persons of
neighbouring property owners. Odour, pesticide drift, nutrients and pathogens, even stray
genetic material, if treated consistently as trespasses, nuisances or public nuisances, would
create liability for damages or even trigger injunctive relief against the agricultural practice
responsible for these torts. On the other hand, farmers who manage their land in a fashion
that reduces its productivity as wildlife habitat do not commit torts, since there is no
physical invasion of the property or persons of their neighbours.
     Another aspect of reference standards that became apparent during the course of the
research on this project has to do with general subjective perceptions of what constitutes
natural. Under a farm subsidy system where payments are decoupled from agricultural
output, there is a clear incentive for farmers to accept a payment and produce no
agricultural product. Anticipating this outcome, the EU introduced requirements that made
receipt of single farm payments conditional on maintaining existing agricultural land in
Good Agricultural and Environmental Conditions (GAECs) so that lands for which
payments are received are maintained for agricultural purposes. Within the European
context, for various social and cultural reasons related to income from tourism and to
aesthetic preferences for certain types of landscapes, the prospect of existing agricultural
land being abandoned to forest was considered unacceptable. Under this view, payments to
farmers could not be justified if land is idled. The GAECs, therefore, represent reference
standards or thresholds. In practice, these standards vary across Member States, indicating
differences in local preferences. However, the perceptions and preferences that are the
motivation for this policy in the EU seem to be quite different from those in the North
American context, where abandonment of farmland to forest has occurred, at least in
selected areas, and is generally regarded as a favourable environmental outcome. Of course,
this land abandonment to forest in the North American context has occurred largely as a
result of market forces, and not because of policy. Whether a single farm payment system
would be acceptable in North America without an equivalent GAEC standard is therefore an
open question. Nevertheless, differences in consumers‟ and citizens‟ preferences and
perceptions, at least as those preferences and perceptions are translated into trade actions,
appear to differ across the jurisdictions considered in this book and also appear to lie at the
heart of the long standing differences between the EU and North America on the use of
hormones in beef production and on the use of genetically modified organisms in
agriculture.
266    Environmental compliance costs in developed country agricultures

Voluntary private standards

Traditionally, economic research on environmental compliance costs has assumed that
government was the institution doing the regulating. Increasingly, however, businesses in
the supply chain that transform agricultural commodities for consumption are setting
environmental performance standards. These are sometimes referred to as voluntary
standards, which they are, but, for many farmers, these standards are a condition of access
to retail markets and food processing companies, without which, their farms would no
longer be economically viable. These voluntary standards are based on the perceptions,
generally of larger retailers, of consumer preferences for non-price attributes of food. These
may be process or product attributes, or a combination of both. Voluntary standards may or
may not coincide with mandates set by government regulations. Typically, where voluntary
standards are set on process or product attributes that are already regulated by governments,
they set higher standards than those required by governments. There appear to be distinct
differences between the monitoring and auditing standards supporting private voluntary
standards and those maintained by government regulators. Inspection and auditing at the
farm level typically takes place at least annually for private standards. Inspection rates for
compliance with government regulations are much lower, typically a small sampling
fraction being inspected in any given year. Voluntary or private standards tend to be
commodity specific, and not necessarily whole farm based. Since these standards are
technically voluntary, some farms might elect to not meet them; however, this is becoming
less and less viable as private voluntary standards become more established.
     A second additional dimension of testing of private standards has not been generally
recognized in the literature. Standards related to the environment, food safety and animal
welfare are largely about requirements regarding production methods and farm
management. Such process standards may vary largely across countries, depending on farm
structures, population density, links between urban and rural population, biophysical
conditions and public preferences. Product standards such as rules regarding product
quality, appearance and other physical attributes of food, like residues of pesticides, are
largely governed by trade agreements. However, this is far more difficult to implement in
process standards. Process standards are hard to compare among countries.
     In any case, these emerging private standards must meet a profitability test. Being
based on retailers‟ perceptions of consumer preferences, when products possessing the
attributes required by the standard come to the market, actual consumer choices either
confirm or contract the retailers‟ perceptions of consumer preferences. If consumers fail to
see value in these novel product attributes, the retailers will have committed an
entrepreneurial error, and will suffer accordingly in the competitive food retailing
environment. On the other hand, if consumers‟ choices confirm retailers‟ perceptions, those
retailers will be rewarded, affording them the opportunity to recoup whatever costs they
incurred in establishing the private standards. From a farmer‟s point of view, however,
private standards can represent a compliance cost just as much as government regulation
can.
                        Environmental compliance costs in developed country agricultures    267

Functional equivalence of environmental regulation

International assessments of differences in compliance costs of environmental regulations
applied to agriculture need to recognize the implications of differences in constitutional and
legal systems across jurisdictions, if they are to avoid misleading comparisons.
Environmental policymaking and responsibility for agricultural policy can take place at
different levels of government, depending on the constitutional structure of a given country.
In federal systems, divisions of authority exist that differentiate between powers of the
national government and the state or provincial governments. In non-federal policies,
divisions of powers can exist that delegate certain regulatory activities to local or municipal
governments. In the EU, the European Commission has initiated a range of agricultural
environmental policies, including the Nitrates Directive. In North and South America,
however, there is no regulatory agency comparable to the Commission. But it would be
incorrect to conclude that environmental regulation of agricultural practices is absent.
National and state or provincial governments, and even local or municipal governments, are
active participants in setting environmental policy that applies to agriculture. Comparisons
of environmental compliance costs for agricultural production need to recognize the
multiple levels of government that can initiate such regulations, as well as the roles of civil
or common law liability, the role of private environmental standards in supply chains as
well as the possibility of voluntary industry standards initiated by farm organizations. It is
only when all levels of government policy as well as private initiatives are taken into
account that we get a complete picture of the incentives that farmers face to achieve
particular environmental performance levels and the costs that they incur to achieve that
performance. Taking this systemic and comprehensive approach, as we have attempted to
do in this book, reveals that every one of the countries included in this analysis has
responded to concerns about excess nutrients, pesticide use, animal welfare, food safety and
wildlife habitat, as well as numerous other issues, within its jurisdiction. The perceived and
perhaps actual severity of national problems in each of these areas, however, does differ
across the countries included in this research.
     In addition to regulation by supra-national, national, regional and local governments,
civil and common law liability for trespass and nuisance also creates incentives for farmers
to meet environmental performance standards. In the UK, the Anglers Cooperative
Association (Bate, 2001) has a long history of using litigation, or the threat of litigation, to
mitigate water pollution problems in rivers.

Input intensity and environmental regulatory response

As illustrated by Goklany‟s (1999) insightful review of US air pollution policy,
environmental policy is generally reactive, not pro-active. Eveland et al. (2005) have
confirmed this generalization for the case of local regulations of large scale livestock
operations in Ontario, Canada. Environmental regulation responds to emerging conflicts,
generally over water and air quality issues, as competing demands for the use of air and
water come into conflict. In the case of agriculture, Rajsic et al. (Chapter 4 of this volume)
and Rajsic (2011) suggest that agricultural input intensity, coupled with increased human
population density, and perhaps with increases in income per caput, are correlated with the
emergence of such conflicts. As livestock density and nutrient application rates increase,
especially in areas of high human population density, conflicts over nutrient use and odours
268    Environmental compliance costs in developed country agricultures

from livestock manure application tend to rise. The hypothesis that follows from this view is
that environmental regulation is likely to emerge in higher input intensity locations. The
simple version of the pollution haven hypothesis is that polluting firms seek out locations
where environmental regulation is lax and hence environmental compliance costs are low. A
more dynamic theory is needed to explain the data that indicate that firms are often located
in areas with higher standards of environmental regulation. Goklany‟s theory offers this sort
of dynamic explanation, in that the regulation is a response to the operations of the firms,
not that the firms locate based on differential regulatory standards.
     This perspective has important implications for agricultural environmental regulation.
As the data presented by Rajsic et al. (Chapter 4 of this volume) show, there are substantial
differences in aggregate measures of input intensity among developed country agricultural
economies. If we accept those measures of input intensity as indicators of emerging or
existing environmental conflicts, then we would expect higher levels of environmental
regulation in those jurisdictions with those higher levels of input intensity. Data on
agricultural input intensity indicate that higher measures of that intensity often are observed
in regions where agricultural price supports and subsidies have, historically, been high. Of
course, there are and have been variations in support levels across countries and across
commodities, historically, and, within the EU, where there have often been similar support
levels across countries, there is considerable variability in input intensity, so a simple
generalization that higher support levels leads to higher input intensities does not do justice
to the more complex microeconomic incentives and effects. It is not difficult to understand,
however, that farmers respond to higher output prices, sometimes linked with input price
subsidies, by farming land more intensively. Over time, this higher level of input intensity
can contribute to environmental pressures and conflicts. And it is in precisely those contexts
where we could expect, following Goklany, environmental regulation of agriculture to be
most vigorously applied. This dynamic perspective is needed to understand many of the
emerging tensions involving agricultural environmental compliance costs.

Part II - Trade, competitiveness and public environmental concerns – a commodity
approach

Chapters in part II surveyed environmental regulation compliance issues for a range of
agricultural commodities with significant trade volumes. Specific regulatory compliance
issues were studied. The specific issues varied over commodities, naturally, as different
environmental conflicts have emerged in different agricultural production systems. Nitrate
management, pesticide residue control, animal welfare, food safety, groundwater quantity
management and use of hormones in livestock production and protection of wildlife habitat
were the most frequently identified environmental policy priorities with implications for
agricultural production in the countries and regions considered in this research.
     The main trade related policy concern addressed was the impact of agricultural
environmental compliance costs on competitiveness and trade. Empirical research on
environmental compliance costs in agriculture has lagged behind research in manufacturing
and resource extraction industries. This volume offers some preliminary findings on
agricultural environmental compliance costs to redress this gap in knowledge. Cross
compliance in the EU, per se, was not found to have a significant effect on trade, largely
because the measures involved were already largely embodied in and enforced through
various national regulations, so cross compliance did not represent appreciable additional
                        Environmental compliance costs in developed country agricultures   269

costs to farmers. But even when overall environmental compliance costs were compared,
the effects on trade were generally minor. This was especially true when the idea of
functional equivalence discussed earlier in this chapter was applied and when environmental
compliance costs were modelled as multilateral, not unilateral policy measures. Clearly, if
one country imposes an environmental regulation on its own farmers and farmers in a
competitive country are not subject to that same regulation, this could put the regulated
farmers at a competitive disadvantage. But our research has demonstrated that parallel
agricultural environmental regulatory processes are in place in all developed country
agricultural economies, so multilateral, not unilateral, regulation is the norm. This means
that only the differential environmental compliance costs would be expected to influence
trade volumes.
     Our research found that agriculture is much like other economic sectors in that
environmental compliance costs, overall, are small relative to production costs in total.
Environmental compliance costs are generally less than 5% of total unit costs, which means
that the incentive for pollution haven type relocation of production to jurisdictions with
more lenient environmental standards is weak. Other cost categories, such as land and
labour costs, logistics for input supplies or output processing, distribution and retailing,
proximity to markets and infrastructure, along with soil and climate factors, would appear to
play a more significant role in determining the location of primary agricultural production
than environmental compliance costs.
     Production costs and quality of produce are critical to competitiveness in international
markets and agricultural entrepreneurs will need to continue to search for competitive
advantages, either by reducing costs and/or by increasing the quality of produce. The
reward for this innovation takes the form of higher revenues or access to markets. A focus
on sustainability could provide competitive advantages relative to other producers, by
targeting at those consumers who are prepared to pay a bonus for food with more
sustainable production methods and/or higher quality of the final product. Such a strategy
seems to be most viable for niche markets and targeted on sales within (continental)
markets. Another opportunity might be knowledge-export of sustainable practices. It might
be less applicable for commodities traded globally.
     Efforts to promote sustainability in agriculture could be risky for bulk products that are
largely traded on the basis of price without much differentiation of product or process
attributes on the world market. Across the globe, there are many different views on
sustainable agriculture, and more specifically on the dimensions „people‟ and „planet‟.
Selling food commodities on the world market that are promoted as sustainable agriculture
is a complex undertaking. In some markets, price is more decisive than in other markets.
This can be explained by differences related to both „people‟ and „planet‟ aspects. Different
countries could have a wide range of views on labour conditions and on poverty. Another
example is the use of GMOs. The EU has only recently lifted a ban on the use on GMOs in
food and feed, whereas genetically modified corn and soybeans have become widely
accepted in Argentina and the USA. Also, restrictions with respect to the environment differ
from region to region (Brouwer et al., 2000).
270     Environmental compliance costs in developed country agricultures

Part III - Emerging policy perspectives

Beyond public goods and externalities

Most economic analysis of environmental problems associated with agricultural production
has been conducted using either public goods theory or externality theory. More recently,
work using club goods theory has offered an alternative view on the provision of
environmental goods and services provided by agricultural and other rural lands. This is
becoming a more important option for promoting the provision of wildlife habitat on
privately owned rural land. Several non-governmental organizations in North America,
sometimes organizations linked to hunting, fishing and outdoor recreation, but also
conservation organizations, are using a club goods approach to create incentives for farmers
to participate in production practices that are valued for their environmental benefits. Ducks
Unlimited, Delta Waterfowl and Trout Unlimited have developed programmes which use
membership contributions and subscriptions to compensate farmers for the costs of
installing conservation practices and/or the lost income that they incur when they provide
wildlife habitat on their farms.

Legalized nuisance and regulation – Market versus non-market failure

The concept of legalized nuisance was introduced to the environmental policy and
economics literature by Coase (1960), although little notice was taken of this concept at the
time. Coase criticized Pigou‟s treatment of environmental externalities as a category of
market failure based on the familiar narrative of the steam locomotive belching out smoke
and sparks as it travels across the countryside. Pigou, and, for that matter, many economics
textbook writers who followed him, used this apparent historical example from the
economic history of the United Kingdom as an example of an externality. The classic
Pigouvian remedy: emission taxes or subsidies to internalize the apparent externality. Coase
pointed out, however, that an important legal and institutional dimension was ignored in
Pigou‟s treatment of this example. Railroads operating in the United Kingdom, at the time
of the putative historical events in his illustration, operated under royal charters. Among
other things, this absolved railroads from liability for what, in the absence of these charters,
would have been actionable nuisances by farmers and other property owners along the
railroad lines. Consequently, what economists have often used as the quintessential
illustration of an externality market failure is actually an example of non-market or policy
failure. Coase‟s exposition of this systemic misdiagnosis are worth quoting directly (Coase,
1960, pp. 26, 27).

      Most economists seem to be unaware of all this. When they are prevented from
      sleeping at night by the roar of jet planes overhead (publicly authorized and perhaps
      publicly operated), are unable to think (or rest) in the day because of the noise and
      vibration from passing trains (publicly authorized and perhaps publicly operated), find
      it difficult to breathe because of the odour from a local sewage farm (publicly
      authorized and perhaps publicly operated) and are unable to escape because their
      driveways are blocked by a road obstruction (without any doubt, publicly devised),
      their nerves frayed and mental balance disturbed, they proceed to declaim about the
      disadvantages of private enterprise and the need for Government regulation. While
                       Environmental compliance costs in developed country agricultures    271

    most economists seem to be under a misapprehension concerning the character of the
    situation with which they are dealing, it is also the case that the activities which they
    would like to see stopped or curtailed may well be socially justified. It is all a question
    of weighing up the gains that would accrue from eliminating these harmful effects
    against the gains that accrue from allowing them to continue. Of course, it is likely that
    an extension of Government economic activity will often lead to this protection against
    action for nuisance being pushed further than is desirable. For one thing, the
    Government is likely to look with a benevolent eye on enterprises which it is itself
    promoting. For another, it is possible to describe the committing of a nuisance by
    public enterprise in a much more pleasant way than when the same thing is done by
    private enterprise.

     Charles Wolf (1979) went on to expand Coase‟s concept of legalized nuisance to a
more general condition that he called derived externalities. According to Wolf‟s definition,
a derived externality resembles a traditional externality, at least physically, in that it
involves the imposition of an uncompensated cost on a third party through the introduction
of some harmful physical substance or force onto the property of that third party. But a
derived externality has a different institutional or legal origin to an externality. Derived
externalities arise because of some prior policy action on the part of government. So what
does this have to do with environmental compliance costs in agriculture? Elizabeth
Brubaker‟s (2007) Greener Pastures documents how the concept of legalized nuisance has
been a contributing factor to pressure for policy change regarding the environmental effects
of farming. One method that several jurisdictions have used to respond to rising pressures,
especially between farm and non-farm neighbours, has been agricultural zoning or what is
sometimes called „Right to Farm‟ statutes. Under this approach, situations that would
normally be treated as farm-based nuisances are treated in much the same way that a royal
charter treated the nuisance effects of UK railroads, by absolving the perpetrator of the
nuisance of liability. Of course, such statutory enactments resolve emerging conflicts over
neighbouring land uses, at least temporarily, by changing property rights through legislative
law, but they do not address the underlying cause of the emerging tensions. And, in many
cases, protection of farmers under „Right to Farm‟ policies is now often conditional on
compliance of those farmers with nutrient and habitat management regulations.
     Technical barriers to trade in agricultural products were not the primary focus of this
research; however, restrictions on imports of beef produced with hormones and on GMO
crops to the EU have been a long standing source of conflict between particularly the EU
and the US and Canada. These tensions appear to be being resolved, but the analysis in
Chapter 12 indicates that technical barriers to trade may be a more enduring obstacle for
less developed countries. Scientific, technical and legal expertise is required to participate
effectively in international fora, especially in WTO disputes addressing technical barriers,
and less developed countries are often undersupplied with these forms of human capital.
And regulation of perceived or suspected environmental and human health risks is difficult
in the face of the scientific uncertainty involved. Demonstration of clear causality is often
beyond the scope of current scientific knowledge. But policy is not driven exclusively or
maybe even primarily, by science. Policy is often driven by preferences, perceptions and
expectations. Harmonization of standards and procedures is difficult and monitoring
compliance with those standards and procedures across international borders requires higher
levels of transparency and trust than are often available. And the outcome of all of the
272    Environmental compliance costs in developed country agricultures

influence of all of these factors, often, is limited access of less developed countries to
developed country markets.
     Consumer demands for health and safety attributes, as well as for environmental
stewardship attributes, are strengthening. Animal welfare concerns, as well, are increasingly
being expressed by consumers, through opinion surveys as well as through purchasing
decisions. Retailers and food processers are demanding higher and audited performance of
farming systems in response to changed consumer demands. In doing so, they promote
sustainable practices in agriculture. Therefore, agriculture must respond to and work with
others in the agrifood chain. Public-private partnerships may be the way forward for
meeting societal demands from the agricultural sector. The incorporation of environmental
concerns in marketing strategies from retailers could change farming practices and also
contribute to reduce efforts needed for meeting public policy objectives.
     It remains to be seen if these social trends toward stronger demands for non-traditional
attributes of food will be deflected by fluctuations in the economic fortunes of consumers.
Since the financial crisis of 2008, concerns about unemployment, government debt and
stagnant economic growth have supplanted many expressions of concern about the
environmental footprint of the food system. It remains to be seen if the return to price and
value considerations is a temporary or a longer term development in food consumption and
retailing. Fears of rising food prices, driven by general inflationary pressures and enhanced
by demand for agricultural materials for biofuel production have changed the perceptions of
the agriculture and food system from even a few years ago, when prosperity was more
generally taken for granted and environmental and animal welfare concerns were being
expressed more fully.
     Consumers and civil society are vital to changes in agriculture and the social dimension
will continue to have a major impact on the economic conditions and environmental
constraints of sustainable development of the sector. A „licence to produce‟ can be defined
as the specifically stated conditions from outside and within agriculture, which determine
the boundaries for agricultural activity. So, the concept of licence to produce indicates the
existence right of the agricultural sector, from an internal and external point of view
(Mureau, 2000; after definitions adopted from Shell). Currently, grazing dairy cows in
Dutch meadows are part of the licence to produce in dairy farming. In the EU context, they
are often seen as an icon of rural (farming) life and the accompanying landscape. Citizens
associate them with romantic impressions of agriculture. They consider it important for
animal health and also landscape matters (Van den Pol-van Dasselaar et al., 2002). But it
remains unknown how viable such systems are over time. Increasingly, dairy cows in the
Netherlands and elsewhere are kept housed inside and the products of the pasture are
delivered to them. This trend is mainly to further rationalize production and costs per unit of
output. Rationalization of production impacts the structure of agriculture (larger holdings
with more cows per farm and a reduction in the number of holdings), technological progress
(milking robot) and environmental regulations. We see responses in society in some regions
where a local premium is offered to the supply of milk from animals grazing outside
(CONO kaasmakers, Beemster). Wakker Dier launched a campaign in which they asked
municipalities to require local farmers to graze their cows. Some consumers indicate that
they are willing to pay a bonus for additional process attributes of food. However, the
cooperatives find that usually the cheapest milk sells best in the supermarket. The question
remains whether this discussion about grazing the cows, and other similar questions related
to structural aspects of modes of agricultural production, are temporary or permanent. Will
                        Environmental compliance costs in developed country agricultures     273

consumers accept the loss of such icons as grazing dairy cows? From a longer historical
perspective, changes in the agricultural landscape have occurred during the last century. The
adaptive capacity of citizens seems to be rather large. There is no guarantee that the next
generation will have the same concerns about grazing cows. Preferences on sustainable
production are subject to continuous change and grazing cows might not remain part of the
licence to produce in the long run.
     Can the agricultural sector benefit from this discussion on an international level? It
remains uncertain whether such a licence to produce could strengthen the agriculture sector
by promoting recreational activities. Can they sell grazing cows in Holland, like wooden
shoes or tulips (Keukenhof and Koeienhof) (Brouwer et al., 2007)? This remains uncertain.
The social dimensions of sustainable development create tensions between producers and
consumers and involve value judgements regarding landscape values, intensity of farm
production and the economic conditions of agriculture.
     The research reported in this volume, among other things, documents the range of
environmental issues associated with agricultural production in developed country
agricultures as well as the range of responses that have emerged in different jurisdictions.
But these various policy responses also illustrate what in other contexts has been called the
environmental policy paradox. The environmental policy paradox is sometimes stated as
follows. Environmental protection is generally viewed favourably by survey respondents
and voters. Environmental policy measures in the forms of environmental legislation and
regulation, since the 1970s, have embodied some of the more powerful policy instruments,
at least on paper, available to governments. For example, the US federal Endangered
Species Act trumps even national security, as habitat protection for the red-cockaded
woodpecker has curtailed military operations on important bases in the US.
     Nevertheless, and here is where the paradox becomes clear, objective assessments of
progress achieved by these theoretically powerful and broadly politically supported policy
initiatives have concluded that we have little to show for our efforts. This has led some
analysts to propose a reassessment of the overall approach to environmental policy, which
has for two generations generally adopted a centralized top-down approach. Hasnas (2009),
Pardy (2005), Morris (2008) and Ackerman and Stewart (1985), in different ways, have all
suggested that decentralized local approaches may do a better job of mobilizing scarce
environmental protection resources. These discussions have generally focussed on
environmental policies in the general economy. But they may have important insights for
future agricultural environmental policy.
